# External Binding

-> [Front Cover] <-

The volume is bound in a cover of plain parchment <note>also known as vellum, a surface made from processed animal skin</note> or leather. The material shows significant reddish-brown staining concentrated along the left edge. <note>This discoloration is likely the result of historical water damage or the natural aging of the organic material.</note> 

Attached to the right edge are two small loops of twine or string. <note>These loops were part of a closure system used to keep the manuscript tightly shut, protecting the internal pages from dust and environmental changes.</note> No titles, inscriptions, or decorative illustrations are visible on this surface.

-> [Spine of the Volume] <-

The spine is covered in the same cream-colored parchment as the rest of the binding. The title is written by hand in a clear, dark brown ink.

# Boccaccio’s
#### CONCERNING
# WOMEN <note>original Latin: "Mulieri." (a shortened or variant form of *mulieribus*, meaning women)</note>
# FAMOUS <note>original Latin: "Claris." (meaning famous, illustrious, or renowned)</note>

<note>This is the title of Giovanni Boccaccio's famous work, *De Mulieribus Claris*. Composed in the 14th century, it is a collection of 106 biographies of historical and mythological women, ranging from the biblical Eve to contemporary medieval figures.</note>

At the base of the spine, there are faint, illegible markings in a purple pigment. <note>These markings likely represent a later library shelf-mark or an inventory number used to organize the book within a specific collection.</note>

--- Page 3 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the description of the spine to the physical decoration of the book's edges. It focuses on the decorative treatment of the page block itself, following the identification of the work as Boccaccio's "On Famous Women."</meta>

-> ### The Decorative Edges of the Volume <-

The <term>fore-edge<gloss>the edge of a book's pages opposite the spine</gloss></term>, along with the top and bottom edges of the <term>book block<gloss>the collective body of pages within a binding</gloss></term>, are decorated with a fine pattern of **marbling**. 

The design features:
*   **Red** pigment applied in delicate, feathered waves.
*   **Blue-grey** tones interspersed throughout the pattern.

<note>Marbling was a popular technique in early modern bookbinding. It involved floating pigments on a liquid bath (usually thickened with seaweed or gum), "combing" the colors into intricate patterns, and then dipping the edges of the paper into the liquid to transfer the design. This not only added beauty but also served a practical purpose by hiding fingerprints and dust that might accumulate from frequent handling.</note>

The volume is held within its <term>vellum binding<gloss>a binding made from treated animal skin, typically calf, lamb, or goat</gloss></term>, which appears a stable, cream color compared to the vibrant marbled edges. Near the edges of the covers, there are traces of fabric **ties**. 

<note>Ties made of linen, silk, or leather were common on parchment-bound books. Because animal skin is sensitive to changes in humidity and tends to warp or "cockle," these ties were used to keep the book block compressed and the covers flat when the manuscript was not in use.</note>

<summary>This page describes the decorative marbled edges of the manuscript's pages, featuring a red and blue-grey pattern characteristic of early modern bookbinding aesthetics and preservation techniques.</summary>
<keywords>marbling, book block, fore-edge, vellum binding, book ties, decorative arts, book preservation</keywords>

-> ### The Marbled Fore-Edge <-

The image depicts the <term>fore-edge<gloss>the outer edges of the pages, opposite the spine</gloss></term> of the closed manuscript.

**Physical Description:**
There is no manuscript text or legible handwriting present on this surface. <note>It is important to note that this part of the book was intended for decoration rather than for carrying the primary text.</note> Instead, the stacked edges of the pages are decorated with a fine **marbled pattern** featuring:
*   **Red and purple** speckled pigments.
*   A dense, "dropped" color style typical of early modern book finishing.

On either side of the decorated <term>book block<gloss>the collective body of pages</gloss></term>, the edges of the worn <term>parchment<gloss>animal skin, such as calf or goat, prepared for writing or binding</gloss></term> or paper binding are visible.

<note>Marbling the edges of a book served two purposes: it provided an elegant finish suitable for a library, and it protected the paper. The pigments helped seal the edges against dust and made it easier to see if a page had been torn out, as the removal would break the consistency of the marbled pattern.</note>

<summary>This page describes the physical appearance of the manuscript's fore-edge, noting the absence of text and the presence of a decorative red and purple marbled pattern on the page edges.</summary>

# Physical Description: The Exterior Edges

-> ### The Marbled Fore-Edge <-

This view documents the <term>fore-edge<gloss>the outer edges of the pages, located opposite the book's spine</gloss></term> of the manuscript in its closed state. 

**Visual Characteristics:**
As noted in the physical survey, there is no manuscript text or legible handwriting present on this surface. <note>It was standard practice in early modern bookbinding to decorate the edges of the paper for both aesthetic and protective reasons.</note> The edges of the <term>book block<gloss>the collective pages of the manuscript</gloss></term> are treated with a decorative **marbled** or "sprinkled" pattern. This ornamentation includes:
*   A fine misting of **red and blue-purple** pigments.
*   A dense application of color that creates a speckled, textured appearance.

The volume is bound in a light-colored, sturdy <term>vellum<gloss>a fine parchment made from animal skin, typically calf, used for high-quality bindings</gloss></term> or parchment. The slightly worn edges of this binding material are visible where they meet the decorated page ends.

<note>Marbling the edges of a book served a dual purpose in a library: it provided a sophisticated finish and acted as a security measure. Because the pattern is continuous across the stack of pages, the removal of a single leaf would disrupt the visual flow of the marbling, making the theft or loss immediately apparent.</note>

# Folio 1 (Recto)

-> ### Blank Page <-

This page is entirely **blank**.

There is no written text or ornamentation present on this surface. The page shows the natural texture of the paper, along with faint **indentations** and <term>bleed-through<gloss>ink from the reverse side of the paper that has seeped through the fibers, making a mirror image visible on this side</gloss></term> from the writing on the following page.

<note>In historical manuscripts, it was common practice to leave the first few pages (known as flyleaves) blank. These served as a "buffer" to protect the primary text from dust, moisture, and wear that occurs when opening the cover. Sometimes these pages were later used by librarians or owners to record shelfmarks, titles, or names, but this specific leaf remains in its original, unwritten state.</note>

<summary>This page is physically blank, serving as a protective flyleaf at the beginning of the volume and showing only minor ink show-through from the reverse side.</summary>
<keywords>blank page, flyleaf, paper texture, bleed-through, manuscript structure</keywords>

# Folio 1 (Verso)

-> ### Blank Page <-

This page is physically **blank**.

There is no original writing, ornamentation, or illustrations present on this side of the leaf. The surface displays the natural, slightly irregular texture of the historical paper. 

Visible on this page is a significant amount of <term>bleed-through<gloss>also known as "show-through" or "ghosting," this occurs when ink or pigments from one side of a page seep through the paper fibers or are pressed into the opposite side over centuries, creating a mirrored image</term>. 

Through this ghosting effect, we can see the faint, reversed outline of an **engraving frame** and several lines of text. 

<note>The presence of such heavy bleed-through usually indicates that the next page (Folio 2 Recto) contains the manuscript's title page or a frontispiece. These pages often used denser inks or heavy pressure from printing plates, which frequently left impressions on the blank protective leaves preceding them.</note>

<summary>This page is a blank flyleaf that displays "ghosting" or bleed-through from the decorated title page that follows it in the volume.</summary>

-> ### Blank Page with Border <-

[This page is blank.]

However, unlike the previous leaf, this page contains a faint <term>border ruling<gloss>lines drawn on a page to define the margins and the intended area for writing</gloss> executed in red ink.

<note>The presence of these red-ink margins indicates that the page was professionally prepared for use. In the manuscript tradition, a scribe or <term>rubricator<gloss>a specialist who added red ink for headings or decorations</gloss> would often rule the pages before the actual text was written. This specific page may have been intended for a formal title, a table of contents, or a dedication that was ultimately never completed.</note>

-> Neither time nor death shall have victory over them,
Though they have been dead a thousand years or more;
For I desire that each of them shall be immortal,
Depicted in marble, or through poem or history <note>In the Renaissance, "history" (historia) and "poem" were seen as the two primary ways to preserve a person's soul and reputation after death.</note>.
For through me alone does one live forever;

<sig>A ii</sig>

# ¶ Vincenzo Baglioni, to his renowned and illustrious lady Lucrezia, daughter of the magnificent lord Rodolfo Baglioni, sends many greetings.
<note>The Latin abbreviation "S.P.D." stands for *Salutem Plurimam Dicit*, a formal Roman greeting.</note>

**C**onsidering one day those profound and golden words of the excellent and most distinguished historian Sallust <note>original: "Crispo Salustio." Gaius Sallustius Crispus was a famous Roman historian.</note> in the preface he wrote for the Jugurthine War <note>original: "historia iugurtina," a history of Rome's war against the Numidian King Jugurtha.</note>, which state in this form: "All things that are born perish, and those that have grown, grow old" <note>original: "Omnia orta occidūt & aucta senescūt."</note>. And seeing this to be true without any exception, I was much saddened and grieved by our misery and human frailty. I grieved even more when I recognized in our time those who not only deserve to live as long as Priam, Nestor, or Tithonus <note>In Greek mythology, these three figures were symbols of extreme longevity: Priam was the aged King of Troy, Nestor was the wise elder of the Greeks, and Tithonus was granted eternal life (though not eternal youth).</note>, and after their long lives to achieve perpetual praise and honor, but would even be worthy of eternal glory and immortality. Among these, my most renowned and most gracious lady, I judged you most worthy without any comparison—both for being born of a high and noble lineage, and for being most striking and beautiful (both of which are no small gifts granted to us mortals by nature); but much more for being endowed with such upright and holy character, and with such various and innumerable virtues, that anyone would judge them sufficient not only for you, but for a queen of this or of the other hemisphere <term>hemisphere (hemispero)</term> <gloss>The author suggests her virtues are great enough to rule half the world.</gloss>. And considering then—not long after, but rather almost in a single moment—how our weak, miserable, and poorly founded life can be said to bring the body to a little dust, and how fame is annihilated not long after, I felt within my heart a passion so great and bitter that the human tongue would not be sufficient to express it. And in this distress and pain I remained, until [sleep came to] my weary mind. <note>The text ends here as a transition to the next page, likely describing a dream or a vision following this period of melancholy.</note>

<summary>In this dedicatory letter, Vincenzo Baglioni addresses Lucrezia Baglioni, praising her noble birth, beauty, and virtue. He reflects on the fleeting nature of life and fame, citing the Roman historian Sallust to express his sorrow over human mortality.</summary>
<keywords>Vincenzo Baglioni, Lucrezia Baglioni, Sallust, mortality, fame, virtue, Italian Renaissance, dedication, Jugurthine War, longevity</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication by Vincenzo Baglioni to Lucrezia Baglioni. On the previous page, the author expressed his grief over the fleeting nature of human life, citing the Roman historian Sallust, and began to argue that Lucrezia’s virtues deserve immortality.</meta>

...I was forced to rest. And having fallen asleep, I saw in a vision a figure who, as soon as he had arrived in such a manner and form, began to speak:

"O how much, my Vincenzo, you are in a very great error, believing that this—or rather our—illustrious and divine lady could, after the fatal course of her long life, pass away without name and fame. For if Helen and Europa, for just a single grace of being most beautiful which they received from the heavenly influences, deserved to be celebrated by poets and historians; and if Lucretia and Virginia, for the single virtue of **purity** <term>pudicitia: a Roman virtue combining modesty, chastity, and sexual honor</term>, were to be most highly praised; and if Artemisia and the younger Antonia <note>Artemisia II of Caria and Antonia Minor were both famous in antiquity for their lifelong devotion to their deceased husbands.</note>, solely for having preserved their **widowhood** <term>uiduita: the state of being a widow, often idealized in this period as a virtuous "second' life of devotion</term> intact, have achieved eternal name and fame; and if Hortensia and Proba, for having been of high intellect and eloquence, are worthy to be venerated by us mortals on earth; and if Sulpicia, for being the only one chosen among a thousand for the building of the modest **temple** <note>original: "podico tẽpio." Likely a reference to Sulpicia, wife of Fulvius Flaccus, who was chosen as the most chaste woman in Rome to dedicate a statue to Venus Verticordia ("the Turner of Hearts") to protect women's virtue.</note>, has been able to reach everlasting glory and praise; and if many others—some for being adorned with infinite **modesty**, wisdom, and shame; some for being armed with **clemency**, **perseverance**, and **justice**; some for being also born of noble and royal blood—have deserved to enjoy eternal name and **immortality**. 

I pass over those who were most famous for unbridled lust, audacity, and greed. How much more, then, should this lady be celebrated by every man in every book and page? For she is not like the others, adorned with only a single grace and a single virtue, but all those virtues which, when found separately, make a person blessed, eternal, and immortal, all sprout, germinate, flower, and overflow within her equally and individually. 

This lady was first formed by a benign nature with incomparable **beauty**—rather, she was formed most beautiful by Love himself. This lady is a shining fountain and mirror of upright and holy **chastity** and modesty; this lady is a perpetual and immortal example of whole and immaculate widowhood; this lady is a honey-sweet, wide, and abundant river of mild, sweet, and angelic speech; this lady is most devoted to the building and restoring of temples..."

<summary>In this visionary sequence, a figure appears to Vincenzo Baglioni to rebuke his fear that Lucrezia’s memory will fade, arguing that she possesses the combined virtues of all the great women of antiquity.</summary>
<keywords>Vincenzo Baglioni, Lucrezia Baglioni, feminine virtue, Roman history, Helen of Troy, Lucretia, widowhood, eloquence, temple building</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the visionary speech begun on the previous page. The speaker in the vision is comparing the virtues of the deceased noblewoman (Lucrezia Baglioni) to the famous women of antiquity, such as Lucretia, Hortensia, and Sulpicia.</meta>

...but [she was] also most fervent toward God: and [given to] continuous fasts, alms, and most diligent prayer. She was an inviolable asylum of infinite modesty, wisdom, and purity; she was the unique lodging and nest of clemency, perseverance, and justice. She was born of a noble lineage and high offspring, the sister of the most reverend Bishop Troilo <note>Troilo Baglioni (d. 1506) was a member of the ruling family of Perugia and served as Bishop of the city.</note> and of that magnanimous, unconquered, and glorious captain Giovan Paolo Baglioni <note>Giovan Paolo Baglioni (c. 1470–1520) was a famous condottiero (mercenary captain) and the de facto ruler of Perugia.</note>; and she was the wife of that strenuous and most excellent captain Camillo Vitelli <note>Camillo Vitelli (1459–1496) was a renowned military leader from Città di Castello and an ally of the Baglioni family.</note>. She was, finally, magnanimous, liberal, and gracious. Who could ever briefly recount every praise and virtue of hers? Who would be the one who, speaking of her, would not rather grow weary than feel satisfied in praising, extolling, and exalting her? 

How happy will he be who can adorn her deeds with such worthy, ample, and laudable subject matter, inciting the envy of even the poet of Smyrna <note>original: "ql de smyrna." A reference to Homer, who was traditionally said to be from Smyrna.</note> and the Mantuan poet <note>original: "del mãtuano poeta." A reference to Virgil, who was born near Mantua.</note>. How much I grieve that I was not born in this happy and golden age to have been able to know her—or at least to have lived after her time—so that I might have been able to understand her singular, egregious virtues and ornaments, and then describe them. But since heaven has been contrary to both—or rather to me alone, who, against my desire, has made me unworthy of such a noble subject and worthy material. 

I want that work *On Famous Women* <term>original: "claris mulierib9" (De Mulieribus Claris). A famous collection of biographies of historical and mythological women written by Giovanni Boccaccio.</term> composed by me and dedicated to Lady Giovanna—which for a long time has been unknown and hidden, not daring to reveal itself for fear of the censorship of malignant and detracting men—to be dedicated to her through you, so that under the shadow of her optimal judgment, it may be defended and safe from every envious person and slanderer." 

And having said these words, he immediately departed. Then, as happens at the end of a vision, I suddenly awoke; and reflecting, I realized that he who named the work must have been our Master Giovanni Boccaccio. And having searched for it, I found it after some days. Wherefore, my magnificent and generous Lady, whether by the command of this author, or also because...

<sig>A iii</sig>

<summary>The narrator recounts a vision of Giovanni Boccaccio, who praises the virtues and noble lineage of Lucrezia Baglioni and commands that his work 'On Famous Women' be dedicated to her for protection against critics.</summary>
<keywords>Giovanni Boccaccio, Lucrezia Baglioni, Giovan Paolo Baglioni, Camillo Vitelli, Troilo Baglioni, De Mulieribus Claris, Perugia, Renaissance virtues, literary dedication</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the general praise of the Baglioni family seen on the previous page to a specific dedication and celebratory sonnet addressed to Lucrezia Baglioni. The author, Vincenzo Bagli, formally presents the work to her, invoking classical authority and comparing her to her famous namesake from Roman history.</meta>

<sig>A</sig>

...for as the Philosopher <note>A standard Renaissance reference to Aristotle, who famously discussed the attraction between like things in his works on ethics and nature.</note> says, every like desires its like: this work being conformed to your innumerable virtues and adorned manners. And since you are more illustrious and distinguished than any other, I wanted to dedicate <note>original: "intitulare"</note> this present and useful work to you, as you are most worthy of it above all others. Thus, if this work produces no offense in you, may you deign to accept it—not because I think this work will be the cause of your immortality, but so that, under the wing and shadow of your true and upright judgment, it may be defended and safe from every biting word and censure. Through your merits, as the author himself desires, may it enjoy perpetual immortality and inextinguishable glory. Farewell, and again, farewell <note>original: "Vale atq; iterum Vale," a traditional Latin closing.</note>.

### ¶ The same Vincenzo Bagli to Lady Lucretia

> ¶ When, my Lady, I well consider and see
> Your excellent virtues and your manners
> Among so many illustrious women and bright deities
> Who are written of here, I compare none to you.
> But I believe the prophetic signs of your parents,
> Seeing you as a light of chastity <note>original: "pudicitia"</note>,
> They called you Lucretia after the sacred river <note>A reference to the Tiber in Rome. The poet suggests her parents foresaw her virtue and named her after the Roman heroine Lucretia, whose legendary chastity was famously tied to the history of the city.</note>,
> The first reason to ascend to the high seat.
> And then, to make it even more manifest,
> Nature created both <note>Both the Roman Lucretia and the Perugian Lucrezia Baglioni.</note> beautiful and kind,
> Noble, prudent, graceful, and honest,
> A lodging of virtue, a mirror, and a fountain.
> But your only difference is this:
> You are Perugian <note>original: "peruſina"</note> and she was Roman.

<summary>This page contains a formal dedication of the book to Lucrezia Baglioni and a sonnet by Vincenzo Bagli that compares her virtue to the famous Roman Lucretia, concluding that the only difference between them is their city of origin.</summary>
<keywords>Vincenzo Bagli, Lucrezia Baglioni, Perugia, Roman Lucretia, virtue, Renaissance poetry, dedication, chastity</keywords>

This page begins a new section: the Preface (Proemio) of Giovanni Boccaccio’s famous work "De mulieribus claris" (On Famous Women). This appears to be a vernacular Italian translation of Boccaccio's original Latin text, likely printed or transcribed in the late 15th or early 16th century.</meta>

# PREFACE

### ¶ Here begins the book entitled *On Famous Women*, composed by the most illustrious poet, Master Giovanni Boccaccio, poet laureate of Certaldo <note>Certaldo is a town in Tuscany, the birthplace and home of Boccaccio.</note>.

In days past, besides other noble and distinguished women, finding myself somewhat separated from the neglectful common crowd and almost released from every other duty of mine, [I turned my mind] to the high praise of the female sex. And certainly, more for the pleasure of friends than for any great utility to the state <note>original: "republica." While Boccaccio often wrote for personal or courtly reasons, he frames his labor here as a service to his social circle.</note>, I wrote a small book. 

Truly, as I thought and turned over in my mind to whom I should first send it—so that it might not remain hidden with me, but rather, aided by another’s favor, might go more safely into public view—and knowing well that it was not suited to be sent to any prince, because it speaks only of the fame <note>original: "clarita," referring to being 'illustrious' or 'clear' in reputation.</note> of women, but rather ought to be sent to a noble lady. 

And as I searched my mind for the most noble among them, there came before me, ahead of all others, that splendid Italian light and singular glory, not only of great women but of kings: Joanna, most serene Queen of Jerusalem and of Sicily <note>Joanna I of Naples (1326–1382), who held the titles of Queen of Naples, Jerusalem, and Sicily. She was a major patron of the arts and a contemporary of Boccaccio.</note>. Thinking upon the splendor of her illustrious lineage, the glorious fame of her most serene ancestors, and the new and highest praises acquired by her through the virtue of her most courageous heart, a desire fell upon me to most humbly and devotedly send this work before the throne of her Royal Majesty. 

Yet, truly, when I consider her glowing and immeasurable splendor, and then look at the small light—almost a half-extinguished spark—of this little book, I fear that by moving from a greater to a lesser light, it might be swallowed entirely by shadows...

<summary>This page contains the opening of the Preface to Boccaccio’s "On Famous Women," where the author explains his decision to dedicate the work to Queen Joanna I of Naples, praising her virtue and royal lineage.</summary>

<keywords>Giovanni Boccaccio, On Famous Women, Queen Joanna I of Naples, Preface, Certaldo, women's history, Italian literature, Renaissance patronage</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the Preface (Proemio) of Boccaccio’s "Concerning Famous Women." While the previous page addressed Queen Joanna I of Naples, the author now transitions his focus to the specific recipient of this dedication: Andrea Acciaioli, the Countess of Altavilla. He explains that while the Queen is the supreme example, he has chosen to dedicate this specific volume to the Countess because of her exceptional virtue and intellect, which he characterizes as "manly" in its strength—a common Renaissance compliment for intellectual women.</meta>

## PREFACE

Having changed my plan with sincere feeling, and having newly examined many women after the most illustrious Queen, I have turned and fixed my devotion entirely upon you. This occurs whenever I recall in my mind your celebrated and gentle manners, or your integrity—the true and supreme ornament of women—or your ornate and wise speech; when I think of and look upon the generosity of your soul and the power of your intellect, by which I believe you far surpass the limits of feminine strength. 

I see also that what nature has withheld from the weaker sex, God, through His liberality and grace, has inspired with marvelous virtue and added into your breast. And so that you might be as adorned by your deeds as you are by your name—for *andros* in the Greek language signifies "men" in Latin <note>The author is making an etymological pun on the recipient's name, Andrea. He notes that the Greek root *aner/andros* means "man," suggesting she possesses a "manly" or courageous spirit that transcends typical gender expectations of the time.</note>—it seems to me that for all these reasons I can worthily equal you to any most virtuous woman, even those of the most ancient times. 

Therefore, since in our day you are a most clear mirror of ancient virtue and integrity to many who occupy themselves with splendid and virtuous things, I have deliberated to add your splendor, as a thing worthy of it, to the title and gift of this little book. I believe that I shall add no less honor to your fame with these few "little letters" <note>original: "letteruzze." This is a modest, self-deprecating way for the author to refer to his own writing.</note> than those who came before us did for the Countesses of Monteodorisio, or presently for the Countess of Altavilla <note>Andrea Acciaioli held both these titles; she was a prominent noblewoman in the Kingdom of Naples.</note>, through which fortune has made you illustrious and noble. 

I send this, therefore, to you, and to your name I direct what I previously wrote concerning noble and famous women. I pray you, magnificent and illustrious lady, by that sacred name of modesty <note>original: "pudicitia." This term encompasses chastity, modesty, and the proper social conduct of a noblewoman.</note> for which you are glorious among the immortals, that you receive this little gift from a worthy scholar with a happy mind. And if you should ever believe anything of me, I advise you to read it from time to time; for without fail, its teachings will give solace to your ears when you take pleasure in feminine virtues and the clarity of these histories. Nor do I think...

<summary>Boccaccio (or his translator) redirects the book's dedication to Andrea Acciaioli, praising her for possessing "manly" intellectual virtues that exceed her sex. He frames the book as a "mirror of ancient virtue" intended to provide both moral instruction and historical pleasure.</summary>
<keywords>Andrea Acciaioli, Altavilla, Monteodorisio, feminine virtue, andros, Greek etymology, modesty, Renaissance dedication</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the Preface (Proemio) begun on the previous page. Having addressed the Countess Andrea Acciaioli and praised her "manly" intellect, the author now offers guidance on how she should read the biographies that follow—warning that they contain both vice and virtue—and explains the spiritual purpose of the work.</meta>

<header>PREFACE</header>

# PREFACE

Your reading will be in vain if, while considering the great virtue of women of the past with praiseworthy envy, your own noble spirit <note>original: "aío," a contracted form of *animo*, meaning spirit or mind</note> is not moved to better itself. And although you will sometimes find vicious things mixed with virtuous ones—as I must do this to observe the true order of history—you should not for that reason stop reading, nor hold the work in disdain. Instead, do as you do when you find yourself in a beautiful garden: leaving behind the thorns and the stinging brambles, stretch out your most delicate hands to the fragrant flowers. 

In this way, leave aside the dishonest things and gather and take up those things you find there to be praiseworthy. And whenever you read of some pagan woman performing virtuous deeds that would be appropriate for a Christian woman, yet you feel those virtues are lacking in yourself, let it sting and rebuke you. Stir up shame in your mind that you—who are anointed with the sacred chrism of Christ <note>original: "xpõ," a traditional abbreviation for Christ using the Greek letters Chi and Rho</note>—should be exceeded and surpassed in honesty, in modesty <term>pudicítía</term>, in constancy, or in any other virtue by a pagan woman. Your noble intellect and strength should be driven toward even greater power. 

Do not merely suffer to be overtaken by them; rather, strive to surpass them with your own virtue. Just as you are splendid in the beautiful form of your body, with joyful youth and flowering beauty, so may you shine above all women (not only those of your own time) through the purity <note>The OCR reads "ímondítía" (impurity), but the context and Latin original *munditia* indicate Boccaccio is praising her "purity" or "neatness" of soul.</note> of your spirit. 

Remember that you ought not to adorn your beauty with ointments or makeup, as most women do, but you must be adorned with modesty, with honesty, and with most holy works. By making your beauty pleasing to God—who gave it to you—you will not only be one of the most famous women in this mortal existence <note>original: "pítura uíta de morte," literally a "painted life of death," referring to the fleeting, transitory nature of earthly life compared to the eternal</note>, but when your soul <note>original: "laía," a contraction of *l'anima*</note> departs from your body, it shall be placed by God himself, the giver of all graces, in the clear and perpetual light of glory. 

And then, if it pleases you, O most shining mirror of the most honest women, give your permission and help to this little book, so that it may boldly reveal itself. For I rightly believe that if it is sent forth from your presence, it will go safely protected from the attacks of slanderers.

<summary>Boccaccio concludes his preface by urging the Countess to emulate the virtues of the women in his book while avoiding their vices, framing the work as a guide for spiritual and moral self-improvement.</summary>
<keywords>virtue vs. vice, garden metaphor, Christian vs. Pagan women, moral adornment, slander, Andrea Acciaioli</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page concludes the first preface to Andrea Acciaioli and introduces the author’s second prologue, which explains his motivation for writing a collection dedicated specifically to women. It follows the author's advice on how to read the book for moral improvement.</meta>

# PREFACE

This little book <note>original: "picciolo libretto," a modest term often used by Renaissance authors to describe their work</note>—together with your own noble fame and that of the women named within it—shall be carried through the mouths of men. Since you cannot be in every place at once, this book, together with your own merits, will make you known to those of this time and leave you with eternal fame for those of the future.

¶ Here begins the second prologue of the entire book, in which the author demonstrates what a great wonder it is that in times past no mention was made of the female sex, especially of virtuous women; considering that one of their memorable acts is more noteworthy than those of men.

Many ancient sages in times past wrote brief books about illustrious men. And in our own time, the noble gentleman and distinguished poet Master Francesco Petrarch <note>Petrarch (1304–1374), a foundational figure of the Renaissance, wrote a famous series of biographies titled *De viris illustribus* (On Famous Men)</note>, our teacher, wrote of them with a more expansive volume and in a diligent style. This was done worthily, for those who commit their study, wealth, blood, and life to advancing themselves through clear and excellent deeds and works certainly deserve that their names be kept in perpetual fame and memory by those who come after them. 

But truly, I wonder greatly how women have had so little influence or favor among noble men that no great memory of them has been preserved in any specific account <note>original: "discretiōe," here meaning a distinct or separate treatment</note>. This is despite the fact that it clearly appears through more extensive histories that women have performed very great deeds with virtue. And if men are to be praised—to whom nature has granted strength when they perform great things—all the more should those women be praised who are by nature weak, soft, and tender of body, and slow of wit <note>A common Renaissance trope: the author contrasts the perceived "biological" weakness of women with the "manly" strength of their actual achievements to make their virtues seem even more miraculous</note>. If they, with a manly spirit <term>uerile aio</term>, with a joyful wit and most clear virtue, perform and have the daring to perform things great and difficult even for men, they are most worthy of praise. Therefore, so that they may not be defrauded or deprived of their merit, it has come into my mind to write of those who were memorable and worthy of memory.

<summary>The author concludes his dedication to a noblewoman and begins a second prologue, arguing that virtuous women deserve their own historical record because their achievements are more remarkable given the "weakness" society attributes to their sex.</summary>
<keywords>fame, illustrious women, Francesco Petrarch, gender roles, virtue, historical memory, manly spirit</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's second prologue, where he justifies his selection criteria for the women included in this collection. He transitions from discussing the lack of historical records for women to explaining why he includes both virtuous and notorious figures, paralleling the way historians treat famous men.</meta>

# PREFACE

...to bring them together into one volume in their honor and glory. And to these, I shall add several of the many women whom either boldness, the strength of their intellect, their industry, the gift of nature, the favor of fortune, or even some great injury has made notable. Alongside these, we shall accompany some others who, although they have not performed deeds worthy of memory, nevertheless provided the cause for great events. 

Nor do I wish it to appear to people as something incongruous or unsuitable if—alongside **Penelope**, **Lucretia**, and **Sulpitia** <note>These three were archetypes of female virtue: Penelope was the faithful wife of Odysseus; Lucretia was a Roman noblewoman whose suicide after being raped sparked the Roman Republic; Sulpitia was considered the most chaste woman in Rome.</note>, who were most honest and prudent women—we find mixed in **Medea**, **Flora** <note>The text reads "Flopia," likely a printer's error for "Flora," the Roman goddess or the famous courtesan often cited in moralizing literature.</note>, and **Sempronia** <note>Medea was a sorceress known for killing her children; Sempronia was a Roman woman involved in the Catiline conspiracy, known for her brilliant mind but lack of morals.</note>, or others like them. These women were possessed of great intellect, regardless of the fact that the word "fame" <note>original: "clarita," referring to the "clarity" or "brightness" of one's reputation</note> has been so strictly tied to immortal verse that it is almost always taken to signify virtue. 

However, with the readers' indulgence, I intend to grant this term a broader meaning. It is my intention to consider as "famous women" <note>original: "dōne clare"</note> those who have become known throughout the world by common report for any kind of notable deed. For I remember having read that among the Scipios, the Catos, and the Fabritii <note>The Scipios, Catos, and Fabritii were families of the highest Roman virtue and military leadership.</note>—who were most illustrious men—there were also the controversial and embattled Gracchi, the duplicitous **Hannibal**, that traitor **Jugurtha**, and those stained with the blood of the civil wars, **Sulla** and **Marius**, and together with them the petty and greedy **Crassus**. 

Truly, historians have elevated those things worthy of memory through praise, and have often lowered through condemnation those things that ought not to be spoken of. This is done not only to lead those of noble spirit toward glory through praise, but also to gradually pull the lazy away from their troubles through condemnation. Furthermore, this work serves to restore or rebuild those accounts which, through the lack of some small ancient writing, appear to have been lost. Wherefore I have proposed to occasionally mix into these histories some enticement toward virtue and to sting the reader with a detestation of [wicked] stories, so that they may take root in the minds of the readers. This is done so that it does not appear that these things, known from men worthy of trust, touch only upon the surface...

<summary>The author explains his decision to include both virtuous and "notorious" women in his collection, arguing that fame applies to any notable deed. He compares his approach to classical historians who included both heroes like Scipio and villains like Jugurtha to teach moral lessons.</summary>

<keywords>female virtue, Penelope, Lucretia, Medea, notoriety, historical methodology, Roman history, Boccaccio, fame</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Proemio" or Preface to Giovanni Boccaccio’s famous work on illustrious women. The author is transitioning from his general intent to the specific methodology of the book, particularly why he chooses to focus on "pagan" women rather than Christian saints.</meta>

# PREFACE

<note>The text begins with an indented line where the initial letter 'S' was likely intended to be illuminated or rubricated by hand after printing, making the first word "Se" (If).</note>

If the outer shell <note>original: "scorteccia," referring to the superficial or literal surface of the text</note>, according to the ancient manner, [is considered], I believe the weight that this present work ventures into—a more ample and long history—to be not only useful, but a necessity. For I judge that these said histories provide no less pleasure to women than to men, although women generally know fewer historical accounts. Consequently, they require a longer discourse regarding the causes [of events]. 

For this reason, it has not seemed fit to me to mix any sacred Hebrew or Christian woman among these pagan women, except for **Eve**, the first mother of us all. This is because there is a disparity and lack of conformity between them, and it does not seem to me that they walk in equal rank. Truly, the holy Christian and Hebrew women constrained themselves and performed virtues of constancy in enduring adverse things by following the tracks, the commandments, and the works of the eternal Preceptor, **Christ**, in order to acquire eternal and true glory. 

On the other hand, those [pagan women] practiced constancy and strength by enduring the greatest and most heavy burdens in their bodies and minds—maintaining themselves as just and chaste—either through a certain instinct and gift of nature, or rather out of a desire to acquire the very brief splendor of worldly glory. 

Furthermore, the holy Christian and Hebrew women not only live in the splendor of true and unfailing light within the uncreated Trinity, but their virtues—their holiness, virginity, and chastity—conquer the stings of fleshly concupiscence <term>concupiscence: strong sexual desire or lust, which medieval moralists viewed as a primary spiritual hurdle</term> through their strength, and they sustained the most cruel torments of tyrants through their merits. We recognize and know that each of these holy women has been written of in her own volume with sacred letters and venerable majesty; however, the merits of these others [the pagans] have not been demonstrated in any special volume made specifically for them, as has been said. 

Therefore, we begin to write some of their deeds, almost as a way of rendering a reward to them. May that God, the Father of all things, be present at this little work and be a helper in the labor I have assumed, so that what I shall write, He may grant to have been written to His true praise.

<summary>Boccaccio explains that he will focus on pagan women because Christian and Hebrew saints already have the Bible to celebrate them, and because their motivations (heavenly vs. worldly glory) differ. He makes an exception for Eve as the universal mother.</summary>
<keywords>Eve, Christian women, Hebrew women, pagan women, worldly glory, virtue, constancy, Boccaccio, De mulieribus claris</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the general preface to the first specific entry in Boccaccio’s collection. Following the author's earlier explanation that Eve is the only non-pagan woman included—due to her unique status as the common ancestor of all humanity—the narrative begins with her miraculous creation in Paradise.</meta>

## On Eve, the Universal Mother of All.

**W**ishing, therefore, to describe the splendors with which noble women have shone, it does not seem an unworthy thing to take—or to have taken—the beginning from **Eve**, the mother of all. Truly, she, our most ancient mother, was adorned with magnificent and high splendors from the very start. For she was not produced in this painful valley of miseries where we mortals are born into toil; she was not fashioned with that same hammer or upon that anvil <note>The "hammer and anvil" is a metaphor for human biological reproduction; Boccaccio is contrasting Eve's divine creation with the physical birth of all other humans.</note> with which we are made. She did not weep with pain for the "sin of being born," <note>original: "peccato del nascere," likely referring to the inherent suffering and the condition of original sin associated with human birth.</note> nor did she enter this miserable life weak and small according to the common birth of others. 

Instead, she arrived in a manner never heard of happening to any other. For the supreme <term>Craftsman</term> <note>original: "fabro," referring to God as the Divine Smith or Architect of the universe.</note> and Maker of all things had already formed **Adam** from the clay of the earth with His own hand, and had transported him from the field (later called **Damascene** <note>A common medieval tradition held that Adam was created in a field near Damascus and then moved into the Garden of Eden.</note>) into the Garden of Delights. And there, having lulled him into a sweet and peaceful sleep, with craftsmanship known only to Him, He drew **Eve** from the side of the sleeping Adam. She was fully formed in substance and ready to be a companion to man, entirely joyful because of the beauty <note>original: "placentia," meaning the pleasantness or loveliness of the environment.</note> of the place where...

<summary>This page introduces Eve as the first and most noble of women, detailing her unique divine creation from Adam's rib in the Garden of Eden, contrasting her birth with the painful toil of ordinary human reproduction.</summary>

## On Eve

[O]nce she was brought forth, and by the knowledge of her immortal **Creator** <note>original: "factore," meaning the maker or architect of the universe.</note>, she was made the lady of all things. And when **Adam** was awakened and she was given to him as a companion, she was then first called **Eve**. What greater thing, what more splendid thing could ever happen at a birth? And when these things are considered, we can clearly enough understand that she was of miraculous beauty of body; for what thing created by the mere word of God would not surpass and exceed other creatures in beauty? Although through the years such beauty may diminish, and sometimes in the middle of the flower of youth it may fail through some small act of infirmity, nevertheless, because women place their principal dowry in it—and through the <term>indiscreet judgment</term> <note>Boccaccio suggests that while the world overvalues physical beauty, it remains a defining trait of noble women.</note> of mortals they have acquired great glory for it—it is not seen as something superfluous among their nobility and fame. Rather, it is placed in her, and in the others who follow, as a singular splendor.

This **Eve**, then, both by reason of her origin and by the obligation of her dwelling, was made a citizen of **Paradise**, clothed in a splendor unknown to us. While she delighted with eagerness together with her companion and husband in the sweetness and delights of that place, the <term>envious enemy</term> <note>original: "invidioso inimico," referring to Satan or the Serpent.</note> of her happiness, with false flatteries, put it into her mind that by going against that single commandment given to her by God, she would achieve greater and more ample glory. She, through <term>female levity</term> <note>original: "feminile leggerezza"; a medieval rhetorical trope suggesting that women were more easily swayed or less rational than men.</note>, believing him more than was good for her or for us, and foolishly thinking to rise to higher things, first used suggestion and flattery to draw her husband—who was already inclined to her will—to her side. Wherefore, acting against the divine commandment, when they had with rash daring tasted the fruits of the tree of...

[the tree] of good and evil led herself and all her future generation from that rest and eternal life into anguish, toil, and misery; from the death of the delightful and sweet homeland <note>original: "patria," referring to the Garden of Eden as the original home of humanity.</note>, they were led among rough thorns and rocks. For when the splendid light in which they were clothed was stripped from them, having disturbed their **Creator**, and reclothed in tree leaves, they were cast out from the place of delights and the terrestrial paradise. Like exiles, they came to the field of **Hebron** <note>A city in the Judean mountains where, according to tradition, Adam and Eve lived and were buried after the expulsion.</note>, where the excellent woman **Eve**—among so many noble women—while her husband turned the earth with a spade, according to what many believe, she first discovered spinning with the distaff <note>The distaff and spindle are the traditional iconographic symbols of Eve’s post-Fall labor, just as the spade is Adam's.</note>. Thereafter she often felt the pain of childbirth, and being distressed and troubled by the death of her children and grandchildren, she lived always in misery. Leaving aside the heat and the cold and the other discomforts she sustained, troubled and distressed in her labors, she reached old age and finally died.

## On Semiramis

...Neptune <note>original: "Neptuno." In classical mythology, Neptune is the god of the sea.</note>, whom the aforementioned poets, through erroneous belief, affirm to be the son of Saturn. Although it is not necessary to believe such things, it is nonetheless an argument that this **Semiramis** was certainly born of noble parents. She was married to **Ninus**, King of the **Assyrians**, to whom she bore a son. She gave this son the name **Saratiel** <note>Commonly known in other historical texts as Ninyas.</note>, but he was called **Ninus** after his father’s name. Truly, while Ninus was ruling all of **Asia** and had already subdued the **Bactrians** <note>An ancient Iranian people living in the region of modern-called Afghanistan and Uzbekistan.</note>, he was killed by an arrow. 

Since Semiramis was still young and her son Ninus was a very tender child, Semiramis judged that it was by no means safe to place the government of such a great Eastern empire into the hands of a boy of such a tender age. She was of such a high and noble spirit that she presumed and dared to govern and dominate through force, wit, and art those provinces which the fierce Ninus had conquered and subjected by arms. 

And first, through a feminine cunning—having weighed a great deception—she deceived the armies of her deceased husband. This Semiramis was very similar in form to her son and only slightly larger in stature. Seeing that this resemblance conformed well to her purpose, and so that nothing occurring in the course of time could harm her, she first covered her head with a head-covering <note>original: "capello." The author refers to a tiara or turban used to conceal her hair and feminine features.</note> and her arms and legs with masculine garments. And so that the novelty of the clothing would not cause wonder among the people, she ordered that a similar style of dress be used by all the people of her country. Thus, by showing that she—who had been the wife of Ninus—was actually her son Ninus, and conversely showing that the young Ninus was Semiramis, she obtained the royal power with marvelous care...

<summary>This page describes how Semiramis took control of the Assyrian Empire following the death of King Ninus by disguising herself as her young son to maintain political stability.</summary>

## On Semiramis

...majesty. And by marvelously observing military discipline in the conduct of arms, and by feigning herself a man with marvelous cunning, she performed great deeds as a man, sparing no effort and fearing no danger. Nor did she doubt herself in the face of anyone's envy, having no fear or shame in revealing and opening publicly who she was, and that what she had done, she had willed to do.

And truly, having accomplished great and eminent things through feminine cunning—deeds worthy of the most noble, strong, and robust men—she wished to show everyone that it is not sex, but rather the virtue of the soul <note>original: "uirtu dellanimo." In this context, "virtu" refers to the Renaissance concept of "virtù": a combination of strength, ability, and courage, rather than modern moral virtue.</note> that is necessary for ruling an empire. This fact induced all the more admiration in those who looked upon her, and so the illustrious majesty of the aforementioned lady ascended and grew.

But so that her great deeds may be shown more extensively, it should be known that after this high and spirited resolution, Queen Semiramis, taking up arms with a manly spirit, not only held and maintained the conquered empire of her husband securely, but by fighting her own battles and first overcoming all of Ethiopia, she added it to her realm. And departing from there with sudden force into the lands of **India**, she went against those where no one, except for her husband **Ninus**, had ever gone.

Having conquered and subjected them, she surrounded the city of **Babylon**—which in those times had been built by **Nimrod** <note>original: "Nembrotto." Referring to the biblical figure Nimrod, often traditionally associated with the construction of the Tower of Babel.</note> in the fields of **Shinar** <note>original: "Senecarum." Referring to the biblical Plain of Shinar in Mesopotamia.</note>, being most ancient and vast—with new walls of baked brick <note>original: "pietra cocta," literally "cooked stone."</note>, and she made the mortar out of sand and pitch <note>original: "pegola." This refers to bitumen, a naturally occurring petroleum product used in ancient Mesopotamia as mortar.</note>. These walls were of such great circumference and of such height and thickness that they truly were a source of the greatest admiration to those who saw them. But so that the multitude of her greatest deeds may appear universal through a single example...

<summary>Semiramis reveals her true identity after successfully ruling in disguise, asserting that leadership depends on spirit rather than gender. She expands the Assyrian Empire into Ethiopia and India and fortifies the city of Babylon with its famous massive walls.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Semiramis, the legendary Queen of Assyria. # On Semiramis

An event worthy of memory, which is affirmed as most true by the ancients <term>as</term> <gloss>original: p~ / per</gloss> a fact, and it should be known that the most noble queen, having subjected all of Asia and having rested all parts of her empire <term>in</term> <gloss>in</gloss> peace, was one day <term>according to</term> <gloss>secondo</gloss> feminine care having her hair combed and, <term>according to</term> <gloss>secondo</gloss> the custom of the country, braided. While she had only half of it braided, it was reported to her that the city of Babylon had rebelled against her empire. Having <term>heard</term> <gloss>intesa</gloss> this thing, she was so troubled that she immediately rose from the said activity; thus, with her hair half-braided, she took up arms and with a very great army went immediately to the said rebel city. Having besieged them, fighting with harsh and continuous battles, she would not turn back and <term>not</term> <gloss>non</gloss> finish braiding her unbraided hair or put it in order until the most powerful and great city of <term>Babylon</term> <gloss>babillonia</gloss>—which had been troubled and <term>fought against</term> <gloss>combattuta</gloss> by her for a very long time—was forced to surrender and return under her dominion. 

Of this spirited deed, a large statue made of metal <note>In the Renaissance, "metal" usually refers specifically to bronze when discussing public statues.</note> was erected for this reason in Babylon, which bore <term>witness</term> <gloss>testimonianza</gloss> for a very long time. This statue was in the form and likeness of a woman who had her hair half-braided and half loose and combed. 

Besides this, **Semiramis** built many cities and performed many unique and <term>very great</term> <gloss>grandissimi</gloss> deeds, which by the <term>length</term> <gloss>lunghezza</gloss> of time are covered over in such a way that almost nothing more is found in her praise. ## On Semiramis

...became mixed, and among other things, [she became] more bestial than human. She had carnal knowledge of her son Ninyas <note>Ninyas was the son of Semiramis and Ninus; he was often depicted in classical histories as an effeminate successor to his warrior mother.</note>, who was a youth of wonderful beauty. Would to God that in the bed where he wasted away in idleness, he had not exchanged roles <note>literally "changed sex"</note> with his mother, so that he had sweated in arms with her instead of [lying] against her. O wicked and most dishonest vice, which we allow to pass among other pleasures and rests; but among the anxieties and cares of Kings, among bloody battles, and like a monstrous thing among exiles and tears, observing no distinction of time, this pestilence flies from feeling to feeling, occupying unwary minds and leading them into failure and sorrow, staining every beauty of virtue with a foul mark. 

By this [vice] Semiramis was stained beyond measure. Thinking to cover with worldly cunning that which she had contaminated with her lust, it is said that she made that law: that regarding venereal and carnal desires, whatever was pleasing should be lawful. And fearing also that her domestic damsels and chambermaids might be joined in carnal use with her son, some say she was the first who invented the use of leg-garments, that is, **breeches** <term>breeches</term> <gloss>original: brache</gloss>. And she locked all her domestic women with keys and laces of the said leg-garments; which thing is still observed and used by those of Egypt and Asia. 

Some others say that Semiramis, having been inflamed with the most ardent desire for the embraces of her son and wishing to live in luxury <note>In this context, the author uses "luxury" (luxuriare) to denote sexual indulgence or debauchery.</note> with him, was killed by him—he being already of mature age—after she had reigned thirty-two years. To which thing certain others contradict, saying that she mixed cruelty with her lust, and those with whom she had...

The first was that he saw that the incest had begun and was spread among many people; the second, because he considered it a shame to have such an unchaste and lustful mother; the third, because he feared that a son might be born from her who would succeed in his unholy power <note>original: "ephõ", likely a contraction for "imperio" (empire or power)</note>. ## On Cybele

likewise the wife of King Saturn, we find nothing of great shame <note>original: "grã scõp" (grande scompiacenza), meaning disgrace or shameful conduct</note> for which she should be so named; we find of her only that with feminine cunning she freed from death her three sons, namely **Jupiter**, **Neptune**, and **Pluto**, together with King Saturn her husband, from the hands of Amphion <note>The text names "Amphione" as the brother, though classical myths usually identify Saturn's brother as Titan. This reflects a variation common in medieval and Renaissance retellings.</note> her brother through certain pacts. For which reason those men placed her in the light of special deity, and she was held as the most noble goddess and mother of the gods. And by public order, sacred temples were made for her with ordained priests, in such a way that great inconveniences <note>The author uses "inconveniences" here to refer to the spread of pagan idolatry, which he views as a negative historical development.</note> followed. Among others, during the Second War of the Romans and Carthaginians <note>This refers to the Second Punic War (218–201 BCE) against Hannibal.</note>, men of great nobility and counsel were sent to Attalus, King of Pergamon, to ask for her statue with great and devout prayer. These men performed a sacrifice to him according to the customs of the ancients, and they took a very large and misshapen stone from a castle called Axio <note>Pessinus was the actual site; the "misshapen stone" was a black meteorite believed to embody the goddess.</note>. This stone they sent to Rome with great care and diligence, and it was received by the Romans with great and supreme reverence. It was then situated and placed in a most beautiful temple as a sublime messenger of God and as a health-bringing messenger of the Republic. For many ages, with many sacrifices and ceremonies, she was cultivated and honored by all Romans and Italians. Now, behold here the wondrous deception of fortune—or rather blindness, or let us say the fraud and deceit of men through whose actions these things are done—that a woman, agitated and wounded by many labors, and then old, dead, and returned to ash and placed in hell, should be believed to be a goddess and be honored by the whole world for such a long time with divine rites and honors.

# ¶ On Juno, Queen of Kingdoms

Juno, daughter of Saturn and Ops <note>In Roman mythology, Ops is the goddess of resources and fertility, identified with the Greek Rhea.</note>, through the verses of poets and the error of the pagans, was considered by the whole world to be most venerable. She was preferred and placed above all the tainted ones <note>original: "infecte." The author uses this term to describe other pagan deities as being "tainted" or "diseased" by the error of infidelity/paganism.</note> within the infection of the infidelity of paganism; so much so that the silent teeth <note>A common literary trope referring to the "teeth of time" which consume and destroy historical memory.</note> of those times lacked the power—even though they gnaw through all things—to prevent a work of such great infamy <note>The author, likely following Giovanni Boccaccio’s *Concerning Famous Women*, views the worship of pagan gods as a historical "infamy" or deception that unfortunately survived through time.</note> from growing until her name became most famous even unto our own age.

In truth, concerning Juno, we can sooner recount her uncertain fortune than report things truly worthy of memory. This woman, then, was born in the same birth alongside Jupiter of Crete. Deceived antiquity and the ancients feigned and said that she was sent during her infancy to Samos <note>An island in the eastern Aegean, famous for its massive temple to Juno (the Heraion).</note>, and she was sent there with great care until she reached the age of marriage. She was then married to her brother Jupiter. This fact was testified for many ages by the statue that stood in the temple of the aforementioned place of Samos—bringing not small glory, but the greatest honor to those people, that the high and betrothed Juno was among them. She was...

# On Juno

...she was regarded by them as goddess and Queen of Heaven. And so that the memory of her would not easily fade, they built a temple, the most marvelous and largest of any other in the world. Having built it in her name, they had her image sculpted on a marble wall in the guise of a married virgin <note>In antiquity, Juno was often depicted with a veil, signifying her status as a matron and the patroness of marriage.</note> and placed it before the aforementioned temple.

Juno being then married to the said great Jove <note>original: "Ioue," the Latin-derived name for Jupiter.</note>, and as the empire and fame grew among his people, carrying her name and following a path to attain not a little, but much and great splendor, it truly seems that through poetic fiction and an unhealthy freedom she was made Queen of Heaven—she who had merely been made Queen of the mortal world. They preferred her over kingdoms and riches.

They did not only entrust her with the marital rights of marriage, but they also believed that she was a helper and savior of women in childbirth <note>Referring to her role as Juno Lucina, the goddess who "brings children into the light."</note>, along with many other things more fit for laughter than for belief. Because of these things, as the enemy of human nature <note>A common medieval reference to the Devil, suggesting that pagan worship was a diabolical deception of humanity.</note> led the people of those times astray, many temples and altars were constructed and built in her honor, as well as many priestly games and sacred things according to ancient custom.

And staying silent about other matters, she was thereafter long honored by those Argive people of Achaia <note>A region in the northern Peloponnese of Greece; "Argive" refers to the Greeks who famously worshipped Juno/Hera.</note> and by those of Carthage; and finally she was brought to Rome to the Capitol <note>The Capitoline Hill, the religious heart of ancient Rome.</note> into the chamber of Jove the Best and Greatest <note>original: "loptimo e maximo gioue," a translation of the Latin title *Iuppiter Optimus Maximus*.</note>. No differently than if she were joined to her husband, she was placed there under the name of "Juno the Queen" <note>original: "Giunone: Regina."</note> by the Roman lords of the world, and was honored and adored with many ceremonies for a long time. This continued even after the true God, Christ Jesus our Savior, appeared on earth.

# ¶ On Ceres, Goddess of Crops and Queen of Sicily.

**C**eres, according to what many wish to say, was Queen of Sicily, and was of such great and subtle ingenuity <note>The author uses "subtle ingenuity" to describe her inventive intellect, portraying her as a human civilizer rather than a mystical deity.</note> that she first devised the labor of the fields in her country. She taught how to tame oxen and bring them under the yoke, and she had the plow and the <term>plowshare<gloss>the cutting blade of a plow</gloss></term> made to till and sow the earth. In that land, she had <term>grain<gloss>original: "frumento," likely referring to wheat or durum</gloss></term> sown, harvested, processed, and made into bread, which she then taught the people of her country to bake and eat. Before this, they were accustomed to eating acorns and wild apples. For these reasons, she was called the goddess of the fruits of the earth and was honored with divine honors; they believed her to be the daughter of Saturn and Cybele.

They say that the said Ceres had a daughter by her brother Jove <note>original: "Gioue," the Italian form of Jove or Jupiter.</note> named Proserpina, who was kidnapped—to the mother’s greatest distress—by Orcus, King of the Molossians <note>The author provides a "historical" explanation for the myth of Pluto kidnapping Proserpina, identifying the god of the underworld as a mortal king of a tribe in Epirus, Greece.</note>. According to what the fables say, she was searched for by her mother for a very long time.

There was also another Ceres in the region of Attica and in the city of Eleusis <note>original: "cleusin," referring to the site of the famous Eleusinian Mysteries.</note> who was also famous among her people for the same merits. For her, they say that Triptolemus <note>A figure in Greek myth who was taught the art of agriculture by Ceres (Demeter) to spread it to the rest of humanity.</note> was a devoted servant. Because the antiquity of those times honored her with the status of a deity and with divine honors, it has seemed right to me to present them both under a single name.

<summary>This page describes Ceres as a historical Queen of Sicily who revolutionized human life by inventing agriculture and bread-making, leading to her deification. It also provides a grounded, historical interpretation of the kidnapping of her daughter Proserpina.</summary>

## On Ceres

...ingenious inventions—concerning both of these <note>original: "amendue íqualí íngegní," referring to the two types of ingenuity: the invention of farming and the resulting social organization.</note> I do not know if I ought to praise or blame them. Who would be the person to blame those who draw wandering and wild men out of the woods? Who would blame those who lead back those who once lived according to the ways of beasts into a human way of life? Who would blame her who transformed <term>acorns<gloss>original: "ghiande," the primary food source for primitive man in classical mythology</gloss></term> into <term>grain<gloss>original: "frumento," specifically wheat</gloss></term>? By this grain, the body becomes more radiant, the limbs better disposed, and nourishment is made more suited to human use. Who would blame those who have reduced the world—which was once filled all around with thorns and wild trees—into cultivated beauty and public utility? Who would dare to speak ill of those who have reduced the crude world into a civil one? Who would speak ill of those who have stirred the minds of the lazy and unteachable toward contemplation? Who would condemn those who have drawn men toward the labors of the fields? Because of this, so many cities have grown and expanded, and so many new ones have been made; so many empires, so many kingdoms, and remarkable and honorable customs have been found. If anyone were so simple as to blame these things, he would be considered unwise.

On the other hand, to the contrary: who would praise a scattered multitude of men living in woods, in forests, or in rural places, accustomed to eating and living on acorns and wild apples, herbs, and the milk of beasts, and drinking water? These people, in their original <term>state of nature<gloss>original: "prío ſtato" (prior state)</gloss></term>, lived with a rested spirit; they were all sober, all chaste, and all free, content only with the law of nature. In their breasts, there was no thought of worldly anxiety, no rancor, and no hatred. And in that time, <note>This describes the mythical "Golden Age" before the advent of property and agriculture.</note> not only did they fear to commit crimes, but such things were not even known to them. Since then, through the multitude of foods and the fields...

<summary>The author weighs the benefits of Ceres' invention of agriculture—such as the rise of cities and civilization—against the loss of the "Golden Age" innocence, where humans lived simply and without greed.</summary>
<keywords>Ceres, agriculture, civilization vs nature, Golden Age, grain, social progress, ethics of invention</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical assessment of Ceres’s legacy. ->On Ceres<-

## On Ceres

Lands and places that used to be held in common began to be divided and distinguished by boundaries with ditches and walls, so that they might be subjected to the <term>anxieties<gloss>original: "sollicitudie," referring to the constant worry and mental burden of managing property</gloss></term> and cares of labor, and to all the other toils of mortals. From this came poverty, anxiety, hatreds, and laments; even bloody battles have come from them and flown throughout the whole world. Inflamed envy has shown that the scythes made for reaping the crops have been converted into pointed knives. From these things followed the navigation of the sea, through which the peoples of the East and the West have come to know one another and, many times, fought.

From those <note>The author refers to the "delicate foods" produced by farming, contrasted with the acorns of the previous age.</note> delicate foods have come the fatness and softness of bodies, the anxiety over excessive clothing, of elaborate tables, and of splendid and outrageous banquets; from them comes venereal concupiscence <note>original: "uenerea cōcupiscētia," or sexual desire</note> and luxury, which had been cold until those times, but then began to heat up, not without the greatest inconvenience and damage to all the world. 

And what is even worse: if perhaps, as the years pass, it happens that some season goes poorly—either through the wrath of heaven or through war, as often happens—the lands respond poorly; and from this <term>scarcity<gloss>original: "carestia," meaning a period of crop failure or high prices</term> follow bitter fasts, and the most cruel hunger enters into the tiny houses of the poor, very often not without great danger and damage to the rich. Furthermore, those aforementioned delicate foods are the cause and beginning of <term>pestilence<gloss>original: "pistolentia," used here to describe diseases arising from over-indulgence or poor diet</gloss></term> and diseases. From them comes filthy thinness; from them, dismal paleness, trembling, and weakness; and because of them, one runs not only into diverse illnesses but into sudden death. 

Having regarded these things along with many others, I say I know that the <term>Golden Age<gloss>original: "eta aurea," the mythical first age of man where food was abundant without labor</gloss></term> was more precious, notwithstanding that it was rude and coarse; I say that it should be preferred to this our <term>Iron Age<gloss>original: "nra ferrea," referring to the current age of toil, greed, and conflict</gloss></term> and proud century.

<summary>The author presents a counter-argument to the benefits of agriculture, claiming that private property and "delicate" foods led to war, social inequality, disease, and the corruption of the human body. He concludes that the primitive Golden Age was superior to the modern Iron Age.</summary>

### On Minerva or Pallas the Virgin

->**M**<-inerva or Pallas was a virgin of such high renown that many believed her birth was not of mortal origin. It is said that she was first known on earth in the time of King Ogyges <note>Ogyges was a legendary king of early Greece, often associated with a great flood that occurred before the time of Deucalion.</note> near Lake Triton <note>original: "lacho tritōio," a body of water in Libya where, according to myth, Minerva was born or first appeared.</note> or far away in the place of Lesser Phrygia. She was believed to be fathered without a mother from the brain of Jupiter and sent from heaven. This error is laughable, and yet it was given more credit because her birth and origin were so obscure; the Greeks maintained that she was a perpetual virgin. And so that this might be more believed, they added that she fought with <term>Vulcan<gloss>The god of fire and the forge; in myth, he attempted to violate Minerva, but she defended her virginity.</gloss></term>. 

Furthermore, they say that she was the one who first discovered the art of <term>wool-working<gloss>original: "larte d̃ la lana," encompassing the entire process from raw fiber to finished cloth</gloss></term>, a thing never known before, showing first how the wool should be purged of its impurities, and in what way it might be softened and <term>carded<gloss>original: "remorbe disse cōi dētí del ferro," referring to the use of iron-toothed combs to align fibers</gloss></term>, and how it might be placed on the <term>distaff<gloss>original: "rocha," the tool used to hold unspun wool</gloss></term> and drawn into threads with the fingers. And then she devised the way of weaving it, and how the threads were interlaced and joined together by the pull of the comb, and in what way the fabric was finished with lime and <term>fuller’s earth<gloss>original: "loto," used in the ancient "fulling" process to cleanse and thicken cloth</gloss></term>. Regarding this work, the great battle of the shuttles and punches <note>original: "collate e pōzoní," referring to the tools used in weaving and embroidery.</note> that took place between her and <term>Arachne<gloss>A mortal weaver who challenged Minerva to a contest and was famously transformed into a spider.</gloss></term> is recounted. And also, she was the one who discovered the use of oil, not...

## On Minerva

[It was] never heard of among mortals until the time that she taught how olives—and the <note>original: "aníme," literally "souls," referring here to the pit or heart of the fruit</note> pits within them—should be processed with millstones, then pressed to extract the oil. Because this appeared to the wise men of that time to be a most useful thing, the people of Athens attributed to her the victory over Neptune <note>Referring to the mythological contest between Minerva (Athena) and Neptune (Poseidon) for the patronage of Athens; the gods judged Minerva's gift of the olive tree more useful than Neptune's salt spring or horse.</note>, a god worthy of being named among the greatest.

They say also that she first discovered the use of chariots, and that it was through her skill that iron was converted into weapons and used to protect the human body. She taught the arrangement of ranks in battle and gave laws and order to all those who go forth to fight.

They say further that she was the discoverer of numbers, and established the systems of counting we observe in all things. They believed that the <term>shepherds' pipes<gloss>original: "ſampogníe," refers to rustic wind instruments like the pan-flute</gloss></term> were first made by her from the bones of a bird or from marsh reeds, and that she composed music for them and sent them down to earth from heaven. [However, she discarded them] because playing them caused the player’s throat to swell and the mouth to become deformed.

Why say more? The ancients, who were lavish in granting divinity, bestowed upon her the title of Goddess of Wisdom. Motivated by this reputation for wisdom, the people of Athens—seeing that their city was well-suited for the studies through which a man becomes wise—chose the aforementioned Minerva as their patroness, protector, and defender. They built a citadel in her name and, in a most noble temple consecrated to her honor, they had her likeness painted in this manner:

First, she was shown with <term>squinting eyes<gloss>original: "glíochí guerzí," meaning cross-eyed or having a strabismus</gloss></term>, signifying that just as one cannot tell where a cross-eyed man is looking, so [it is with the depth of a wise person's thought]...
