### On Minerva, or rather Pallas

One can understand what the perception of the wise man aims for and intends. They also depicted her with an <term>iron helmet<gloss>original: "capello del ferro," literally an iron hat</gloss></term> on her head, wanting to signify that the counsel of the wise is hidden and protected. They depicted her dressed in a <term>coat of mail<gloss>original: "cocta de ferro"</gloss></term> to show that the wise man is covered and armed in every part where blows might fall. They also made her with an extremely long lance to show that the wise man arranges his foresight and thoughts from a great distance. 

Furthermore, they depicted her with a crystal shield on her arm, in which was fixed the head of a serpent called a <term>Gorgon<gloss>Referring to Medusa, whose gaze turned onlookers to stone</gloss></term>, to show that the wise man must have in his defense clear coverings and arguments, so well-placed and armed with a serpentine skin that by their very gaze and appearance, those looking upon them seem to turn to stone <note>The author suggests that the "stony" effect represents how a wise man’s logic can stun or paralyze an opponent in an argument.</note>. And for her guardian they placed a <term>bat<gloss>original: "ueſpertilio"</gloss></term> which sees at night, to show and signify that the prudent and wise man sees as well in the darkness as in the light <note>While the owl is the traditional bird of Minerva/Athena, this text identifies her nocturnal companion as a bat.</note>. 

Meanwhile, the reverence and fame of this woman spread far and wide, and the error of the ancients favored her so much that temples were built and established, and divine sacrifices celebrated, throughout almost the entire world. This error grew so much that the Romans, on the <term>Capitoline Hill<gloss>original: "campo doglio," referring to the Campidoglio in Rome</gloss></term> right alongside the greatest and best Jupiter, built her a chapel among the most special gods; and this Minerva was held in equal reverence with Queen Juno. There are nonetheless some—and indeed many very wise and great men—who say that the aforementioned things were not done for just a single woman named Minerva, but were attributed to several different women named Minerva who lived at different times.

<summary>This page provides a symbolic interpretation of Minerva's iconography (helmet, mail, lance, and shield) as metaphors for wisdom and describes her high status in the Roman pantheon on the Capitoline Hill.</summary>

### On Venus, Queen of the Cypriots

**V**enus was, in the opinion of many, a woman of Cyprus. But as to who her father and mother were, there is a conflicting opinion, for some say she was the daughter of one named <term>Cyrrus<gloss>original: "Cirro"</gloss></term> and of a woman called <term>Syria<gloss>original: "Siria"</gloss></term>. Some others say she was the daughter of Cyrrus and of <term>Dido<gloss>original: "Didone"; here referring to a Cypriot woman of that name, not the famous Dido of Carthage</gloss></term>, a woman of Cyprus. For her, perhaps to elevate the renown of her beauty, they say she was fathered by <term>Jove<gloss>Jupiter, king of the gods</gloss></term> and the aforementioned Dido. Truly, by whomever she was sired, I have chosen to place her among famous women rather for her illustrious and marvelous beauty than for the shameful discovery and proven adultery of her life <note>The author, following Boccaccio's "De mulieribus claris," often contrasts physical beauty or fame with moral failings.</note>. And truly she was endowed with such beauty of face and body that often those looking upon her were deceived by wonder. ### Queen of the Cypriots

<margin>c ii</margin>

...[some] though some said that she was a star of the heavens that we call **Venus**. Others said that she was a celestial woman sent to earth from the womb of **Jove** <note>Jupiter, the king of the gods</note>; in short, all were blinded by a thick fog, for despite knowing that she was born of a mortal woman, they asserted and said that she was an immortal goddess. And they said further—and with all their power they affirmed—that she was the mother of that ill-fated love which they called **Cupid**. Nor was she lacking in art, nor in capturing in various ways and deceiving the minds of foolish men who looked upon her. For which reason, they—not knowing how to resist her hidden and wicked deeds, which deeds were harmful to some—held her to be the daughter of Jove and a most venerable goddess.

Not only by those of **Paphos** <note>An ancient city on the coast of Cyprus, legendary as the birthplace of Venus</note>, a most ancient castle of the **Cypriots**, was she appeased and honored and perfumed with incense; for they thought that dead luxury and a libidinous woman would take delight in that scent with which, while living in <term>brothels<gloss>original: "meritricoli luochi"</gloss></term>, she used to perfume herself. But truly she was also honored with divine honors by all nations, and most especially by the **Romans**. They, in times past, built the temple of **Venus Genetrix** <note>Venus the Mother, an ancestral deity of the Roman people</note> and offered other great honors. 

But what need is there to say many things: it was believed by many that she had two husbands. But to which one she was first married is not well certain. Some hold that it was **Vulcan**, King of **Lemnos** and son of **Jove of Crete** <note>The author follows a historical theory called euhemerism, which argues that gods were originally mortal kings; here distinguishing a Cretan king Jove from the god</note>, after whose death she married **Adonis**, son...

## On Venus, Queen of the Cypriots

...son of **Cinyras** and **Myrrha**, King of Cyprus. <note>In mythology, Adonis was born from the incestuous union of King Cinyras of Cyprus and his daughter Myrrha</note> This seems more likely to me: that they mean to say that **Adonis** was her first husband. However, either by a vice of her constitution or truly by the imperfection of that <term>region<gloss>original: "legione," likely a transcription error for "regione"</gloss></term>—in which it seems that <term>lechery<gloss>original: "lassciuia"</gloss></term> has a singular power—or perhaps because she did it through the malice of her own corrupt mind, once **Adonis** was dead, she allowed herself to fall into such a stimulus of <term>lust<gloss>original: "luxuria"</gloss></term> that, without the clouding of her beautiful eyes, through many and frequent fornications she darkened and stained all the clarity of her marvelous beauty.

Because her vices were already known to the surrounding regions, and she had been found by **Vulcan** <note>The god of fire and Venus's husband, who in myth trapped her in a golden net</note> fornicating with a young man—from which event arose the fable one reads concerning **Mars** <note>The god of war; the "young man" mentioned here</note>—she finally desired to remove and cover a bit of the shame of her impudent brow. To give herself wider license for lechery and fornication, she devised a most filthy and abominable malice, almost better to be kept silent than spoken of. 

Through this, it appeared that she was the first who invented the public use of <term>prostitutes<gloss>original: "meretrice"</gloss></term>, ordering the <term>matrons<gloss>noble or married women</gloss></term> to begin such a dishonest practice. To this abominable work, the customs of the **Cypriots** for a very long period of time gave testimony, sending their virgin daughters to the seaside so that they might practice intercourse with strangers. And thus they showed that they had paid to **Venus** the sacrifices of their future <term>chastity<gloss>original: "castita"</gloss></term>, and in the end they would earn their <term>dowries<gloss>original: "dote"</gloss></term>. This abominable madness even passed to the **Italians**, who at certain times established public places for prostitutes.

<summary>This page discusses Venus's relationship with Adonis and how her legendary infidelity with Mars led her to institutionalize prostitution in Cyprus to normalize her own behavior. It describes the historical practice of young women earning dowries through ritual labor at the seashore, a custom the author claims spread to Italy.</summary>

## On Isis, or Io, Queen of the Egyptians

**ISIS**, who was first called **Io**, was not only a most famous Queen of the Egyptians, but finally became to them a most holy and venerable deity. However, as to what time she lived, or from which father or mother she was born, the illustrious writers of history remain in doubt. There are some who say that she was the daughter of **Inachus** <gloss>original: "Yuaro," a likely transcription error for Inacho</gloss>, King of the **Argives**, and sister of **Phoroneus** <gloss>original: "Foruncho"</gloss>, whom we know were rulers at the time of **Jacob**, son of **Isaac**. <note>Boccaccio, following medieval tradition, attempts to align Greek mythology with the biblical chronology of the Patriarchs.</note> Some others say she was the daughter of **Prometheus** while **Phorbas** ruled the **Argives**; this claim resulted from a time long after the first era mentioned. Many others affirm that she lived during the time of **Cecrops** <gloss>original: "Cichopro"</gloss>, King of **Athens**. And besides these, some say she flourished in the time of **Lynceus**, King of the **Argives**.

Such <term>uncertainty<gloss>original: "uanita," literally vanity or emptiness, referring here to the lack of historical consensus</gloss></term> among wise and noble men needs no argument, for she was outstanding among the women of her time and most worthy of memory. She was the daughter of King **Inachus**, whom the ancients <term>imagined<gloss>original: "fingieuano," to feign or fashion a story</gloss></term> was pleasing to **Jupiter** because of her beauty; and by him her virginity was taken, and to hide his sin she was <note>The text ends mid-sentence, likely leading into the myth where Jupiter transforms Io into a heifer to hide her from his wife, Juno.</note>

<summary>This page introduces Isis (also known as Io), discussing her status as an Egyptian queen and goddess while detailing the conflicting historical timelines regarding her parentage and era. It begins to recount the myth of her beauty and her encounter with Jupiter.</summary>

# About Isis, or rather Io, Queen of the Egyptians

she was transformed into a cow and then given to **Juno**. And being no longer called **Io** but **Isis**, all these things do not disagree with the true history; for there are some who say that **Isis**, having been violated by **Jupiter** <gloss>original: "gioue"</gloss> and for fear of her father, entered into a ship with some of her own people, which carried as its ensign <note>A figurehead or painted symbol on the prow of the ship.</note> a cow, and she went away and arrived in **Egypt** with a prosperous wind and remained there. But by what means she obtained the lordship of **Egypt** is not found written; nevertheless, it is believed that she found there hardly anyone but rude and coarse people, almost ignorant of all human things. And they lived more like beasts than according to the custom of men. To whom, not without great effort, she taught how to cultivate and sow the lands, and she taught them how they should gather themselves together <term>into a society<gloss>original: "reducere in uno," literally to bring back into one</gloss></term> and showed them how to live according to <term>civil laws<gloss>original: "leggie ciuile"</gloss></term>. And she did so much that they easily believed and affirmed that she had been sent from heaven. And upon her death, they honored her with divine honors; and her fame grew so much that in **Rome**, already mistress of all things, a very great temple was built for her. And this <term>error<gloss>original: "errore," referring here to the "error" of pagan idolatry from a Christian perspective</gloss></term> passed into barbarian lands and into the parts of the West. There was also as a husband for this most illustrious **Isis** a man named **Apis**, whom <term>chronicled antiquity<gloss>original: "erronicha antichita," likely a scribe's error for "chronica," meaning historical records</gloss></term> thought was the son of **Jupiter** and **Niobe**, daughter of **Inachus**. Which **Apis**, they say, having left the realm of **Achaia** <gloss>original: "Chaya"</gloss> to **Aegialeus** <gloss>original: "gialeo"</gloss> his brother, having already ruled the **Argives** for thirty-five years, went...

# Regarding Queen Europa

**W**hich was carried to **Crete**. <note>This phrase completes the sentence from the previous page describing the ship with the bull figurehead.</note> And truly, I believe it is not wise to grant too much liberty to young maidens <gloss>original: "uergene"</gloss> to wander about alone and permit them to have their ears open to hear the words of everyone. For I have read many times that because of these two things <note>Wandering alone and listening to strangers.</note>, shameful marks have been exchanged for virtue. These marks, even if the sin itself does not exist, cannot fail to diminish the beauty of perpetual chastity. From these two circumstances, it clearly appears how the fable arose which we read: that **Mercury** drew the herds of **Phoenicia** to the shore, and **Jupiter** <gloss>original: "gioue"</gloss>, transformed into a bull, carried the maiden away, swimming with her to **Crete**.

And truly, regarding the time of this abduction <gloss>original: "rapina"</gloss>, many authors vary and disagree. For those who place it in more ancient times say it was when **Danaus** ruled the **Argives**. Others say it was at the time of **Acrisius** <gloss>original: "Accixio"</gloss>. The most recent authors say it was at the time when **Pandion** <gloss>original: "Pādiō"</gloss> ruled **Athens**. But it seems to me that it occurred during the time of **Minos**, the son of **Europa**.

Others claim that she was only deflowered <gloss>original: "spolzellata"</gloss> by **Jupiter**, and was then married to **Asterion** <gloss>original: "asticho"</gloss>, King of **Crete**, by whom she then gave birth to **Minos**—or rather **Minos**, **Rhadamanthus** <gloss>original: "rodomāte"</gloss>, and **Sarpedon** <gloss>original: "Serpā dōe"</gloss>. Others say they were the sons of **Jupiter**. Some say that **Europa** was renowned for her noble lineage. For the **Phoenicians** were, by their own merits in their day, great and most notable men, illustrious and surpassing all others. But truly—whether by the honor of her divine husband, or by the grace of her sons who became kings, or by her own special virtue—the third part of the world <note>The continent of Europe, then considered one of the three parts of the world alongside Asia and Africa.</note> is named after her. Indeed, I judge that **Europa** was a notable and praiseworthy woman...

## Regarding Libya, Queen of Libya

### Regarding Libya, Queen of Libya.

**Libya**, according to the accounts of the most ancient authors, was the daughter of **Epaphus** <term>Epaphus: in mythology, the son of Zeus and Io who became King of Egypt</term>, King of the **Egyptians** <term>Egyptians</term>, and of **Cassiopeia** <term>Cassiopeia</term> his spouse. She was joined in legitimate marriage to **Neptune** <term>Neptune: the Roman god of the sea, often used in these biographies to denote a powerful sea-faring ruler</term>, a foreign and powerful man. And by the aforementioned **Neptune** <term>Neptune</term> she gave birth to a son to whom she gave the name **Busiris** <term>Busiris: original "Guxiride", a legendary Egyptian king known for his cruelty</term>. He was afterwards a most cruel tyrant of Upper **Egypt** <term>Egypt</term>. 

The magnificent works of this woman are believed to have been consumed by the passing years. But that they were very great indeed is demonstrated by the fact that she was a figure of such authority after her time that all that part of **Africa** <note>original: "laffrica"</note> which was ruled by her was called **Libya** <term>Libya: a name anciently applied to the entire continent of Africa or its northern regions</term> by her name.

## Regarding Marpesia and Lampedo, Queens of the Amazons

**M**arpesia, or otherwise Martesia, and Lampedo were sisters, and together they were queens of the **Amazons** <term>Amazons: a legendary nation of all-female warriors in Greek mythology and ancient history</term>. Because of the illustrious glory of the battles they fought, they called themselves the daughters of **Mars** <note>Mars: the Roman god of war</note>; of whom, because the history is so ancient, it is rare and little known. 

In the time, therefore, when the land of **Scythia** <term>Scythia: a vast region of northern Eurasia inhabited by nomadic peoples in antiquity</term> was inhabited by the aforementioned women—being a wild land into which no stranger had ventured—it happened that two royal youths named **Silixios** and **Scolapro** <note>These figures are traditionally known as Ylinos and Scolopitus in classical accounts of the Amazon's origins</note>, having been driven out of their land by the factional strife of their elders, came near the **Thermodon** <term>Thermodon: a river in modern-day Turkey, traditionally cited as the home of the Amazon capital</term> river of **Cappadocia** <term>Cappadocia: an ancient district in east-central Anatolia</term> with a portion of their people. 

And here, occupying the dwellings of those of **Syria** <note>original: "Cirria"</note>, robbing and storming the land, and living by plunder and robbery for a span of time, the men of that country were eventually consumed <note>original: "consumarosi", meaning the men were wiped out through constant warfare</note> so that almost all of them died; for which reason the women remained widows. They took such great offense and were so strongly inflamed with rage that, with those few men who were left to them, they went to fight against their enemies. Their assault was so fierce that they defeated them and drove them out entirely.

<summary>This page introduces the sisters Marpesia and Lampedo as joint Queens of the Amazons and recounts the legendary origins of their warrior society following the exile and eventual death of their men in Scythia and Cappadocia.</summary>

# Regarding Marpesia and Lampedo, Queens of the Amazons

...driven out of their borders, and from that time forward they gave trouble and battle to all their neighbors. And it appearing to them that marrying strangers was more like servitude than marriage, and seeing that the women were sufficient for battles and arms without men, by common counsel they all rose up against [the men]. There remained few of their husbands, and they killed them all. 

And having done this, they turned their fury against their enemies, and they fought so powerfully and so harshly and troubled all the surrounding neighbors that they led them to ask for and desire peace with them. 

After which peace was made: the said women, to ensure succession, used at times to stay with their neighbors, and once they had conceived, they returned to their own dwellings. And when it came to the act of giving birth, they kept to this custom—a thing not only cruel, but inhumane <note>original: " ihūana" (inhumana)</note>—that they killed all the newborns who were male, and the females they kept and always trained in horsemanship. 

To these small young girls, they burned the left breast <term>breast: original "mammella"</term> with fire, or otherwise with medicine they hindered its growth, so that in adulthood it would not hinder the exercise of archery <term>archery: original "saiectare", literally "to throw arrows"</term>; and the right breast they left intact so that they could nourish [children] with it. For which reason they obtained the name of **Amazon** <note>This reflects the popular ancient etymology that "Amazon" derived from the Greek "a-" (without) and "mazos" (breast), suggesting they removed a breast to better use their bows.</note>. 

Nor did the said women care to raise the said girls with the distaff or the needle <note>original: "rocha o de acho", referring to the tools of spinning and sewing, symbols of traditional domestic womanhood</note>, but they raised them to ride, to joust, to shoot arrows, to hunt, and in all virile things required for arms. By which arts they not only held and possessed the fields and countries of **Scythia**, but they occupied the greater part of **Europe** and conquered a large part of **Asia**. And they became so feared by those surrounding them that almost everyone feared them. Then they ordained two queens...

# Of Thisbe

...under their rule were Marpesia and Lampedo, under whose governance—as I have shown above—their empire grew marvelously. And truly these two, Marpesia and Lampedo, being very illustrious in military discipline, divided the provinces so that when one went to the conquest of foreign nations, the other remained to guard the kingdom. 

But at the end, while leading and conducting her army against the enemies, Lampedo was attacked by barbarians who had trapped and enclosed her with ambushes; and Marpesia, trusting too much in her own power with a part of her army, was killed, leaving behind two daughters. But what followed afterward for Lampedo, I do not remember having read.

## Of Thisbe, a virgin of Babylon.

**T**hisbe, a virgin of Babylon, is remembered among mortals more for the end of her unhappy love than for any other noble work of hers. Although among our ancestors <note>original: "nr̃i magiori" (nostri maggiori); the author likely refers to classical sources like Ovid</note> we do not find written who her father or mother was, nonetheless, she had her home in Babylon, next to that of the youth Pyramus.

### Of Thisbe, Virgin of Babylon

...her age is clearly believed. Because these two, in their childhood, being together by reason of neighborhood, began to love each other with a youthful love; and as they grew, this multiplied into a most powerful passion. When they reached a more mature age, they began to reveal their feelings to one another through signs. But Thisbe, having now grown into a young woman, was restricted by her father and mother to staying <unclear>inside</unclear> the house, a situation that was most distressing to them both.

They began to think of how they might be able to speak to each other; and with Love <note>Love is personified here as the classical deity Amor or Cupid</note> teaching and urging them, they found in a secret place in the shared wall of the houses a tiny crack, never seen by others before, through which they often spoke to one another. Now, as the heat of their sweet and most fervent love grew, they took counsel to flee from their homes.

And they arranged to depart the following night and go into a woods near the city, and there, at the fountain called the Spring of Ninus <note>Ninus was the legendary founder of Babylon; his tomb was the traditional meeting place in this myth</note>, they would wait for one another. When night fell, Thisbe—perhaps being the more passionate—went out of the house covered in a cloak <term>palio: a mantle or rectangular piece of cloth used as an outer garment</term> and, all alone, she headed toward the woods. 

Having reached the fountain and there waiting for her beloved Pyramus, she saw a lioness approaching from a distance. Upon seeing her, Thisbe left her cloak behind and fled <unclear>heedlessly</unclear> into the forest. The lioness, having reached the fountain thirsty and bloodied from her recent prey, drank; and finding the cloak, as is her nature, she wiped her bloody mouth upon it. Having bloodied it and torn it to pieces, she departed. 

Meanwhile, Pyramus, who had made his way from his house into the woods, arrived at the fountain and found the cloak so bloodied, from which he imagined that some wild beast had devoured her. He began to weep and to grieve...

envious fortune did not permit to be joined together with peaceful embraces: 
it could not prevent the unhappy blood of both from being joined together and

such a cruel and bloody misfortune <term>infortunio: a tragic event or ill-fated accident</term>. Certainly, the inor-
dinate appetites of youth [must be managed] with great feeling and moderation...

## Of Queen Hypermnestra

...ought to be restrained. So that through too sudden and harsh punishment they are not led into despair, <note>original: "despatõe", an abbreviation for "desperazione"</note> since the passion of desire comes from unmoderated vigor and is like a pestilence; and acts almost as a scourge and torment to the young, for whom elders ought to maintain a patient soul. For nature, thus willing the order of things, has made it so that while we are young we fall into love and the desire for children, so that the human race does not run into decline, which would happen if the act of coitus were disdained in old age.

## Of Hypermnestra, Queen of the Argives and Priestess of Juno.

<note>A large woodcut of Hypermnestra occupies the left half of this section. To its right, the text begins with a decorative historiated initial 'I'.</note>

These men did not have equal fortune in their children, despite having an equality in the number of children they had; for Danaus had fifty daughters and Aegyptus had fifty sons. ### Concerning Hypermnestra, Queen of the Argives

...of one of his nephews. Having a very great fear of this, because among such a great number of nephews he did not know which one of them it would be, it followed that Aegyptus <note>original: "Egisto"</note> requested the marriage of Danaus’s daughters to his own sons. To which request Danaus, thinking of a most cruel deed, immediately consented. And when the day of the wedding arrived, Danaus admonished and instructed his daughters, saying that if they loved his life, each one of them, on the first night, must kill her husband when they had fallen back asleep. 

And so it followed that each one killed her own, except for Hypermnestra. But she instead woke him and revealed the matter to him, advising him to flee; so he escaped death. In the morning, Danaus had her imprisoned and strongly rebuked <note>original: "labiafimo", from the verb "biasimare," to blame or condemn</note> her. This Danaus—either having been driven out, or fleeing of his own accord, or indeed having been summoned to Greece—occupied and held the kingdom of the Argives <note>original: "gliargiui"</note> by both wit and force. 

For which reason, and in which place, some wish to say that the aforementioned sin was perpetrated by him. But in whatever way it was done and wherever, the said Danaus was killed by Lynceus <note>original: "linceo"</note>, who took his place. The said Lynceus ruled the Argives and was their king. And having drawn Hypermnestra out of prison with the greatest grace, as a beloved spouse joined to him, he made her a partner in his kingdom. 

To her, not only was honor given as Queen, but she was made a priestess of Argive Juno <note>original: "Iunone de Argiua"</note>. For this reason, she appeared adorned with a double whiteness <note>original: "cãdore", referring to her moral purity and her ritual garments</note> and a bright splendor. And while her other sisters fell into dishonest infamy, this Hypermnestra, through her commendable piety <note>original: "pieta", meaning moral duty and devotion</note>, left her name notable and worthy of praise even until our own time.

## Concerning Niobe, Queen of Thebes.

Niobe, who is very well known to the common people among noble women, was the daughter of Tantalus <note>A figure in Greek mythology famous for being punished in the underworld by being "tantalized" with food and water he could never reach.</note>, the most ancient King of Phrygia, and was the sister of Pelops and the wife of Amphion. At that time, Amphion was the most illustrious King of the Thebans, both because he was the son of Jove <note>Jupiter/Zeus.</note> and because he was virtuous in his own right and possessed great eloquence <term>facundia</term> <note>original: "facundia," meaning the ability to speak with fluency and grace.</note>.

As the authority of the kingdom persisted and grew through his efforts, the said Niobe bore him seven sons and seven daughters. This circumstance, had she been wise and humble, should have been a cause of greatness and well-being for her. However, the opposite occurred because she was uplifted by pride. 

Due both to the splendor of having so many children and to the greatness and honor of her ancestors, she rose to such a height of prideful elation that she presumed to speak against the gods. This Niobe came into the midst of the people where the ceremonies of the aforementioned sacrifices were being held; as if in a frenzy, she began to shout: 

"What madness is this that you Thebans commit in arranging these sacred rites? And why do you honor Latona <note>original: "Latonia," the mother of the gods Apollo and Diana.</note>, a woman and the daughter of Coeus <note>original: "Ceo," a Titan.</note> the tyrant, who never bore more than two children—and those through adultery—and prefer her to me, who am your Queen and born of..."

<summary>This page introduces Niobe, Queen of Thebes, detailing her noble lineage and her fourteen children before describing the moment her pride leads her to publicly insult the goddess Latona.</summary>
<keywords>Niobe, Thebes, Tantalus, Amphion, Latona, hubris, Greek mythology, pride</keywords>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues Niobe’s speech where she boasts of her lineage and children, followed by the tragic consequences of her pride and a moralizing commentary on her downfall. The catchword "Non uol do..." suggests the start of a new section or sentiment on the following page.</meta>

# Concerning Niobe, Queen of Thebes.

...King Tantalus <note>This sentence completes Niobe's defiant speech from the previous page, where she compares her noble lineage to that of the goddess Latona.</note>, and I have fourteen children by my noble husband Amphion. These honors and ceremonies belong to me." Not long after these words were spoken, it happened that the aforementioned children of Niobe, in the flower of their youth and in a very short space of time, all died of a pestilence. Their father Amphion, through a sudden illness, became blind, because of which he fell into such great despair that he took his own life with a knife.

Niobe, left a widow of her husband and bereft of so many children, became mute through excessive grief, so that she appeared to be an immobile and unfeeling stone <note>In Greek mythology, Niobe was said to have been turned into a weeping rock on Mount Sipylus.</note> rather than a woman. I say that proud women are a troublesome and unbearable thing; the reason is that men are produced by nature with a high and broad spirit, whereas women are produced with a low spirit and humble intellect, being more suited and disposed to the mouth and the spindle <note>The spindle (original: "fuso") was the primary tool for spinning wool, serving here as a symbol of domestic, private life as opposed to public rule.</note> than to the ruling of empires. 

For this reason, it is not much to be wondered at if the wrath of God <note>The author uses "wrath of God" (original: "lira de dio") to frame this pagan myth within a medieval moral context.</note> stoops more readily upon proud women than upon men; they are more cruelly judged and punished whenever, through pride, they overstep and exceed the limits of their weakness and nature, just as the foolish and presumptuous Niobe did. She, deceived by Fortune, did not recognize the grace of having such a multitude of children as coming from anywhere other than her own merit <term>virtu</term> <note>original: "uirtu," referring here to her own perceived power or excellence rather than moral virtue.</note>. This Niobe, acting more impulsively than wisely, deserved to weep for her misfortune during her lifetime, and deserved that for ages to come her name would be unpleasant and distasteful to all those who follow after her.

## Concerning Hypsipyle, Queen of Lemnos.

Hypsipyle <note>Hypsipyle was a legendary figure in Greek mythology. While the other women of Lemnos murdered their male relatives, she famously saved her father.</note> was a noble and notable woman, both for the devotion she showed to her father and for her unhappy exile; likewise for the sake of the child for whom she was a nurse, and for finding her sons again in her great hour of need. Truly, Hypsipyle was the daughter of Thoas, King of Lemnos <note>Lemnos is an island in the northern Aegean Sea.</note>, who reigned at that time. A fury entered the minds of the women of that island: a desire to be entirely rid of every burden and to withdraw their necks from under the yoke <term>yoke: a wooden beam used on oxen to pull heavy loads; here used as a metaphor for the subjection of women to men</term> of the lordship of men. Therefore, despising the rule of the old and aged Thoas, they all determined together with Hypsipyle that on the following night they should take up arms and kill every male.

And so they did. But it appearing to Hypsipyle an inhumane thing to stain her hands with her father's blood, she put him in a ship and sent him to Chios—called Scio today—and he escaped. Having suddenly built a rich and grand tomb, she gave the women to understand that she wished to make a burial for her father. This was believed and they placed their faith in her, and by them she was made Queen. Truly, piety <note>original: "pieta" — in this context, it refers to "filial piety," the ancient virtue of duty, respect, and devotion to one’s parents.</note> is a most holy thing, especially that of children toward their father. For what thing is more fitting for us, more of a duty, more just, or more praiseworthy than to return to a father...

...render to her father his due honor. And there, taking to the land, he took the island by force. Being received by Hypsipyle with great honor, he slept with her and stayed for a long time. Upon his departure, he left her pregnant with two sons, whom she later bore.

And being compelled by their law to send them away from the island—as some authors claim—she sent them to their grandfather, that is, to her father. When this matter was discovered by the other women of Lemnos, and seeing that they had all been deceived by Hypsipyle, they suddenly rose up in a fury against her. She, fleeing and boarding a ship, was captured by pirates and led away to be a slave.

Ultimately, she was given as a slave to King Lycurgus <note>The King of Nemea.</note>, who had a small son named Opheltes, who was dear to him beyond measure; he gave the boy into the care and governance of Hypsipyle. It happened by chance that while the said Hypsipyle was carrying the child in a meadow, King Adrastus <note>One of the Seven against Thebes.</note> passed through that country with a great army on his way to Thebes. Seeing Hypsipyle, he begged her to show them a fountain or other water where they might refresh themselves. Leaving the child among the grass, she went with them and showed them the water. 

Truly, as Adrastus walked together with Hypsipyle, and she appearing to him to be a person of high character <term>character: "costume" in the original, referring to her noble bearing and moral conduct</term>, he asked her who she was and how she had come to be there. As she explained all her past misfortunes to him, she was recognized by Euneus <note>The text uses "Allante," a variation of the name Euneus, Jason's son.</note> and Thoas <note>The text uses "Lhoante," likely referring to the younger Thoas (Nebrophonus).</note>, her sons, who were already grown and noble knights in the service of the said King Adrastus. And so, she was suddenly raised to the hope of a better fortune for having found her two sons...

### Of Medea, Queen of Colchis.

...her grace <note>Referring to her overwhelming passion mentioned on the previous page.</note> worked so effectively that through the plots <note>original: "subductione", implying secret uprisings or deceptions.</note> born among the men of her country, war was raised against her father. Because of this, Jason had the opportunity to put his purpose into effect. What wise man would ever be able to judge or think that from a single glance of the eyes should follow the ruin of a most wealthy King? The sin having then been committed, Medea immediately and secretly fled with the said Jason, carrying with her all the treasure and every wealth of her father. Not content with such a great evil—and because the most wicked and cruel woman <note>original: "semia", likely a transcription error for "femina" (woman) or "semen" (offspring).</note> thought that her father would pursue her—to give him cause and reason to delay and to give herself time to flee, she devised an unheard-of thing. She commanded that his limbs be placed step by step through the fields, so that while the father was occupied collecting them and shedding tears over them, she would have more time to escape; and it happened just as she had planned.

After much wandering, the said Medea arrived with her Jason in Thessaly, where her father-in-law <note>Aeson, the father of Jason.</note> was. Because of Jason’s return and the noble kinship he brought with him, the father felt such joy that he almost seemed to have turned from an old man back into a youth. <note>In myth, Medea literally restored Aeson's youth with a magical potion; the author here treats it more metaphorically.</note> Here, the said Medea, so that Jason might obtain the kingdom of Pelias <note>original: "Pelleo", Jason's usurping uncle.</note>, used her magic arts to sow such discord and scandal between the said Pelias and his own daughters that, being incited against him, they killed him. But leaving aside many things that could be said of her, after many years—having been despised and abandoned by Jason, who had taken Creusa, daughter of Creon <note>original: "Creōte"</note>, King of the Corinthians, in her place—the impatient and enraged Medea planned many most cruel things against Jason. ### Of Medea, Queen of Colchis.

...[the] daughter of Creon <note>Creusa or Glauce, whom Jason married after abandoning Medea.</note>, in the sight of Jason, she killed the two sons she had by him. Then she fled to Athens, where she married King Aegeus; and having conceived by him a son whom she named Medus <note>original: "Pomedo", a likely corruption of Medus.</note>, she attempted to poison Theseus, the son of Aegeus. But her scheme being in vain and the matter discovered, she fled for a third time. And having returned to Jason’s favor, she was driven out of all Thessaly along with him by Acastus <note>original: "Agelao", referring to Acastus, the son of Pelias, who took the throne after his father's death.</note>, the son of Pelias. Thus driven out, they went to Colchis, where they restored her old and banished father, Aeetes <note>original: "Oete".</note>, to his lordship. But what followed afterward for them, I do not recall having read or heard.

But lest I leave some things unsaid, I say that too much license should not be granted to the sight of the eyes. For through them we recognize all aspiring splendors, and envy is brought into us, and through their disordered looking, <term>concupiscentie: strong, often lustful or worldly desires</term> and disordered desires arise within us. Through them, greed is exercised and beauty is praised; by their judgment, the squalor of poverty is unworthily condemned. And since the eyes are untaught and unlearned judges, they believe only in the surface of things. They prefer the most wicked things to the sacred, the shameful to the true, and the feigned and false to the light and joyful, the agonizing and the sad. And while they commend and praise deceitful and false things that ought to be rejected, it often happens that the soul is stained with a most foul mark. 

Those who do not know better are captured by dishonest loveliness and beauty—with biting nails <note>A metaphor for how physical beauty "grips" the observer.</note>, and by lascivious signs and acts, and by youthful infirmity—by which things they are often snatched, pulled, and held fast. And because the eyes are the gate of the heart <note>original: "pecto", literally "chest" or "breast".</note>, through them he <note>Likely referring to Cupid or the personification of Desire.</note> enters and sends his messengers to the mind and...

### Of Medea, Queen of Colchis.

...[while the eyes] commend and [yield] to desirous <term>libido: unrestrained desire or lust</term>; through them, Cupid blows sighs and the fires of blind flames into the heart, displaying outwardly sad laments and shameful effects. If anyone recognized these things for what they are, they would either abandon them, or direct them toward heaven, or cast them down to the earth, since between these two paths there is no safe way. These things, if they cannot be completely avoided, must be restrained with sharp and stern countenances; for this reason, nature has placed "doors" <note>Referring to the eyelids.</note> upon the eyes, not only so that they might be closed within the breast, but so that they might be a shield against harmful things. Certainly, if the powerful Medea had kept them shut, or turned them toward the earth, or directed them elsewhere when she looked upon Jason, her father’s power would have lasted longer, and her brother's life and the honor of her virginity would have remained intact. All of these things perished and failed because of the shameful gaze of her eyes.

Ancient writers claim that she was the first <note>The text likely continues to describe her as the inventor of weaving or the first to use a loom.</note>...

<meta>This page continues the biography of Arachne of Colophon, transitioning from her parentage to her legendary skills and the pride that led to her downfall.</meta>

->### Of Arachne, a woman of Colophon.<-

She discovered the use of spinning linen and first made nets for bird-catching and fishing. Her son, whose name was Closter <note>original: "clostroro"; in mythology, Closter is credited with inventing the spindle (closter in Greek means spindle).</note>, first discovered the spindles suitable for the trade of linen. Some believe that she was the one who began the art of weaving; they say that she possessed such subtle <term>ingegnio: innate talent or creative intellect</term> that with her fingers she devised and wove things that a perfect painter could not have depicted better. Her fame had spread across almost the entire world.

Because of this, she rose to such great pride that she presumed to enter into a competition against Pallas <note>Pallas Athena, the goddess of wisdom, weaving, and strategy.</note>, the inventor of this art. Not being able to endure with a patient mind the prospect of being defeated by her, she ended her life by hanging herself with a noose. This event gave rise to the fiction of the poets' fable <note>Referring to Ovid’s myth where Athena transforms the hanging Arachne into a spider so she may weave forever.</note>. Others say that although she hanged herself with the noose, she was helped and did not die, but out of grief, she abandoned her craft.

Now, it follows that if there is any person who believes they surpass others in any art, I pray that Arachne herself might tell them whether they think they can turn the heavens so that all its virtues are drawn into themselves alone. Or perhaps they think they can make the benevolent God, the giver of all things, open the bosom of His magnificence and courtesy and be constrained to infuse His gifts into them alone, depriving everyone else.

But why do I ask this? The nature of heaven turns all things upside down with eternal law, giving art and ingenuity anew to all people. And just as through sloth and sadness these talents become lazy, I know those who through study and good practice become brilliant, their minds becoming capable of the greatest things. By the adoption of that same nature, we are led to desire great things, even though we may not be led by that same diligence—or perhaps fortune is so contrary that many are unable to reach their goals.

<summary>This page describes Arachne's invention of linen spinning and weaving, her hubristic challenge to the goddess Pallas Athena, and her subsequent suicide. The author concludes with a moral reflection on pride, the divine source of talent, and the importance of practice over sloth.</summary>

<keywords>Arachne, Colophon, weaving, Pallas Athena, pride, Closter, invention, artistic competition, sloth vs diligence</keywords>

is the very same thing. And so, if anyone thinks of themselves alone among such a great multitude to surpass everyone, and glories and boasts of it, truly this is the work of a most foolish mind. To desire that there were only one Arachne in the world is a mockery and a thing to laugh at, since those who possess such great madness are without number; when they rise in the presumption of such great pride, they are the reason that we laugh at them no less than at spiders.

### Of Orithyia and Antiope, Queens of the Amazons.

**O**rithyia <note>original: "Ortia"</note> was the daughter of Marpesia and lived together with Antiope, whom some believed was her sister. After her mother Marpesia, she became queen. Above all other things, she is to be commended for her perpetual virginity. She was of such great valor, together with Antiope, in battles, that through many victories she expanded the empire of the Amazons with the greatest honors. And she was so worthy of praise in military discipline that Eurystheus <note>original: "Erastro"; likely a reference to King Eurystheus of Mycenae, who ordered the Labors of Hercules.</note>, King of Mycenae <note>original: "Micena"</note>, thought it would be a difficult thing to be able to obtain her belt <term>balteo: a decorative military belt or girdle, which in mythology was a symbol of her authority</term>. And therefore they say that she brought it to Hercules as a man most worthy and never vanquished. But the greatest glory and fame belongs to a woman who shines with...

## Of Erythraea, also known as Eryphile, the Sibyl.

Because *Sios* <note>original: "sylo"; likely a corruption of the Aeolic Greek 'Sios' for God</note> in the Aeolic dialect means God, and *bylos* <note>original: "bíles"</note> means mind; and therefore "Sibyls" means "with a divine mind," or rather a person who has God in their mind. Of all these Sibyls, the wise say that this Erythraea was the wisest, and that she was born in Babylon a little before the battle of the Trojans <note>the Trojan War</note>. Notwithstanding that many think she prophesied in the time of Romulus, King of the Romans. And according to what some say, her name was Eryphile, and she was called Erythraea because she lived for a long time on an island called Erythraea <note>original: "Yrítea"</note>, in which many of her verses were found. There was, therefore, in this Erythraea such great power of intellect, speech, and devotion, and she was of such merit in the sight of God through her long study and honorable works—not, however, without the divine gift—that if what is said and written of her is true, she predicted future things so clearly and truly that her writings appear more like Gospels than divinations. And being asked by the Greeks, she wrote so lucidly of their battles and their adversities and prosperities until the burning of Ilium <note>Troy</note> that nothing was better known than that. And likewise she wrote of the Empire of the Romans. And what seems an even greater thing is that the secret of the divine mind—which [had been revealed] in no other way than through the figures of the Old Testament and through the obscure words of the Prophets—was revealed by her. Rather, through the Holy Spirit <note>original: "fpíríto fancto"</note> which spoke through her mouth, she opened and declared the incarnation of the Divine Word <term>Verbo díuíno: the Logos, or Jesus Christ</term>. And she spoke and wrote of all the things that followed so clearly and so openly that rather...

## Of Medusa, daughter of Phorcys

**M**edusa was the daughter of Phorcys and heir to his riches; he held his kingdom in the Atlantic Sea, which was abundantly wealthy beyond measure. Many believed this kingdom was the Hesperides islands <note>mythical gardens at the western edge of the world where golden apples grew</note>. This Medusa—if one can give faith to the ancients—was of such marvelous beauty that she not only surpassed all others, but was something beyond nature. And she was so wonderful that many men from distant lands traveled to see her. It is said that she first had abundant and long hair, more shining than gold or the rays of the sun. Her face was of a most special and lovely beauty, and her body was large and well-proportioned in perfect measure. But among other more singular things, she had eyes of such living vigor, of such greatness and sweetness, and of such a lovely and pleasing gaze, that whoever was kindly looked upon by them became immobile and almost without feeling <note>The author provides a rationalist explanation for the myth of Medusa turning men to stone: they were simply "petrified" by her extreme beauty</note>. Because, attracted by the sweetness and pleasure of such a pleasing object, nothing else...

...was felt. Furthermore, many say that Medusa was highly skilled and learned in the art of agriculture <note>original: "coltiuare"; the author suggests her "monstrous" reputation stems from her mastery over the earth</note>, and therefore she was nicknamed "Gorgon." Because of these skills, she not only preserved her riches but increased and multiplied them beyond those of any King in the eastern parts. Therefore, both for her marvelous beauty and for her immeasurable wealth, industry, and sagacity, she achieved such great fame that it spread among the Argives <note>The Greeks of Argos</note>. There, Perseus—a prudent young man of high and noble heart—hearing these things and being desirous not only of seeing her but of acquiring her great treasure, boarded a ship which carried as its ensign a horse <note>original: "cauallo"; this rationalizes the myth of Pegasus, the winged horse born from Medusa</note>. With marvelous speed he arrived in the East, and being in the presence of Medusa, with prudence and with arms he seized her; he took the Queen and her treasures, and with very great booty returned to his own country. From this arose the fable of the poets which we read: that Medusa the Gorgon used to turn those she looked upon into stone.

## Of Medusa, daughter of Phorcys

Oh alas, how unhappy is the possession of gold <note>original: "loro," likely a contraction of "l'oro"</note> when it is hidden, for it is of no comfort or profit to its possessor; and if it shines in the open, it gives birth to a thousand snares. If it remains stored away, the fear of being robbed or forced never leaves the possessor; and what is more, the possession of it drives away peace and rest of mind, causes the loss of sleep, increases fear, causes faith to fail, and multiplies suspicion. In short, the entire use of life is subjected to wretched gold. And if it happens for any reason that it is lost or goes missing, it follows that the miser, now made poor, wastes away through anxiety and grief. Then the generous man says "it served him right," the envious man laughs at it, the needy man enjoys it, and all the common folk gossip about the cause of the sufferer's misery.

## Of Iole, daughter of the King of the Aetolians.

Iole, daughter of Eurytus <note>Eurytus was the legendary King of Oechalia, often associated with the region of Aetolia in these accounts</note>, King of the Aetolians, was a maiden most beautiful among all those of that region. And according to what some say, she was the most beloved of Hercules, the subduer of the world. To him, the said Eurytus promised to give her as a wife, but then he refused her, following the opposition and counsel of his son. Hercules, being indignant, waged a most bitter war against the said Eurytus; and conquering all his land by force and killing him, he took and carried away the most beloved Iole. 

She, more sorrowful over the death of her father than content with her marriage to her husband, and being desirous and longing for revenge, with marvelous constancy and cunning—and with feigned and simulated love—concealed the hatred <note>original: "lanío," likely a contraction of "l'animo," referring here to her inner spirit or hidden intent</note> she held against Hercules. With sweet feminine acts, she drew him into such a fervent love for her that she clearly saw and knew he would not deny her anything she might want. 

And so she first commanded him to lay aside his great club <term>mazza: the heavy wooden club which was the iconic weapon of Hercules</term>, and that he should not wear the skin of the dead lion <note>The skin of the Nemean Lion, Hercules's traditional armor</note>, and that he should leave behind his quiver <term>troccafcio: an archaic term for a quiver used to hold arrows</term>, arrows <term>saiecte: a term for arrows or bolts</term>, and bow. And this not being enough for her, with premeditated malice <note>original: "antipensate malitie"</note> she began to move against her disarmed enemy with greater assurance. First, she told him to adorn his fingers with rings, and to anoint his head with Cyprian ointments <note>Perfumed oils from Cyprus, considered a height of luxury and effeminacy in the ancient world</note>, and to comb his curly and disordered hair and his rough beard...

# Of Iole, daughter of the King of the Aetolians

[She commanded that] he should anoint himself with nard <note>original: "Nardo," an expensive aromatic oil or ointment used in antiquity</note> and that he should adorn his head with garlands of young flowers. Then she made him wear the purple mantle; the malicious girl judging that it was a greater and more glorious honor for her to effeminate such a robust man through lust than if she had killed him with a knife.

And also, she led him to stay with a woman among the women-folk; she commanded him to tell the stories of his labors, and giving him with her own hands the wool-combs, she made him spin at the distaff <term>rocha: a staff used in spinning to hold the unspun fibers</term>. 

And truly, this is no small argument for those who wish to guard themselves against the cunning of women in the failing of human infirmity. With this plan, the artful young woman, shaming Hercules with a mark of eternal infamy—not with arms but with eternal lusts—avenged her father's death; and for herself, she acquired an infinite name. For over whatever triumphs of Eurystheus <note>The king who assigned Hercules his twelve famous labors</note> and other monsters the victorious Hercules triumphed, Iole triumphed even more gloriously over Hercules.

It behooves us, therefore, to be vigilant and to arm our hearts with fortitude, because [temptation] cannot harm us if we do not wish it. It is necessary for us to resist at the beginnings and first to restrain the eyes, so that they do not look upon lovely and vain things; and to make one's eyes [closed] to those things like the asp <note>The asp was traditionally said to stop its ears to avoid hearing the charmer; the author here suggests closing the eyes to temptation in a similar fashion</note>, and with continuous and honest studies to overcome lust. 

For the enemy [of virtue] shows itself pleasant in its own appearance; and if he is received by us with joyful hope and delight at the entrance, he then counsels the ornaments of the bodies: beautiful and ornate clothing, the "prudence" of city life <note>original: "creptadínanza," likely a variant of *cittadinanza* or citizenship/urbanity</note>, feasts, dances, songs, composing verses, games, banquets, and similar things. And when, with such vain approval, he has seized and deprived man of his liberty, binding him with chains, the min[d]... <note>the text cuts off mid-sentence at the end of the page</note>

## Of Iole, daughter of the King of the Aetolians.

[The appetites] of men, prolonging vows and promises beyond hope, then awaken sighs and sharpen the wit in artifice, making no distinction between virtue and vice. Provided that he obtains his desire, the hearts of the unhappy and lustful go back and forth, seeking the beloved object with a will that knows no restraint. And so, having gone and returned, seen and seen again, they always find within themselves new fires of passion.

Then it happens that—not out of true penance, but for his own ends—he weeps and sheds tears; he composes verses and words of sweet prayers; he instructs and sends messengers <note>original: "mezzani," go-betweens or procurers</note> with promises and gifts; he spends, throws his wealth away, and with prodigal giving, he deceives the guards.

And thus, he sometimes achieves the desired embraces. Then the enemy of honor and chastity, being greedy, puts honesty and shame to flight. He prepares the "delights of the pig" <note>A common Renaissance metaphor for base, animalistic carnal filth</note> and pours himself into the most dishonest couplings <term>coiti: sexual acts</term>. Then, wretched lewdness, having driven away every act of sobriety, calls upon Venus along with Ceres <note>Goddess of agriculture and bread</note> and Bacchus <note>God of wine</note>; and thus he consumes the whole night in harmful and burning lust <note>original: "luxuria," which in this period specifically referred to the sin of lust rather than just material wealth</note>. 

Nor for all that is the wicked appetite extinguished; rather, the more it is indulged, the more it grows. Because of these things, one falls into that detestable disgrace into which Hercules fell; and by subduing his honors and consuming his riches, hatreds are armed, and very often one incurs the perils of life. These things are not without affliction or pain, because there arise disputes, quarrels, and battles, a lack of peace, suspicions, jealousies, and anxieties that consume the soul and the body. 

Oh, how sweet is this love, how pleasing and suave it seems! Notwithstanding that we ought to flee and drive it away from us entirely, we nevertheless allow it, love it, and cultivate it...

### Of Deianira, wife of Hercules.

He <note>Referring to the centaur Nessus, who was just shot by Hercules's poisoned arrow.</note>, feeling himself wounded and considering himself already dead, immediately stripped off his garments; and giving the shirt soaked in the poisoned blood to Deianira, he told her that if she could manage to have Hercules wear it, it would have the power to bind him <term>original: "catenare"; literally 'to chain'</term> to her in love, regardless of what other woman he might desire. Deianira, believing this to be true, took it and kept it secretly in place of a great gift. Later, she sent it by a servant of hers named Lichas <note>original: "Licho"</note> to Hercules, who at that time was in love with **Iole**. Hercules then, having put it on, and the heat of his sweat dissolving the blood infected with poison—which was then absorbed <note>original: "beuēdōlō"; literally 'drinking it'</note> through his pores, which had opened due to the heat—became so anguished and enraged that he judged himself to be burning up; and thus, while burning, he died. Deianira, widowed of such a great man, thinking to draw him back from the love of others to herself, instead lost him and—unintentionally—avenged the death of Nessus.

## Of Jocasta, Queen of Thebes.

conceived a son. Because of a contrary response <note>The famous prophecy that any son born to Laius would kill his father and marry his mother.</note> that **Laius** had from his oracles, he commanded that the child be sent to the fields and abandoned to the wild beasts. Laius, thinking that the boy had been devoured and was dead, was mistaken; the child was instead nurtured by the King of the Corinthians and taken as an adopted son. And having already grown and reached a mature age, Laius was killed by him <note>Oedipus killed Laius at a crossroads, not knowing the man was his biological father.</note>. Afterwards, **Jocasta** remaining a widow, she unknowingly <term>original: "disavedutamente"; literally 'unwittingly' or 'without realization'</term> took her own son for a husband. And by him she had two sons, **Eteocles** <note>original: "Ehiocle"</note> and **Polynices** <note>original: "Pollinice"</note>, and two daughters, **Ismene** <note>original: "Ysmena"</note> and **Antigone** <note>original: "Anthigona"</note>. And while it seemed to her that she was quite happy, both because of the kingdom and because of her children, she learned through the response of the gods that he whom she thought was her husband was actually her son.

This revelation was a cause of great displeasure and grief to her. It was even more so for her husband and son <note>Oedipus, who was both her husband and her son.</note>, who, on account of the sin committed, desiring eternal night <note>A poetic way of describing blindness or the desire to no longer see the world.</note>, immediately gouged out his eyes and renounced the kingdom. Taking up this kingdom, his sons fell into discord and came to battle against each other. To Jocasta, their mother, it was a great sadness seeing them go to fight one another many times; but it was greater still when she saw them kill each other with a single blow.

At which grief, the wretched mother and grandmother <note>She was mother to the warring brothers, but in this twisted family tree, their father was her son, making her their grandmother as well.</note> being unable to endure it, and seeing **Creon** <note>original: "Creonte"</note>, her brother, holding the kingdom by force, and her husband-son blind in prison, and Ismene and Antigone her daughters left to their tangled and failing <term>original: "labente"; meaning slipping or falling away</term> fortune—old and anguished by so many sorrows, refusing to live any longer—with a knife she ended her anguish together with her life. Nevertheless, there are some who say that the said Jocasta could not sustain such great sorrows, but that as soon as she saw **Oedipus** <note>original: "Edippo"</note> gouge out his eyes, she killed herself.

# Of Amalthea, or rather Deiphobe, the Sibyl.

**Amalthea**, a virgin whom some call **Deiphobe**, daughter of **Glaucus** <note>In mythology, Glaucus was a sea-god who loved the Sibyl.</note>. It is believed that she had her birth from **Cumae** <note>original: "Cimia"</note>, an ancient castle of **Chalcis** <note>original: "Caladisy"; referring to the Euboean Greeks from Chalcis who founded Cumae.</note> in Campania. It is said that she was named one of the **Sibyls** and that she flourished in fame at the time of the desolation of Troy, and that she lived until the time of **Tarquin the Elder** <term>Tarquino prischo: Lucius Tarquinius Priscus, the legendary fifth king of Rome (reigned 616–579 BC).</term>, King of the Romans. 

And truly, this **Amalthea**—it is the opinion of many—was of such virtue and virginity that, according to what the ancients testify, in such a great and long span of time, there was never anyone who "knew" her <note>A historical euphemism for sexual intimacy; the text emphasizes her perpetual virginity despite her long life.</note>. Notwithstanding that through the writings of the poets it is found that she was loved by **Phoebus** <note>Apollo, the god of prophecy.</note>, and by his gift she lived for a long space of centuries and received from him the gift of prophecy <term>original: "uaticinio"; the divinely inspired ability to foretell the future.</term>, that is, the science of divining and predicting future things. 

But certainly, I believe that by her own merit she was illuminated by that Light and true Sun <note>The author here subtly shifts from the pagan Apollo to a Christian interpretation of the "True Sun" (God/Christ) as the source of her visions.</note> who enlightens every man in this world, and that she had the grace of prophesying and predicting many things to those who came after her. It is also found regarding her that on the shore of **Baiae** <note>original: "Baiano"; a Roman seaside resort town.</note>, near **Lake Avernus** <note>original: "lacho dauernia"; a volcanic crater lake believed by Romans to be the entrance to the Underworld.</note>, there was a noble oratory <note>A small chapel or place of prayer.</note> which I have seen and heard is called after her even to the present day; which oratory...

# Of Amalthea, or rather Deiphobe, the Sibyl.

Although it has been corroded by long periods of time and diminished by little care, it <note>The author likely refers here to the cave or sanctuary of the Sibyl at Cumae mentioned on the previous page.</note> nonetheless still retains its ancient majesty and creates admiration for its greatness in those who see it. There are some who say that she guided **Aeneas** to the underworld <note>A reference to Book VI of Virgil's Aeneid, where the Sibyl leads Aeneas into the land of the dead.</note>, a thing which I do not believe, and I have spoken of it elsewhere. Those who say that she lived for a long span of time affirm that she came to **Rome** in the time of **Tarquin the Elder** <term>Tarqno prisco: Lucius Tarquinius Priscus, the fifth legendary King of Rome.</term> and brought him nine books, for which she asked a certain price. 

Because **Tarquin** did not wish to give her that price, suddenly in his presence she burned three of them, and then asked the same price for the six remaining that she had asked for the nine. She said that if it were not given to her, she would burn more, and would keep only three. On the following day, the price was given by **Tarquin**. Having saved them, **Tarquin** and then his successors saw that they contained all the future deeds of the **Romans**. With the greatest diligence they were preserved, and according to the needs of the moment, they recurred to them for advice and counsel on future things as if to a most true oracle.

And truly, for me it is very difficult to believe that she was the same person as **Deiphobe**; nevertheless, we read of her that she died in **Sicily**, where for a long time the men of the country showed her tomb. Therefore, one can clearly understand—first through the grace of **God**, and then through our own study (two things which were never denied to whoever made themselves worthy)—that the wisdom of truth is acquired. For if we, being idle and lazy, would look and consider well the time lost from our birth until the time we die old and are buried, we would see that if through care, study, and vigils, women attain to divinity <note>The author uses "divinity" here to mean divine wisdom or prophetic insight.</note>, then what should wretched men think, who have a more ready aptitude than... <note>The sentence breaks off, continuing on the next page.</note>

# Of Nicostrata, or rather Carmenta, daughter of King Jove.

### ¶ Of Nicostrata, or rather Carmenta, daughter of King Jove.

**N**icostrata—to whom the Italians later gave the name Carmenta—was the daughter of Jove <note>original: "Ioue"; referring to the mythological Jupiter, here treated as a historical King of Arcadia.</note>, King of Arcadia. According to some, she was married to Pallas of Arcadia, while others say she was his daughter-in-law. However, she was not only illustrious and notable for the splendor of her kingdom, but also for her virtue and her most subtle and elevated intellect. Because of this, she was not only highly learned in literature and the Greek language, but through her admirable intellect and diligent, watchful study, her prophecy <term>vatticinio: the power of foretelling the future; vaticination.</term> penetrated even into future events. 

She revealed things to come through verses, which she often wrote herself. For this reason, her name was changed by the Latins to **Carmenta**, derived from the Latin verses called poems <term>carmini: from the Latin "carmina," meaning songs, poems, or ritual incantations.</term> which she used above all others in those times. This Carmenta was truly the mother of **Evander**, King of the Arcadians <note>Evander was a hero who, according to legend, brought Greek culture and the alphabet to Italy before the founding of Rome.</note>. This Evander...

# Of Nicostrata, or rather Carmenta, daughter of King Jove.

According to the fables of the ancients—either because he was eloquent and persuasive, or because he was clever and wise—they say that he [Evander] was conceived by **Mercury** <note>The Roman god of eloquence and messages; the text suggests Evander's wisdom made people think he was the son of a god.</note>. According to some, having killed the man who was his father, or according to others due to a rebellion of his citizens, or for some other reason, he was driven out of his kingdom by the counsel and persuasions of his mother, **Carmenta**. Through her power of prophecy, she promised him great things if he would go to inhabit that country [Italy]. 

**Carmenta** and **Evander** set out together; they boarded a ship and, after sailing, reached the mouth of the **Tiber** in the place where **Ostia** was later located. When Carmenta reached the hill named **Palatine** by them—the place where **Rome** was later built—she constructed the castle of **Pallante** <note>Also known as Pallanteum, the mythical settlement founded by Evander before the rise of Rome.</note> there. However, Carmenta found the men of that country to be almost wild; although in a previous age they had been instructed in farming by **Saturn**, they knew no letters except for Greek, and even those were few. 

Looking far ahead with her divine mind and seeing what great fame and magnificent grandeur was being prepared and destined to follow in that place—namely, **Rome**—and wishing to record these great deeds so that they would be known to her successors, she thought it an unworthy thing to reveal those matters to future centuries in a foreign language. Therefore, she taught the people new and proper letters, distinct from those of other nations. 

Having discovered new characters <note>original: "caratole"; meaning the shapes of the letters themselves.</note> according to the Italian idiom, she showed and taught their combinations, contenting herself with having carved sixteen figures <note>original: ".xvi. figure"; the original Latin alphabet is traditionally said to have consisted of 16 letters.</note>, just as a long time before, **Cadmus**, the founder of **Thebes**, had invented and found for the Greeks. These letters, to the present day, we call **Latin**, despite the fact that certain wise men have necessarily added others to them; of which letters the Latins, besides...

# Of Nicostrata, or rather Carmenta, daughter of King Jove.

...beyond measure, they marveled at it. Because of this discovery—such a great thing—they absolutely did not believe she was a mortal woman, but rather a goddess. For this reason, having honored her with divine honors during her life, after her death they dedicated a sacred place to her name. Although it was small, it was located beneath the lowest part of the **Capitoline Hill** <note>Original: *monte de Campidoglio*</note> where she was accustomed to live; and to perpetuate her memory, they called the adjacent places by her name. Even after Rome had reached its great state, it did not wish for these honors to be removed; rather, they increased them, for they called a gate of the city—which they had built out of necessity—the **Carmenta Gate** <note>A gate in the Servian Wall of Rome, located near the Capitoline Hill.</note> for a long time.

Truly, in the past, Italy was not only flourishing with more gifts than any other region, but was almost a splendid and celestial light. It is not found that any province ever acquired under its sky so much light nor such splendid radiance. From **Asia** came an abundance of wealth, royalty, and the nobility of clear blood. The Greeks first received <term>arithmetic</term> <note>The art of counting and calculating.</note> from the Trojans, and the Egyptians gave them the art of <term>geometry</term> <note>The study of shapes, sizes, and the properties of space.</note>. They truly also received <term>philosophy</term> <note>The study of the fundamental nature of knowledge, reality, and existence.</note>, eloquence, and <term>mechanics</term> <note>The branch of applied mathematics dealing with motion and forces.</note> from the Greeks, as well as works of the leaders <note>Could refer to specialized intellectual or technical crafts known only to a few elites.</note> known by few. 

Furthermore, the exiled **Saturn** taught them the cultivation of the gods. From the **Etruscans** <note>Original: *etusy*</note>, **Numa Pompilius** <note>The legendary second king of Rome, credited with establishing many of Rome's religious and political institutions.</note> took the public laws (which originated earlier in **Athens**); in the council of the **Senate**, he consulted with the emperors; and **Simon Peter** brought the high priesthood and sincere religion from **Jerusalem**. The ancient **Romans** set the military art in order, by means of which—through the force of arms and of virtuous and strong bodies—complete charity [was maintained] in the republic...

### Of Nicostrata, or rather Carmenta, daughter of King Jove.

...just as they conquered the empire of the whole world, so too they believed she was a goddess; they believed that Carmenta gave us the doctrine of **Grammar** <note>The set of structural rules governing the composition of clauses, phrases, and words.</note>. This grammar, through the succession of time, was amplified and extended by the followers of **Priscian** <note>A famous Latin grammarian from around 500 AD whose work became the standard for teaching Latin.</note> into a more ample abundance. For this kind work, she was the maker, or rather the giver, of the letters <note>Original: *gre*, likely a shorthand for *lettere* (letters).</note>, so that through the use of her invention, the use of the **Hebrew** and **Greek** languages diminished. All of **Europe**, the greatest part of the world, uses our letters found by Carmenta. From these letters, infinite volumes of books and writings in many branches of knowledge and science shine forth.

In these books are written many great sciences of worthy men, known to us because they were preserved in perpetual memory through these letters. This was done so that those things which we ourselves cannot see—because they are distant or past—may notify us of our intentions through them. With these letters, we join in friendships with people from distant countries, and through them, we maintain those bonds. 

These letters discern and define God for us, as far as it is possible for you to ask; they describe for us the heavens, the earth, and every living thing. There is nothing you could possibly ask that you cannot know and understand from them if you wish. To conclude briefly, I say that whatever thing you cannot comprehend or retain with your mind, you may entrust to letters as a most faithful guardian and guide. 

And of such an excellent and noble gift, we have lost some part and given some to others; and many we still retain, although more in name than in fact—whatever the cause may be, whether it was willed by our sin or by fortune. Nevertheless, neither **German** rapacity, nor the fury of the **Gauls** <note>The French.</note>, nor the cunning of the **English**, nor the ferocity of the **Spanish**, nor any other barbarian nation, nor the assault of any other province, has been able to take this so great, so useful, and so remarkable glory from the Latin name; nor would they dare to say...

From this it follows that the further they are carried, the more the honor and praise of the **Latin name** <note>The cultural and linguistic identity of the Roman and Italian people.</note> grows and expands. They bear a clearer witness to our most ancient and noble genius and preserve an incorruptible proof of our **perspicacity** <note>Sharpness of sight or intellect; here, the Roman capacity for invention.</note>, even though barbarian, unfaithful, and envious nations distance themselves from it. For such great and exalted splendor, we ought to thank God who gave it to us; nevertheless, we must also give much praise, fame, and credit to Carmenta. Therefore, so that we cannot be justly reproached by anyone, it is a most pious thing that we hold her name in our minds with all our strength.

# Of Procris, wife of Cephalus.

...with happy and pleasant love, yet misfortune followed them because a woman of marvelous beauty, whose name was **Aurora** <note>The goddess of the Dawn, who in mythology fell in love with the mortal Cephalus.</note>, fell in love with **Cephalus**. Desiring to be together with him, she was unable to even for a very short time, because Cephalus had his soul set upon his **Procris**. During that long time, [Aurora] tempted him with sweet prayers, and being unable to draw him into her love, she said these words to Cephalus in her indignation: "Cephalus, you will yet repent having loved Procris so fervently, and you will find for certain that if someone tempts her, she will prefer another's love to yours."

Cephalus, having heard this and being desirous to test if this could be so, feigned going to some distant place; and without leaving his own country, he had a certain **mediator** <note>An intermediary or messenger, often used in romantic schemes to test a lover's fidelity.</note> tempt his Procris. At the first beginning, he found her strong; but as the mediator pursued her with flatteries and multiplied promises and gifts, Procris yielded and agreed to do the will of the requester.

When Cephalus learned of this, he was grieved and revealed to Procris that he had discovered her intent through fraud and had recognized that her love was not true and whole. She, overcome by shame and grief, fled and went to stay in the forests. But Cephalus, who loved her beyond measure, pardoning her with pleas and great effort, called her back into his grace.

But truly, forgiveness is a short remedy against the **bite of conscience** <note>original: "morso de la conscientia"; a persistent feeling of guilt or psychological unease.</note>. This conscience always led Procris to think vain things and to have jealousy and suspicion that the pleasantries and flatteries of Cephalus were feigned and done by the advice of Aurora. Therefore, setting out secretly and following [Cephalus], a hunter of **Diana** <note>The goddess of the hunt; Cephalus was a renowned hunter frequently associated with her.</note>, she went through mountains, through caves, through valleys, and through ditches. And Cephalus following her <note>This likely describes the confusion of their movements as they shadowed one another.</note>, and Procris going and hiding herself stay—
