Now I do not know well which of two things I should judge to be greater: the power of gold on earth, or the folly of seeking that which a man would not wish to find. These two things—while the foolish Procris was [tempted]—drew upon her an indelible mark and a death which she in no way sought. 

But let us leave aside the disordered appetite for gold; I pray those who are jealous to tell me what profit they have from their jealousy, and what honors, praise, and glory follow from it. Truly, in my judgment, this jealousy is a ridiculous foolishness and an infirmity of the mind, which begins from the faint-heartedness <term>pusillanimita</term> <note>Pusillanimity: a lack of courage or spirit; a weakness of character where one feels small or inferior.</note> of the jealous man, who esteems himself to be of such little virtue that he easily believes everyone else is preferred over him.

# Of Argia, wife of Polynices and daughter of King Adrastus.

[She left] a complete, illustrious, and perpetual testimony of perfect matrimonial love toward her dear husband. Because of this, her name became shining and splendid with perpetual clarity even to our days. This woman, then, was married to **Polynices**, son of **Oedipus**, King of **Thebes**, who was banished from his country. Having already had a son whom she named **Thersander**, and knowing and perceiving that through the deceit of his brother, the said **Polynices** was occupied and distressed by great anxiety and biting thoughts, she shared with him all his tribulations and labors. 

She did not only beg her father, who was already old, with devout and effective prayer and with tears, that he should go to help her husband at **Thebes** against **Eteocles** <note>original: *Theochle*. This refers to Eteocles, who fought his brother Polynices for the throne.</note>, the brother of **Polynices**, or rather against **Creon**, his kinsman <note>original: *cūyo* (cognato) — brother-in-law or kinsman.</note>, who had recklessly seized the kingdom. But so that his expedition would not be prevented by a fateful prophecy <note>The seer Amphiaraus had foretold his own death in the war and tried to avoid going.</note>, she went beyond the nature of women and was generous in giving to **Eriphyle** <note>The text says "Euridice," but the historical and mythological figure who took the bribe was Eriphyle, the wife of Amphiaraus.</note>, the wife of the seer **Amphiaraus**, a necklace <term>formaglio</term> <note>A "formaglio" was a decorative brooch or jeweled clasp used to fasten garments, highly prized as an heirloom.</note> of gold and precious stones, such as the women of **Thebes** used to wear at that time. This was so that the wife might persuade her husband to provide the response needed for the expedition; and so it followed, even though the outcome was unhappy.

But after the great battle and the mortality and the most cruel slaughter was done, and the majority of all the dukes and great lords were dead, King **Adrastus** remained alone and half-defeated. **Argia**, having learned the sorrowful news and having heard that her **Polynices** lay unburied, immediately departed from the royal palace and the temple of her father. Setting aside every feminine act and fear, and despising the clothing and every adornment of her body and of her royal chamber, she went toward the field, and there among the wretched...

# Of Argia, wife of Polynices and daughter of King Adrastus.

[She had] no fear of those who rob on the roads, nor of wild beasts, nor of demons <note>original: *d̃ moni*. The text refers to the superstitious fears of the time.</note> which, according to what fools believe, haunt such places. But what is even stronger and more praiseworthy is that she defied the fierce command of **Creon**, who had decreed that no body [of the fallen enemies] should be buried under penalty of death. All these things could not prevent **Argia**, with her sorrowful and burning spirit, from going and entering at midnight into the field where the dead lay. There, she searched and turned over now one body, now another, accompanied only by a single young girl, until at last, in her grief, she found him. 

Oh, wonderful thing! Oh, wonderful affection! His face, dried by the rust of his armor and covered in dust and decaying blood, could not be hidden from her; nor could the wounds of his stained and infected face keep her from bathing him with her tears. But **Argia**, having sought for any remaining spark of life <note>original: *ſpirito uitale* — the "vital spirit" or breath of life.</note> through many embraces, and having called to her dead husband with tears and loud, sorrowful cries, took the body of her **Polynices**. According to their custom, she placed him in a great fire, and once he was burned and entirely consumed, she took the ashes and placed them in an earthen vessel. 

Truly, I find that many women have often wept for the illnesses of their husbands, their imprisonments, and many other misfortunes, hoping for better fortune or driven by the onset of a more cruel fear. While such a thing is laudable and a sign of love, it cannot be called the "extreme sign of affection and love" in the way it can be said of **Argia**. For **Argia**, in order to weep for and find her husband, chose to turn over with her own hands the chest of a stinking, dead body. Though she could have commanded others to do it, she performed the funeral rites and built the royal fires herself. Furthermore, considering the danger of the time, it would have been enough to bury him secretly, yet she wept for him with loud and feminine screams where she might have acted quietly to save herself. Nor did she have any fear of the dead, and...

### Of Manto, daughter of Tiresias.

### ¶ Of Manto, daughter of Tiresias.

**Manto**, the daughter of **Tiresias**—the very great soothsayer <note>original: *idouino* (indovino) — a diviner or prophet.</note> of **Thebes**—was considered most venerable during the time of King **Oedipus** and his sons. And truly, under the instruction and teaching of her father, she possessed such a subtle, prompt, and elevated genius for the art of chiromancy <note>original: *chiromāzia* — traditionally palmistry, though here it refers more broadly to the occult sciences.</note>—which was discovered in the most ancient times by the **Chaldeans** or perhaps by **Nimrod** <note>original: *Nebroth*. In medieval and Renaissance tradition, Nimrod was often cited as the first practitioner of magic and the builder of the Tower of Babel.</note>—that she understood and knew how to practice it so marvelously that in her time no one knew it better. For by the movements of flames, by their colors and their crackling <note>original: *mormoramenti* — literally "murmurings" or "whisperings," referring to the sounds made by sacrificial fires.</note>—and I know not by what diabolical art—she knew and foretold future things. Beyond this, she understood the bleating of sheep, the lowing of bulls, and the various actions of any animal with such marvelous intuition that very often (according to what some believe) she used her art to compel unclean spirits <note>original: *spiriti immondi* — demons or malevolent spirits summoned through necromancy.</note> to have to speak...

<summary>This page concludes the eulogy for Argia’s marital loyalty and introduces Manto, the daughter of the blind prophet Tiresias, detailing her mastery over various forms of divination and the occult.</summary>
<keywords>Argia, Creon, Manto, Tiresias, Thebes, Oedipus, divination, chiromancy, Nimrod, Chaldeans, prophecy</keywords>

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Manto, the daughter of Tiresias. It bridges the transition from her life as a famous seer in Thebes to the various legends regarding her exile and the eventual founding of the city of Mantua.</meta>

...to you and give an answer. Truly, once the **Argive Kings** who had besieged **Thebes** were already dead in the battle, and **Creon** had seized lordship, this **Manto**—according to what some say—fled the new King. Having gone into **Asia**, she established the temple of the bright **Apollo** <note>original: *chiaro Appoline*. This refers to the famous Oracle of Apollo at Claros, which tradition says Manto founded.</note> and was most marvelous in divination. There she gave birth to **Mopsus**, a great soothsayer of his time, although the place of his birth is not clearly known. Others say that with certain of her companions after the battle of **Thebes**, she wandered for a long time through the world and finally arrived in **Italy**. Having arrived there, she conceived a son by **Tiberinus** <note>The river god of the Tiber.</note>, whom she named **Cytheone** <note>More commonly known in classical mythology as Ocnus.</note>, though others call him **Bianor**. Departing from there, she went into **Cisalpine Gaul**—that is, **Lombardy**—where she found marshy and watery places bordering the lake of **Renagho** <note>original: *Renagho*. Likely referring to Lake Benaco (Lake Garda) or the marshy waters of the Mincio river that surround Mantua.</note> which were strong by their very nature.

There, so that she might more freely attend to her enchantments—or perhaps to be more secure within the marsh, made strong by the surrounding waters—she settled, stayed, lived, and died; and there she was buried. Some say that around her tomb, **Cytheone** built the city and, out of reverence for his mother, called it **Mantua**. Some others say that she, with most constant purpose, preserved her virginity until death. This would have been a flourishing and most holy work in her, and much to be praised, had she not contaminated it with her diabolical art <note>The author reflects a common medieval and Renaissance view: while Manto’s discipline and virginity were "holy" virtues, her use of magic was seen as "diabolical" because it was not directed toward the Christian God.</note>; for had she reserved it for the true God, some honor would be reserved and declared for her.

<summary>This page describes Manto's flight from Thebes to Asia and eventually to Italy, where she settled in the marshes of Lombardy. It explores the legendary origins of the city of Mantua, named after her by her son Cytheone (Ocnus).</summary>

## Of the Wives of the Minyans.

The number and the names of the wives of the **Minyans** <note>original: *Menia*. The Minyans were a group of Greek heroes, often identified as the descendants of the Argonauts.</note>, whether through the fault of the writers or for some other reason due to the antiquity of the time, have been taken from us, and we do not find them written down. This is certainly an injustice, for they did not deserve to be placed in special glory only through common words and ordinary deeds. But since envious fortune has willed it so, we, without naming them, shall adorn them with whatever art we can of worthy praise, as women who have well deserved to be remembered by all those who come after us.

For the **Minyans** were magnificent young men <note>The text specifies "duo" (two), though they were historically a larger group of refugees.</note> of no small nobility who, having gone in the company of **Jason** to **Colchis** <note>The destination of the Argonauts in their quest for the Golden Fleece.</note> and returned to **Greece**, departed from their own country of **Minya** and came to dwell in **Lacedaemonia** <note>Ancient Sparta.</note>, where, on account of their nobility and wealth, and the courtesy and nobility of the citizens, they were not only honorably welcomed and made citizens, but in the course of time, they were elevated to the government of the republic among the highest elders. However, dishonoring themselves after such grace and so splendid a gift, like ungrateful men, they conceived the thought and had the audacity to want to subject... <note>The text cuts off mid-word: *sottomet*, likely meaning *sottomettere* (to subjugate or overthrow).</note>

<summary>This page introduces the story of the unnamed wives of the Minyan heroes, who settled in Sparta after the voyage of the Argonauts. It begins to describe how the Minyans rose to power in Sparta before plotting to overthrow the government.</summary>
<keywords>Minyans, Jason, Argonauts, Sparta, Colchis, Lacedaemonia, Greek mythology, women in history</keywords>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative from the previous page, completing the sentence regarding the Minyans' plot to overthrow the Spartan government and transitioning into the heroic rescue staged by their wives.</meta>

<note>original: "tere"</note> ...subject the public liberty to ignominious servitude; and truly these men, being young, rich, and bold—both by their noble lineage and by having married the most prominent women of **Lacedaemonia** <note>Lacedaemonia is the ancient name for the region of Sparta.</note>, who, being noble and likely very beautiful, shone with a double splendor—and failing to recognize such grace, moved by pride, they were carried away by such presumptuous fury. Thinking themselves superior to all others, they dared to attempt to seize power, and setting aside their own kinsmen to occupy the republic, they sought to make themselves lords over those who had welcomed them as brothers. 

When this matter was discovered, they were captured and put in prison, and afterwards, as enemies of the fatherland, they were judged by public authority and condemned to **capital punishment** <term>capital supplicio: a death sentence</term>. Since they were to be given into the hands of the **executioner** <term>carnifice: the official tasked with carrying out death sentences</term> and put to death the following night, according to ancient Lacedaemonian custom, their sorrowful wives gathered together and formed an unheard-of plan, nor did they delay in putting it into effect. 

Immediately, they dressed themselves in mourning and sorrowful garments, and having veiled and covered their faces with eyes full of tears, it being already night, they went to the prison as if to see their husbands one last time before their deaths. Because they were women of the highest nobility, they easily obtained entry from the guards. Having reached the presence of their husbands, they did not waste time in tears; but immediately, having revealed their plan and advice, they swapped clothes with them. With their faces veiled, the husbands—with heads bowed, pretending to weep and be overcome with grief, keeping their eyes and faces toward the ground—left the prison thus dressed and veiled, aided by the darkness of the night...

<summary>This page describes how the Minyan settlers in Sparta attempted a coup, were condemned to death, and were ultimately saved by their wives, who swapped clothes with them in prison to facilitate their escape.</summary>

does not want what the other does; and of this love, the unity is so
sweet and pleasing that through its continuation, nothing
is bothersome, and nothing is done lukewarmly. O, even a

great enemy—much more than any other, submits itself to endure
toils and dangers; and most solicitously and vigilantly, it thinks and
advises on safety, seeks and finds remedies: and if necessity
requires it, it awakens and employs deceptions. This
most sweet love, established for lawful living,
constrained the minds of the wives of the **Minyans** <note>original: "Menii"</note> with such
fervor that those deceptions which, before the circumstance of
their husbands being imprisoned, they had not...

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the wives of the Minyans, who have successfully infiltrated the prison to swap clothes with their condemned husbands. It concludes with a moral reflection on the reversal of gender roles through courage and cowardice.</meta>

## On the Wives of those from **Minya**.

...they would not have been able or known how to imagine or see, even seeing them in danger. Pressing the forces of their ingenuity, they found and carried out the deception; they prepared the tools, gave the orders, and conducted the matter in such a way that they blinded the watchful and cruel guards. Having driven away every cloud of **feminine ignorance** <note>original: "ignorantia feminile" — a common Renaissance trope suggesting women were naturally less capable of complex reasoning, which the author here argues they overcame.</note>, they understood and knew that no honorable thing should be left undone for a friend. 

And so, awakening piety from the depths of the heart to free their husbands from **peril** <note>original: "picolo," likely a shorthand for "pericolo"</note>, they possessed such high daring and cunning that they loosened and freed those whom the authorities, for the public good, had bound in prison and condemned to death—doing so through the chaste love of marriage. And what is a greater thing to say: once they had deceived the majesty of the laws, that public decree, and the authority of the elders, and broken the intent of the deliberation of the entire city, they did not fear nor were they afraid to be locked in the prisons under the power of the deceived guards. 

Instead of their rightfully condemned husbands, they placed themselves in prison. Truly, I cannot help but marvel at such sincere faith and love; for I am certain that if they had loved their husbands submissively and timidly, or if they were bound by a weak and thin bond, they would have stayed in their homes in leisure. They would not have attempted to do so great a deed. To conclude many things in a few words: I say and dare to affirm that the said women and wives of the Minyans were **true and certain men**, and the young men of the Minyans were **women**, just as they feigned.

<summary>This page concludes the story of the Minyan wives, praising their ingenuity and courage in swapping places with their condemned husbands and arguing that their bravery effectively made them "men" in spirit while their husbands acted as "women."</summary>

# On Penthesilea, Queen of the Amazons.

<margin>
[Woodcut of Penthesilea, Queen of the Amazons, standing in a hilly landscape wearing classical robes and a crown]
</margin>

**P**enthesilea was Queen of the **Amazons** <note>The Amazons were a mythical nation of all-female warriors in Greek mythology, said to live on the edge of the known world.</note> and succeeded to the kingdom after the two Queens: **Orithyia** and **Antiope**. But from which parents she was born, I have not truly read. Regarding her, it is found by ancient writers that in describing the beauty of her illustrious face and lovely person—and all her delicacy and softness overcoming every hardship—she had the daring not only, like her predecessors, to clothe herself in armor and cover her golden hair with an iron helmet, to arm her hips with bows and arrows, and to mount a horse not like a woman but like a manly knight, but with a very great soul beyond all her predecessors, she rendered herself marvelous in arms, in military discipline <note>original: "militare disciplina"</note>, and in strength and prowess of body. To her, we know certainly that, besides the aforementioned virtues and gifts, she did not lack the subtlety of a noble intellect; since it is read of her that she was the first to discover the use of the poleaxe <term>poleaxe</term> <note>original: "lastore" — likely referring to the bipennis or double-headed battle-axe, which classical tradition often attributes to the invention of the Amazons.</note>, which was unknown until her time. This Penthesilea, as it pleases some to say, hearing the fame of the great virtue of **Hector of Troy** without ever having seen him, fell in love with...

And truly and loftily she fought, so that she made those who saw her marvel, and especially **Hector**, who with great wonder and pleasure many times watched her in the battle. 

But then one day, this wondrous maiden having entered into the fierce battle and desiring to please her beloved **Hector** not only with her beauty, but with her virtue and noble <term>knighthood</term> <note>original: "cauallaria" — referring to the chivalric conduct, skills, and status of a knightly warrior.</note> and prowess, she threw herself with great will into the midst of the ranks and the strongest armies of the Greeks. ## On Penthesilea, Queen of the Amazons.

After Hector was dead, and after she had performed the aforementioned feats of prowess and met her end at the hands of the Greeks in a most cruel and harsh battle in the midst of the enemy army, there might be some who would marvel at how women still dared to arm themselves, or truly had the heart to go against men—unless that wonder were removed or laid to rest by the true reason of how one's nature can be transformed. Through such practice, Penthesilea and other women made themselves into men far more valiant in arms, and much more worthy of being called "men" than many who were made male by their sex, but whom idleness <note>original: "locio ſita" — likely referring to *oziosità*, the vice of sloth or unproductive leisure.</note>, lewdness, and cowardice have made into women; for her deeds were performed with a helmet upon her head.

## On Polyxena, the Virgin Daughter of King Priam.

[She] was promised by Hecuba; yet, while still relying on a promise of good faith <note>original: "ſotto fede" — referring to the truce or trust under which Achilles entered the temple.</note>, [Achilles] was led in the night into the temple of Apollo and called to his death. Because of this, after the Trojan forces had completely failed, and Ilium <note>Another name for the city of Troy.</note> was cast down and burned, and the great city of Troy was entirely destroyed, the said Polyxena was undeservedly taken by Neoptolemus <note>The son of Achilles, also known as Pyrrhus.</note> to the tomb of the deceased Achilles to be sacrificed for vengeance and to please his father’s enemies <note>While the text says "enemies" (nimici), classical tradition usually states she was sacrificed to appease Achilles' ghost or "manes."</note>. 

Here one may give credit to the burial rites of the ancients: seeing the noble virgin and the cruel youth with a sword in his hand to kill her, and seeing many around her weeping for this cruel fate, the most innocent Polyxena turned toward the sword and extended her neck with such a **steadfast spirit** and such a steady face. She had first arranged her clothing around her body so that, in her death-struggle, she could not reveal any private part. This moved the onlookers to pity and tears, no less for her <term>fortitude and constancy</term> <note>original: "fortezza e conſtãtia" — these were considered the highest virtues of the Roman and Renaissance mind, representing physical strength of character and moral endurance.</note> than for the pity of her undeserved death. 

Truly, it was a great thing and worthy of memory that neither her tender age, nor her female sex, nor her royal delicacy, nor her change in fortune could dismay the noble spirit of this virgin—especially under the sword of the victorious enemy, which caused the courageous hearts of even the most excellent and strongest men to tremble. Therefore, I easily believe that this was the work of a generous nature, which wished to show through this act of contempt for death what kind of woman she had produced, if only hostile fortune had not so quickly suppressed her and deprived her of life.

## On Hecuba, Queen of the Trojans.

Many others say that she was led with the other prisoners into servitude, so that no misery would be lacking from her misfortunes. She saw, by the command of Clytemnestra, Cassandra strangled after the death of Agamemnon.

-># On Cassandra, daughter of Priam, King of the Trojans.<-

**Cassandra** was the daughter of **Priam**, King of the Trojans, and according to what the ancients say, she knew the art of **divining** <note>The art of foretelling the future or prophecy.</note>; which art—whether she acquired it through her own studies, or received it as a gift from God, or rather through the deceits of the **devil**—is not entirely certain to us. Nevertheless, this is confirmed about her by many:

namely, that the said **Cassandra**, a long time before **Helen** was abducted by **Paris**, and before the coming of **Tyndareus** <note>Tyndareus was the earthly father of Helen; his "coming" or the events of his house set the Trojan War in motion.</note> and the long siege of the Trojans, and the ultimate destruction of **Priam** and of **Ilion** <note>Ilion is another name for the city of Troy.</note>, she often cried out with a clear and loud voice. But because no faith was given to her words, they say she was punished and beaten many times by her father and her brothers; and because of this, the fable was created that she was loved by **Apollo** <note>In Greek mythology, the god Apollo gave Cassandra the gift of prophecy to win her over, but when she rejected him, he cursed her so that no one would ever believe her predictions.</note>. He did not take away the gift he had given her, but added that no faith should be given to her sayings; and so it followed that what Cassandra said was never believed.

This **Cassandra**, it is found, was given in marriage to a noble youth named **Coroebus**, who, before he could sleep with her, was killed in the battle. Then, losing her father and her brothers and all her possessions, having been taken by the **Greeks**, she fell as a portion to

### On Clytemnestra, Queen of Mycenae.

...some say that by the flatteries and suggestions of the old **Nauplius** <note>King of Euboea and father of Palamedes; he sought revenge against the Greek leaders for his son's death by inciting their wives to adultery.</note>, father of **Palamedes**, she fell into his [Aegisthus'] embraces. Following this sin—whether out of fear of the crime she had committed, or because of the return of **Agamemnon**, or through the persuasion of her lover **Aegisthus**, or truly because of the indignation she felt regarding **Cassandra** (whom she had understood **Agamemnon** was bringing with him to **Mycenae**)—the vile <note>original: "laiofa," likely a variant of "iofa," meaning base or vile.</note> and cruel **Clytemnestra**, with fraud, a wicked spirit, and an unbridled plot, rose up against her husband. 

When he returned from the great victory of **Troy**, exhausted by many misfortunes and storms, the said **Clytemnestra**, dissembling with a kind face, received him into the royal chamber. According to some, while the said **Agamemnon** was dining and warming himself with wine and food, he was killed by the hidden lover **Aegisthus**. 

Others say that while **Agamemnon** was putting on the garments acquired from the victory of **Troy**, this adulterous and shrewd woman had royal and very rich clothes made in the Greek fashion, but without a "capitale" <note>An opening for the head.</note>—or rather, clothes meant for the head—which she made him wear as if to signal a brighter and future celebration. Having put those on, and being unable to stick his head out or pull it through, and having already dressed his hands, arms, and his whole torso—thus enclosed and trapped in the said garment—he was killed by **Aegisthus** at her command. 

And so, without being able to help himself, the most noble prince was killed. After this most iniquitous deed was done, they occupied and took the whole realm. They held and ruled the country for **seven years**. During this time, **Orestes**, the son of **Agamemnon** and her, had grown up; he had been taken away and protected from his mother’s fury by friends and relatives. ## On Clytemnestra, Queen of Mycenae.

...to me it happened in such a way <note>The text likely transitions from the description of Agamemnon's murder to Orestes's retribution.</note> that he killed together both his mother and her lover; and truly I do not know which I should blame more: the sin or the audacity. The first [the murder of Agamemnon] is a great evil, because the illustrious **Agamemnon** had not deserved it; as for the second [the audacity of the Queen], the less it belonged to such a wicked woman, the more it is abominable. But I certainly have something to praise, that is, the virtue of **Orestes**, who for a long time did not allow himself to be held back by pity for his unchaste and wicked mother, but rather, as an eager <note>original: "aioso," meaning spirited or eager.</note> avenger, he went and avenged the undeserved death of his father. And he performed a virtuous act in going against his mother, who had deserved every evil; she deservedly suffered that thing and cruelty which the father had undeservedly endured at the hands of the adulterous priest <note>original: "facerdote"; this may be a metaphorical reference to Aegisthus acting as a sacrificial slaughterer, or a transcription error for "facitore" (the doer/perpetrator).</note> by the command of that incestuous and most wicked woman. It happened that she, full of sin, brought it back upon the heads of those by whose actions and command the paternal blood was spilled, and that by the shedding of their blood, the blood of the father was restored and avenged.

### On Helen, wife of Menelaus.

...which the ancient Greeks and Latins say after them was so very great and so marvelous that it is an easy thing for her to be preferred above all others. And truly, leaving aside many others, this woman greatly taxed that man of divine genius, **Homer**, before he could describe her in suitable verses. Also, many painters and many carvers of various things—all solemn and excellent—undertook the same labor to the end that they might leave to their posterity some effigy or image of such great and admirable beauty. Among these, for a great sum of money, was brought by the **Crotonians** <note>original: "Crethensi"; likely a transcription error or archaic variant for the people of Croton in Southern Italy, famous for the story of Zeuxis.</note> (called now *Crethy*) **Zeuxis of Heraclea** <note>original: "Heracleotes"; the famous Greek painter active in the late 5th century BCE.</note>, a most famous painter in his time and preferred before all others. He applied his genius with all the forces of art to envision her figure with the brush. And since he had nothing for an example but the verses of **Homer** and common fame, he thought to be able to capture her divine effigy through many other most beautiful forms; and so to those who asked for it, he showed her in a painting. Wherefore by the Crotonians he was first shown several youths <note>original: "fanciulli"; while this can mean children, in this context it refers to the beautiful young men of the city.</note> of most beautiful form; and beyond this they sought out and showed him five of the most beautiful young maidens, of higher beauty than all others. And having portrayed from all of them only one single form with all the forces of art and his celebrated genius, it is hardly believed he could succeed in reaching his desire. Nor do I marvel at this, for who could there be who with a brush might portray in a figure the joy of the eyes, the pleasures and the sweetness of the mouth, the celestial smile, the pleasant movements, and the various beauties of the face, the quality of words, and of actions, since such things are the work <note>original: "officio"; literally the office, duty, or specialty.</note> of Nature? He made, therefore...

## On Helen, wife of Menelaus.

The most subtle and great painter <note>Referring to Zeuxis of Heraclea from the previous page.</note> left what he could and what he painted to his successors as a nearly celestial figure and the singular honor of all images and statues. Wherefore keen spirits and the most subtle men invented the fable in which they write of Helen—because of the starry brilliance of her eyes and a light never before seen by mortals; for the wonderful beauty of her face; for the tresses of her glittering golden hair beating against her shoulders; for her mouth of roses and cinnamon; for her sweet and suave angelic voice; for her noble actions and manners; for her splendid and spacious brow; for her white throat, polished and delicate more than ivory or pearls never before seen; those delights, the apples of Paradise <note>A poetic metaphor for her breasts.</note> of her raised breast which were moved only by her breathing—[they wrote her] to be the daughter of Jove <note>The Roman name for Zeus.</note> transformed into a Swan, so that the beauty she possessed might be understood and comprehended by all as having received divine beauty as a gift from Jove. 

This special and admirable beauty could not be portrayed by human wit with brush and colors. Because of this beauty, a man named **Theseus of Athens** was called even to Helicon <note>The text likely refers to the area near Sparta where Helen lived, though the manuscript says "Elichona".</note>; before any other, he dared, while she was in her small and tender age, to seize the most beautiful virgin Helen. While she was amusing herself according to the customs of royal virgins in her father’s palace, she was thus captured by him and led away to his country. And although he could have but a very few kisses from her, he nevertheless left some rumor among the common people regarding her virginity; notwithstanding that she was afterwards returned to her brothers, **Castor and Pollux**, by Aethra <note>original: "Electra"; the manuscript likely confuses Electra with Aethra, who was the mother of Theseus and Helen's captor.</note>, the mother of Theseus. Or, as others say, her said brothers demanded her back through **Proteus, King of Egypt**, in the absence of Theseus...

# On Helen, wife of Menelaus.

...was sent back and restored, but being of marriageable age, the said Helen was married to King Menelaus, to whom she bore a single daughter named Hermione. And many years after this, Paris returned to Ilion <note>The ancient name for the city of Troy.</note> from Mount Ida, where he had been sent because of the omen seen by his pregnant mother. Having lived there unrecognized, he defeated his brother Hector in a wrestling match; having escaped death and being recognized by his mother by the displayed <term>crepundia</term> <note>original: "chrepundii"; these were small tokens, jewelry, or distinctive clothing used in antiquity to identify a child who had been exposed or lost at birth.</note>, that is, royal childhood garments, he remembered the promise of the most beautiful woman which Venus had promised him while he was on Mount Ida. Or, according to what others say, wishing to demand the return of Hesione <note>original: "Exiona"; the sister of King Priam who had been captured by Hercules.</note>, he had ships built at Ida, and accompanied by a royal company, he sailed to Greece. 

He was received by King Menelaus and honorably hosted. And there, seeing Helen—marvelous for her celestial beauty and for her adorned and charming manners, joyful, wandering, playful, and desiring to be gazed upon, living in royal ease and under light guard—Paris fell in love with her as they looked at one another. Seeing hope in her playful gestures, and soliciting her with his sparkling eyes, he easily kindled the fire of his love in her unchaste breast. Fortune then favored the princes, for by a singular cause and need, it was necessary for Menelaus to go to Candia <note>The medieval name for the island of Crete.</note>. 

Having thus departed, he left Paris in the royal palace together with Helen. For this reason, some like to say that, kindled by an equal flame and discovered by their actions, they were in agreement. And because of this, it appeared to Hecuba <note>The Queen of Troy and mother of Paris.</note> that Paris carried the torch of fire into his country, and that the greedy warnings were fulfilled when the said Paris came away by night with a very great part of Menelaus’s treasures...

## On Helen, wife of Menelaus.

...on the Laconian coast <note>The region of Greece surrounding Sparta.</note> with the said Helen, or truly—according to what others believe—Paris abducted her on the Greek island called Cythera. Helen had gone there to perform a sacrifice to Venus and was keeping watch in the temple; thus she was captured there, placed in the prepared ship, and finally arrived with him in Troy. There, she was received by Priam with the greatest honor, as he thought he had removed the stain from his eyes regarding the injury done to Hesione <note>original: "Exiona"; Priam's sister, who had been taken captive by the Greeks in a previous generation. Priam saw Helen as a "bargaining chip" or a balanced retribution for his sister’s loss.</note>.

Wherefore, because of the injury and shame that the Greeks felt regarding this abduction, they were all moved to action, seeing as all the Greek princes weighed and valued the insult done to them by Paris more than the unchastity of Helen. Having demanded her back many times and the Trojans not wishing to return her, they all agreed with one will and of one mind, swearing the destruction of Troy. Having gathered all their forces with more than a thousand ships, they besieged Ilion <note>The city of Troy.</note>, notwithstanding that those of Phrygia <note>The region in modern-day Turkey where Troy was located.</note> resisted them in vain.

And truly, Helen was able to see just how great the power of her form and beauty was when, from the walls of the besieged city, she saw the entire shore full of enemies. She was able to see, and did see, everything all around being desolated and destroyed with fire and iron, and the people beginning the most cruel battles, and the wounded falling dead.

The siege lasted for the space of ten years. Hector and Achilles had already died, and Paris had been killed by the hand of the most fierce youth Pyrrhus <note>Also known as Neoptolemus, the son of Achilles.</note>. Helen—perhaps to atone for having sinned in taking her first husband—entered into a second marriage with Deiphobus <note>A son of Priam and brother to Hector and Paris.</note>. While she was married to him, she was tempted toward betrayal by the Greeks, who realized they could not win through battle...

...to be able to take Troy. This Helen, just as she was the cause of the siege <note>original: "scedio", referring to the ten-year Trojan War.</note>, so she was and wished to be the cause of its destruction. Wherefore, to earn the right to return to Greece with her first husband, she brought herself to commit treason. For while the Greeks pretended they wanted to depart and withdrew some distance, the Trojans—weary from past hardships—held a marvelous celebration over the Greeks' departure. They spent almost the entire night eating, drinking, and dancing, and becoming tired and heavy from the food, they all fell asleep.

When Helen saw this, while she herself was dancing and making merry <note>original: "treschando", referring to a lively traditional dance called a tresca.</note>, she lit a great torch and showed it from the high windows to the Greeks, who were waiting in ambush. When they saw the signal, they immediately returned to Troy. Some of them entered the city secretly and, finding it buried in wine and sleep, they set fire to many parts of it and burned it all. And Deiphobus <note>The Trojan prince who married Helen after the death of Paris; his death marked the final fall of the Trojan resistance.</note> having already been cruelly and shamefully killed, the Greeks took back Helen and restored her to her Menelaus, twenty years after she had been abducted.

Some others say that Helen was abducted by Paris against her will, and for that reason deserved to be received back by the Greeks and by her husband Menelaus. While returning with Helen to Greece, he was forced by storms and the power of the winds to go to Egypt. Arriving there in the lands of King Polybus, he was welcomed and greatly honored. Then, departing from there with a favorable wind, he went to his own country where, as lord with his reclaimed wife, he was received with a very great celebration eight years after the destruction of Ilion <note>The city of Troy.</note>. But how long Helen lived, or where and when she died, I do not recall having read.

# About Circe, daughter of the Sun.

**[C]irce**, through her enchantments, as the verses of the poets testify, was a most famous woman even up to the present day. She was the daughter of the Sun and of Perse <note>A sea nymph, one of the Oceanids.</note>, daughter of Oceanus, and she was the sister of Aeëtes <note>original: "Oethe," the King of Colchis who possessed the Golden Fleece.</note>, King of Colchis. 

According to my own thoughts, she was called the "daughter of the Sun" for one of several reasons: either because she flourished with a most singular beauty, or because she was most learned in the knowledge of herbs and their virtues, or because all her deeds were performed with great prudence <note>In this context, "prudence" implies a high degree of technical skill or worldly wisdom.</note>. All of these qualities, for various reasons and dispositions, are things that the <term>Mathematicians</term> <note>In the Renaissance, this term often referred to astrologers or those who calculated the movements of the stars to determine human fates.</note> and Astrologers say the Sun gives to the existence of all things, or rather to all men who are born. 

However, by what pact or cause she left Colchis and came to Italy, I have not read. That she inhabited the mountain of the Volsci <note>The Volsci were an ancient Italic people; the mountain mentioned is the modern Monte Circeo.</note>, which even to this day is called by her name, **Circeo** <note>original: "Circelli".</note>, all histories testify. But since nothing is read of her except poetic fables, these poetic histories speak of such a celebrated woman only succinctly and very briefly. With whatever wit is granted to us, we shall enlighten the minds of the believers <note>Boccaccio often sought to provide rational, "historical" explanations for mythological stories, a method known as euhemerism.</note>. They maintain, then, before all other things, that whatever sailor of that time, by his own purpose...

### About Circe, daughter of the Sun.

->About Circe, daughter of the Sun.<-

**[Successiva]ment** <note>This completes the word "successivamente" from the previous page.</note>, or rather driven by the force of the winds, whoever approached the shores of the island, it is said that through various enchantments by Circe, or rather by beverages mixed with poisons, she converted them into wild beasts of various species. Among the others so transformed were those who went in the company of the wandering **Ulysses**. This same Ulysses, through the counsel of **Mercury** <note>The messenger god, known as Hermes in Greek, who gave Ulysses the magical herb 'moly' to resist Circe's spells.</note>, threatened the enchantress with death by his sword and made her return his companions to their former shape; and thus he made them regain their human form. Afterward, it pleased him to sleep with her for the space of one year, and they say they had a son whom he named **Telegonus** <note>In mythology, Telegonus eventually seeks out his father, Ulysses, and accidentally kills him.</note>. Then he departed from her, full of counsel. For which reason, I estimate that under the weight of this manner of speaking, great meanings are hidden.

There are some others who say this woman lived much further along from a castle in the countryside called **Gaeta** <note>original: "Chareta". Gaeta is a coastal town in Italy near Mount Circeo.</note>, and was most powerful in force and might. She used to say that chastity and modesty mattered nothing, and were but an illusion, provided that a man attained his desire; nor did she care for any other mark of infamy. And thus, this woman—with flattering and ornate speech—not only induced many of those who came to her shore to her abominable vices, but she also transported them toward plunder and robbery on the sea, making them become <term>corsairs</term> <note>Pirates or privateers who raided ships.</note>. Putting aside all honesty, she incited them with wicked deceptions to carry out merchant trades, and many, through her singular affection, she caused to rise up in pride.

And thus, by her influence, human reason was taken away. We can understand these things well enough if we look closely at the customs of men and women; for in many places, there are many women who are "Circes," and we see many more men who, through their lusts and sins, have become beasts and wild animals. But Ulysses, who was so instructed and taught by Mercury—which Mer... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence, likely continuing with Mercury's role as the personification of reason or eloquence.</note>

### About Circe, daughter of the Sun.

<note>The initial "Mer" of the first word "Mercurio" is missing in the source text, likely intended for a large decorative dropped initial that was not printed.</note> **[Mer]cury** <note>In this context, Mercury represents human reason or wisdom.</note> signifies the wise and prudent man whom the snares or arts of flatterers cannot deceive; but rather, with his wisdom and mastery, he loosens, untangles, and saves those who are bound and entangled. Wherefore much is prepared that must pertain to the history, and through it we can understand that **Ulysses** stayed for some time with **Circe**. 

It is also told that this same woman was the wife of **Picus** <note>A legendary king of Latium.</note>, son of **Saturn**, King of the Latins; and that she taught him the art of divination. Out of jealousy, because she believed he loved the nymph **Pomona**, she had him transformed into that bird of his name called the woodpecker <note>original: "Pycho". In Latin, 'picus' means woodpecker; the story explains the origin of the bird's name through metamorphosis.</note>. Because Picus had domesticated a woodpecker, by whose songs and movements he divined future things, he then divined his own life according to the actions of the woodpecker. It was according to the verse that says Picus had become a woodpecker. However, when, or by what death, or in what place the said Circe died, I have not found.

# About Camilla, Queen of the Volscians.

**Metabus**, as a consolation to himself, gave her the name of her mother <note>Camilla's mother was named Casmilla.</note>, except that he removed one letter—the "s." And truly, for this **Camilla**, from the very day she was born, fortune was cruel to Metabus. For a short time after the mother died, because of a sudden and unexpected uprising of his citizens, he was deposed and driven out of his kingdom. In his flight, he was unable to carry anything with him except his little Camilla, who was still in her swaddling clothes and whom he loved above all things.

Fleeing wretched, alone, and on foot, he reached the river **Amaseno** <note>original: "damaſceno". A river in the Lazio region of Italy, famous in the stories of Virgil's Aeneid.</note>, which he found so swollen by rain that it could not be crossed except by swimming. Thinking that he could not swim across with the young girl, he fell into a necessary and marvelous plan, inspired by God, who did not permit that the future and celebrated virgin should be overtaken by such a vile fate. Suddenly, taking a piece of cork bark <note>original: "zugaro"</note>, he wrapped her in it; thus enclosed, he tied her strongly to his spear. He then turned to heaven with tears and most piteous prayers, and immediately made a vow to the goddess **Diana** <note>Goddess of the hunt and protector of virgins.</note> that he would dedicate the girl to her service if she would preserve her and keep her safe. 

Having said this, he took up the spear and, with his strongest arm, he cast the spear together with the child. The spear stuck into the opposite bank of the river. Immediately following it by swimming across, he found her without any injury. Joyful in the midst of his great misery, he entered into the dark and deep forest, and there he raised his daughter. As she grew in robust strength and became hardened by that life, even as a small child she began to throw with a sling, and later to carry a bow and quiver. She would hunt deer and roebucks with the swiftest pace. Becoming more dextrous and faster from day to day, and utterly despising every feminine task, she prepared herself to preserve <note>The text cuts off here, but refers to her vow of perpetual virginity.</note>...

<summary>This page recounts the dramatic childhood of the warrior-maiden Camilla, whose father Metabus saved her from an uprising and a flooding river by tying her to a spear and dedicating her to the goddess Diana.</summary>

## About Camilla, Queen of the Volscians.

She preserved her renowned virginity, renouncing all company and the lustful love of youths. By completely rejecting the games, dances, and marriage proposals of many, she resolved to serve **Diana** in accordance with her father’s promise. Being greatly beloved and obeyed by everyone, she never changed—nor wished to change—her holy and firm purpose regarding the preservation of her virginity, and in this way, she wished to end her praiseworthy life. 

During this time, **Aeneas** <note>The legendary Trojan hero and ancestor of the Romans.</note> had already arrived from **Troy** and had taken **Lavinia** as his wife. Consequently, a war broke out between the said **Aeneas** and **Turnus**, King of the **Rutulians**. **Camilla**, having been called into the fray, went with a very large host of her **Volscians** <note>The Volsci were an ancient Italic tribe known for their resistance to Roman expansion.</note> to favor and aid **Turnus**. 

Having entered the battle, she performed marvelous feats of arms many times, killing and putting to flight whoever appeared before her. One day, after she had performed almost incredible deeds, she pursued one of her enemies named **Chlorebus** <note>original: "chorebo"; identified in Virgil's Aeneid as Chloreus, a former priest.</note>, who was a priest of **Cybele** <note>An ancient Mother Goddess of Anatolia, whose priests often wore ornate robes.</note>. 

Desiring to strip him of his armor, which was very rich, she was struck from the side by an arrow <note>original: "faíecta"</note> under her left breast by one of the enemies named **Arruns** <note>original: "orote". In classical accounts, Arruns is the soldier who ambushes Camilla.</note>. From this wound, she fell as if dead; she was carried away by her people and, to the great loss of the **Rutulians**, she ended her life. 

Now, I wish for the maidens of today to look upon her and see how she, as an adult and an expert, followed the path and the will of reason. See her now running through the fields of fierce battles, now through the woods and forests and the haunts of wild beasts. See her with her <note>“lacho” is likely a printer's error for “larco” (bow).</note> bow and with her quiver, suppressing and overcoming with constant labor the lustful appetites for things less than honorable. See her fleeing the delights of soft living, afternoon snacks, banquets, and mixed drinks. See her, with a most disciplined spirit, not only loathing and renouncing the embraces of young men...

Certainly, they should fulfill [their duties] by not giving ear to vain things; no less than honest women, they should also know how to restrain the mouth, not only with little speaking but with much silence. They should constrain their ears with gravity and compose their acts and customs in all their deeds with the millstone of honesty. They should strive, with great effort, to avoid idleness, [extravagant] tables, and disordered eating, and to let go of the company of young men. They should feel and know that desiring what pleases and doing what is [merely] permitted <note>The author suggests that true virtue goes beyond doing what is legally allowed, requiring self-restraint from even "licit" pleasures.</note> is not a thing well-conformed to chastity. This is so that, being more safely preserved in flourishing and laudable virginity, they may become wise and obedient to their husbands in sacred marriage.

### About Penelope, wife of Ulysses.

...opposed [her suitors], but certainly in vain, because she could never be swayed in any direction. Being a young maiden of honest beauty, she was loved by many people. She was married by her father to **Ulysses** <note>original: "Vlixe"</note>, to whom she bore a son named **Telemachus**. After this, Ulysses was called by the Greeks—not only with great entreaties but almost by force—to the expedition of the **Trojan War**. Having gone, he left his **Penelope** together with **Laertes**, his father (who was already old), and **Anticlea** <note>original: "Antidia"; she is the daughter of Autolycus and mother of Ulysses.</note>, his mother, along with their little son Telemachus.

But as the war continued, the said Penelope endured no other injury except that she remained a widow for the space of ten years beyond the time of the siege of the city of Troy <note>The author refers to the ten years Ulysses spent wandering after the ten-year war had already concluded, totaling twenty years apart.</note>. For once **Ilium** <note>Another name for the city of Troy.</note> was burned, as the noble Dukes and strong and most powerful men were returning to Greece, many perished due to storms and other dangers, and some were carried away to other regions. Thus, the majority were scattered in such a way that few returned; yet the fate of everyone was known except for that of Ulysses. Of him alone, it was not known what had happened to him nor where he had arrived with his ship. 

Because a very great time had passed without any news of him, it was estimated and believed by many that he was dead. When this was reported to his old and miserable mother, she, believing it, strangled herself <note>original: "se stragolo"; while the *Odyssey* suggests she died of a broken heart, other medieval and early modern traditions sometimes interpreted her death as a more active succumbing to grief.</note> out of grief. Therefore, if Penelope already bore bitter sorrow from not hearing any news of her Ulysses, her pain was doubled and multiplied by the death of the mother. But after she had called for him with many tears in vain, believing him almost certainly dead, she resolved—all other reasons being set aside—with a most fervent and constant spirit, to grow old and die in perpetual <note>The sentence ends mid-thought, likely concluding with "widowhood" or "chastity" on the following page.</note>

## About Penelope, wife of Ulysses.

[She] remained in widowhood and chastity together with the old father **Laertes** and with her son **Telemachus**. But truly, since she was young, of most beautiful form, of most honorable character, and of royal blood—and thus in every way to be desired and loved—the hearts of many noble and high-born men of **Achaia**, **Cephalonia**, and **Sicily** were moved to not only seek her hand, but to harass and trouble her in various ways to obtain her in marriage.

And because **Laertes** had gone to his country estate <note>original: "uilla"</note> because he could not bear the persistence of these harassers, the situation reached a point where, as hope for the return of **Ulysses** <note>original: "Vlixe"</note> faded more each day, almost the entire royal house was occupied by the aforementioned suitors <note>original: "domãdatori"; literally, "those who ask/demand."</note>. With prayers, flattery, and every means they knew or could muster, they harassed and pressured her to take one of them as a husband. 

But the virtuous and prudent lady, fearing that force might violate her firm resolve, and seeing that almost all ways to stall the suitors or deny her marriage further were failing her, was enlightened by divine light. With cunning and specific terms, she devised a plan to deceive those who were harassing her, at least for a certain time. 

And having begun to weave a certain cloth <note>original: "tela"; historically identified as the shroud for her father-in-law, Laertes.</note> according to royal custom, the virtuous lady requested of her suitors that she be permitted to wait for her husband <note>The text implies she asked for the time it would take to finish the task before choosing a new husband.</note> until she had finished weaving the cloth she had started. Once this was granted to her, the said **Penelope** would unweave at night everything that she had slowly woven during the day. By this trick <note>original: "arte"</note>, she mocked them for a certain time; and as she could no longer stall them with this artifice, the suitors continued to consume the goods and royal wealth of her husband in banquets and various expenses.

# On Dido, or rather Elisa, Queen of Carthage.

...by the industrious and most illustrious peoples <note>This refers to the Phoenicians, noted in antiquity for their maritime skill and for founding colonies across the Mediterranean.</note> who, coming finally from **Egypt** to the shores of **Syria**, built many noble cities; of these, **Agenor** <note>A legendary king of Tyre and the father of Cadmus and Europa.</note> was King among others. He was not only a most brilliant splendor and famous in his own time, but remains so even in ours. It is from this **Agenor** that the illustrious and noble lineage of **Dido** is believed to have its origin and beginning. Her father was **Belus**, King of the **Phoenicians**, who, having subjected the island of **Cyprus**, died there and left the government of the **Phoenicians**—and their loyalty—to this young girl **Dido**, together with **Pygmalion**, her brother, who was still a child although he was older than she was.

The **Phoenicians**, having established the said **Pygmalion** upon the throne of his father **Belus**, married this girl **Elisa**, who was of wonderful beauty, to **Acerbas** <note>original: "acerbo"; the historical name of Dido's husband, often identified with Sychaeus.</note>—or rather **Sychaeus** or **Sicarbas**—who, according to what some say, was one of the Priests of **Hercules** <note>The Tyrian Melqart, often identified with the Greek Hercules by ancient and medieval authors.</note> and the most honored man among the **Tyrians** after the King. Truly, this **Dido** and her husband, joined together, loved each other with a most perfect love. 

But **Pygmalion** was the most greedy and avaricious of all mortals, while **Acerbas**, the husband of **Dido**, was the wealthiest. Each of them knew and estimated the greed and the treasure of the other. **Acerbas**, fearing the King's disposition, hid and concealed the greater part of his treasure in secret places. However, he could not truly hide the fame of that wealth; because of his desire to possess said treasures, and disregarding all else, the King cautiously betrayed **Acerbas** and killed him. **Elisa** felt such grief at this that she nearly took her own life. ## On Dido, or rather Elisa, Queen of Carthage.

...that they should take just revenge upon him, or indeed by her own counsel, or as some prefer, having been called in a dream <note>In Virgil’s Aeneid, the ghost of Sychaeus appears to Dido in a dream to reveal the truth of his murder and show her where his gold is hidden.</note> and warned by her dead husband **Sychaeus**, she resolved to flee from under the lordship of her brother, fearing that she might be killed by him. Setting aside all womanly softness and weakness, she completely steeled her spirit with manliness and courage. For this reason, she acquired the name **Dido**, because "Dido" in the Phoenician language means the same as what the word "virago" <note>original: "virago"; a Latin term for a woman who possesses masculine strengths or virtues, such as bravery and leadership.</note> means in the Latin language. 

And before all other things, she drew away many princes of the city whom she knew to be enemies of **Pygmalion** and brought them to her own will. Taking all of her brother’s ships, she commanded that they be armed and supplied with everything necessary. Having done this, and pretending to go on a pilgrimage of devotion, she took by night all the treasures of her husband and her brother. 

Then, having anchored with premeditated cunning, and in the sight of the people, she had many sacks of sand loaded, pretending they were the treasures of her husband. Once they had left the port and were on the high sea, she commanded that all those sacks of sand be thrown into the depths of the sea. At this thing, all those who were not in on the plan felt the greatest wonder; and in the presence of everyone she said: 

"I have found the death which I have so greatly desired with tears. I have avenged my husband by sinking these treasures. But only one thing is heavy to me, and I have compassion for all of you who are here with me. For I do not doubt that if **Pygmalion** finds us, we will be tortured and killed with the most cruel torments. But if you wish to escape together with me, I promise you by the immortal gods never to abandon you." 

At these words, they all resolved to go...

### On Dido, or rather Elisa, Queen of Carthage.

...into perpetual exile together with her; and having turned the prows of the ships and given the sails to the wind, they arrived in **Cyprus**, where the young girls pay tribute to the goddess **Venus** and perform sacrifices to her. **Dido**, with the company of the high priest, took all her maidens to follow that dishonest custom <note>This refers to the ancient practice of ritual prostitution associated with the temple of Aphrodite/Venus in Cyprus; in the legend, Dido’s men took these women as wives to ensure the future population of their new colony.</note>. The priest prophesied to **Dido** that she was destined to achieve very great and lofty things in her flight. 

And so, having departed from **Cyprus**, sailing and leaving behind her the island of **Crete**, and leaving **Sicily** on her right hand, she arrived at the shores of **Africa**. Following the coast of the **Gaetulians** <note>The Gaetuli were an ancient Berber tribe of North Africa, inhabiting the regions south of the Mediterranean coast.</note>, she entered the port; and it appearing to her to be a very good and secure station, she ordered rest for the exhausted sailors. There, the neighboring people came around, eager to see them, and bringing food and other things, they began to make friends with one another. 

It appearing to her a suitable country and the people suitable to receive them—and ambassadors having already come and gone from **Tyre** who were content with her staying—notwithstanding that she felt the loss of her murderous brother <note>original: "fratello homicidiale"; referring to Pygmalion, who murdered her husband.</note> in battle, and not discouraged by any fear, she resolved to make her stay there. She asked for as much land to buy on the shore as could be surrounded by a single **bull’s hide**. 

O, great cunning and wit of a woman! According to the agreement, she had the hide brought, and she commanded it to be cut into the thinnest possible pieces and joined together. Arranged in this way, it occupied much more land than the sellers had thought. And with the omen of the **horse’s head** <note>According to legend, the find of a horse's head during the digging of the foundations was an auspicious sign that the city would be powerful in war.</note>, she built the warlike city, which she named **Carthage**, and the citadel **Byrsa**, named after the "hide" of the bull <note>original: "Byrſa dal chuoro dl boue"; the name Byrsa is a Greek pun on the Phoenician word for "citadel," as the Greek word "byrsa" means "hide."</note> from which the magnificent city began. To her people she gave and ordained laws and every way of living, so that the city became most noble. Her fame grew together with that of her beautiful person, and her virtue and chastity [spread] throughout...

<summary>Dido arrives in Africa via Cyprus and Crete, where she uses the famous "bull's hide" trick to acquire enough land to found the city of Carthage and its citadel, Byrsa.</summary>

## On Dido, or rather Elisa, Queen of Carthage.

Africa: and although the Africans are men very inclined to lust, it followed that the King of the Gaetulians <note>In classical mythology, this is King Iarbas, a local North African ruler who provided the land for Carthage.</note> was overcome with a fervent desire and concupiscence for her. He sent his ambassadors from among his princes to request her hand in marriage, making it known that if he were refused, he would wage war and destroy the city. 

The ambassadors, forced to deliver this message, devised a malicious way of speaking through which they might bend the Queen to their will and that of the King. They spoke to her thus: "Queen, the King of the Gaetulians says that he wishes to bring his barbarian people to order and law, following your own example, by means of your teachers. He asks that some of the most learned be sent to him so that his people may be taught the ways of human living; and he threatens death and battle against this new city in the event they are not sent."

Immediately the Queen, not suspecting their trickery, turned to them with a pleased expression and said: "Dearest and most eminent citizens, what a cause for contention and suspicion this is for us! Do you not know that we are all born first for our father and then for our fatherland <note>original: "pría al padre e poí a la pría [patria]"; Dido is arguing that civic duty to one's country is the highest calling.</note>? Certainly, I do not judge a man to be a true citizen who—when circumstances and necessity require it for the public safety—not only refuses to endure some discomfort, but is even unwilling to face death for its sake. Therefore, go forth cheerfully, and for a small danger of your own, be willing to remove and take away the dangerous fire of battle."

Because of these words and her reprimand, it appeared to the princes that they had obtained what they sought; and they immediately and clearly spoke the King’s true demands. Upon hearing this, **Dido**, realizing that by her own words she had approved the marriage, became sorrowful beyond measure within herself. Not daring to contradict the trickery of her princes after such a response, she immediately took counsel with herself regarding what was ne- <note>The text cuts off at "bí-", the start of "bisogno" (need/necessity), as she begins to plot her escape from this unwanted marriage proposal.</note>

Departing from them, she went into her palace and to the highest part, overlooking the entire city. Feigning a desire to make a sacrifice to the gods, she ordered a massive pyre built in memory of Sychaeus, her husband. And once she had completed all her ceremonies regarding her vows, she drew out a knife which she had carried in secret. Having placed the point against her very strong and most chaste breast, and having called upon Sychaeus, she spoke thus: "Excellent citizens, as you wish, so I go to a husband!" <note>original: "Optimi citadi come uoi uolete cosi a marito uado"; she uses "husband" ironically, referring to joining her dead husband Sychaeus in the afterlife rather than marrying King Iarbas.</note>

And having barely finished these few words, to the supreme sadness of all who watched, she let herself fall upon the sharp knife. Since no help could provide her any aid, and the blade having already pierced and passed through her vital organs, shedding and pouring out her most chaste and modest blood, she moved toward death. And so ended the life of the most illustrious and noble Queen Dido. 

O honest modesty and inviolate beauty! O most venerable, eternal, and singular example of uncorrupted widowhood! I wish that widowed women would turn their eyes toward you, and especially that Christian women of great merit would look upon your constancy and strength. May they consider you with all their minds as you shed your most chaste blood—I say this especially to those for whom it has been a light thing to fly and run not only to second marriages, but to third and fourth ones, through many marriage vows. Oh, I pray you, tell me: what will the Christian observers say...

# On Dido, or rather Elisa, Queen of Carthage.

...[those] marked with the character of Christ <note>Refers to baptized Christians, who bear the "character" or mark of the sacrament.</note>: seeing a foreign Gentile and pagan woman—to whom Christ was entirely unknown—follow a perishable and fleeting praise with such a persevering spirit and with such strong daring even unto death; and this not by another’s hand but by her own, before she would consent to enter into a second marriage, or rather, before she would allow the most venerable purpose of her observance <note>Her vow of perpetual widowhood to her late husband Sychaeus.</note> to be violated or broken. 

And truly, I think some woman will say—since women are most keen at making excuses for themselves—"My father and my mother were dead, and likewise all my brothers, and no person was left to me; and all around me were agents <term>procuratori</term> <note>In a legal or social sense, these are men managing her affairs or legal guardians who might pressure a widow into a politically advantageous marriage.</note> who urged me that I ought to take a companion, and that it was not good for me to be alone; I could not oppose them nor well resist them; I am, after all, made of flesh and not of iron." 

Oh, what a laughable thing! Tell me, Dido, in whose help did she have trust? She, who was a fugitive and banished from her own country, having no one but a brother, and that brother was an enemy <note>Referring to Pygmalion, who murdered her husband and forced her to flee Tyre.</note>—did not many agents go to her so that she might marry? Certainly they did. Was Dido perhaps made of iron, or of stone, or of wood, more than those women of today? Certainly not. Therefore, I recognize only the virtue and the valor of her noble soul, which thought that while living she could not escape from the illicit and dishonest demands of the requesting King <note>King Iarbas of Libya.</note>.

## On Pamphila, daughter of Plateo.

...part of her praise. Truly, I never knew of any art that was begun and discovered anew by someone—however easy it may later be to read or follow—that was not evidence of a subtle and noble genius; and therefore, the quality of the thing discovered ought to be rewarded with equal glory. Trustworthy authors maintain that Pamphila was the first person to gather **cotton** <note>The original uses both *coctone* and *bambagio*. While cotton was known in antiquity, its processing was often confused with types of silk or fine wool in medieval descriptions.</note>, which blew in the wind and came from the trees; and after gathering it, she cleansed it of every impurity with a **comb** <note>This refers to the process of carding or combing fibers to align them for spinning.</note>. Once purged and clean, she placed it upon the **distaff** <note>original: *roccha*. A tool used in spinning to hold the unspun fibers.</note>, and having drawn it into thread, she had it woven. Thus, she introduced and was the discoverer of the use of cotton, which until that time had been unknown. If we reflect well on this matter, we find that this reasoned invention easily demonstrates the virtue and value Pamphila must have possessed in her other deeds.

## On Rhealia, a Vestal Virgin. <note>Better known to history as Rhea Silvia, the mother of the twins Romulus and Remus.</note>

...and from **Aeneas** <note>The legendary Trojan hero who fled to Italy after the fall of Troy.</note>: from whom **Silvius** <note>The son of Aeneas and Lavinia, according to legend.</note>, having become King of the **Albans** <note>The inhabitants of Alba Longa, the ancient city that preceded the founding of Rome.</note>, there descended all the Kings named after him. From this lineage, **Rhealia** (or rather, **Ilia**) <note>Rhea Silvia is often called Ilia in Roman poetry, suggesting a connection to Ilium (Troy).</note> had her origin; for she was the daughter of **Numitor**, the King of the Albans. 

When Numitor had fathered this Ilia and her brother **Lausus**, he—while serving as King of the Albans—was deprived of said kingdom by his brother **Amulius**, who acted out of a desire for the realm with both cunning and force. Amulius locked Numitor in prison, though a final sense of mercy prevented him from wanting to kill him. However, he deprived his nephew Lausus, the son of Numitor, of both his life and the kingdom.

He kept his niece Rhealia (or Ilia), the sister of the aforementioned Lausus, alive; yet, while she was still a young girl, in order that she might have no hope of a husband, and consequently no heir who could occupy his usurped lordship, he placed her with the **Vestal Virgins** <note>Priestesses of the goddess Vesta, who were bound by a strict vow of chastity to maintain the sacred fire of the city.</note> and constrained her to promise and make a vow of perpetual chastity. 

Once she had grown and reached a mature age, she was troubled by sexual urges—in what manner or by what pact is not well known—but she broke her forced vow. This was made manifest by her large and pregnant womb, from which she gave birth to **Romulus and Remus**, who were later the first builders of **Rome**. 

For this sin—notwithstanding that she was a royal woman, the daughter of a King and the niece of a King—by the command of King Amulius, her **uncle** <note>original: *Cyo*, an archaic form of the Italian word *zio*.</note>, she was buried alive according to the ancient law, and her children were carried away and abandoned in the woods. But truly, even if her body was entombed and covered by the earth, the very high and **extraordinary** <note>original: *egregia*. In this historical context, it means outstanding or distinguished.</note> deeds of her sons raised her most famous renown to a sublime height, and made her name...

It concludes with her pregnancy, the birth of the twins, and her execution by being buried alive.</summary>

## On Rhealia, the Vestal Virgin.

...venerable to all those who came after her. Despite his efforts, the tyrant **Amulius** could not contradict or forbid this. But certainly, as I look upon **Ilia** dressed in sacred garments and enclosed with the other nuns in the city—and using this habit to conceal, for a brief time, the fruits of **Venus** <note>A poetic way of referring to sexual desire or the resulting pregnancy.</note>—I cannot restrain myself from speaking about the insane mind and lack of sense of some people. 

Driven by wretched greed, in order to withhold a small part of a **dowry** <note>The money or property a bride's family was expected to give to her new husband; a significant financial burden for Renaissance families.</note> from their daughters, under the guise of devotion, they force them into **monasteries** <note>In this context, the author is drawing a parallel between ancient Vestal Virgins and contemporary Christian nunneries.</note> while they are still little girls, or sometimes even when they are grown. I do not know whether to say they lock them away or lose them. 

They claim they have dedicated a virgin to God, whose pure and devout prayers will increase and multiply all their fortunes from good to better; and when she dies most holy and enters **Paradise**, she will obtain for them the "seat of the righteous." Oh, what a foolish thing to laugh at! Certainly, such men do not know how an idle woman soldiers and serves under **Venus**, and how often—comparing their monasteries and cloisters to the tiny rooms of public **prostitutes**—they feel the greatest envy toward them. 

And how, when they see married secular women with their beautiful and ornate dresses, the festivals, and the dances, and they see themselves deprived of these things forever without any hope—widowed by force from every act of **marriage** from the very beginning of their lives—how they languish! And truly they grieve and weep most bitterly over their fortune with all their heart and mind; they curse their father, mother, and relatives, their own lives, the monasteries, and whoever first established them; nor do they think of any remedy or consolation...

<summary>This page uses the story of Rhea Silvia's forced vows as a springboard to criticize the contemporary practice of forcing young women into convents to avoid paying dowries. The author vividly describes the psychological suffering and resentment of women denied secular lives and marriage.</summary>

## On Rhea Silvia, the Vestal Virgin.

...[reflection] on their sorrowful life to nothing else except how they might escape and break their **prison** <note>The author uses the word *pregiōe* (prison) to describe the monastery, highlighting the lack of freedom for these women.</note>, or bring young men into it; who might do in secret and through **incest** <term>incesto: in this context, sexual relations involving a nun or Vestal Virgin, who were considered "brides of God" or the state, making any union a religious crime</term> what they could have done openly and through **marriage** <note>original: "matrimōio"</note>, had they not been locked away by flattery or by force. 

Now these are—I do not say for all, but for the majority—the contemplations and the prayers they offer to God. These are the "sky-piercing" <note>Italian: *penetranti a' cieli*. This implies prayers so fervent or sharp they reach the heavens, used here with heavy irony.</note> pleas for their relatives, and for their fathers, mothers, and brothers: [praying] that they shall be saved and their wealth increased and multiplied—all those who have imprisoned them! 

And alas to those wretched fathers and all others who think they can demand that others endure that which they themselves flee, and which they would be unable to bear! And what follows from this? Often they weep over the filthiest lineages and shameful births, and the infamy of grandchildren brought to their homes; or indeed those women who have died a forced and shameful death to cover the shame of their kin and of the **monastery** <note>original: "monasterio"</note>, from which they flee in the most dishonorable ways. In the end, thus disgraced, the fathers are forced to nourish and raise that "corrupted" woman, whom they could have honorably married off. 

Let those foolish fathers therefore hear and know—those who refuse to measure the strength of others against their own—that ignorant virgins should not be dedicated to God. Not the little ones, nor those of immature age, nor those forced; but only those who are disposed toward **holiness** <term>sanctita: a genuine spiritual calling and state of purity</term>. And they should be women who, having been raised and educated in their father's house since their childhood, are of a mature and complete age. They should be such that they recognize and understand with a healthy and sound mind how they are committing themselves to the yoke of **perpetual virginity** <term>uirginitá: a lifelong vow of celibacy</term>, not by force, but of their own free [will]...
