## On Gaia Cyrilla, wife of Tarquinius Priscus.

**G**aia Cyrilla <note>Also known in historical records as Tanaquil, the wife of the fifth king of Rome. Boccaccio uses the name Gaia Cyrilla, which was traditionally associated with Roman wedding chants.</note>, although I have found nothing regarding her origins, I estimate was either a Roman or a Tuscan woman; and certainly we hold on the authority of the ancients that she was the most beloved spouse of Tarquinius Priscus, King of the Romans. Even though she was of the most refined intellect and the wife of a King, we find that she did not wish to remain idle; rather, it is recorded that she devoted herself to spinning wool and making cloth. This pursuit, in that era among the Latins, was considered an honorable practice; and this work made her such an excellent and diligent worker in the matter of honest **household management** <term>maſſarítía: the management of a household's resources; in the Renaissance, this was a highly valued civic and domestic virtue</term> that even into our present times the fame of her name has endured. Nor did her reputation fail in her own day, nor was she without public honor. Because she was reputed by the Romans to be a most upright and admirable woman—the Romans at that time not yet having lapsed into "Asian" luxuries and softness <note>Boccaccio reflects a common classical view that Rome's later decline was caused by the "softness" and luxury imported from the East.</note>—it was ordained by public statute that women...

Boccaccio attributes the origin of the name used in this rite to the virtuous Gaia Cyrilla.</note>; it was as if, by this name, a good omen and the triumph of great abundance should follow the household. Although this custom may seem a small thing to the lazy minds of modern people, I do not doubt that among the wise of that time, with its thoughtful simplicity, it was considered a far greater and more excellent judgment of a woman worthy of praise.

She was not content merely to know how to join one let-

but was occupied and gripped by her love as if by an intol-

man did not respond, nor incline himself to her desire,
Sappho, grieving and shrinking into herself, and seeing his obstinate hard-
ness, sang tearful and most pitiful verses, which one would
not have thought were the verses of a woman, and which they repute...

## On Lucretia, the Wife of Collatinus.

...and Collatinus together with them, perhaps heated by wine, fell into a discussion about their wives: and each one wishing, as is the custom, for his own to prevail and be set above the others. They came to this decision: that they should go immediately and see the activity, the conduct, and the manner of all [the wives]; and according to what they witnessed, they were to judge which was the most virtuous <note>original: "honesta"; meaning honorable, modest, and virtuous</note>. And having first gone to Rome and found the royal young women dancing, singing, and feasting, and having departed, they went to Collatia and found Lucretia neither singing nor dancing. 

But in the midst of her maidens, she was occupied together with them in the <term>work of wool-spinning</term> <note>original: "lanificio officio"; the traditional Roman domestic duty of preparing wool, symbolizing a virtuous and industrious wife</note>, and dressed in simple household clothing. For which reason, she was judged by all to be by far the most virtuous and most worthy of praise; and the said young men being received by Collatinus and by Lucretia kindly and most greatly honored, it followed that Sextus, the eldest son of Tarquin <note>Sextus Tarquinius, son of Tarquin the Proud, the last king of Rome</note>, directed his dishonest eyes upon the virtuous beauty of the most chaste Lucretia and, inflamed by a wicked fire, he resolved within himself to have the embraces of Lucretia by force if he could not have them by any other means. 

Not many days passed when, driven by insane and carnal desire, he secretly left the camp and went to Collatia, where, as a relative, he was graciously received and honored by Lucretia; and when night had come and it seemed right to him, and he knew that every person in the house was sleeping, he took a knife and with it went to Lucretia’s chamber; and having revealed himself to her, in a low voice he told her his desire, adding that if she screamed or did not consent, he would kill her. She, seeing Sextus, and being prepared to die rather than lose her honor or consent to the sin, he resorted to a most wick- <note>original: "pes-"; likely "pessima" or "wicked." The narrative here usually describes Sextus threatening to frame her for adultery with a slave to ensure her posthumous disgrace.</note>

### ¶ Concerning Tomyris, Queen of Scythia.

Tomyris was the most illustrious Queen of the Scythians <note>Scythia was an ancient region in Central Eurasia, covering parts of modern-day Ukraine and Russia</note>; who, because they live in harsh and sterile lands under a frozen region of the sky, and are joined with high and rugged mountains facing the North Wind <note>original: "borea"</note>, are known by few others than themselves. This is the reason why we do not know who her relatives or parents were, nor to whom she was married. 

But nevertheless, she appears to have been of noble lineage because she ruled over many peoples and most fierce men. Her reign was in the time when Cyrus <note>Cyrus the Great, founder of the Achaemenid Persian Empire</note>—more to acquire fame and glory than for any other expansion of his empire—conceived a desire to acquire the realm of Scythia. Perhaps this was permitted by God so that Tomyris might be more clearly known and the pride of Cyrus might be humbled. Truly, Cyrus had heard...

## Concerning Tomyris, Queen of Scythia.

...that the men of Scythia were harsh, poor, and wild; a people who, until his time, could not have been conquered or subjected. Driven by a desire because of this to test his strength, and having raised a very great army, Cyrus went against the widowed Queen. She, having foreknowledge of his arrival—and although the said Cyrus was feared at that time not only by all of Asia but by the greater part of the world—Tomyris was not dismayed. Nor did she even send any peace terms through a mediator. Instead, having gathered her people and made herself the Leader <note>original: "Ducha"</note> of the battle, she allowed him to pass. Although she could have opposed him with ships as he crossed into the country, she let him pass the River Araxes <note>The Araxes, now known as the Aras River, served as a boundary between the Persian Empire and the Scythian territories</note> with his entire army. This most wise woman judged that she could better disperse the rabid pride of the said Cyrus once he was inside her country than from the outside. Knowing for certain that the said Cyrus had entered quite deep into her realm, she gave a third part of her army to her only son and commanded him to go out to meet Cyrus and fight with him. But Cyrus, considering the condition of the country and being informed of the customs and manners of the people, heard that the youth was coming with the army. He decided to fight him with cunning and trickery rather than with weapons. Leaving his camp full of an abundance of food and wine—which those people were little or never accustomed to drinking—and many delicacies and furnishings, he feigned fear and haste. Thus he abandoned his camp and, positioning himself in a place suited to his plan, he awaited his time. ## Concerning Tomyris, Queen of Scythia.

...hearing this and believing the retreat to be genuine, they arrived in the said camp. Finding things laid out for their rest, they stopped there; and having filled themselves with food and wine, and then falling asleep, they were all killed or captured by Cyrus that same night. Not one, or very few, escaped; among the dead was the said son of Tomyris. After this deed, Cyrus, as if victorious over all, moved on and advanced further. 

But Tomyris, having learned the sorrowful news—especially concerning her only son—although she felt immeasurable grief, did not, according to the habit of women, waste her time in tears. Instead, she transformed her grief into a <term>loving vengeance</term> <note>original: "amoroſa uendetta"; in this context, it refers to a revenge fueled by her maternal love for her son</note>. Having learned the tactics used by Cyrus, she thought to defeat him by almost the same art. She succeeded well; she did not leave her camp full of food, but by feigning fear and appearing to flee, she led the said Cyrus—who was greedy for victory, despite being a most astute prince—little by little into the rugged and barren places of her country. 

And there, surrounded by her people, they were all destroyed and killed by hunger and the sword. Nor could Cyrus escape the wrath of Tomyris. She had him sought out among the dead, and having found him, she placed him in a very large <term>vessel</term> <note>original: "utrio", likely referring to a wineskin or leather bag, as described in classical accounts like that of Herodotus</note> of blood collected from his own people, burying him there <note>original: "fecie ſepellire"; this is a metaphorical burial by submerging his head in blood</note> while saying: "Satiate yourself, proud man, with the blood for which you thirsted." 

Having felt that she had provided a worthy burial, her anger was satisfied. I do not remember reading or hearing anything else of this Tomyris; but she appears all the more illustrious because the empire of Cyrus was so great.

# On Helen the Harlot.

<note>original: "De Helena Meretrice"; while "meretrice" translates as "harlot," the author uses it to contrast her legendary beauty and status with her scandalous reputation in classical tradition.</note>

**E**len, I believe, was a Greek woman: and although she was full of lust <note>original: "cupida"; meaning she was driven by desire or passion</note>, nonetheless, it seems right to me to describe her among the famous women, on par with chaste and honorable ladies and illustrious queens. For, as I have said above: under the name of **fame** <note>original: "clarita"; literally "clarity" or "brightness," but used by Boccaccio to mean renown or celebrity, whether for good or for ill</note> I intended to include not only chaste women, but also any other who was notable for some significant deed, whether good or evil. 

And beyond this, we are so bound to **virtue** that we must not only exalt it when we find it in a high and noble place, but also even when we find it in a low place; and even if it is covered by a cloak of dishonor, we must strive to bring it back to the light it deserves. This is because virtue in itself deserves to be praised wherever it may be, for in every place it is precious; nor can it be contaminated or made ugly any more than a ray of the brightest **Sun** could be by falling into the mud. 

Thus, if we sometimes see virtue within a breast full of detestable vice, we must detest the vice in such a way that the virtue does not lose its praise. Because virtue, when residing in such a person, is all the more praiseworthy and more worthy, and

## On Helen the Harlot.

<note>original: "De Helena Meretrice"; while the text identifies her as Helen, the historical figure Boccaccio is describing is actually Leaena, a famous Athenian courtesan associated with the "Tyrannicides" Harmodius and Aristogeiton.</note>

...is all the more wonderful: the more it seemed to be removed from its usual place. For this reason, the memory of harlots <term>harlots: women of the city who lived outside traditional social or moral bounds</term> should not always be despised or dismissed; on the contrary, much rather when through some merit of virtue they have made themselves worthy of remembrance, they ought to be exalted with public praise. Because virtue found in them generates a blush of shame <note>original: "rubore: e rossore"; a literal "redness" or blushing caused by embarrassment</note> in lewd and lazy queens. This is because their slothful negligence makes the slippery lusts of the harlots seem more excusable. And also, so that it may appear among these illustrious titles that noble spirits are joined to them; and that virtue does not disdain being in someone, provided that person desires it: I judge, therefore, that Helen must be added to such a celebrated army and multitude of women so that in that part where she acted wonderfully, she may be well praised as a person who truly deserves it.

Helen, therefore, because she gave herself to dishonest harlotry and the detestable practice and servitude of lust <note>original: "luxuria"; in this context, it refers specifically to sexual vice rather than material wealth</note>, caused her origins, birth, and homeland to remain unknown. In her time, while Avitus <note>The text likely refers to a local ruler; historically, this event took place in Athens</note> ruled over Macedonia <note>The author/translator mistakenly identifies the location as Macedonia, though the story of Harmodius and Aristogeiton is famously Athenian</note>, and the excellent Harmodius and Aristogeiton <note>original: "Armonio & Ariston"; the famous "Tyrannicides" of Athens</note> having killed the most cruel tyrant Hyperion <note>original: "Ypericon"; likely a corruption of Hipparchus</note>—either to free their homeland subjected to such dishonest tyranny, or because they were pushed and induced by some other cause—among the others captured by the successor and heir of the dead tyrant Hyperion, this Helen was taken. She was seized as a person who was an accomplice to the deed and a regular companion, being most intimate with the said two young men who had killed the tyrant. She was examined and constrained with the most cruel torments so that she would say what she knew of the matter, and that she might reveal the conspirators and accomplices in the death of the said tyrant: the lustful woman...

# On Helen the Harlot.

...revolving in her discreet mind how venerable the holy name of friendship was, she never wished to commit violence or outrage against it to free herself. But first she endured most cruel and diverse torments; she manfully <note>original: "virilmente"; Boccaccio frequently uses this term to praise women who exhibit courage or strength he considers superior to the supposed nature of their sex</note> steeled her noble soul in constancy, and though provoked many times, she never said anything. Then, as the cruel torments continued and multiplied, she feared that because of the fragility of <gloss>of</gloss> the afflicted and weakened body—weakened by the many tortures inflicted upon her—just as the strength of the body had failed her, the strength of her soul might also fail. Transporting herself into a new and greater strength, and thinking that nothing was more certain for maintaining silence than to remove the power <note>referring to the physical ability to speak</note>, she bit her tongue with her teeth with a most sharp bite, cut it off, and spat it into the presence of her inquisitors. And thus with a single act, most clear and notable, she removed from herself the fear of speaking and took from them the hope of knowing what they were asking her. Who then <gloss>adonque</gloss> will be <gloss>seranno</gloss> those <gloss>quelli</gloss> who <gloss>che</gloss> will say <gloss>diranno</gloss> that <gloss>che</gloss> Helen fell into the sin of fornication except by <gloss>per</gloss> a fault of <gloss>de</gloss> fortune? Certainly he who said that women do not know how, nor are able, to keep silent except about what they do not know, did not know Helen. And alas, how often a disordered and lewd house—through excessive abundance and riches, the excessive indulgence <term>arbitrio: the unchecked freedom or authority parents allow their children</term> of fathers and mothers toward their daughters, lack of correction, and many pamperings <note>original: "vezzi"; implies being spoiled or over-indulged with luxuries and affection</note>—has led and induced young virgins into the act of lust, whose prompt and ready liberty runs into lewdness if it is not <gloss>non</gloss> restrained and restricted with a stern bridle, or especially <gloss>maximamente</gloss> guarded and held back by mothers with diligent watchfulness and good rules.

## On Athaliah, Queen of Jerusalem.

...having become so intoxicated and inflamed against the entire Davidic line <note>The royal lineage of King David</note> that she never rested until she had all the males killed: nor did any remain in the field except Joash <note>original: "Yoas"</note>, the son of Ahaziah <note>original: "Ozzia"</note>: who, being small, was stolen away and escaped from her inhuman cruelty. For here we see a woman, in order to rise to the royal state, not sparing even her own kin. Athaliah was truly a Queen of the royal crown; but certainly, she was more notable for being covered in purple blood than for any royal mark. 

But truly, just as she was cruel beyond measure and with the blade killed the innocent bodies of the Davidic line, so too could she feel the others against her—those who were desperate and cruel. # On Athaliah, Queen of Jerusalem.

Thus also all her kinsmen and relatives—save for only one who escaped—were all struck down and killed with the sword. 

And finally, so that this wicked woman should not remain unpunished for the shed blood of the royalty, after she had reigned for seven years, she saw herself opposed by **Jehoiada** <note>original: "Iohadan"</note>, the <term>High Priest</term> <note>original: "somo Pontifice"</note>. As all the people shouted against her, she saw herself placed in the hands of ruffians; and though she cried out in vain, she was dragged as far as the gate of the city walls, and there she was cut into pieces according to her merits. This was done so that she, being summoned, might go to hell by no other path than the one she had sent the innocent ones she had murdered. 

And thus acts **Divine Justice**, which, though it may delay and prolong its arrival, never forgets; it punishes with harsher and more cruel torment those whom it sees have become hardened and refuse to change their ways. Yet, while we do not wish to weigh these matters, nor believe them, nor care to amend ourselves, we constrain ourselves with even greater crimes. Then, when we least expect it, we are swallowed up and drowned by the waves of the sea, and we wretchedly weep for the sins we committed when it no longer does us any good.

# On Athaliah, Queen of Jerusalem.

It happens by chance, or rather, it is by necessity that one fails there: either by fraud or by violence. And if by fraud, it is fitting that it be arranged by means of deceits and of griefs, with ambushes and betrayals, with perjuries and falsehoods and similar things. And if by violence, it is necessary that you be harassed by pressing <note>original: "lactante," likely meaning harassing or straining forces</note> forces, by tumults, by noises, by cruelty, and by rage. And whichever way one wishes to go, first it is necessary for you to have seized and prepared forces which are, however, the work of wicked and corrupt men, to whom, if you do not know how to be a servant, you cannot be the Lord of the Realm <note>The author suggests that a usurper is paradoxically enslaved to the criminals who help them take power</note>. 

Either one is born to the royal throne, or one is a mocked King. Truly, it is necessary that he stops up his ears to complaints, to tears, to sins, and to killings; and that he hardens his heart like a stone to requests; that cruelty be armed and pity closed off; that reason be denied and injury cultivated; and all power being taken from the sacred laws, let lust <note>original: "libidie," referring to unrestrained desire or whim</note> and outrages be obeyed. Let one proceed through malice, and through sagacity <note>used here in the sense of "cunning"</note> let purity be mocked and scoffed at; let the fullness of greed, gluttony, and luxury be praised and commended. And let no pardon be given to divine and human things, and let sacred things be mixed together with the dissolute; and through the highest crimes and sins, let one walk in blood. And let the pious be trampled by the worst and most impious men; let virgins be violated and noble minds be held in mockery and abomination; and let virtue be utterly despised and damned, and let vice be pardoned. In every place let peace be discarded <note>original: "dissentíata," likely meaning sown with dissent or cast out</note> and let discord triumph. 

O spectacular accession of Kings! Now you have ascended and gone onto the royal throne through the shedding of blood:

<meta>This page continues the moralizing reflection on the reign of Queen Athaliah, moving from the specific crimes of her ascension to a broader philosophical critique of tyranny. # On Athaliah, Queen of Jerusalem.

...and through unworthy deeds. Would to God that in whatever way the Realm was acquired, one might at least live innocently afterward. But what follows? Presently, they are constrained by suspicion; the greatest men are confined and banished; the wealthy are made poor; and old friends are abandoned for new ones. Grandsons <note>Italian: "nipoti" can mean either grandsons or nephews; in the context of Athaliah’s biblical story, she famously attempted to kill all her royal grandsons</note>, brothers, children, and relatives are held in suspicion; they are treated as enemies, imprisoned, and put to death. No love, no faith, and no justice is observed; one lives in anguish and sleeps with difficulty, nor is food tasted without fear. The faithful are driven away and dismissed; life and all other possessions are committed to the wicked. 

O seemingly beautiful and praiseworthy possession acquired with such anxiety! Certainly, it would be a holier thing to have entered the small cottage of a poor man—filled with darkness and peace, and void of such fears and cares—than these high stations. For the more fervently they are sought and acquired, the more they are possessed with greater fear. Therefore, when the faithful have been driven away from us through the procurement of evil deeds or sins, we entrust ourselves to the unfaithful <note>original: "infideli," meaning those who are disloyal or treacherous</note>. And it often happens that just as we gained our entrance through their works, through those same works we have a far more detestable exit. To our shame and death, that which was worked for and acquired over a long time, in diverse and shameful ways, and with great restlessness, is snatched away in a single hour. 

This is something that Athaliah realized too late. And **Cloelia** <note>original: "Clodia"; the author refers to the Roman legendary heroine Cloelia, famous for her escape from the Etruscans</note>, a Roman virgin who was given as a hostage for the peace made with **Porsenna**, King of Tuscany <note>original: "Tuscia," the land of the Etruscans</note>; she fled during the night with all the other maidens and crossed the Tiber <note>original: "Teuere"</note> on horseback, one by one; and here her great courage <note>original: "grande aīo," likely "grande animo"</note> is noted.

<summary>This text reflects on the misery and paranoia of tyrants who seize power through crime, concluding that the simple life of a pauper is holier than a kingdom held in fear. It transitions to the story of the Roman heroine Cloelia as an example of noble courage.</summary>

## On Clodia, the Roman Virgin.

**C**lodia was a most noble and venerable Roman woman. We find no record left to her descendants concerning which parents she drew her origin from; or perhaps it has been obscured by the antiquity of time. But that she was born of most illustrious parents can be well estimated, as witnessed by the generosity of her noble spirit, and because she was also given as a hostage <term>hostage<note>original: "staggio," a person delivered as a security for the fulfillment of a treaty or agreement</note></term> among other noble Roman men and women to Porsena, King of the Tuscans <note>original: "Tusci: cioe Toscani," referring to the Etruscans</note>, during the time of the war against Tarquin the Proud. Tarquin had been expelled from the royal lordship of Rome because of the detestable and enormous sin committed by his son Sextus against Lucretia <note>referring to the rape of Lucretia, which led to the overthrow of the Roman monarchy</note>. 

Therefore, so that the said Tarquin would not succeed in the fraud he was plotting to return to power, the Romans engaged in open battle with him. In this conflict, Porsena, King of Chiusi, arrived, led by the pleas of Tarquin to restore him to Rome by force. ### On Clodia, the Roman Virgin.

...[the bridge] behind him was cut. And then Porsena, frightened by the praise and the warning of Mucius Scaevola <note>Gaius Mucius Scaevola was a Roman youth who attempted to assassinate Porsena; when captured, he thrust his right hand into a fire to prove his resolve, leading Porsena to seek peace out of respect and fear.</note>, having entered into an agreement with the Romans, and having received from them several hostages <term>hostages<note>original: "staggi," individuals held as security for the fulfillment of a treaty</note></term> for the sake of observing the peace, it followed that with several other virgins, Clodia was sent. To her, perhaps because it seemed <unclear>pue</unclear> <note>original: "parue," meaning "seemed"</note> beneath the honor of the Republic that so many virgins should be held as hostages of a foreign King, and being thus armed with audacity in her virginal breast, she deceived the guards and mounted a horse—perhaps one never before ridden by anyone—which she found on the bank of the Tiber grazing on the grass. 

In the middle of the night, she led all the virgins to the bank of the Tiber. Putting them on the horse's back one by one, she ferried them across, and then returned to ferry the next. Having no fear of the depth or the current of the water, she crossed them all safe and sound and restored them to their parents. When this matter became manifest the following morning to Porsena, he made a complaint about it to the Romans. Immediately, the Senate, with great concern, issued a command that Clodia be returned, for the King demanded her alone, protesting <note>In this context, "protesting" means "solemnly declaring" or "promising."</note> to them that at the proper time she would be returned to her parents inviolate. 

But the King, marvelling at the virtue of the young woman and taking delight and pleasure in her audacity, not only freed her so that she might return to her parents, but he also granted that she could take with her whichever of the other hostages she wished. Having accepted this grace, she chose and led away with her the boys aged fourteen and under. This deed was commendable for the virtuous virgin and most welcome to the city of Rome, because she had liberated those of an age that was most <unclear>acce</unclear> <note>The text likely intended "accetta" (susceptible) or "accesa" (vulnerable/at risk).</note>

<summary>Clodia (historically known as Cloelia) leads an escape of female hostages across the Tiber on horseback. King Porsena, moved by her bravery rather than angered by her flight, grants her freedom and allows her to liberate the youngest male hostages.</summary>

...[kept safe] from insult. For this reason, she was honored with a well-deserved honor: a statue was made for her in which she was sculpted in the form of a woman on horseback; this statue was placed at the highest point of the Sacred Way <note>The *Via Sacra* was the main street of ancient Rome, leading from the top of the Velian Hill through the Forum to the Capitoline Hill; it was the traditional route for triumphal processions.</note>, where it remained intact for a very long time.

## On Hippo, a Greek woman: who, being captured by sea pirates, and hearing them discuss among themselves their desire to take her virginity, threw herself into the waves of the sea because she would not consent to it.

Hippo <note>original: "Yppa"</note>, as can be quite well understood from the books of the ancients, was a Greek woman. I can hardly believe that she was famous for only one excellent deed, for we often leave very high things aside for even greater ones <note>The author suggests that while history only remembers her final sacrifice, she likely lived a virtuous life leading up to that moment.</note>. Indeed, no one becomes supreme suddenly or abruptly. However, because the passage of time <term>the defect of antiquity<note>original: "diffecto de lantichita," a common Renaissance phrase referring to the loss of historical records over centuries</note></term> has hidden her lineage, her homeland, and all her other deeds from me, I intend to bring to light what is known, so that her knowledge does not perish and her merit and honor are not taken away. We have found that this Hippo was at sea...

<sig>K ii</sig>

...[at sea and] was captured by enemy sailors. Because she was perhaps beautiful, and hearing those who had captured her taking counsel among themselves to rob her of her purity, she judged that the honor of her chastity could not be defended except by death. Immediately, not wishing to await their violence, she leaped into the midst of the waves. Thus, by depriving herself of life, she showed how dear her preserved chastity was to her. 

Now, who is there who would not praise such a severe resolution? For the sake of a few years by which she might perhaps have prolonged her life—thereby losing her chastity—through an untimely death she acquired for herself a most long-lasting ornament of honor. This virtuous deed could not be hidden by the waves of the stormy sea, nor could the deserted shore take it away; it was instead preserved with her honor in the light of day as a perpetual monument of the ancients. 

For her body, after being tossed for a time by the waves of the sea, now here, now there, was pushed by them onto the Erythraean shore <note>The coast near Erythrae, an ancient Greek city in Ionia, on the western coast of modern-day Turkey.</note>. By the inhabitants of that place, she was buried according to the custom for shipwreck victims, without any ceremony, as if she were a poor person of no account. But after the truth was made known by the aforementioned sailors, a most beautiful sepulcher was constructed with the greatest veneration at the site of the burial on the shore. It was built to endure for a very long time as a testimony to her preserved honor, so that we may know that no adverse fortune or darkness can obscure or hide virtue.

### On Megullia Dotata <note>original: "Mecchulia dotata"</note>, a Roman woman: who was illustrious because she was the first to receive a dowry <term>dowry<note>original: "dota"; the property or money brought by a bride to her husband at their marriage. In early Rome, this was a significant legal and social milestone.</note></term> from her family of one hundred thousand bronze coins <note>original: "denari de Metallo"; specifically referring to *aes grave*, the heavy bronze currency used in early Rome before the introduction of silver coinage.</note>; and for this reason, she was remembered in history.

## Concerning Megullia, the dowered Roman woman.

**M**egullia: whom the ancient Romans surnamed "the Dowered" <note>Italian: *dotata*. This nickname was given to her because of the unprecedented size of her marriage portion.</note>, I judge and think was a Roman woman, and noble for that austere time. Or rather, I should say it was a time belonging to her, in which the Quirites <note term="Quirites">The formal name for Roman citizens in their domestic, civil capacity, emphasizing their role as a community of equals.</note> of great wisdom and the very lap of poverty had not yet gone into the splendors of Asia and the riches of the great Oriental kings; in those days, that very poverty produced excellent and singular men. 

This Megullia achieved this honor of the dowry—or so I judge and believe—more through the lavishness of her family than for her ancestors or any other merit of her own deeds. For when five hundred thousand copper coins <note>The original text mentions *denari de metallo*, referring to the early Roman currency of heavy bronze or copper (*aes grave*).</note> were given to her husband as a dowry in that century, it appeared a terrible and monstrous thing to the people who received it. Thus, the surname "the Dowered" was introduced and persisted for a long time. So much so, that if ever something was given to a maiden beyond the accustomed usage of other citizens, it was immediately said: "That one is a Megullia Dotata." 

O good simplicity and praiseworthy poverty! That which you made to seem and be esteemed as a monstrous thing would deservedly appear ridiculous and low in today’s lasciviousness <note>In this context, "lasciviousness" refers to luxury, excess, and a lack of moral restraint regarding wealth.</note>. For from every side, we have excessively surpassed the measure of all those things which you...

<summary>This page introduces Megullia Dotata, a Roman woman whose large dowry became a historical proverb. Boccaccio uses her story to contrast the austerity and "praiseworthy poverty" of early Rome with the perceived greed and luxury of his own era.</summary>

# Concerning Megullia, the dowered Roman woman.

**Y**ou will not find even a humble craftsman, a blacksmith, a shoemaker, a village laborer, a merchant, an apothecary, or even a wood-splitter who is willing to take a wife into his home with only a small dowry. Nor is it a wonder that every common woman of the lower classes now uses and wears the crowns of queens, as well as the belts and buckles from their garment borders, and all other things once considered venerable and rich. They do this so confidently and without any shame—indeed, most superciliously—as if such things were rightfully theirs. 

Alas, I do not know if I should say that their spirits have grown and expanded, or rather what I judge and deem to be the truest case: we concede and permit one another to do so much that, through our vices and errors, excesses and ambition have multiplied to the point that the desires of mortals are inexpressible <note>Boccaccio is lamenting the "inflation" of dowries and the breakdown of social classes, where commoners dress like royalty.</note>.

### Concerning Thamyris <note>original: "Tamaria"; likely Timarete, a Greek painter mentioned by Pliny the Elder</note>, daughter of Micon <note>original: "Anchone"</note>.

...Olympiad: <note>This completes the sentence from the previous page: "The ancients place her around the ninetieth Olympiad."</note> she was the daughter of Micon <note>original: "Macchone"</note>: but regarding which Micon of the two whom we read to have both been most solemn painters, and to have flourished and come to Athens at the same time: they do not distinguish except with these few words: that she was the daughter of Micon surnamed "the Younger." But truly, whichever of the two she was the daughter of, she pursued her father's art with such marvelous genius, and so completely despised every feminine duty <note>Reference to domestic chores like spinning and weaving.</note>, that while Archelaus was reigning over the Macedonians, she acquired the most singular glory in painting. This was to such an extent that among the citizens of Ephesus—where the place of Diana was honored and preserved with admirable honor—a panel was drawn and painted by her of the said Diana. For a very long time afterward, the said panel was guarded and preserved by them as a most celebrated and noble thing. This Thamyris, therefore, having persevered in the said art through a very long life, left behind such a great testimony of herself that even to the present day she remains memorable. And certainly, she is much more praiseworthy if we look upon and consider her brush, rather than the <term>distaff</term> <note>A tool used in spinning wool or flax.</note> and the <term>spindle</term> <note>A weighted rod used for twisting fiber into yarn.</note> of other women.

# Concerning Victorina of Rome <note>In historical sources, this figure is typically named Veturia, the mother of the Roman general Coriolanus.</note>.

<margin>
A woodcut illustration is positioned to the left of the first thirteen lines of text. The initial letter "V" of the first word "Victoria" is a large historiated woodcut initial.
</margin>

**V**ictoria, a lady of Rome of noble blood and most rich in years: through her praiseworthy works, she carried her years not only in youth, but this Victorina held them in perpetual [fame]. She had a son of extraordinary virtue named Gnaeus Marcius, a man of great physical strength, of great spirit, subtle, and quick of hand and counsel. Since it happened that he was with the Roman army to attack a stronghold of the <term>Volscians</term> <note>The Volsci were an Oscan-speaking Italic tribe, well known in the history of the early Roman Republic as frequent enemies of Rome.</note> called <term>Corioli</term> <note>original: "Choriolano"; referring here to the town of Corioli.</note>, he exerted himself with such prowess, wisdom, and daring in its capture that it appeared to everyone that through his valor the said stronghold was won for the Romans. For this reason, he was forever after surnamed Coriolanus. He was highly esteemed both by reason of his noble blood and for his great virtue and prowess, as well as for his skill in undertaking, doing, and saying every great thing. Wherefore, it happened that the city of Rome was suffering from an extreme famine <note>original: "charestia"; a period of severe food shortage.</note>, and by the order of wise and wealthy men, provision was made, and a great quantity of grain had already been brought from Sicily. ### Concerning Victorina of Rome.

...[he argued] that because of that grain, rights should not be restored to the commoners until the honor taken from the nobles shortly before at the **Sacred Mount** <note>The "Sacred Mount" (Mons Sacer) was the site where the plebeians withdrew during a mass strike in 494 BCE to gain political rights; Coriolanus was a fierce opponent of these concessions.</note> was returned to them. For this reason, the people, inflamed with fury, were about to rush upon the said Coriolanus; and they would have done so, except that the **tribune** <note>The Tribunes of the Plebs were officials elected to protect the commoners from the power of the aristocrats.</note> assigned him a day on which he would have to come to excuse himself and say why he had spoken so.

But the said Coriolanus, being indignant, did not obey the command and was banished from Rome. Wherefore, having departed, he went into the land of the **Volsci** <note>The Volsci were an Italic tribe and frequent enemies of early Rome.</note>, who a short time before had been enemies of the Romans. There—because virtue is appreciated in every land—he was most notably received by all the Volsci. And there, through his own sagacity and the industry of **Attius Tullius** <note>original: "Azzotulio"; a leader of the Volsci.</note>, he stirred up a new war with the Romans and was made their Leader and Captain. 

He led his host even to the trenches and hemmed them in at the **fourth mile-gate** <note>original: "quarta porta"; this likely refers to the Cluilian trenches located a few miles outside the city walls.</note> of Rome. He brought the Roman Republic to such an extreme end that solemn ambassadors were sent by the Senate to the banished Coriolanus to treat for peace with just and new laws; however, they were sent back to Rome with a hard and harsh response. Sent again by the Senate, they were not heard. Ultimately, the **High Priests** <note>original: "Pontifici"; the highest-ranking religious officials in Rome.</note> went, veiled and arrayed with their insignia, and having begged him, they could obtain no agreement; wherefore despair had entered the souls of the Romans. 

But in this time, as the Roman women went many times to **Victorina**, the mother of Coriolanus, and **Volumnia**, his wife, they obtained that Victorina—since she was a woman of noble blood—should go together with Volumnia to speak to her son, so that the Republic, which could not be defended with arms, and <note>The text cuts off here as it prepares to describe how the women's embassy successfully persuaded Coriolanus to turn back.</note>

## Concerning Victorina of Rome.

...[that the city] was defended by the men with prayers and tears. Moved by maternal reverence and piety, he rose from his seat and went out to meet her, intending to receive her gently as his mother. 

**Victorina** <note>original: "Victorina"; most classical accounts call her Veturia, but the author here uses a variant name.</note>, holding her daughter-in-law <note>Volumnia, Coriolanus's wife.</note> with one hand and the children of the said Coriolanus with the other, did not sooner see her son than she set aside all maternal love and was inflamed with anger and boldness. "Hold your step, and come no further, O most hostile and proudest young man. I wish to know, before I come into your embrace, whether you are to receive me as a mother or as a captured enemy—for as an enemy I believe I am treated. Woe is me, wretched one! Has the long span of time in this mortal life reserved me for this: that I should see you condemned in **exile** <note>original: "exilio"</note> and an enemy of Rome? 

Oh, tell me, my son, I pray you: do you recognize in what square or in what place you are, where you stand armed as an enemy? I believe you surely know it; but if not, I shall tell you. This is the place and the **fatherland** <note>original: "patria"</note> where you were conceived, where you were born, and where you were nurtured by me with the greatest labor. Therefore, with what spirit, with what heart, could you take up hostile and enemy arms against her? Oh, tell me, when you in..."

## Concerning Victorina of Rome

...[entered] into this fatherland: did the honor you owe to your dear father not come to your mind? Or the sweet love you owe your wife? The tenderness and mercy for your children, and the great reverence for your country? All these things could not move your hard heart to pity, nor humble your wrath, however many indignities you may have suffered. 

When you first saw the walls of Rome, did it not occur to you? Did your conscience not tell you: "Here are my homes, and those of my grieving kinsmen and my fellow citizens. Here is my wife and my children"? And here, because of my own actions, is my unfortunate, sorrowful, and most unhappy mother. The ancient Roman senators <note>original: "padri" (fathers), referring to the *patres conscripti* or the Senate.</note> came to you; the high priests <note>original: "Pontifici"</note> came to you; and they could not move your hard, cruel, and diamond-hard heart to do through their most humble prayers what you ought to have done of your own free will. 

O weary, wretched me! For now I see that my **fertility** <note>original: "facundita"; likely a translator's variant of *fecundità* (fecundity/fertility), as she is discussing the act of giving birth.</note> has worked against me and my country. Where I believed I had **given birth** <term>partorito</term> <note>The OCR "ptorito" is a common contraction for *partorito* (given birth to).</note> to a son and a citizen, I see that I have brought forth a most cruel, wicked, and wretched enemy. Truly, I believe it would have been a more sacred thing had I never conceived you. 

For then Rome might have remained secure and without siege through my sterility, and I, a wretched old woman, might have died in a free country. Truly, I can endure nothing so shameful that is not even more shameful for you; nor can I long remain in this misery. But think of these, your children, if you still proceed further: they will either be dead before their time, or they will live forever in **servitude** <note>original: "ſeruitudie" (servitù).</note>. 

To the words of Victorina followed tears; there followed embraces and weeping...

### Concerning Victorina of Rome.

...from his wife and from his children, together with sweet and pious embraces, and the humble and loud cries, prayers, and pious weeping of all the other women. Because of this, it followed that what was done was for the honor and reverence of his mother—which the majesty of the ambassadors and the reverence of the priests could not achieve. This alone was the cause of placating the mind of the most powerful **leader** <note>original: "ducha" (duke/leader), from the Latin *dux*.</note> who, having thus changed his purpose, gathered his men, and having retreated, removed the entire camp from Rome.

Regarding this matter, so that through ingratitude no outrage should be done to the glory of the woman, it was ordained by the counsel of the Senate that in that very place where Victorina had softened the anger of her son, a temple of **brick** <term>petra cocta</term> <note>literally "cooked stone."</note> and an altar to **Female Fortune** <note>Fortuna Muliebris, a Roman deity representing the fortune of women.</note> should be built for perpetual memory. This temple, being so ancient, appears even in our present days, though it is a time almost faded from memory.

It was also ordered that when women passed along the street—where until then no honor or only very small ones were shown to them—men should stand up and give way to them; which custom is used in our country and is practiced at the present time. And it was ordered that it should be lawful for them to wear rings in their ears, according to the noble women of the **East** <note>original: "orientale"</note>, and that they could wear garments of purple with gold fringes and rings. And it is said further that by the counsel of the Senate it was ordained that women could receive the inheritance of men, which previously was not permitted by law.

Now, whether the merit of this woman should be held in greater contempt by men or in greater favor by women, there are some who believe I should take a doubtful opinion; however, I do not doubt it is most certain, since through such ornaments the riches of men...

## Concerning Victorina of Rome.

...consume them; and the women go about adorned with royal ornaments, while the men become poor because of it. But when the inheritance of their ancestors falls to the women, they, having attained it, become rich. They are honored by noblemen, and for those who are not noble, many hardships follow from the men; yet for other women, many advantages follow. And truly, I would curse what the women have attained through Victorina, were it not that through her prayers and wise speech, the liberty of Rome followed.

But that which exceeds the generosity of the **Senate**—the custom having persisted for so many centuries—I cannot praise. For [those privileges] would have been a better gift, and the temple would have seemed a sufficiently great thing for **female fortune** <note>Fortuna Muliebris, the Roman goddess of the "fortune of women."</note>. But such is the way of things: a woman is a woman, and a man is a man. Regarding this matter so much against the interests of men—which the long passage of time, though it has adopted and extinguished many useful things, has not been able to shift or extinguish—the women, persevering in holding every right of theirs most tenaciously, thanked her name.

Let them cherish and honor her every time they are honored with garments of **purple** <term>purpura</term> <note>A luxurious dye and fabric that was legally restricted to certain social classes in Rome.</note>, with gold, and with precious stones, or when men rise to their feet for them, and when the **estates** <term>substantie</term> <note>original: "substantie" refers to the property or wealth left by the deceased.</note> of the dead are granted to them while they remain in leisure.

# Concerning Queen Artemisia of Caria.

**A**RTEMISIA, Queen of **Caria** <note>original: "Acharía", a region in western Asia Minor (modern-day Turkey).</note>, was a woman of high and most noble spirit, full of the most chaste and holy love; in her later life and her end, she stood as an everlasting example of complete and perfect **widowhood** <term>ueduíta</term>. Even though it is not clear to us today from which parents this Artemisia was born, or from what land, it is enough for the praise of her nobility that we know she was the wife and most beloved spouse of **Mausolus** <note>original: "malcho". Mausolus was a ruler of Caria whose tomb, the Mausoleum at Halicarnassus, became one of the Seven Wonders of the Ancient World.</note>, the most powerful King of Caria. He loved her above all other things, and was loved by her with such integrity and perfect love that after she was left a widow, she could never forget him.

Therefore, if credit can be given to the ancient and famous writers, as soon as her most beloved husband closed his final day, she received and raised up his dead body with high and exquisite honors. Once the funeral rites were completed, she could not bear that the ashes of the cremated body—collected and preserved most diligently according to ancient custom—should be placed in a vessel. In her soul, she judged every urn to be unworthy and unfit to hold the ashes of such a beloved husband, except for her own breast, in which the flames of her long-standing and most honorable love burned...

<summary>This page introduces Queen Artemisia of Caria, establishing her as a historical paragon of marital devotion and perfect widowhood following the death of her husband, King Mausolus.</summary>

She mixed them bit by bit with small drinks of other liquids and, over a period of time, she drank them all <note>Ancient historians like Aulus Gellius recorded that Artemisia was so devastated by the death of Mausolus that she mixed his ashes into her daily drink to become a living tomb for him.</note>. Having dedicated the remainder of her life to perpetual tears, consumed by love, and believing she was going toward her beloved and deceased husband, she became joyful in the face of death. 

She performed and achieved the highest deeds. Since it was the custom for royalty to erect and build excellent tombs, Artemisia, setting aside every act of greed, created a marvelous sepulcher—one superior to all others ever made. Nor was she content to call upon a single master for this task; instead, she ordered **Scopas**, **Bryaxis**, **Timotheus**, and **Leochares** <note>original: "Schopa Briaþez Timoteo: Leocarem". These were the four most famous sculptors of the 4th century BCE, each responsible for one side of the monument.</note>—whom **Greece** <note>original: "Gretia"</note> at that time considered and judged to be the most prudent and supreme masters in all the world—to come to her. 

According to their judgment, she had a magnificent **Mausoleum** <term>mauſeolo</term> designed. Once the marbles were prepared, she had it built so that through this singular and admirable work, the name of her most beloved husband would be eternal, even if it were remembered for nothing else. Regarding this work: because the artistry and the immeasurable expense exceeded and surpassed almost all the buildings in the world and their carvings, it was for a very long time named one of the **Seven Wonders of the World**. I do not think it is in vain for me to write of it and make singular mention here, so that the fame of these craftsmen may be revived, and the magnificence of this noble woman—along with the masters and craftsmen of the work—will be made even clearer. 

In **Halicarnassus** <note>original: "Icarnaſo", the capital city of the kingdom of Caria.</note>, the principal city of **Caria** <note>original: "Acharia"</note>, having arrived by command of the Queen, they formed the base in a square shape and the fa...

...those sides which faced the southern parts they extended to a length of sixty-three feet; and the others were shorter, rising to a length of 140 feet. And so that the entire building might be enclosed, they desired thirty-six marble columns. They say that **Scopas** sculpted the part facing the East; **Baasem** <note>This is likely a corruption of the name Bryaxis, one of the four famed sculptors of the Mausoleum.</note> made the part facing the North; **Leochares** took it upon himself to do the part facing the West; and the fourth side was left to **Timotheus**. 

In sculpting the statues, the histories, and all things pertaining to and required for the perfection of so noble a building, these masters, with such solemnity and diligence, sharpened the powers of their wits. Each was desirous of surpassing the other in mastery, so that it was often believed by those who looked upon it that living faces emerged and were produced from the marble. This was true not only in those times, but for very many years afterward, it seemed to whoever saw the work that the hands of the masters were still in competition, each of them seeking the glory of their own artistry. 

But **Artemisia** could not see so celebrated and wonderful a work perfected, because she was first taken away by death. Nevertheless, the work they had begun was not abandoned by the craftsmen on account of her death; rather, judging that it would be a most certain testament and glory to their own talents, they brought it from the beginning to its conclusion. Then came **Iteron** <note>Likely referring to the architect Pythius or Satyrus.</note>, the fifth master, who matched or rather equaled the height of the upper **Pyramid** by twenty-four steps <note>The roof of the Mausoleum was a pyramid with 24 steps or tiers.</note>. There was also joined to the said masters a sixth sculptor called **Pitie** <note>Pythius, the architect and sculptor.</note>, whose work was the marble **four-horse chariot** <note>The original text uses "quadrezza," referring to the quadriga or four-horse chariot that stood at the very top of the monument.</note> which was placed upon the peak and summit of the entire building. No less famous is the remarkable and wonderful tomb of the strong, chaste, and marvelous heart <note>The original Italian 'pecto' (breast/heart) refers to the legend that she consumed her husband’s ashes to become his living tomb</note> of **Artemisia**, in which she placed the ashes of her most beloved husband. We also read of her that for daring, and manly strength of heart and body, and for military discipline, she was much more clearly excellent; and many more glorious triumphs and magnificent victories adorned the majesty of her name.

Truly, it happened that although there were many times she was engaged in conflict, we read of two instances where, after the death of her husband, having set aside her tears, she took up arms: the first to defend the safety of her own homeland, the second as required to observe her allied faith <note>This refers to her loyalty to her treaties and political alliances</note>. And truly the city of the **Carians** <note>Halicarnassus</note> is eminent and overlooks the **Icarian Sea**; it is situated in a secluded place, strong by nature and by its position, having two ports. One of these is called the "lesser port," and this is entirely hidden under the city with a very narrow entrance, situated beneath the royal palace. Thus, often without being seen or disturbed—not only by foreigners from outside, but even by those of the city itself—every need of the royal house can be provided for. The other port, which is called the "greater port," lies along the walls of the city and is open to the sea. Wherefore, knowing...

## Concerning Queen Artemisia, Queen of Caria <note>original: 'Accharia'—a variant spelling of Caria, the ancient region in western Anatolia</note>.

When Artemisia [learned of] the coming of the Rhodians against her, she commanded that all her men remain armed in the lesser port; and having ordered everything necessary, both of sailors and of fighting men, as was required to carry out the plan formed in her noble mind, she commanded the citizens that when they saw a certain signal, they should call to the Rhodians from the walls to come safely to take the city, for they intended to make no defense against them. And with every gesture and manner they led them as far as the plaza; and so they were led, assuming through their pride and little sense that the speech of their enemies was true. 

On the other side, Artemisia, in a most concealed manner—the Rhodians not seeing her from the lesser port—came with her armed people into the open sea. And seeing by the given signal that the Rhodians had been called by the citizens and had entered the city, and had abandoned their ships and were running like victors toward the plaza, she struck with her fleet. 

First, she immediately captured the empty fleet, and then, shouting to those in the city, she attacked the enemies from every side. And because they were fought from both front and back and could not flee, they were all cut down and killed, so that none escaped. This being done, she immediately manned the ships of the Rhodians and decorated them with garlands <note>It was a custom to garland ships to signal a great naval victory</note>; she commanded that their prows be turned toward Rhodes. 

When they arrived nearby, those of Rhodes on the walls—believing it to be their own fleet returning victorious—opened the gates and, keeping no guard, let them enter. And thus the city was immediately occupied and taken by Artemisia and her people. After this was done, Artemisia commanded that the leaders [of Rhodes] be put to death, and so it was done; afterwards she commanded that for the triumph...

### Concerning Queen Artemisia of Caria <note>original: 'Accharia'—a variant for Caria</note>.

She was seen to behave so manfully that if **Scipio** <note>A reference to the Roman hero Scipio Africanus, used here as a standard of legendary bravery</note> had been so bold, or if the body of **Xerxes** had been so robust and strong, he would not have turned the prows of his fleet to flee as they fled before her. 

There are, nonetheless, some who say that this was not **Artemisia** but **Artimidora**, who was Queen of the Carians <note>original: 'Carnasi'</note>; asserting in their testimony that the naval battle of **Xerxes** took place before Salamis <note>original: 'Salamone'—likely a scribal error for 'Salamina' (Salamis)</note> in the 74th Olympiad. # Concerning Virginia, a Roman Virgin.

...cruel father: through whom followed the second liberation of **Rome**. This woman, then, during the second year that the ten men <term>Decemviri: a commission of ten men established to write the laws of Rome, who eventually usurped power as tyrants</term> ruled the city of **Rome**, was betrothed by her father **Virginius** to **Lucius Icilius**, a former tribune and a spirited young man; and perhaps her wedding had been delayed because of the military expedition undertaken by the **Romans** at **Algidus** <note>Mount Algidus, a site of frequent conflict between Rome and neighboring tribes</note> against the **Aequi**. For in the said war, **Virginius** was serving abroad; while things stood in this state, it happened by the misfortune of **Virginia** that **Appius Claudius**—who was one of the ten men appointed to rule the city of **Rome**, and had remained alone with **Spurius** <note>Spurius Oppius, his colleague</note> of all his companions—fell so deeply in love with the beauty of **Virginia**; and in such an immoderate way he was inflamed with desire for her that, having sought to draw her to his will by various means—using flattery and charms, promises and gifts, and all those things by which such goals are usually pursued—and all of them being in vain because of the great and firm integrity and honesty of the constant virgin, he even turned to force through threats and the authority of his office, and every other thing he knew to do. But nothing could quench the flame of his dishonest appetite, which burned more fiercely every hour; and the more he burned, the less hope he found. It followed that he was ignited with such foolish fury and rage that, his burning mind turning over such diverse thoughts, and counting on public power, he turned his mind toward deceit and fraud. He conspired with **Marcus Claudius**, a man who had been a client of his and of great audacity: that the first time he saw the said **Virginia** passing through the street next to the plaza <note>The Roman Forum, the heart of public life in Rome</note>, he should seize her and claim that she was his runaway slave; and he should lead her to his house; and if perhaps he encountered such resistance that he could not take her, he should immediately bring her to trial before him <note>Appius Claudius himself, acting as judge in his own conspiracy</note>; and so...

# Concerning **Virginia**, a **Roman** Virgin. <note>original Latin: "De Virginea uergine Romana"</note>

After many things were argued by one side and the other, they were led to the court before the judge—who was her admirer [Appius Claudius]—from whom they obtained with great difficulty a delay from that day until the next to postpone the sentence <term>Sententia: the formal judgment or decree delivered by a Roman magistrate</term>.

During this interval, word was sent to her father. It was of no use for **Appius** to have ordered that he be detained by the commander of the army; he [Virginius] came immediately to **Rome** together with his daughter, and with **Icilius** her husband, and all the rest of his household. He stood before the dishonest and brazen judge, and **Marcus Claudius** was asked how the virgin was his slave. After many things were said, and **Virginius** not being heard by the brazen judge, it was judged that **Virginia** was indeed the slave of **Marcus Claudius**, having fled from him.

Following this sentence, as **Marcus Claudius** prepared to take the virgin, the father—turning toward **Appius** and feigning a calm expression while masking his immeasurable rage and grief with words—spoke thus: "**Appius**, I pray you that I may be permitted to speak a little with the girl’s nurse, so that, having been informed of the truth of the matter, I might leave this 'slave' with less shame and sorrow." ## Concerning **Virginia**, a **Roman** Virgin. <note>original: "De Virginea uergine Romana"</note>

Then, with the bloody knife in his hand, stirring up the people and followed by many, the said **Virginius**, together with **Icilius**, caused the second uprising <term>Secunda secessio: The "Second Secession of the Plebs," a major political crisis where the common people left the city to protest the tyranny of the ruling class</term> of the people. When he arrived, he was seized, chained, and cast into prison. 

**Appius**, feeling himself guilty, and in order to avoid his deserved dishonor and satisfy the blood of the innocent young virgin, ended his life and his reckless, daring villainy within a few short days by steel and by poison. **Marcus Claudius** was not punished as he deserved because he fled immediately; he was condemned to perpetual exile, and all his property was confiscated—along with that of his master **Appius**—for the public treasury. 

Nothing is more dangerous than an unjust judge. For whenever he follows the commands of his own wicked mind, it is necessary that every order of reason be perverted, every power of the law be dissolved, and every work of virtue be obscured and overtaken. The reins are abandoned to vice, and in short, every public good is pulled into ruin. ## Concerning the **Roman** virgin **Virginia**. <note>original: "De Virginea uergine Romana"</note>

Regarding **Appius**, consider the things that follow, which show and bring the truth to light. For when his wicked lust took hold and the powerful man abandoned all restraint, he used a freedman <note>Marcus Claudius, who falsely claimed Virginia was his slave</note> as a tool of fraud; he turned a free woman into a slave, a virgin into an adulteress, and a noble girl into a vile harlot. Because of his detestable sentence, it followed that a father was armed against his own innocent daughter: piety was transformed into cruelty. And so that this incestuous and wicked man might not rejoice in his goal sought through fraud, the innocent daughter was killed. This caused an uproar in the city, dissolution and tumult in the army <note>original: "lhoſte"—referring to the Roman military camp where the rebellion began</note> and among the leaders; it was the cause of scandal and division between the Fathers <note>The Senators/Patricians</note> and the People, and nearly led to the destruction of the entire **Roman** republic. 

O governor <term>Preses: A title for a presiding official or magistrate</term> and illustrious judge and eminent giver of laws! That which you should have punished in others with harsh torment, you were not ashamed to do yourself. How many times have we been endangered by this mortal plague? How many times, without merit, are we driven back and forth into exile, oppressed and subjected? With a most crooked yoke we are seized, despoiled, and killed by this urgent and constraining wickedness. What evil is this? 

Prefects and judges have no shame; that very thing which was created to temper lust <note>The Law</note> is itself turned into license and the fear of the most high God <note>The text suggests they have lost the healthy fear of divine judgment</note>. For it is necessary that a judge should have eyes, mind, and speech that are equally honest and mild, and solemn and holy customs, with hands entirely revolted by and clean of gifts <note>bribes</note>. But these men are not only lewd in their eyes, but possessed of insane and most corrupt minds. They do not follow the judges of the laws, but rather the procurers and pimps...

<summary>This page reflects on the political and moral ruin caused by Appius Claudius's lust for Virginia, leading to her death and a revolt. It concludes with a stern critique of corrupt judges who prioritize their own desires over the law and justice.</summary>

...centiousness. Proud men are not humbled unless a little harlot commands them; they are not appeased. They not only receive gifts, but they demand them, seize them, and make a trade of them. Because of such a disordered appetite, when they cannot achieve their ends otherwise, they resort to public violence. Thus, lust on one side and money on the other make them "excellent" <note>The author uses "excellently" ironically here to mock the skill with which they twist the law.</note> interpreters and expositors of the laws. In vain does one ask for justice <note>original: "ragiõe"—reason or justice</note>, unless it is provided with those two things, or at least one of them.

## Concerning Irene, daughter of Cratinus. <note>original: "De Yrene figliola de Cratino"</note>

**Concerning Irene, daughter of Cratinus**, who was famous because she excelled and surpassed every other person in painting; and it seemed that in her paintings nothing was lacking except the spirit of life, so perfect were they.

**I**rene <note>original: "Yrenes"</note>—it is uncertain whether she was a Greek woman, or in what age she flourished; there is no certain record. Yet it is believed she was Greek, and it is certain that she was the daughter of a man named **Cratinus**, a painter. I judge her all the more praiseworthy because, through her art and her fame, she quickly surpassed her master. However, that man was named **Cratinas** and not **Cratinus**. This **Irene**...

Because such achievements are duties outside the usual customs of women, they cannot be acquired without the greatest force of genius. Since such genius is very rarely found in women, I judged her worthy of being celebrated with praise.

### Concerning Leontion, a Greek woman.
<note>original: "De Leuntia femina Greca"</note>

...[greatness] of her highest and most subtle genius, the glory of her fame would have been more lucid and more splendid. Certainly, by the testimony of ancient writers, I find that she was worth so much and was so excellent through the study of letters that—whether moved by envy or pushed by presumption—she was so bold as to write against **Theophrastus** <note>a Greek philosopher and successor to Aristotle</note>, the most celebrated philosopher. However, what she actually wrote, I have not seen. 

Truly, since after so much time her fame has come down to our own age, we can reasonably estimate that her work was no small thing, nor indeed of small ability; although [her writing against Theophrastus] is a most certain argument of an envious mind. Because she excelled through such splendid studies, I do not believe I have read that she drew her origin from the common <term>Plebeian</term> <note>the lower social class in Roman/Greek context</note> people. For very rarely does a sublime genius arise from them; if sometimes such a gift is infused into any of them from heaven, its clarity is often oppressed by the darkness of extreme fortune <note>referring to the "darkness" of poverty or low social status</note>.

But what brightness can the noble blood of an ancestor provide where there is a failing of morals? If we give credit to many [historians], this woman, once she had cast aside shame and driven from herself the honor of feminine modesty <term>pudicitia</term> <note>a Roman virtue signifying modesty or chastity</note>, was not only a prostitute <term>meretrice</term>, but a common harlot. 

Alas, what an unworthy and filthy fact: that in the cells of the public brothels, among abandoned prostitutes, was found the mistress of all things—that is, philosophy <term>phya</term> <note>an abbreviation for "philosophia"</note>. There she was, mixed and entangled, trampled and subjected to the most dishonest acts and unchaste traces, submerged and obscured in a foul sewer—if indeed the splendor of philosophy can be obscured and darkened by an unchaste heart. And truly, it is to be lamented that a wicked genius, given as a gift...

### Concerning Olympias, Queen of Macedonia.

**Olympias**: and besides this, her brother was **Alexander**, King of **Epirus**. Following the death of her husband **Philip**, she had that son, that **Alexander** of whom one reads: of whom there were such lofty and magnificent deeds that no man born before him is remembered to have surpassed him in empty glory. This fact added no small brilliance to **Olympias**; for truly, to a mother, the glory of such a vigorous, magnificent, and illustrious son is great. However, the light of such a sun could not penetrate her soul so deeply that it was not at times darkened by exceptional infamies—notwithstanding that she was made even better known because of them.

For one reads of her that, in the flower of her age, she was accused and found guilty of unchastity and the sin of adultery. I judge that almost nothing of greater infamy or shame could happen to a Queen than this most dishonest charge: that **Alexander** was conceived through adultery. This suspicion so moved **Philip** that he not only said at times that **Alexander** had not been fathered by him, but he also stained the honor of **Olympias** with a mark of <term>repudiation</term> <note>a formal legal rejection or divorce</note>—that is, the renunciation of their marriage—and took as his wife **Cleopatra**, the daughter of Alexander the Epirot <note>original: "figliola de Alexandro Epirota"; the author here appears to conflate the lineage of Philip's last wife, Cleopatra Eurydice, who was actually the niece of the general Attalus</note>.

## Concerning Olympias, Queen of Macedonia

...hanging on the cross for the murder committed by him against Philip, through the orchestration of Olympias. On the following morning of the day he was placed on the cross, he was found covered and crowned with gold. And a few days later, his body was burned according to the Macedonian custom, honored atop the remains of Philip with the most solemn funeral rites <note>Boccaccio is describing Olympias's public honoring of Pausanias, Philip II’s assassin, which many contemporaries saw as proof of her complicity in the king's death.</note>. After she had brought Cleopatra <note>Cleopatra Eurydice, Philip’s final wife</note> before her and killed Cleopatra's infant daughter by dashing her against a rock, she embittered the woman with such words of infamy that she forced the wretched mother to strangle herself. 

In time, after Alexander had grown and achieved many high victories, and after he had taken poison in Babylon <note>At the time this was written, it was a common belief that Alexander the Great had been poisoned rather than dying of illness</note>; and after his brother-in-law Alexander <note>Alexander of Epirus, Olympias's brother</note> died among the Lucanians; and after Arrhidaeus, King of Macedonia <note>Philip III Arrhidaeus, Alexander the Great's half-brother</note>, and his wife were killed—those who had prohibited Olympias from power—the favor of the ancient Macedonians returned to her. Coming from Epirus, they entered Macedonia, and Olympias, then a widow and sole Queen, obtained the kingdom. 

But because she became too intoxicated with the blood of both the nobles and the commoners, it followed that, having earned the wrath of all, she was besieged in the city of Pydna <note>original: "Epvdua," a transcription error for the ancient city of Pydna</note> by Cassander. She was eventually captured through hunger along with the citizens, and she surrendered herself to Cassander’s trust upon agreed terms. Cassander, however, falsely stirring up the relatives of those she had killed, ordered that her person be demanded for vengeance. 

And having thus arranged matters, he sent executioners to the place where she was being held. No sooner did she see them—clearly judging that they had come to kill her—than, leaning on two handmaidens and showing no fear, she rose to her feet and composed her garments...

# On Claudia the Vestal Virgin.

And proceeding further with bold acts and audacity—moving beyond reason and the expected dignity of a tribune <term>tribune: a Roman official elected to protect the common people, who possessed the power to veto state actions</term>—he foolishly and violently laid hands upon the man celebrating his triumph. With all his strength and power, the tribune sought to pull him and force him down to the ground from the triumphal chariot.

When the virgin Claudia, who had come to witness her father's triumphal festival, saw this, she was suddenly moved by an urgent and faithful piety <note>original: "pieta," referring to the Roman virtue of *pietas*—the devotion and duty owed to family, country, and the gods</note> for her father. She mastered <note>original: "domesticho," here meaning she overcame or suppressed the constraints of her position</note> not only her religious habit but even the limitations of her female sex. She ran toward the chariot; and with both her words and her hands, she cleared a path through the crowd, interposing herself between the arrogance of the tribune and the glory of her father. She acted in such a way that the tribune was forced to descend from the chariot in disgrace, while her father, freed and continuing his triumph, was able to ascend to the Capitol <note>original: "Cāpīdoglio," the Capitoline Hill, the religious center of Rome where triumphs concluded at the Temple of Jupiter</note>.

O, sweetest love! O, unbroken devotion! Who would believe that such strength was lent to a tender and virginal body? Who could cause her to forget her religious habit—and almost her very self—other than the sight of him being harmed? She remembered that he had not only fathered her, but from her infancy had nourished and raised her with sweet affection, teaching her and leading her to an age of chastity. 

But since it is not enough to have said only this: who would be the one to rebuke this virgin nun <note>original: "mōacha" (monacha); the author uses this Christian term for a nun to help a Renaissance reader understand the sacred, cloistered status of a Roman Vestal Virgin</note> for having, in such a case, forgotten her religious order and all feminine gravity? Who would be so bold as to enter into the tumults and noise of men? Who would say that she was presumptuous for daring to interpose herself against the power of a tribune? For from such a beautiful and memorable act of piety came the defense of her father’s honor. This tender virgin acted in such a way that even a most robust and strong young man could not have...

### On Virginia, the Roman wife of Lucius Volumnius.

### On Virginia, the Roman wife of Lucius Volumnius: who was famous because, while she was in the temple to sacrifice with the other women <note>The OCR reads "uergīē" (virgins), but the historical context refers to the "matronae" or married noblewomen</note>, she was cast out by the other women because she was married to a man of lower rank <note>original: "pmıſta," implying she had "mingled" her noble blood by marrying a plebeian</note>. **V**irginia was truly most illustrious among the Roman women. Although she shares her name with the other Virginia mentioned above, the daughter of Aulus, this one was born of a Patrician <term>Patrician: a member of the ancient, land-holding noble families of Rome</term>. This Virginia, besides the splendors of her noble life, was in her time worthy, by merit of her chastity <note>original: "castita," referring to *pudicitia*, the Roman virtue of feminine modesty and marital fidelity</note>, to be placed before all other Roman women.
