<meta>This page continues the biography of Virginia, transitioning from her noble background to the specific conflict regarding her marriage to a plebeian and her exclusion from patrician religious rites.</meta>

## On Virginia, the Roman wife of Lucius Volumnius, regarding patrician chastity:

In that chapel, when **Quintus Fabius**, **Quintus Publilius**, and **Decius** were consuls <note>These figures were Roman consuls around 297–296 BCE</note>, by the ordinance and command of the Senate, it was ordered that supplications <note>supplications: formal prayers or offerings to the gods to seek favor or avert disaster</note> be made to address omens, just as was done in other temples. And this was to be performed only by patrician women, most chastely, according to ancient custom.

Wherefore, this **Virginia** being present together with the other patrician women, she came forward to perform the sacred sacrifice. However, because she had been married to **Lucius Volumnius**—a man of the people <term>man of the people: a plebeian, a member of the common citizen class as opposed to the noble patrician class</term>—the previous year, she was pushed aside and removed from performing the sacrifice by the command of the other women, even though her husband had already served as a consul.

From this followed an argument and a feminine clamor in the sacred temple. But Virginia, feeling she had received an insult and being rightfully indignant, transformed that indignation with a greater spirit and a grand exaltation of herself. First, she demonstrated that she was a chaste patrician, having been married as a virgin to only one man <note>original: "maritata a uno solo," referring to the *univira*, the Roman ideal of a woman who remained faithful to her first and only husband</note>. Then, she spoke of the virtues of her husband most highly, honoring him with magnificent and marvelous praise.

Leaving the patrician women, she returned to her own quarters <note>original: "cella," meaning her private room or part of the house</note> completely indignant. Grieving over the injury, she added an outstanding deed to her words. For having several houses on the street where she lived with her husband, she set aside enough of them to create a beautiful chapel. Having established the altar and every sacred space there, she sent for the chaste and plebeian women of Rome. She recounted to them the great pride and arrogance of the patrician women and the insult she had received. Then she spoke her mind, praying to them and encouraging them: just as the men of Rome continually fight for the cause of virtue and their honor, and every man strives with all his strength to excel, so too should they, each and every one, desire to compete and strive to excel in ho- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely intending "honor" or "honesty"</note>

->M ii<-

# On Virginia, the Roman wife of Lucius Volumnius

...[hon]or of the chastity of plebeian women by laboring in such a way that this sacred chapel, consecrated by me and dedicated in your presence, shall not be surpassed in any holy thing—whether in its solemnity or in the honor of its sanctity—by the chapel of the patrician women. Rather, if it can be done, let this one surpass that one. And let it appear that this altar is cultivated and honored by more honest and more chaste women, so that through your holy and good works, it may appear manifest that celestial souls are infused not only into patrician women.

O worthy and most holy words of a woman! O praiseworthy indignation! O invention and labor found to raise oneself to the stars with glory! These women were not encouraged or induced by **Virginia** to take the wealth of their husbands or to use ornaments of lewdness, but rather to flee from such things and from every dishonest act, and every shadow of concupiscence <term>concupiscence: an intense desire or lust, particularly for worldly or sensual pleasures</term>, with both their eyes and their hearts; so that by acting so holily, they might merit the highest glory of chastity <note>original: "pudicitia," the Roman virtue of feminine modesty and sexual virtue</note>. 

And with an excellent ordinance established and holy forces, the altar <note>original: "lara" (l'ara)</note> was founded in such a way that for a very long time afterward, no woman was granted the right to sacrifice in that temple unless she were of renown and special chastity, and a woman who had been married to only one husband <note>The Romans highly valued the *univira*, a woman who married only once; remarrying was often seen as a sign of lack of self-control</note>. Thus, the lustful hope of those who looked on with eyes less than honest being entirely broken, this altar was made equal in sanctity to the patrician altar. I do not doubt that for many women, through the desire for glory and for fear of the infamy of being separated or barred from the sacrifice, this served as a motivation and cause for observing chastity.

## On Flora the Harlot, Goddess of Flowers and wife of Zephyrus.

**F**lora <note>The text uses "Floria," a variant of Flora, the Roman goddess of flowers and spring</note> was a Roman woman, as ancient authors testify: Although her shameful reputation <term>ignomiabile fama: a reputation marked by public disgrace or "ignominy"</term> took away from her honor’s adornment, favoring fortune increased and added to it. According to the assertions of everyone, she was a very wealthy woman—but of the worldly sort: for some say that Flora consumed all her flourishing youth and bodily beauty among lustful fornicators and wicked young men in public harlotry <note>original: "meretricolo," referring to the profession of a "meretrix" or prostitute</note>. And she shrewdly <note>original: "sagacemente," implying a sharp or clever cunning</note> took now from one and now from another of her fornicators; who, seized by the pleasure of her most beautiful body and her clever actions and manners, let themselves run wild in spending and giving to her; and from this she made such great and marvelous wealth. 

Others judge her more honestly, or at least they tell and recount a strange and ridiculous story: asserting that in Rome, while the temple of Hercules was idle <note>meaning no religious ceremonies were currently taking place</note>, she began a game of dice <note>original: "tessere," referring to the ancient gambling game played with *tesserae*</note> using both hands: assigning the right hand to Hercules and the left to herself. They say that she made a pact: that if Hercules were defeated by her, he should prepare a dinner from the income and revenue of the temple and feed her as a friend <note>original: "amicha," which in this context often implied a mistress or lover</note>; but if Hercules were the victor, she...

## On Flora the harlot, goddess of flowers, and wife of Zephyrus.

[It was agreed that the loser] should provide the dinner from their own money. And Hercules, being used to winning and overcoming fierce monsters, was victorious; they affirm that Flora prepared a noble dinner for Hercules. And while Flora was sleeping in the temple, they say that while she slept, she was with Hercules and had <term>sexual intercourse</term> <note>original: "concuberse," from "concubitus," meaning to lie together</note> with him. Through Hercules, it was told to her that she would receive her payment for this union from the first man she encountered upon leaving the temple. 

She first met Favitius <note>Historically identified as Tarutius, a wealthy Roman of the Etruscan era</note>, a very rich young man, and she was immediately loved and taken home by him. Having stayed with him for a long time, when the said Favitius died, she remained his heir, and thus she was richly endowed. 

There are some who say that this woman was not Flora, but Acca Larentia <note>A figure in Roman mythology said to have been the foster-mother of the twins Romulus and Remus</note>, who nursed Romulus and Remus; but truly I do not care about this discrepancy, since it is certain that Flora was both wealthy and a harlot. To move toward the point I intend: coming to the end of her mortal life and having no children—as I believe—and having a desire for honor and perpetual fame, with feminine cunning she left the people of Rome as the heir to all her riches. She reserved a certain portion, which she ordered should be given every year for public games to be held on the anniversary of her birth. 

In this, her opinion did not deceive her, for having obtained the favor of the Roman people through this inheritance, she easily achieved her goal: that annual games should be held in her memory and honor. In the sight of the people, among other dishonest and filthy things, naked harlots would perform <note>original: "loffitio de murari," likely a transcription error for "mimari," referring to mimes or theatrical performances</note> with the greatest pleasure of the onlookers. Because of Flora, the founder of these games, they were called the <term>Floralia</term> <note>A festival in ancient Rome held in honor of Flora, known for its licentious and theatrical atmosphere</note>. 

Truly, after a long time, the Senate became aware of the origin of these games; and being ashamed that Rome, now the mistress of the world...

<summary>This page recounts the legendary origins of the Roman goddess Flora, describing her as a wealthy prostitute who tricked the public into honoring her memory by bequeathing her fortune to Rome to fund annual games (the Floralia).</summary>

While Flora was actually a wealthy prostitute who bequeathed her fortune to the city for the purpose of holding annual games, later authorities created a mythological backstory to hide what they considered a shameful history.</meta>

## On Flora the harlot, goddess of flowers, and wife of Zephyrus.

[...that the city] was stained by such a foolish and vicious reputation, and that in praise of a harlot all the people should gather. Knowing that the said feast could not easily be abolished, and to avoid the infamy of such a shameful thing, they added to it a detestable and ridiculous error. Truly, they invented a story of a noble benefactor out of the splendor of Flora and recounted it to the ignorant people, affirming that Flora in times past had been a Nymph of wonderful beauty called a native <note>original: "indigena," referring to a local or indigenous deity</note>, and now called Chloris <note>original: "Chora," likely a variant of the Greek name Chloris, the goddess of flowers</note>. 

They claimed she was most ardently loved by Zephyrus the wind—who in Latin is called Favonius <note>original: "Fauno"; the author likely conflates Faunus with Favonius, the Latin name for the West Wind</note>—and that at last he took her as his wife. They said the said Zephyrus was numbered among the gods, and to her divinity they had given, almost as a dowry, the office of adorning the meadows and the trees in the time of early spring. They held that she was their lady and goddess, and hence from Chloris she was also called Flora. Furthermore, because fruits follow the flowers, so that her divinity might be appeased by the games, she granted through her great liberty to convert the said flowers into fruits. For this reason, the sacred altar was brought to the said goddess of flowers at the games of the ancients. 

Through this fallacy, the people were led astray. Flora—who while living had always spent her time with <term>fornicators</term> and, as a public harlot, had subjected herself to them for any price—was spoken of as if the said Zephyrus had raised her up and carried her to heaven to be with Juno <note>The queen of the Roman gods</note>. And so Flora, with her shrewd wit and by the gift of favoring Fortune, used the ill-gotten money of a harlot to become a sacred nymph and goddess. She gained a marriage to Zephyrus while residing in the temples, and acquired from mortal men the honor of divinity and the name; and she was so celebrated with divine honors, that not only of...

<summary>This page describes the mythological "rebranding" of Flora from a wealthy Roman prostitute into a divine nymph and wife of the wind god Zephyrus to hide the festival's scandalous origins. It explains how her role as a goddess of spring and flowers was an invention designed to make the public Floralia games more respectable.</summary>

# On the Roman young woman

...of Flora: but through manifest and public harlotry <note>original: "meretricolo"</note>, she was made famous in her time in all places.

**Concerning the Roman young woman: who was famous because, when her mother was condemned to death in prison and it was ordered that she be given nothing to eat, the said young woman, by favor, went to see her every day and nourished her mother with her own milk; and for this reason, she was freed from death, as appears in the text.**

**T**his Roman woman was a young girl who—if I am not mistaken in my opinion—did not derive her origin from the plebeian dregs. Though her name has been lost through the malignant fortune of her parents and her husband (no record of it having survived to take away any part of her deserved honor), nevertheless, so that it does not appear that she has been overlooked by me for not having named her among the famous women, I intend to place her among the others by recounting the celebrated piety <note>The word "pieta" here refers to "filial piety," the Roman virtue of duty and devotion to one's parents and country.</note> of the girl. 

She was, then, a maiden of honest birth on her mother's side, but unhappy; for at Rome, in the praetorian tribunal <term>praetorian tribunal: the court where a Roman magistrate (praetor) administered justice</term>, I know not for what reason, her mother was condemned to capital punishment <term>capitale supplitiō: the death penalty</term>. She was handed over to the praetor <note>A high-ranking Roman official.</note> so that he might carry out the sentence, and then, for the same reason, she was delivered to the guardian of the prison, and to him it was ordered...

The author argues that despite her lost name and humble circumstances, her act of filial piety (pietas) merits her inclusion among the most famous women of history.</summary>

# On the Roman Young Woman

...ordered: so that for less shame of the woman and her parents—because they were noble—she should be put to death in the night. The jailer <note>original: "p̄gioníero", here referring to the keeper of the prison</note>, being inclined toward mercy <note>original: "píeta"; the Roman virtue of duty and compassion</note>, did not wish to kill her immediately with his own hands, but thought to lock her up and leave her to die by herself. 

Being in this state, this woman’s daughter came to see her. She was first thoroughly searched to ensure she brought nothing to eat, and so she entered; it was granted to her that she might speak with her mother. And while there, not being seen by anyone, she suckled <note>original: "alactaua"</note> her—she who had once been suckled by her—and así comforted her famished <note>original: "famellicha"</note> mother. 

As this continued for many days, the guard marveled that a woman could last so long in life without food. He secretly set himself to watch what the girl did with her mother, and he saw clearly that the mother was suckling. Seeing this thing, the discovery was recounted to the jailer, and the jailer to the praetor <term>praetor: a Roman magistrate</term>, and the praetor to the public council. Because of this, it followed by common consent that out of compassion for the daughter's piety, the mother was freed from her deserved death. 

Now, if the ancients gave to those who defended a fellow citizen with their prowess a crown of oak leaves <term>civic crown: the "corona civica", a wreath of oak leaves awarded to a Roman who saved the life of a fellow citizen</term>, with what crown did they crown her who preserved and nourished her mother? Certainly, for such a pious act you will not find a worthy crown among all the foliage. For this piety is not only holy <note>original: "ſcã", abbreviation for "sancta/santa"</note> but marvelous, because it is not only to be matched [by other deeds], but rather it is something set forth and dictated by nature. 

By this gift [of nature], pious children are taught to repay the care they received when they reach a more mature age, and with this to rescue and free their parents from death. Therefore, wonderful are the forces of piety: for they move not only feminine minds, which are easily moved to tears and passion, but often pass through and penetrate the fierce <note>The text breaks off at "fer", likely for "ferrei" (iron-like) or "feroci" (fierce) hearts.</note>...

<summary>A daughter saves her condemned mother from starvation by secretly breastfeeding her in prison. Upon discovery, the Roman authorities are so moved by this act of filial piety that they pardon the mother.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the story of the Roman daughter who saved her mother from starvation through breastfeeding, transitioning into the biography of Marcia, a famous Roman painter and sculptor. It continues the philosophical reflection on filial piety from the previous page.</meta>

...honored? Even the most hardened and solidified breasts—where diamond-like obstinacy has taken its seat beside the **precordials** <note>original: "p̃corali", referring to the area of the chest over the heart, then considered the seat of the soul and emotions</note>—are moved. That is to say, the heart first softens every hardness with flexible humanity. Like a seeker and inquirer of opportunities, she acts and works in such a way that her tears mingle with those of the unfortunate. Thus, failings and extreme hardships are endured—at least through the strength of desire—and even when remedies are lacking, people submit themselves to the chance of death. Such actions create such great affection that we should marvel less when our children do some pious thing for their fathers and mothers; for we are greatly bound to "repay" <note>original: "uendere", literally to sell, but here used in the sense of returning a debt or transaction of care</note> that which we once received, and to preserve it with due restitution.

**M**arcia, daughter of Varro, was a perpetual virgin recently discovered in Rome; but from which branch of the Varros, or in what age she lived, I have not yet read. This woman, having preserved her virginity, I think should be considered all the more magnificent and worthy of even more exceptional praise, seeing as she was a woman...

### On Marcia, daughter of Varro, perpetual virgin

...one who, by her own spontaneous will and not constrained by any superior, served and preserved [her virginity] more entirely. For she was not obligated by the **Vestal priesthood** <note>original: "sacerdotio uestale"; the Roman priestesses of the goddess Vesta who were legally bound to thirty years of celibacy</note> nor by the vow of **Diana** <note>The goddess of the hunt and a symbol of perpetual virginity</note>; for I find many times that others are constrained or held back by such things. But with the sole integrity of her mind, she completely overcame the carnal impulse—to which even very often the most constant and excellent men have submitted themselves. Pure and immaculate from the contagion of any masculinity, she served and preserved her virginal body until death.

And truly, if for this so commendable constancy she ought to be praised, she should be no less commended for the power of her subtle and wonderful **talent** <note>original: "ingiegno", meaning innate genius or creative wit</note> and for the craftsmanship of her own hands. Whether she learned under a teacher or had this gift from nature showing the way is uncertain to us; but that which appears most certain is that, despising womanly occupations <note>original: "muliebri maestri"; likely referring to domestic labors like spinning or weaving, which were considered the standard "masters" or teachers of a woman’s time</note>—and so that **idleness** <note>original: "ocio", referring to leisure or sloth, which was traditionally thought to lead to vice</note> should not constrain her—she gave herself entirely to the study of painting and sculpture.

She painted and sculpted so skillfully in ivory and other materials that she surpassed and went beyond **Sopolis** and **Dionysius** <note>Greek painters active in Rome during the 1st century BCE</note>, who were most famous in that craft in her time. Of this fact there was very well-known proof in the paintings held and kept by her, which were valued more dearly than any others. And what they affirm even more wonderfully is that she not only painted most excellently, but truly had hands so ready and fast that no one ever possessed such speed. For a long time, notable examples of her work were seen, among which one was her own **image** <note>original: "effigie"</note> and figure, which she herself portrayed. She did this so well that, by looking at herself in the mirror, she captured the colors so perfectly—the face, the mouth, and the eyes and every other part of it, as well as the clothing, the folds, and every other thing—that no one who saw it could doubt that it was the woman herself.

<summary>This page describes the Roman artist Marcia’s commitment to lifelong virginity and her exceptional skill as a painter and sculptor, specifically her ability to surpass famous male contemporaries and create a perfect self-portrait using a mirror.</summary>
<keywords>Marcia Varronis, virginity, painting, sculpture, Sopolis, Dionysius, self-portrait, artistic talent, women artists in antiquity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biography of the artist Marcia, daughter of Varro, focusing on the intersection of her professional skill and her moral reputation. It then begins a new chapter on Sulpitia, another Roman paragon of virtue.</meta>

# On Marcia, daughter of Varro, perpetual virgin

...and beyond this: so that we may come to her singular and noble habits—painting with the brush or carving with the **chisel** <note>original: "scarpello"; Marcia was known both as a painter and a sculptor in ivory</note>—she very rarely portrayed or painted any but figures of women, and not of men. This was because her public modesty and her perfect virtue were the cause of this habit; for in ancient times, men were most often painted or carved naked or semi-naked. Therefore, it seemed to her that if she represented men either "imperfectly" <note>clothed or poorly rendered to avoid detail</note> or "perfectly" <note>anatomically accurate and naked</note>, she would appear to have forgotten the **modesty of virginal shame** <note>original: "pudore uergineo"; a central theme in Renaissance biographies of women, where professional skill had to be balanced against social expectations of "shamefastness" or extreme modesty</note>. To avoid falling into either one of these faults, she judged it a more just and holy thing to abstain from the one, the other, and both.

## On Sulpitia, who was the wife of Fulvius Flaccus, a Roman woman

Servius Paterculus <note>original: "Seruio patricolo"; Servius Cornelius Paterculus</note> and Fulvius Flaccus <note>her husband, Marcus Fulvius Flaccus</note> were both noble men. By the command of the Senate given to the **Ten Men** <term>Ten Men</term><note>The "Decemviri Sacris Faciundis," officials who guarded and consulted the Sibylline Books</note>, and according to the ancient custom found in the Sibylline Books <note>a collection of oracular utterances consulted during Roman state emergencies</note>, a decree was made and ordained that an image—that is, a statue—should be consecrated in the city. This was done so that virgins and all other women might not only abstain from lust, but rather incline themselves toward praiseworthy **modesty** <term>modesty</term><note>original: "pudicitia"; a Roman virtue encompassing both sexual chastity and social decency</note>. 

It was commanded, according to the order of the Ten Men, that the most chaste of all the women then in Rome should dedicate the statue. To find the most chaste woman by the selection of the said ladies, one hundred were first elected from the whole multitude as the most famous and most modest. Among these, Sulpitia was one. Then, from those women, ten were chosen who were the most "shining" in their virtue, of whom Sulpitia was again one. Out of those ten, only one was elected, and Sulpitia was that woman, chosen with the supreme consent of all. 

At that time, it was a great honor for her to have consecrated and dedicated the statue of **Venus the Changer of Hearts** <note>original: "ueticordia de la tēpesta de **V e n e r e**"; a reference to Venus Verticordia, who was believed to turn women's hearts from passion to purity</note>. Truly, it was a beautiful thing for her to be judged by such a great multitude of women as being more famous by their testimony than all others. For she was not only viewed with respect by the eyes of all the excellent people present—like a most certain, celestial light of modesty—but her name should also be spread to future generations for all time, even through veneration and almost infinite glory. 

But I pray you, let someone explain: if one hundred women were elected as modest, why was it said of her alone, more than the others, that she possessed such virtue? Why was she the one placed before all the rest? Deservedly, she stands in public before those who judge and decide as having not only abstained from the bed of...

## On Sulpitia, the Roman woman who was the wife of Fulvius Flaccus

...others than her husband. If one wishes to know and look upon these things with a healthier eye, they will see that **modesty** <term>modesty</term><note>original: "pudicitia"; a Roman virtue representing both physical chastity and a disciplined, respectable public life</note> does not consist solely in abstaining from the embraces of all strangers. That is a thing which many women do against their will or by force. 

But certainly, for a woman to be called entirely modest, it is required that before all other things she must restrain her lustful and wandering eyes. She must keep herself strictly within the borders of her own garments <note>a metaphor for staying within the social and physical bounds of her station and home</note>. Furthermore, she should not only ensure that the words she speaks are virtuous, but she should speak few of them. She must also flee from idleness at all times, for idleness is the most dangerous <note>original: "picoliſſimo" - likely a scribal error or OCR variant for "periculiſſimo" (most dangerous)</note> enemy of modesty. 

She must abstain from the revelries of drinking and eating, for without the help of **Ceres** <note>the goddess of grain and food; the text alludes to the proverb "without Ceres (food) and Bacchus (wine), Venus (lust) freezes"</note>, **luxury** <term>luxury</term><note>original: "luxuria"; in this context, it refers specifically to lust, excess, or lewdness</note> cools down. Songs, dances, and feasts she must let go and flee from, as if she were dodging the blades and spears of lust. She must attend with diligence to the governing and preservation of her **household goods** <term>household goods</term><note>original: "maſſaritie"; the management of domestic assets and the running of the home</note>, and to every form of sobriety and honest saving for the house. 

She must keep her ears closed to every vain tale or talk that is less than virtuous. She should leave aside ointments and fragrant perfumes, despise superfluous ornaments, and subdue with all her strength vain thoughts and unvirtuous appetites. Instead, she should think upon and insist on sacred meditations, prayers, and masses; she should keep vigils and exercise herself with pure **devotion** <term>devotion</term><note>original: "diuotione"; religious piety and dedicated service</note>. 

And so as not to list every single witness of entire modesty: she must love only her husband with supreme love; she should love others with a fraternal love and ignore everyone else. Even into the embraces of her husband, she should not go except with a sense of shame <note>original: "uergogna"; here meaning a modest or bashful restraint</note> for the purpose of the **procreation** <term>procreation</term> of...

## On Harmonia, daughter of Gelon

¶ On Harmonia, daughter of Gelon of Sicily, brother of **Hiero of Syracuse** <note>Hiero II was the King of Syracuse; the text identifies Gelon as his brother, though historically Gelon II was his son</note>: who, when the people rose up and killed all the males and females of that kindred, her nurse put her royal garments on another servant, who was killed in her place. The said Harmonia could not bear this and wished to die together with her companion.

**H**armonia, a young Sicilian woman, was the daughter of Gelon, brother of Hiero, King of Syracuse. Although she was illustrious by her royal birth, she was much more worthy of remembrance for her **piety** <term>piety</term><note>original: "pieta"; in this context, it refers to familial devotion, loyalty, and a high sense of duty</note>. Some claim that she died a virgin; and others say that she was the wife of a man named **Themistus** <note>original: "Themisteo"</note>. But regarding these opinions, let one take whichever pleases them more; for nothing of her glory is taken away by the diversity of these opinions. Due to a certain and sudden uprising of the Syracusans, the people rose up, killing all those of the royal house, both male and female; and the youth <note>likely referring to the male heir or a young prince of the dynasty</note> being already dead...

## On Harmonia, daughter of Gelon.

...the King Hiero: and Andronodorus: and Themistus, relatives of the King: and Damarata and Heraclea <note>original: "Indimata & Eradia"; these were the daughters of Hiero II</note>, daughters of Hiero: and Harmonia, daughter of Gelon: while the tumult of the multitude of killers was happening, a plan was devised by the sagacity of the nurse of the said Harmonia. Royal garments were placed upon a maiden of the same age and stature, and she was given into the hands of the pursuing killers. This maiden certainly did nothing to oppose or go against the nurse's purpose. So it was that, seeing the multitude approaching with the points of their blades, she neither fled nor cried out; nor, being terrified, did she reveal her true condition to those who were killing her. She did not accuse the lurking and hidden Harmonia, in whose place and stead she saw herself being murdered; but she stood silent, receiving death. 

Truly, Harmonia was equally happy and unhappy: happy for the **faith** <term>loyalty</term><note>original: "fede"; refers to the unwavering devotion of the servant who died in her place</note> shown to her, and unhappy for the killing and the loss of such a faithful one. But meanwhile, Harmonia came out from the hidden place where the persevering and firm faith of the innocent girl had been concealed. Seeing the virginal blood shed because of that renowned **piety** <term>sense of duty</term><note>original: "pietate"; here meaning the servant's sacrificial devotion</note>, and shedding tears, she could not bear the pain of looking upon that innocent blood. Nor could she, nor did she wish to prolong her life any further through such a deed and faith reserved for her by another. She deemed it a holier thing to die a premature death while young, and together with such a faithful girl, than to wait for gray hair among unfaithful and cruel citizens. 

O piety! O supreme faith! She who had escaped came into public and into the open, calling the bloody swords back to herself. She confessed at once the trickery of the nurse, the faith of her dead companion, and her own true identity. Furthermore, she offered up her blood alongside that of the dead girl; as much as she could, she made an effort to fall beside the body of the dead maiden, to whom that which piety...

### On Bussa, or Paulina of Canosa in Puglia

¶ Concerning Bussa, or Paulina of Canosa in Puglia: who, after the defeat dealt by Hannibal to the Roman people at Cannae, when as many as ten <note>The text says "diece" (ten), though historical accounts such as those by Livy specify that 10,000 Roman survivors fled to Canosa. This may be a shorthand or a printer's omission of "thousand."</note> Romans fled wounded and naked toward Canosa, the said Bussa received them all. She saw that they were treated and clothed, all without any act of Avarice; and for this she is famous, no less than Alexander <note>Alexander the Great, often cited in the Renaissance as a model of magnanimity</note> for his courtesy.

**B**ussa (named perhaps after her lineage), whom some call Paulina, was a woman of Puglia, and was by birth and origin from Canosa. That she was noble by her generous blood is evidenced by that magnificent and unique deed—the only one left behind her by antiquity. For while Hannibal was waging a most hostile war against the Romans, wasting all Italy with fire and sword; and since it happened that near Cannae the battle was fought by the said Hannibal against the entire force of Rome, and that [battle] was not only...

## On Bussa, or Paulina of Canosa in Puglia

...was overcome by him <note>Referring to Hannibal, the Carthaginian general</note>: but almost the entire force of Italy was broken; and from that greatest defeat, a vast number of people fled by night through back-roads and off the main paths. Of these, about ten <note>original: ".x."; historical accounts, such as those by Livy, state that 10,000 Roman survivors fled to Canosa. The text likely contains a printer’s error or shorthand for "ten thousand."</note> arrived at the city of Canosa, which at that time served as the seat of the Roman company. They all arrived there weary, bloodied, wounded, disarmed, naked, and struck with the most diverse wounds. 

The aforementioned Bussa, undaunted by such a terrible defeat, nor by the great power of the victor, received them into her own possessions. She comforted them with sweet words and immediately summoned physicians. With maternal affection and singular diligence, she had all the wounded treated. For those who were naked and disarmed, with marvelous **liberality** <term>extraordinary generosity with money or gifts</term>, she provided arms and clothing. From her own personal wealth, she paid for the daily expenses of every one of them. 

Thus comforted, organized, and restored to hope, when they wished to depart, she gave to everyone according to the necessities of life that their journey required, without holding back her hand in any act of charity. 

Certainly, this was a miraculous act of charity, of courtesy, and of magnificence for a woman—and much more praiseworthy than if it had been done by a man. For although we read of **Alexander** <note>Alexander the Great, King of Macedon</note>, the conqueror of the world, among his other most notable deeds—asserting that he had the custom not only of giving precious jewels, silver, and gold most generously, but also of giving great principalities and splendid realms to his friends; and many times, having conquered a King, he would restore his rank and his kingdoms. These things, although they were of the greatest nobility and magnificence, and worthy of perpetual and splendid praise, cannot be equated to the magnificence of Bussa. This is because **Alexander** was a man, and Bussa was a woman, for whom tenacity <note>original: "tenacità"; here meaning a tendency to cling to wealth or stinginess</note> and avarice are not only...

<summary>This page describes the extraordinary generosity of Bussa of Canosa, who provided medical care, clothing, and funds to Roman soldiers fleeing Hannibal's army. The author argues her charity surpasses even that of Alexander the Great because such magnanimity is rarer in women.</summary>
<keywords>Bussa, Paulina, Canosa di Puglia, Battle of Cannae, Roman history, Hannibal, Alexander the Great, charity, magnanimity, women in history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the comparison between the generosity of Alexander the Great and the noblewoman Bussa, who provided for the Roman survivors of the Battle of Cannae. ## On Bussa, or Paulina of Canosa in Puglia

...magnificent: but her actions were innate and of her own nature; and those of Alexander <note>original: "d piccolo aĩo"; literally "of a small spirit," implying Alexander's motivations were lesser compared to Bussa's innate virtue</note> are of a lesser spirit. 

**Alexander** was a very great King; **Bussa** was a private woman. He gave that which he had taken violently and by force; she gave that which she possessed through hereditary possession. He gave that which perhaps he could not have kept; Bussa gave that which she had long preserved and could have continued to preserve. Alexander gave to those who were not his friends; Bussa gave to the unknown and to strangers. Alexander gave while increasing himself and his own reputation; Bussa gave while diminishing and stripping herself of her wealth for the aforementioned needs. Alexander gave when he was among distant nations; Bussa gave while staying under her own sky among her own people. Alexander did it for the gain of glory and to be praised; Bussa did it only to help those in need. 

Why is it necessary to say many things? If we consider well the mind, the sex, and the quality of each person, I do not doubt that under an equal and just judge, Bussa would acquire more glory from her **liberality** <term>extraordinary generosity with money or gifts</term> than Alexander from his **magnificence**; but let whoever wishes have the clearest praise among them. 

Bussa used her substances and riches rightly, because she held them as a servant to her duty. She did not drag wealth from the darkest hiding places of the earth into a public place only for it to be reburied in secret in a tomb—which is what greedy people always do with those things that they bury, lock away, and guard. They bury it as if it were to be born again another time; but truly, wealth is for whoever might use it for the common convenience and profit first, and then for your own honorable brilliance and the common living of friends. And if any remains, it should be given to succor those prostrated and beaten down by the injury of **fortune** <note>In the Renaissance, "Fortune" was often personified as a fickle goddess who brought sudden ruin or success</note>. To those exhausted by the wrath of heaven <note>A reference to natural disasters or "acts of God"</note>; to those oppressed unworthily by poverty; to those imprisoned for the sins of others; to all those afflicted by anxious poverty, we ought to [help] with the...

<summary>This page concludes a comparison between Alexander the Great and Bussa of Canosa, arguing that Bussa's charity was superior because it was selfless and personal. It transitions into a moral reflection on the proper use of wealth to help the suffering rather than hoarding it.</summary>

...to administer aid with a liberal mind: so that we may be good; not merely so that we *appear* good, but because we profit by being good. We give not to gain for ourselves, but so that we may be generous; we ought to grant such comforts, always using the **tempering of reason**. This is so that when we help others, we do not bring necessity or misery upon ourselves. For if we did, we might then be forced not only to seize the goods of others by force, but we would find that we could no longer even protect our own well-being through any means. <note>The author cautions that charity must be balanced with "reason" so the giver does not become a pauper who is then tempted to steal.</note>

## On Sophonisba, Queen of Numidia

At the time that Hannibal was troubling Italy: she being of a flowering age: and of marvelous beauty: and of excellent form: she was joined in marriage by her father to Syphax <note>King of the Masaesyli tribe of western Numidia.</note>, the most powerful king of Numidia. Nor indeed did her father do it as much out of a desire for kinship, as because that shrewd man <note>Hasdrubal Gisco, her father.</note> desired to draw the said Syphax away—since there was war between the Romans and the Carthaginians—through the efforts and flatteries of his daughter, from the side of the Romans: to that of the Carthaginians, with whom he was [now] joined. And he was not deceived by his premeditated cunning: for once Syphax had already celebrated the wedding with the pleasing young woman—her father favoring her through her high beauty, and fine manners, and her wise speech: Syphax was so drawn away by his delight in her: that he did not think he could have any consolation without her: and so the unhappy Syphax being inflamed with love for Sophonisba: and hearing that Scipio <note>Scipio Africanus, the Roman general.</note> intended to cross with his fleet and the Roman armies from Sicily into Africa: Sophonisba, [prompted] by her father Hasdrubal, knew how to so flatter her husband: that she not only drew him to abandon the Romans: to whom he was bound by faith <note>original: "fede"; referring to a formal treaty or oath of alliance.</note>: but she drew him to be the principal captain for the Carthaginians against the Romans. For which reason, having thus broken his faith: and the promise made previously to Scipio: he forbade and blocked the passage through those lands: but the most noble youth Scipio: blaming and reproving the iniquity of the Barbarian King: then coming to battle with him through the agency of Masinissa <note>A rival Numidian king and Roman ally.</note> his companion: and of Laelius <note>Gaius Laelius, Scipio's second-in-command.</note> his lieutenant: [Syphax] was defeated: captured: and bound with chains: and led to Cirta <note>The capital city of Numidia.</note>, the royal city: where Sophonisba was: who, having seen him bound: the gates of the city were immediately opened by the citizens and surrendered: and [the city] gave itself to King Masinissa: who: because

### On Sophonisba, Queen of Numidia

Laelius <note>Gaius Laelius, a Roman general and close friend of Scipio Africanus.</note> had not yet arrived inside; and Masinissa, being dressed in royal armor, encountered Sophonisba. When she saw him—being well aware of her own misfortune and perceiving who he was, that he was the King—she knelt at his feet and spoke to him in this way: "Noble King, it has pleased God and your good fortune that you may do whatever you wish with us, who only a short time ago were King and Queen. Therefore, if it is permitted for a prisoner to supplicate with humble words—to pray to the victor and master of her life and death—let it be allowed for me, prostrate and kneeling at your feet, to touch your victorious right hand. I pray to you by your majesty, in which I myself was held a short time ago, and by our royal lineage and the common name of the Numidians <note>Sophonisba appeals to their shared North African heritage to distance herself from the Romans.</note>. And since you have been raised to better grace and fortune, let it be that Syphax does not part from me <note>Original: "che Siphace non si parta da me"; she refers to their shared fate as captives.</note>—she whom adverse fortune has caused Numidia to be yours by right and subject to you—and may it please you to do with me what seems merciful and good to your eyes, so that I am saved and kept from being given over to the troublesome judgment of the Romans. For you can easily understand what I—their enemy and the daughter of Hasdrubal, let alone the wife of Syphax—might and should fear. And if every other way in this matter is taken away, I pray and beseech you that I should rather be killed by your hands than given into the power of the Romans."

Masinissa—who was a Numidian, and like all his people inclined to passion <note>original: "libidine"; Renaissance authors often attributed "hot-blooded" temperaments to North African figures.</note>—first looked upon the noble beauty of Sophonisba's praying mouth; and misfortune having added a certain moving and pitiable quality to the supplicating woman, he was moved and attracted by both human pity and desire. ## On Sophonisba, Queen of Numidia

[Masinissa] took her right hand, raised her up, and entering the temple, he joined with her as her husband, celebrating the wedding amidst the clamor of the soldiers. He thought that in doing so, he had found a way to satisfy his own desire and to provide an escape for the woman who had pleaded with him.

The following day, Laelius <note>Gaius Laelius, a prominent Roman general and close associate of Scipio Africanus.</note> arrived. By his command, Masinissa—dressed in royal finery, and bringing all the plunder and his new wife—went into the camp. There, they were all honorably received by Scipio and highly commended for their great achievements. However, Masinissa was later amicably rebuked and censured by Scipio for the marriage he had entered into with a prisoner of the Roman people <note>As a Roman ally, Masinissa was expected to turn over high-value captives like Sophonisba to Rome rather than marry them.</note>. 

Having entered his tent and thinking upon the fate of Sophonisba, Masinissa wept most bitterly. After many sighs and tears, he called for one of his faithful servants and immediately commanded him to prepare a certain poison <note>Original: "uẽneo" (veleno); a common resolution for noble figures in antiquity to avoid the shame of a Roman triumph.</note> and carry it to Sophonisba. He told the servant to say that he would have gladly kept the promise he had freely given her, but because the power of choice had been taken from him by those who had the authority to do so, he was instead turning to the alternative that she herself had requested. He sent her that proof of his faith as best he could, with the greatest sorrow and grief. 

He added that she should remember her father <note>Hasdrubal Gisco, the Carthaginian general.</note>, her country, and the two Kings she had taken as husbands <note>Referring to her first husband Syphax and her brief marriage to Masinissa.</note>. And if, while she lived, she did not wish to fall into the hands of the Romans, she should drink the potion and take that course and counsel that seemed best and most pleasing to her. 

Having heard these words, Sophonisba spoke to the messenger with a most steadfast expression, saying: "I accept this wedding gift, since no other thing can be given by a new husband to his wife. But for my part, I tell him that it would have been more fitting for me to die than to remain..."

# On Sophonisba, Queen of Numidia

...welcomed her death. Nor were the words more harshly spoken than the act itself: she took the beverage <note>The poison sent by Masinissa.</note>. Not only for a young Queen, who was then just beginning to feel and taste the sweetness of human life and royal status, would this have been a miraculous feat and worthy of note: to go so boldly and without any fear against and run toward certain death.

# On Theoxena, daughter of Herodicus, Prince of Thessaly: who, seeing herself pursued by King Philip, chose to die in freedom rather than fall into the hands of the enemy; and so, embracing her husband, she threw herself into the sea.

**T**heoxena of Thessaly was a woman of noble origin: on one hand through sweet piety, and on the other through severe ferocity, she left a celebrated testimony of herself to those of the future. ### On Theoxena, daughter of Herodicus of Thessaly.

With the father <note>Herodicus, a prince of Thessaly</note> having been killed by the aforementioned Philip: the King, persisting in his evil disposition for a long space of time, then deprived the two said sisters <note>Theoxena and Archo</note> of their two husbands by killing them. Each sister was left with one young son. It followed that Archo <note>Original: "learcho"</note> remarried a man named Poris <note>Original: "Porido"</note>, a prince of their people, and bore him many children. 

But **Theoxena**, although she was sought in marriage by many noble men, remained in a long widowhood. However, when her sister Archo died, leaving behind many children, Theoxena felt compassion for her nephews. To ensure they did not fall into the hands of a stepmother and might be raised with great care, she married the aforementioned Poris. At that time, no law prohibited such a marriage <note>Marrying a deceased sister's husband was a known practice in certain ancient contexts to preserve family continuity.</note>. She began to raise the children with that same love as if she had given birth to them herself. She raised them so well that it was clearly shown she had not remarried Poris for any personal comfort or pleasure. 

While things stood in this state, it happened that King Philip—a restless and cruel man—once again set his heart on moving to war against the Romans, who at that time flourished in the height of their prosperity. And therefore, having removed and cleared almost all the ancient inhabitants from the coastal lands of Thessaly, he sent them in crowds to the region of Paeonia <note>A region north of Macedonia</note>, which was later called Emathia. He gave the fortified towns of these emptied lands to the Thracians <note>Original: "Tratii"</note> to inhabit and guard, whom he considered—according to his own words—to be faithful and more suited for the coming battle. And having heard the blasphemies and curses sent his way by those who were being forced to leave, he decided that nothing was safe for him unless he killed all those who had been offended by the deaths of their fathers... <note>Philip feared that the children of the noblemen he had executed would eventually seek revenge as they came of age.</note>

## On Theoxena, daughter of Herodicus of Thessaly

...done <note>Original: "fati." Likely referring to the King's orders or the displacement of the families mentioned on the previous page.</note>: who, having commanded that they should be captured and placed under heavy guard to be killed—not all at once, but bit by bit over the course of time—it happened that **Theoxena** learned of the wicked King's command. Remembering the deaths of her father and her husbands, and the fate of her sister Archo, and knowing that her own children and nephews were to be demanded from her, she judged that if they fell into the King's hands, they would not only be cruelly treated by him, but even subjected to the outrages <term>fastidii: in this context, it refers to physical or sexual violations and insults</term> of the guards. 

And therefore, to flee and escape such a fate, she immediately turned her mind toward a bitter thought and a most fierce deed. She told her husband <note>Poris</note>, the father of her nephews, that if the children were demanded, she would rather kill them all with her own hand than suffer them to fall into the power of **Philip** <note>King Philip V of Macedon</note>. 

Hearing this, Poris, considering the wicked thing that might happen and wishing to please his wife and ensure the safety of the children, decided to flee. Having pretended for a time to offer a sacrifice to the statue of **Aeneas** <note>The legendary Trojan hero; the city of Aeneia, where they were staying, was named after him</note>, and having spent the day there in solemn ceremony, dining with his wife and children, he waited for nightfall. During the third watch <note>the period between midnight and 3:00 AM</note>, when everyone was asleep, he secretly pretended to return to his homeland but instead boarded his whole company onto a prepared ship. 

And although he had intended to sail not to Thessaloniki but to **Euboea** <note>Original: "Chubee"</note>, the opposite of his plan occurred. They had barely left the shore of Aeneia when the wind rose completely against them, driving them back toward the place from which they had departed. Despite struggling all night with great effort to reach their intended destination, when [morning] came...

# On Theoxena, daughter of Herodicus of Thessaly

...as the day began to brighten, they found themselves near the shores. When the guards saw this, they realized they were fleeing and immediately sent an armed vessel <note>Original: "legnio," literally "wood," a common term for a ship or galley</note> to force them to return. And certainly **Poris** <note>Theoxena's husband</note>, knowing the immediate danger, begged the sailors to row with all their might; he himself prayed to the gods to grant them escape.

Seeing this, **Theoxena**, well aware of the danger, immediately prepared the poison and drew out the knives. She first placed them before her own son and then her nephews, and said: "Children, death alone is that which can now provide us vengeance and safety. This drink <note>The poison</note> and the knife are the paths by which one can flee the cruel wrath and pride of the King. Therefore, my young children, awaken in yourselves your noble and courageous spirits. And you, who are older, act manfully: take up the blade or drink the poison, whichever death pleases you more, and freely turn to it and flee, since the force of the stormy sea forbids our escape into life."

The enemies were already upon them, and the most fierce woman, devoted to death, stood and comforted them, compelling the hesitant youths to seek liberty through death. Therefore, some having succumbed to one death or another, half-alive and still gasping, were thrown from the ship into the waves of the sea by the command of **Theoxena**.

And when this strongest and fiercest woman had led into liberty through death those whom she had so affectionately nourished—so that it might not seem she had reserved for herself that servitude which she had spared the others—with a noble heart she embraced her praying husband and plunged with him into the surging sea. She judged it a far more holy thing to die free than to live languishing in filthy and brutal servitude <term>seruitudine: in this context, it refers to both political enslavement and the personal degradation threatened by the King</term>.

### On Veronica, Queen of Cappadocia

And thus, having left the empty ship to the enemies, she deprived **Philip** of the pleasure of his wicked and most evil desire. In this way, the illustrious and most resolute woman brought forth an action worthy of eternal memory.

### On Veronica, Queen of Cappadocia, also called Laodice, daughter of Mithridates, King of Pontus, and wife of King Ariarathes; who with a courageous spirit avenged with her own hands the death of her two sons, as appears below.

This was not only due to the fervent love she held for her son—with which a mother’s heart is most often inflamed—but rather for the merit of her noble daring which she displayed in avenging her son. 

This matter—so that it does not seem to have been left in neglect <note>Original: "ne la pena," literally "in the pain/penalty," here implying the state of being forgotten or left unrecorded</note>—must be written down with no small number of words and letters. ## On Veronica, Queen of Cappadocia

[dau]ghter: and she was married to **Ariarathes**, King of Cappadocia. After **Ariarathes** was killed by **Gordius**—through the machinations of **Mithridates**, Veronica’s brother—two sons remained. And truly, **Nicomedes**, King of Bithynia, having occupied Cappadocia because, following the death of the King, it was almost vacant; **Mithridates**, greedy for the realm, pretending that out of love for his nephews he wished to secure the kingdom for them, went to war against **Nicomedes**. 

But **Mithridates**, having tested and learned that Veronica had remarried **Nicomedes**, converted his feigned piety into truth <note>Meaning he dropped the pretense of being a protector and showed his true, aggressive intentions</note>. After he had driven out **Nicomedes**, he restored the paternal kingdom to her eldest son. Yet, later repenting of this good deed, he had him killed through fraud. And the other son, whose name was **Ariarathes** <note>In the text: "Ariactorinocho," likely a corrupted form of Ariarathes</note>—as it pleases some to believe—was also put to death through the work of **Mithridates**. 

The unhappy parent, a mother bereaved and a widow of two sons, bore these things so painfully that, constrained by excessive grief and forgetting her female sex <note>A common rhetorical device in historical texts to describe a woman performing "masculine" feats of war or vengeance</note>, she took up arms like a person possessed. Having mounted a chariot with well-ordered and swift horses, she did not stop until she reached **Cencho**, who had been the henchman <note>Original: "fatelite," a transcription of "satelite" (satellite), meaning a loyal follower or accomplice</note> and executor of the wicked deed. 

He fled with great impetus and speed; and being unable to reach him with her spear, she struck him in the middle of a rocky place <note>or "with a stone"</note> which threw him to the ground dead. Then, passing over him with her chariot and trampling him entirely, she did not cease from riding among the armed men and through the blades; nor did she have any fear of her brother, who was her enemy, until she reached where the body of her dead son lay. There, the wretched woman surrendered to sorrowful and motherly tears and performed the funeral rites. O good God! O invincible forces of nature!

### On the wife of Orgiagon the Galatian

### On the wife of Orgiagon the Galatian

[...valuing] revenge more than liberation or life. But on the contrary, she concealed her most indignant soul in silence until the proper time. But when the agreed-upon money arrived to ransom the prisoners, her renewed anger flared up and sparked. Once she was set free, she pulled aside her people and told the Centurion <note>The Roman officer who had captured and raped her during her imprisonment</note> to have the gold weighed. While the said Centurion was focused on this task with his eyes and mind, the lady said to her people in her own idiom and a language unknown to the Romans that they should kill the said Centurion. And this being done, she had his head cut off; she took it and placed it in her lap. And having come into the presence of her husband, she told him what had been done to her by force and the revenge she had taken; then she threw the head of the Centurion at her husband's feet, as if to restore and cleanse her violated honor. 

Who is there who would not say that she was not only like a Roman, but of the ranks of Lucretia <note>Lucretia was a legendary Roman noblewoman whose suicide after being raped led to the overthrow of the Roman monarchy; she was the Renaissance archetype for female honor.</note> rather than a barbarian woman? Standing before her still were the prison, the chains, the weapons of the victors, and the axe and the executioner's blade of the judge. And she was hardly at liberty when the indignation of her contaminated and violated body drove her honest breast with such great force that she was prepared—if it were necessary—to be chained, imprisoned, and killed once more without fear. And the spirited and illustrious woman, to avenge the wicked fate she had suffered, led her servants with a sure and constant command against the ill-fated violator. In what place will you find a man so sharp, more spirited, or more severe when compared to her, who was a most vigilant and careful guardian of womanly honor <term>womanly honor: original "dōneſcha hōeſta," referring to the contemporary ideal of a woman's sexual purity and social reputation</term>? The illustrious woman saw with admirable consideration that it would be a holier thing for her to walk into certain death than to return...

### On Tertia Emilia, wife of the first Africanus

...shines with a tawny <note>The original "fulvido" refers to a reddish-gold or amber color, often used to describe the glow of gold or sunlight.</note> splendor. For Scipio—who, when he was a young man, restored the virgin bride (preserved in all her honor) to Prince Allucius <note>original: "Lutero". This refers to the "Continence of Scipio," a famous historical anecdote where Scipio returned a beautiful captive to her fiancé, Allucius, during the conquest of New Carthage in Spain.</note>, being then in the flower of his early youth and the virgin bride being of miraculous beauty—not only did he return her with all the treasures of her dowry (which had been confiscated for the sake of her recovery), but even added to it from his own personal treasure.

Yet, having later reached a mature age, he could not, and did not know how to, restrain himself and hold back from unholy lusts; thus, so great a man as he fell into affection and intercourse with one of his female servants. And since it is extremely difficult to deceive the awareness of honest love, he could not conduct himself in so secret a manner that, over a period of time, his lady Tertia did not become aware of it.

There is no doubt that for her, enduring this was a most bothersome and immense pain. For it is affirmed that nothing more injurious, more intolerable, and more contemptible can be done to a married woman than for that which they say is hers by right of marriage to be granted by her husband to a foreign woman. And certainly, I believe this most easily. For, whether it happens through feminine fragility, or because she holds a less than good opinion of herself, that most suspicious "feminine animal" <note>This phrase reflects the author's use of a common medieval/Renaissance trope regarding the supposedly inherent nature of women as suspicious or irrational.</note> immediately judges and deems everything done by the husband with another woman to be a detriment and a loss of the love owed to her. 

But however difficult it was or seemed to her, nevertheless, with patience and a most constant heart, this illustrious woman endured it with such silence and in such a concealed manner that not only did no one else notice it, but Scipio himself never realized or believed that she knew of it.

# On the third wife of the first Africanus <note>Tertia Aemilia (d. 163 or 162 BCE). In many Renaissance texts, she is referred to as "the third wife," though "Tertia" was actually her name, indicating she was likely the third daughter of her father, Aemilius Paullus.</note>

Because that most discreet wife considered it a thing far too indecent and unseemly if it were to become publicly known that Scipio—who had subjected so many noble kings and foreign and strong nations through his renowned virtue—was himself subjected to the love of a servant girl. Nor did it seem enough to this most holy and perfect woman to have hidden and concealed this while Scipio was living. But even after he was dead, she remained mindful of the memory of her renowned husband, [fearing] such an infamous mark might be left upon it if the secret should somehow be breathed out in any direction. Furthermore, she acted to remove any cause whereby she [the servant] who had shared the bed <term>concubito</term> <note>original: "concubito," referring to sexual intimacy or cohabitation.</note> of such a famous man might be dishonored by any chance, or lest through her own lasciviousness and defect she might associate with another man in a less than honorable way. By such a thing, the desire <note>original: "concupiscentia," meaning lust or strong sexual desire.</note> of that most great and noble man might be mocked.

And first, with a generous spirit, she set the woman free; and then afterward, she married her to one of his freedmen <term>liberto</term> <note>original: "liberto," a former slave who has been legally granted their freedom.</note>. Oh, woman worthy to be raised to heaven with holy praise! On one hand, with a patient spirit, she bore the injuries to her marriage; on the other, she paid the debts of her dead husband toward the unfaithful servant with a generous soul. The rarer such a thing is seen, the more we should judge it to be splendid. 

Another woman would have complained most bitterly to sympathetic neighbors and to her husband's relatives with the longest gossip and with marvelous complaints and regrets, saying that she had been overlooked and despised by her husband, and made a widow during his lifetime, and set aside for a most vile servant and little harlot <term>meretricola</term> <note>original: "meretricola," a derogatory diminutive for a common prostitute.</note> of the lowliest sort. And not only would she have struck her down and chased her away immediately, but she would have viewed her with shame, and would have hounded her, and...

## On Sempronia Graccha.

It happened to her that she was summoned to judgment before the people by the Tribune of the People <note>term: Tribune; a powerful Roman official elected to represent the common people and protect their rights.</note>, much to her great displeasure and disdain. There, in the presence of the multitude, she was told that she should kiss Equitius of Firmium in Picenum <note>A man of low birth who falsely claimed to be the son of Tiberius Gracchus to gain political favor during a time of unrest.</note> as her nephew and the son of her brother Tiberius Gracchus. She was commanded to receive him as a member of the Sempronian family, despite the public nature of the setting. 

In that place, where even the leading men of the city were afraid—surrounded on all sides by an unlearned and poorly informed mob riled up by rumors, and scolded and threatened by the turbulent face of the Tribune's high and sublime authority—she was not moved in any way, nor did she feel fear. Instead, with a most steady countenance and a very constant mind, she resisted them all. 

She remembered well that her brother Tiberius had only three sons: one had died as a young man while serving as a soldier in Sardinia; another had died in Rome shortly before their father’s downfall; and the third had died while still in swaddling clothes <note>original: "ne le fascie," referring to infancy.</note>. With a most steadfast heart and a sharp gaze, being neither moved nor frightened by any side, she rejected the outsider and reckless Equitius. He, through deceitful and false displays, strove to cloud the most illustrious lineage of the Gracchi by shamefully forcing himself into it. Neither by the command of power, nor fear, nor threats could she be induced to do what everyone believed she rightfully ought to do. 

By her courageous action, she expelled and nullified the insolence of the foolish and insane Equitius. Once his falsehood and malice were recognized by the better-informed Tribunes and the matter was more closely examined, the perseverance of Sempronia’s noble soul was praised. There will perhaps be some who say that by the merit of her ancestors, Sempronia deserved...

## On the Fifth Roman Woman, Claudia

This constancy ought to be placed among famous women, for by a certain innate character, her family members are of an obstinate and inflexible opinion, possessing an immovable persistence. But I—granted that I am not an unbiased judge—nevertheless judge that they should be praised if they align themselves with the truth. To this truth, without doubt, Sempronia insisted and defended herself. There are also some who wish to say that she was of such an untamed neck <note>term: untamed neck; a classical idiom meaning headstrong or stubborn.</note> and hard-headedness that she left nothing done against her unpunished or unavenged. And for that reason, they believe she played a part in the death of Scipio, her husband. For once Numantia <note>A city in Spain famously besieged and destroyed by Scipio Aemilianus in 133 BCE.</note> was destroyed, and when he was asked to give his opinion, he judged that Tiberius <note>Tiberius Gracchus, Sempronia's brother.</note> had been killed rightfully; having no regard for their kinship, he praised the cruel death of that seditious man.

# On Claudia Quinta, a Roman Woman

...was reputed to be more splendid and charming <note>The text suggests she was seen as too focused on her appearance, leading to rumors about her character.</note> than was proper, and she was held by the Roman women to be not only less than honest, but even less than modest and chaste. While Marcus Cornelius and Publius Sempronius were consuls, in the fifteenth year of the second African war <note>The Second Punic War (218–201 BCE), fought against Hannibal.</note>, the Mother of the Gods <note>The goddess Cybele, also known as the Magna Mater or Idaean Mother.</note> arrived from Pessinus <note>An ancient city in modern-day Turkey, the center of Cybele's cult.</note> to Rome at the mouth of the Tiber. To receive her and draw her from the ship according to the response of the oracle, the entire Senate [chose] Nasica <note>original: "Cato che nauſa"; the text likely intended Scipio Nasica, who was historically chosen as the "best man in Rome" to welcome the goddess, though the OCR shows a corruption of the name.</note>, the best man of the city, and ordered that all of Rome with all the women should go to that place where the ship was approaching. As the sailors wanted to approach the land, they drew near to the mouth of the river Almo <note>original: "Lare"; likely referring to the Almo, a small tributary of the Tiber where the goddess's image was ritually washed.</note>, with which the image was being pulled. And it so happened that while the multitude of young men was pulling it, the ship could not be moved. Claudia, having joined the other women and being conscious of her own virtue, openly getting on her knees, made a prayer and prayed to the goddess that if she judged her to be chaste, she should follow her belt <note>term: belt (cingolo); a sash or girdle worn by Roman women.</note>. Rising up after the prayer was made, and with great confidence—hoping that the thing she had supplicated for would be performed—she commanded that the ship be tied with her belt and that all the young men who were pulling should be removed. No sooner was this done than Claudia, pulling most easily, drew the ship from the shallows to that place where she wished. Such a miraculous success followed that the contrary opinion held by everyone turned into the greatest praise for her. And so she, who had gone to the shore stained with the most shameful mark of lewdness, returned with the most notable splendor of **modesty**. But truly, even if that which Claudia requested followed according to her vow and desire, God forbid that I should consider it to be of a sound mind—no matter how innocent the person may be—to have the audacity to do such things. For anyone wishing to do that which is shown to be without fault, things beyond...

### On Claudia, the fifth Roman woman

...by nature: and it is better to test God than to purge the stain of the alleged sin <note>The author argues that Claudia’s willingness to let the goddess judge her was a greater proof of faith than merely trying to defend her own reputation.</note>. It is fitting for us to act frankly toward a holy life. And if we are esteemed less than good, it is not without our benefit that He sustains it. Truly God wishes that our patience be strengthened, and that elation and pride be removed from us; thus the virtues are exercised, and we may rejoice within ourselves when God knows that others are unworthy, and we are much—indeed very much—worthy, if God alone is our witness to our good work and good living. And therefore, if men imagine and judge of us less than well, even when we do good, we should not torment ourselves; but we should persist with all our strength, so that they may rather be the ones holding bad opinions than we being the ones doing bad deeds.

## On Queen Hypsicratea of Pontus

...spirit <note>The text likely refers to her "merits" or "spirit" adding splendor to her name.</note> with perpetual radiance to her name. And truly by exerting herself, and Mithridates toiling with diligence, through cunning and various ways of assaults and battles against the Romans—even though, according to barbarian custom, he had many wives and concubines—nevertheless Hypsicratea was his true and most principal one. She was kindled with the most honorable and elevated love; whether Mithridates traveled through vast regions, or whether he fought by sea or by land, she was always joined to him in most faithful and inseparable company. For it did not seem right to her that her most loving husband should, without her, receive truly faithful service from any other person. Because most of the time the services of servants are forced and not truly faithful, she wished that she herself might administer all necessary things for her most beloved lord and husband. 

Notwithstanding that following him on his heavy rides <note>The Italian 'cavalcate pesse' refers to the arduous, long-distance military marches on horseback.</note> was a mark of the greatest faithfulness, she nevertheless resolved to follow him wherever he might go. And since it seemed an incongruous and not very decent thing that a woman should go at the side of a bellicose King, she dressed herself in the habit of a man. And principally that thing in which women greatly glory—that is, her beautiful golden hair—she cut off; and the singular, lovely beauty of her delicate face she not only gave up, but endured covering it with a helmet. 

She did not care to cover her face with lotions <note>original: "lisci" — cosmetics or skin-smoothing pastes used by noblewomen of the era</note>, but rather with sweat, with dust, and with the rust of weapons. She covered her ivory and delicate breast, once dressed in purple garments, with a coat of iron; she put on greaves <note><term>gambaroli</term> — leg armor protecting the shins</note> and thigh-guards, and stripped off her rings, jewelry, belts, and every feminine ornament. In exchange for those, she carried iron-tipped spears, bows, arrows, and quivers <note>original: "torchasi" — a term for quivers, likely derived from the Persian 'tarkash'</note>; she learned to ride and every other warlike craft...

<summary>Hypsicratea, the primary wife of King Mithridates VI, demonstrates her extreme devotion by cutting her hair and donning armor to accompany him into battle against the Romans. She rejects the comforts of the court and the service of untrustworthy servants to personally care for and fight alongside her husband.</summary>

# On Queen Hypsicratea of Pontus

...acting in such a way, and so courageously, that from a beloved young royal lady she became like a strong and hardy knight. And if this should seem a small or light matter, consider this: she was accustomed to the most delicate and royal things, to adorned chambers and the richest beds, and perhaps was never used to leaving the royal gardens and palaces; yet now she learned anew to sit upon swift horses. And as one endowed with a manly spirit <note>original: "animo virile" — a common Renaissance praise for women who showed courage or strength traditionally associated with men</note>, laden with armor and never weary, following her husband, she went without any fear among the caves of the strongest and most fearsome beasts, often passing the whole night without a moment's sleep, always accompanying her husband. 

At last, having passed through infinite and heavy labors—arduous not only for a young female Queen, but for any robust, hardened, and strong knight—after Mithridates was overcome, vanquished, and defeated by Gnaeus Pompey, he fled with few followers through the regions of Armenia <note>The text uses "l'isola" (the island), which may be a geographical error or a reference to a specific isolated territory</note>, and through the borders of Pontus, and through diverse, harsh, and rugged nations and regions. She was always at his side by day and night, comforting him toward better hope: now with wise and mature words, now with sweet and pleasant actions to which he was inclined. In such a beautiful way she consoled him that he was never so troubled, nor so breathless with grief or fatigue, nor in such harsh or unpleasant places, that he did not take comfort, and it seemed to him as if he were in his own chamber and marriage bed. 

O breast of merit and sanctuary of pure sweetness! O auspicious virtue of friendship <note>original: "amicitia" — in this context, it refers to the deep bond of loyalty and companionate love between spouses</note>! With what holy things you strengthened the feminine soul without fail. Never was there a wife who bore such things, nor ever so heavily yet so willingly; for whose merits antiquity turned and labored to grant her perpetual praise, and with good reason...

## On Queen Hypsicratea of Pontus

...posterity has little reason to admire him. Truly, for so many labors, for such complete and illustrious faith, this perfect wife did not receive a worthy reward from her husband. For when Mithridates was old—having boldly killed the son he had with her—he was forced by the power of the Romans to retreat not only into his own kingdom, but into his own fortress and royal palace. Even as he struggled against his enemies and imagined doing great things, sending embassies and legations at great expense, and seeking out various and strange schemes against the Romans, he was nonetheless abandoned and turned into an enemy by his own son, Sciphace <note>original: "Sciphace" — likely a scribal error or variant for Pharnaces II, who rebelled against his father Mithridates</note>, who rebelled against him because of the inhuman and great cruelty Mithridates had begun to use toward his own children and friends. 

Recognizing this, and finding his son moving wretchedly against him, Mithridates despaired of any remedy. Judging that the final ruin of all his affairs had arrived, he caused the said Hypsicrateia—who had performed so many good services and suffered such great hardships for him—to die by poison along with his daughters and concubines, so that she would not remain and live after him <note>historical context: Mithridates ordered his wives and daughters to drink poison to save them from the humiliation of being captured by the Romans and paraded in a triumphal procession</note>. Truly, the ungrateful act of Mithridates toward Hypsicrateia could not diminish her deserved glory. For although her mortal body was taken away by an untimely death through poison, her name lives on through the monument of venerable literature <note>original: "monumento de uenerande littere" — a reference to the classical authors who recorded her loyalty</note>, and she shall live with celebrated fame until our time and forever; nor can she be cheated or deceived by the long passage of posterity.
