## On Sempronia of Rome

Sempronia of Rome: we do not recall having read very often of another woman of such celebrated intellect <term>intellect (original: ingegno)</term> as her who was mentioned above: but for the most part, she was inclined toward dishonest and most vicious things. This woman, by the testimony of our ancestors, was splendid in her origin and lineage, as well as in the shapeliness <term>shapeliness (original: formosita)</term> and beauty of her body; and she was quite fortunate in her husband and children, whose names were omitted.

She was of a subtle, quick, and most ready wit; she understood matters readily <note>original: "sp̃ste" — likely a contraction for "spesso" or "sapute," indicating she grasped subjects quickly</note>, and she practiced and performed even as she understood whatever thing she had seen anyone else say or do. Hence, she not only possessed knowledge of Latin letters <note>In this context, "letters" refers to being well-read in literature and capable of formal writing.</note>, but she also composed Greek verses with such insight that they moved to admiration whoever heard or read them. And beyond this, she possessed such aptitude and charm in every matter that, when she wished, she could feign modesty and temperance, or indulge in laughter and games, softness and harshness; she was also of graceful and delightful habits in speech, and in whatever manner...

### On Sempronia of Rome

...manner of speech she delivered: she expressed and uttered words so charming and sweet to her listeners, and so full of delightful manners, that it was a wonderful thing. Furthermore, she knew how to play instruments, sing, and dance so skillfully, and do every other thing. These are all talents to be highly commended in a woman. 

But along with these things, she was filled with the worst vices, which she practiced so boldly that they would have been shameless for any wicked man; often igniting herself with such lust <term>lust (original: luxuria)</term> and fire that she sought out men more often than men sought her. May it not please God that I should therefore think her the root of it, or that I should blame Nature. For although the forces of Nature are great regarding the beginnings of things—which are so inflexible that with little effort Nature leads them where it wishes—nonetheless, once a person is born and thus neglected and abandoned, they always return to a worse state. 

Therefore, because of the excessive license and boldness granted by elders and given to young girls—as I judge and estimate—many times the minds <term>minds (original: ingegni)</term> of virgins are spoiled and corrupted. As often happens, once license is given to them, declining into lasciviousness little by little, they abandon womanly restraint; and their audacity breaks out, increased and multiplied by a certain foolish opinion which asserts that whatever suits them or seems convenient is lawful for them to do as they please. 

Once they have overstepped <note>original: "trascorsa" — meaning to have crossed the boundary of social propriety</note> and cast off shame, anyone who wants to pull them back or correct them labors in vain. For because of such things, not only do women allow themselves to be drawn into the lust <term>lust (original: libidine)</term> of men, but they themselves provoke and draw men to it. In addition, this same Sempronia, having gone astray in this way, was most greedy for gold and desirous of acquiring it through every most dishonest act and mo... <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "modo" (way/manner).</note>

# * On Sempronia of Rome

...quence; and so she had no measure in her spending: and thus beyond measure she was greedy and prodigal—a most wicked vice and an evil. In a woman, the desire for gold <note>original: "loro," a contraction of "l'oro" (gold)</note> is a manifest testimony of a corrupt and vitiated breast. Much more detestable is her prodigality: which, when it enters the feminine mind—to which nature has instilled tenacity and thrift <term>thrift (original: avaritia, here used in the positive sense of household management/frugality)</term>—no hope can ever be held for her regarding her honesty or her possessions. For they are no sooner acquired than they are led into misery along with her lost honor. To a woman belongs thrift: the guarding and preserving of an orderly household, and that which is acquired and given into her keeping by her husband in good faith. Just as greed and the desire for gold in an inordinate woman are to be detested and blamed, so is orderly and moderate thrift to be praised and commended. For just and moderate thrift is a true and noble helper, an increase of wealth, the health and salvation of the household, testimony of an intact mind, rest and solace from labors, and the true foundation of a splendid end. 

But so that we may conclude all the vices of Sempronia in a single deed: while the mind of that most dangerous and seditious man, Lucius Sulla Catiline <note>The author appears to conflate Lucius Cornelius Sulla and Lucius Sergius Catiline, or refers to Catiline's origins among the Sullan faction</note>, was raging and burning, seeking and deliberating with wicked counsels and a great number of conspirators for the perpetual ruin of the Roman Republic; this most corrupt woman—with even greater force, seeking to acquire wider joy in her lusts and lewdness, and desiring that thing which would have been horrid and abominable even to the most deadly of men—joined the conspirators. Whence her house was always open to those wicked counse[ls] <note>The page ends with the catchword "conſe," likely completing "consegli" (counsels)</note>

## Of the wives of the Cimbri

...as much as was possible for them: all in agreement as one, they requested that they might be joined with the [Vestal] virgins in the city of Rome. Which thing—although it seemed a most honorable <note>original: "hōeſtiſſima"; implying a request to serve as sacred virgins to preserve their chastity</note> request and a testimony of a sincere mind—they were unable to obtain or secure. For it is said that the Romans responded that it belongs not to the conquered, but to the victors, to ordain and establish the terms of peace and agreement.

There followed an obstinate perseverance in their vow: and they transported themselves into a most cruel and most marvelous spectacle <note>original: "scō," likely short for "scontro" or "spectaculo"</note> of liberty, to save their freedom and chastity <note>original: "caſtitude"</note> by whatever means was possible for them. And having killed their little children by striking them against the ground and dashing out their brains, they all killed themselves by various ways of death with cords, bridles, and halters. 

Others, seeing the strength of their men broken, would have gone to meet the victors with disheveled hair, weeping, crying out, and asking for mercy with most humble prayers. And what is even worse, many, with flatteries, embraces, and every other impudent act, would have asked and sought the safety of their worldly goods <term>massaritie|Household possessions or portable property</term>—not only of their lives or returning to their fathers—having no thought for feminine honor <note>original: "honeſta"</note>; or they would have allowed themselves to be taken and led away like sheep.

But the Cimbrian women preserved themselves in a better fortune with a constant breast and soul. And they did not endure, nor did they consent to wanting to stain the glory of the majesty of their people for fear of death. But when they fled from shameful slavery with the noose of honor <note>original: "lacio de lhoneſta"; a play on words referring to both the literal noose used for suicide and the figurative "bond" of virtue</note>, they demonstrated that their husbands were surpassed and conquered not by force, but by a sin of Fortune; and having been despised for some years, that they could be...

### Of Julia, daughter of Gaius Caesar

In times past, Gaius Caesar—the fifth King of the Romans <note>Historical note: Caesar was never technically "King" (Rex), but rather Dictator. The text may be conflating his status or using "King" in a general sense of a supreme ruler.</note> and a man notable above all others for the glory of his battles, his triumphs, and his <term>dictatorship</term> <note>A Roman office of absolute power granted during times of crisis</note>—married his daughter to the great Pompey, who was at that time the most illustrious man in Rome. This Pompey, by defeating kings, deposing them, and making new ones, and by subduing foreign nations and clearing the sea of pirates, obtained the favor of the Roman people above all others. Acquiring tribute from the kings of the whole world, he wearied not only the earth, but the very heavens. This Pompey loved that illustrious woman [Julia]—though she was but a young girl and he was of a more advanced age—so ardently that it led to her death. 

For when Pompey was in the company of the sacrificial victims to offer the sacrifice—that is, the <term>hostia</term> <note>The animal offered to the gods</note>—the victim, which struggled because of the wound it had received, thoroughly soaked the said Pompey in blood. Stripping off his blood-stained garments, he sent them home by a servant to have other clothes brought to him. It happened that the first person the servant encountered was Julia, who was then pregnant. Seeing her husband's bloody clothes and without asking the cause, she imagined he had been wounded and killed. From the sudden, overwhelming grief she felt, she fell upon her left side, closed her eyes, clenched her fists, and died. Certainly, this was not only a misfortune and hardship for her husband and the citizens of Rome, but a most great injury to the entire world <note>The death of Julia, who was the bond between Caesar and Pompey, is historically cited as a primary cause of the civil war that followed.</note>.

## Of Portia, Daughter of Cato of Utica

**P**ortia was the daughter of that Marcus Cato <note>Known as Cato the Younger (95–46 BCE), a Roman statesman famous for his stoicism and stubborn resistance to Julius Caesar.</note> who—after leading the remaining remnants of Pompey’s armies through the burning wilderness of Libya in Africa—was unable to endure the victory of Caesar and killed himself in Utica <note>Cato committed suicide in the city of Utica to avoid living under Caesar's rule, earning him the title "Uticensis" (of Utica).</note>. Truly, this noble woman was seen to be in no way inferior to the strength and perseverance of her father. 

Setting aside all the most famous deeds of her virtue: while her father was still alive, she was married to Decimus Brutus <note>The text reads "Detio Bruto," likely a common Renaissance confusion between Decimus Brutus and Marcus Junius Brutus, the famous conspirator who was Portia’s husband.</note>. She loved him so completely and chastely that every other concern of hers was lesser than her love for him. In critical moments and opportune occasions, she was able to conceal or suppress the flames <note>original: "fiae," likely a variation of "fiamme"</note> of her most honorable and strong love within her strong and chaste breast. These virtues, because they rise in perpetual praise, offer themselves to further extend her fame. 

The pestilential tumult of the civil wars was already everywhere, and the knights and friends of Pompey were being oppressed by Caesar. It was then—when that <term>perpetual dictator</term> <note>A title Caesar took in 44 BCE, signaling the end of the Roman Republic's democratic norms</note> began aspiring to the kingdom, as was clearly seen—that the healthiest part of his Senate conspired against him. Among these conspirators was this Brutus. He, being aware and certain of the integrity of his wife, revealed to her the secret of the planned deed <insert>the assassination of Caesar</insert>...

# Of Portia, Daughter of Cato of Utica

...to act: the night before the day Caesar was destined to be killed, as Brutus was leaving their bedroom, Portia took a razor—as if to trim her nails—and, pretending it happened by accident, she intentionally wounded herself. Her handmaidens <note>original: "ancille," a term for female domestic servants or slaves</note> who were nearby, seeing the blood, quickly called Brutus back. Being scolded by Brutus for trying to perform the barber’s task, Portia said nothing in the presence of the handmaidens. But once they had departed and she was left alone with Brutus, she said to him: "What you think is a foolish and simple thing. For what I have done is only to test with what hand I might kill myself with a knife, and whether I can endure death, if the enterprise you have begun should turn out poorly for you."

O author of inexhaustible vigor, and happy man to have such a woman for a companion! After Caesar was killed, they survived the first wave of fury, but not without suffering. Because everything turned out contrary to their expectations and estimates: though the Senate initially deemed them not guilty, the people of Rome pursued the killers as <term>parricides</term> <note>In Roman culture, the assassination of a political "father" of the country was likened to the killing of one's own father, the most heinous of crimes.</note>. In the end, they all met a tragic end. 

But Brutus and Cassius, having gone to the East and gathered a great multitude of people against Octavian and Antony (the heirs of Caesar who pursued them with massive armies), were defeated at **Philippi** <note>The Battle of Philippi in 42 BCE was the final battle between the forces of Mark Antony and Octavian and the leaders of Caesar's assassination.</note>. The forces of Brutus and Cassius were scattered, and the said Brutus was killed. When Portia learned of this, believing she could never again experience joy, she immediately returned to her former resolution. Not having a weapon ready at hand to satisfy her willful mind’s desire, she turned to burning coals; these she took and... <note>Ancient sources, including Plutarch and Valerius Maximus, report that Portia committed suicide by swallowing hot coals after being deprived of weapons.</note>

### Of Curia, wife of Quintus Lucretius

**Concerning Curia, wife of Quintus Lucretius: who, finding her husband Lucretius involved in a certain conspiracy against the Roman people, kept him hidden in her house beyond what anyone would believe, using most clever methods, as will clearly appear below.**

Curia was a Roman woman, and if we give credit to her name, she was of the lineage of the **Curiones** <note>An ancient Roman family of the Scribonia gens, often associated with political leadership.</note>. She was a woman of marvelous constancy and of the most upright faith, a splendid ornament of antiquity. For in that whirlwind and turmoil of affairs, through which—by the command of the **three men** <term>Triumvirs</term> <note>Specifically the Second Triumvirate of Octavian, Mark Antony, and Lepidus, who ruled Rome after Caesar's death.</note>—the new tables of the **proscribed** <note>The "proscripti" were individuals declared outlaws whose property was confiscated and who could be killed for a reward.</note> were posted, Quintus Lucretius, having been found proscribed along with many others...

## Of Curia, Wife of Quintus Lucretius

...to <note>The text begins mid-word, completing the term "proscripto" (proscribed), referring to those legally hunted by the Roman state.</note>: and while everyone else was leaving in swift and sudden flight, abandoning their own fatherland—hardly finding a safe place among the dens of wild beasts, the solitude of mountains and woods, or among the adversaries and enemies of the Roman name—Lucretius alone, following the advice of his lady Curia, hid himself intrepidly and without fear within the rooms of his own domestic house. 

And with such industry, such sagacity, such caution, and such integrity of faith did she act, that certainly except for one **maidservant** <term>ancilla</term> who knew of it, no one else in his own house ever imagined or thought he was there. How many times can we believe this woman went out in public with garments of mourning and sadness, using a deception full of tears—with her hair disheveled and in disarray—behaving almost like a person out of her mind? With sighs and sorrowful, piteous actions, she wandered the streets and squares. 

And coming with a trembling and broken voice into the sight of the people to honor the gods, she would ask the friends she encountered if they had seen her Lucretius, and if they knew if he was alive, or into what country he might have fled. With what companions? With what hope or hardship? She said with the greatest effort that she desired to have been his companion in his flight. 

And so she did many things, such as unhappy women are accustomed to do; we must also consider by what actions and methods she kept the privy maidservant <term>ancilla</term> firm in her resolve, and filled her soul with pity. On the other hand, with what consolation must we estimate she comforted the hope of her trembling and fearful husband? She drew him back from sadness to joy; and while others were suffering through that same **pestilence** <note>original: "pestilentia"; here referring to the political purge and the danger of being hunted.</note> among harsh places, amid the greatest suspicions, heat and cold, and on sea and on land, and in...

### Of Hortensia, daughter of Quintus Hortensius

### Of Hortensia, daughter of Quintus Hortensius: she was such a perfect orator—following in the footsteps of her father—that through her eloquence <note>original: *facundia*, meaning fluency and persuasive skill</note> she took up a burden in defense of the women of Rome, and succeeded before the Roman officials <note>Referring to the Triumvirs who governed Rome at the time</note>.

<margin>Hortensia, daughter of the distinguished orator Quintus Hortensius, worthy of praise, ought to be elevated and exalted; for not only with a lively spirit did she maintain her father's eloquence, but she also observed that vigor of speech which the situation required.</margin> ...which very often is found lacking, even among most learned men. # Of Hortensia, daughter of Quintus Hortensius

...was required to pay money <note>Referring to the heavy tax imposed on the 1,400 wealthiest Roman women to fund the war against Caesar's assassins</note>. And as no man could be found who had the courage to offer help in this matter, only Hortensia had the courage to defend the cause of the women before the three men <term>tre huomini</term> <note>The "Three Men" refers to the Second Triumvirate: Octavian, Mark Antony, and Lepidus</note> with a most constant spirit. She proceeded to speak, and she continued with unusual eloquence <note>original: *facundia*</note> to the great admiration of those listening, who believed that Hortensia had changed her sex and that her father had been resurrected <note>Her father, Quintus Hortensius, was a famous rival to the orator Cicero; the crowd felt his talent had returned to life in her</note>. And certainly, this noble work was not taken up by Hortensia in vain; for just as no part of her speech was lacking through any defect of broken oratory or for lack of praiseworthy demonstration of her reasoning, so nothing of what was desired and requested was denied by the three men. Instead, it was freely granted by them that the greater part of the imposed money should be removed and struck off. They judged that while a holy silence <note>original: *sanctiturnita*, a virtue of quiet modesty</note> ought to be praised in public under the matron’s robe <term>stola matronale</term> <note>The *stola* was the traditional garment of a Roman wife, symbolizing her honor and status</note>, noble eloquence is nonetheless elevated when the opportunity demands its display. By this deed, and with the greatest brilliance from Hortensia, the remaining portion—which was a small matter for the matrons—was exalted and settled. What shall I say I have seen, that the spirit of such ancient lineage breathed into the woman Hortensia, except that she deservedly achieved and was recognized by the most illustrious name of Hortensius?

## Of Sulpitia, wife of Lentulus

**S**ulpitia, wife of Lentulus Cruscellio <note>Lentulus Cruscellio was a Roman politician and son of a consul; he was marked for death by the Triumvirs</note>, gained eternal fame through an almost equal benevolence <note>original: *egual beniuolentia*, referring to her devotion being as great as her noble status</note>. For when Lentulus had been proscribed <term>proscribed</term> <note>Proscription was a public decree that declared a person an outlaw, meaning they could be killed for a reward and their property seized</note> during the same storm <note>A metaphor for the political chaos of the Second Triumvirate</note> mentioned above, he fled to Sicily in a sudden escape, and there he lived in the greatest misery. Sulpitia, having been made certain of this fact, decided to share in all his hardship and pain. And judging it to be an indecent and unlawful thing not to share with her husband both his joyful honors and his unhappy fortune; and as she could not fulfill her purpose unless she went where he was; and because she was secretly and with the utmost diligence guarded by her mother, Julia, so that she would not follow her husband into exile; but she, choosing her moment and dressed in the clothes of a servant <note>original: *seruili uestimētī*</note>, having deceived her mother and the guards, and accompanied only by two handmaidens and as many servants, this splendid woman followed him into exile. Although she could have remained safely at home according to the law of the proscription and renounced her unhappy husband with a new marriage, this illustrious woman had no fear of entering the storms of the sea, nor of searching through the rugged mountains of Italy for the uncertain tracks of her beloved husband; but

# Of Sulpitia, wife of Lentulus

Through the strangest and unknown regions she went, by day and by
night: through the rains, the winds, the heat, and the cold; she never rested
until she joined with him. This most honorable and
valiant woman judged it much more honorable for her to follow a life of
danger and hardship <note>original: *aspnabile uita*, likely meaning a life to be despised or full of hardships</note>, and to be with her husband, who was tossed by
fortune and tribulation, than to rest in her absence in her
homeland with whatever delights. And truly, such a noble judgment,
deliberation, and high and whole mind is more manly than feminine;
for one ought not to always shine with gold and gems; not
always indulge in finery or ornament; not always seeking
ease and pleasures; fleeing the sun in the summer and the rains in the
winter; not always huddling by the hearth and in beds; not always sparing
oneself and fleeing labors and discomforts; but honorable
and illustrious women ought—if the situation grants it or necessity
requires it—to submit themselves to harsh labors with their husbands:
to suffer exiles, inconveniences, and with a strong and fierce spirit to bear dangers. And this is the high
and venerable military service <term>military service of wives</term> <note>A common humanist metaphor comparing a wife's loyalty during political strife to a soldier's duty on the battlefield</note> of wives; these are their
righteous battles; these are the victories and the triumphs of those women:
namely, to have overcome the softness of foul lust, and ease, and domestic
idleness with constant honor; and to have modestly conquered,
and entirely subdued and mortified these things in themselves.
And here is acquired for them good and beautiful fame and glory; let all
those be ashamed, then, who with all their efforts follow the shadow of
vain happiness; and even more those who, for the common good of marriage,
fear seasickness <note>original: *il uomito*, literally "the vomit," referring here to the physical toll of the sea voyage to Sicily</note> and are weary at every little discomfort; they hold
foreign lands and strange nations in horror; they turn pale at the roaring of the

# Of Cornificia the Poetess

Concerning Cornificia the poetess and sister of Cornificius, a very great poet. She lived in the time of Octavian <note>Augustus Caesar, ruled 27 BC – 14 AD</note>: and Saint Jerome commends her letters and verses, and he himself testifies to it.

<margin>
Whether Cornificia was a Roman woman or a foreigner, I have not read. But truly, by the testimony of the ancients, she was most worthy of memory. Because during the reign of Octavian, she shone with such poetic splendor that she seemed nourished not by Italian milk 
</margin>

but from the Castalian spring <note>A spring on Mount Parnassus sacred to the Muses; to drink from it was said to grant poetic inspiration</note>. And she was equal in glory to her brother Cornificius, a most great poet of his time. Nor was she merely content to have known and excelled through her splendid faculty—applying herself to the sacred scriptures and sciences, and writing Heliconian verse <term>Heliconian verse</term> <note>Poetry written in the tradition of Mount Helicon, the home of the Muses; likely referring to hexameter</note>—but very many times she set her most learned hands to the pen, casting aside the distaff <note>The *rocca* or distaff was the primary symbol of female domestic labor; "casting it aside" represents a woman's rejection of household chores in favor of intellectual pursuits</note>. She wrote many, and many more notable epigrams mentioned above, which in the time of the most holy man Jerome, as he himself testifies, were held in dear and high esteem. O womanly honor and ornament, to have neglected feminine things and the...

Certainly not a common, low, or ordinary name, but that most famous and excellent one which is found in only a few men.

surpassed all other women; indeed she was judged to be a celestial image rather than a mortal one. In this belief, the testimony of Mark Antony, one of the Triumvirs <note>original: *a tre huomini* — literally "of the three men," referring to the Second Triumvirate that ruled Rome</note>, was not lacking. For since Mariamne had a brother from the same parents named Aristobulus, who was her equal in age and beauty, their mother Alexandra—their father Aristobulus having already died—desired and sought with great joy and effort that the office of the High Priesthood <note>original: *principato del sacerdotio*</note> be given to him by King Herod, Mariamne’s husband. And through the persuasion and advice of Dellius <note>original: *Gelio* — Quintus Dellius, a political opportunist and friend of Mark Antony</note>, her friend, she sent to Egypt—as some say—the likenesses of both children painted on a panel by a most excellent painter, to be given to Antony the Triumvir. Antony was a man of extreme luxury, and the goal was to excite his lust and consequently draw him to her desire. When Antony saw these figures, he remained in long admiration of them first within himself. Afterward, it is claimed that Antony said the two children in the painting, in terms of beauty, were certainly the children of a god, adding with an oath that he had never seen not only faces more beautiful, but even similar ones, by a long margin. I say then: that Mariamne was endowed not only with a marvelous splendor of beauty, but with a most lofty and strong spirit. When she reached marriageable age, she was joined in marriage—to her greatest misfortune and tragedy—to that ill-fated man, Herod, son of Antipater <note>Herod the Great, whose father was Antipater the Idumaean</note>, King of the Jews. Because he loved her beyond measure for her beauty, and gloried in seeing himself as the sole possessor of such divine beauty in the world, he immediately fell into fear and jealousy; he thought that no one could ever equal him in possessing her.

# Of Mariamne, Queen of the Jews

was shown not only during his life, but even in his death. And for this reason he gave an order: first when he was called by Antony <note>Mark Antony, the Roman Triumvir</note> to Egypt to excuse himself for the death he had caused of Aristobulus <note>Aristobulus III, the last Hasmonean High Priest and Mariamne's brother</note>, the brother of his lady; and later, after Antony was dead, when he had to go to Octavian <note>The future Emperor Augustus</note> to clear himself of the infamy he held for having given aid to Antony against Octavian. He left Cypros <note>original: *Cyprinea* — Herod's mother</note> and other of his most trusted friends with instructions that if by any chance death should befall him, as soon as they learned of it, they should kill Mariamne, so that no one after him might ever boast of possessing her. O ridiculous folly! And most hard-hearted King: to trouble himself over the uncertainty of another and to afflict himself over a thing not yet done, feeling envy for it as if it were already a fact.

Although he arranged this in secret, it nevertheless reached the ears of Mariamne; and since she had already conceived a very great hatred toward Herod because of the death of her brother, and knowing herself not to be loved by him except for the use of her physical abundance—that is, for his lust—and having been twice wrongly accused by him, she was transported into such anger and hatred against him. This was despite the fact that she had borne him two sons, Alexander and Aristobulus. She not only refused to join herself with him in the marriage bed, but despised him, saying that he was not worthy of her royal lineage <note>Mariamne belonged to the Hasmonean dynasty, the traditional Jewish royalty, whereas Herod was an Idumaean "outsider"</note> and that he was a stranger and a barbarian, and not a Jew, but a man ignoble, wicked, and inhuman; and far worse than a cruel and most evil wild beast.

Although Herod bore these things with great displeasure, he did not yet dare to use cruelty against her. But as matters proceeded from bad to worse, as it pleases some to say, through the schemes of Cypros, the mother of Herod, and of Salome <note>original: *Salamonia*</note>, his sister—toward whom Mariamne felt a most heavy resentment—it was arranged that by means of a defiled...

# Of Mariamne the Queen

<note>This text continues from the previous page's account of King Herod's paranoid jealousy.</note>

...by a servant, Mariamne was accused of having wanted to give Herod poison through him. Or, as others would have it, that after she had conceived a great hatred against Herod, she sent her own most precious portrait <note>original: *effigie* — a likeness or painting</note> to Antony <note>Mark Antony, the Roman Triumvir</note> by her own initiative to provoke him to hate her husband. Herod, believing these things and inflamed with an extraordinary fury, first lamented the matter with his friends, and then, with the consent of all, he condemned her to death as a person seeking the destruction of his majesty. 

When Mariamne learned of this, she stirred and awakened within herself such a lofty and noble spirit that, despising death and not changing her expression at all, she never offered any pleas for mercy. With a most joyful countenance, as if in a lawful victory march <note>original: *triumpho* — a reference to a Roman triumph</note>, she went toward her death, receiving it as a thing desired above all others. Wherefore, through such unshaken and firm security and constancy, she not only thwarted the envy of that most wicked and cruel King, but she increased the fame of her name for many more centuries of years than Herod could have granted months to her mortal life, even if he had been moved to mercy by her prayers and pious tears.

### Of Cleopatra, Queen of the Egyptians:
Who, through ingenuity and fraud, and by causing the death of her brother, obtained the title of Queen. She possessed no true nobility <note>original: *clarità* — meaning fame or moral brightness</note> within herself, except for that of her royal lineage and her physical beauty; and she was, beyond all measure, given to luxury and lust <note>original: *luxurioſa*</note>.

# Of Cleopatra, Queen of the Egyptians

**C**leopatra <gloss>The ornate initial 'C' contains a woodcut of a bird, possibly a swan or eagle</gloss>, an Egyptian woman who became a legend to the whole world: through many ancestors, she traced her origin back to the Macedonian Ptolemies, the Kings of Egypt. She was descended from the son of Lagus <note>Ptolemy I Soter, the founder of the dynasty</note> and from Ptolemy Dionysus Auletes <note>Ptolemy XII, known as the "Flute-Player"</note>; or, as some prefer, she was the daughter of King Hymenaeus. 

And she came to rule the said kingdom through wickedness. She did not shine, nor is she noted for any true renown <note>original: *clarita* — the "fame" or "brightness" associated with the virtuous women in Boccaccio's work</note>, except for her lineage and her physical beauty; rather, she was known for her greed and lust, for which things she was notorious throughout the world. Because, as some believe—so that we may begin with the origin of her rule: Dionysus Auletes (or Hymenaeus), a great friend of the Roman people, at the time of the first consulate of Julius Caesar, as he approached death, left written in his will <note>original: *tauola* — literally 'table' or 'tablet'</note> that his eldest and first-born son—whom some name Lusania—should take Cleopatra as his wife (she being older than any of his other daughters) and that they should reign after his death. This was a most shameful and foul thing. For although it is forbidden only for mothers to take their sons, and fathers to take their daughters, among the Egyptians it was the custom and permitted <note>Boccaccio refers here to the Ptolemaic practice of sibling marriage, which the author views with moral disdain</note>. With <meta>catchword "Co", likely the beginning of "Costei" (She) or "Come" (As)</meta>

# Of Cleopatra, Queen of the Egyptians

...it followed: that Cleopatra had her brother as her husband. And truly, being inflamed with the desire to possess the kingdom alone—as it seems to many—her brother and husband, at the age of fifteen years, was killed by her by poison; and thus she alone possessed the kingdom. 

But at that time, it is affirmed that the great Pompey <note>Pompey the Great, the Roman general and rival of Julius Caesar</note>, having already occupied all of Asia by force of arms, went into Egypt. Finding there the younger brother of the deceased brother and husband of Cleopatra, also named Ptolemy, he put him in possession of the kingdom of Egypt; at which thing Cleopatra, being indignant, took up arms against him. 

And while things stood thus, the battle between Pompey and Caesar followed, in which Pompey was defeated. Fleeing to Egypt as if to a safer place, he was killed by the ministers of Ptolemy through extreme treachery, to great misfortune. And then Caesar, going into Egypt after his victory, found that they were making war between themselves. 

Having ordered that they come before him to state the cause of the war, that cunning woman <note>original: *malitiosa femina* — Boccaccio uses this term to emphasize her manipulative nature</note>, adorned in royal garments in attire beyond measure rich and lovely, trusted in her beauty and her sweet speech with perfect cunning. With these things, having come before the conqueror of the world <note>Julius Caesar</note>, she thought to turn him to her desire; and so it happened for her. 

Sleeping many times with him in Alexandria, she conceived by him a son, whom she named with the name of the father; and he was called by her Caesarion. Finally, the young Ptolemy, released by Caesar at the persuasion of his people and pushed by them, turned his arms and all his force against his liberator Caesar; who forestalled him as he fled, and having entered into a flat boat <note>original: *piacta naue* — likely a barge or river galley used on the Nile</note> to escape and flee. 

Thus, once things were pacified and the surrender of the Alexandrians was made, and as Caesar had to go against Pharnaces <note>The text ends "contra forna," referring to Pharnaces II of Pontus, whom Caesar defeated at the Battle of Zela</note>...

## Of Cleopatra, Queen of the Egyptians.

...[after defeating] the King of Pontus <note>Pharnaces II, whom Caesar defeated at the Battle of Zela</note>: who had been in the favor of Pompey: almost as if to give Cleopatra the reward for her nights <note>A reference to her sexual relationship with Caesar</note>: and also because she had been made faithful to him: he gave her the kingdom of Egypt, which she desired above all other things: taking away with him her sister Arsinoe <note>original: *Ansiōia*</note>: so that she could in no way plot against him.

And so, having acquired the realm, Cleopatra followed her own desires and dishonest lusts: becoming known among the Eastern Kings as a manifest and notorious harlot <note>original: *meretrice*</note>, greedy beyond measure for gold and jewels. Not only did she strip all those who had dealings with her of these things by her subtle art: but it is clear from more ancient writers that she even stripped the temples and sacred places, and left them empty.

And therefore, Caesar being already dead, and Brutus and Cassius killed by Octavian and Antony, she had to go into Syria. There she went to meet him <note>Mark Antony</note> to seize the lustful man with her art: and thus she took and bound him in her love in such a way that he was disposed to her every will. And the wretched woman led him to act so that—just as she had caused her brother to die by poison—to remove every suspicion regarding her rule, she brought it about by the arm of Antony that her sister Arsinoe <note>original: *Anfionia*</note> was killed in the temple of Diana, where the unhappy girl had fled only for her safety.

And this was the reward and private gift for her adultery committed with her new lover. And having learned the character of Antony, she was not ashamed to ask him for the kingdoms of Syria and Arabia. But it appearing to Antony that the demand was too excessive, he gave her some portions of them, adding all the cities that are situated on the coast of Syria between the river Lycus <note>original: *Vcorio*</note>—or Eleutherus—and Egypt, while withholding for himself the cities of Sidon and Tyre; which cities...

But Herod, recognizing and perceiving her malice, not only abstained [from her] out of reverence for Antony, but to free Antony from the infamy he bore from such an incestuous woman, he was even disposed to kill her by the sword, had it not been that his friends advised him against it. 

Cleopatra, however, finding herself deceived in her expectations, and not wishing to show that she had been slighted, obtained from Herod the revenues of Jericho. # Of Cleopatra, Queen of the Egyptians

...he joined with her and took her as his wife. And so that I do not recount the Arabian ointments and the fragrant perfumes of Sheba <note>Saba (Sheba) was a kingdom in southern Arabia, famous in antiquity for producing frankincense and myrrh.</note>, [let it be said] that according to Antony, diverse, rich, and strange dishes were prepared for Cleopatra continuously in her meals. Antony, almost as if to magnify and praise Cleopatra for such grand banquets, asked what more could be done than what she already did in her daily dinners. To this, the wanton woman replied that in a single dinner, if she wished, she could consume [the value of] ten million sesterces <note>The Italian text "sei volte il terzo" is likely a corrupted or idiosyncratic reference to the sum mentioned by Pliny the Elder: 10,000,000 sesterces.</note>. Antony, judging this to be impossible for her to do and desiring to see the proof, made a wager with her and placed a stake, not expecting her to win. Lucius Plancus <note>Lucius Munatius Plancus (c. 87 BC – 15 BC), a prominent Roman senator and diplomat who served as the judge of the wager.</note> was made the judge of the matter.

On the following day, the meal had not exceeded the usual custom of the ordered dishes, and Antony was already mocking the promise she had made. Cleopatra then commanded the servants to bring the second course. Having been instructed and forewarned, they brought nothing else but a vessel of very strong vinegar. When Cleopatra saw it, she immediately took from one of her ears a piece of jewelry—which was of inestimable value, worn by her as an ornament according to the custom of the Orientals. Having placed it in the vinegar, it was immediately dissolved, and she began to drink it.

As she reached her hand toward the other [pearl] which she wore in her other ear to do the same, Lucius Plancus stepped forward and declared that Antony was defeated. Thus the second <term>union<gloss>From the Latin 'unio,' referring to a large, singular, and uniquely perfect pearl of immense value.</gloss></term> was saved by the Queen's victory. This pearl was later divided and split in half and taken to Rome; it was placed in the Pantheon on the ears of the statue of Venus <note>The Roman goddess of love and beauty. Cleopatra frequently styled herself as the living embodiment of Aphrodite or Isis.</note>, serving as a testament for a very long time to those who looked upon it of the...

## Of Cleopatra, Queen of the Egyptians

...half of Cleopatra's dinner. And furthermore, beyond other things: because the desire for the realm and the greed of Cleopatra increased more from day to day, so that she might gather everything into one for the inflamed Antony. Perhaps when he rose from that grand dinner, she asked him for the Roman Empire, as if granting it were in his hands. Antony, valuing himself and his forces more than those of the Romans, unwisely promised it to her and gave his word to give it to her. O good God, how truly great was the audacity of the woman making the request, and no less was the folly of the man making the promise. O generous man—nay, rather a madman—alienating an empire hardly acquired over so many centuries, with such difficulty, with so many excellent works, with so many battles and shedding of blood, and the deaths of so many most valiant commanders <term>imperadori</term> <note>While "imperadori" later meant Emperors, in this context it refers to the Roman Republican "imperator," a title for a successful military commander.</note>, kings, and most notable men, and of so many peoples; in fact, and without counsel, he promised to give it to the petitioning woman, no differently than if it had been the lordship of a tiny little cottage. 

But already, through the repudiation of Octavia <note>Octavia the Younger, the sister of Octavian (Augustus) and the legal wife of Mark Antony. Antony’s abandonment of her for Cleopatra was a primary cause of the civil war.</note>, there was born not only disdain but hatred, leading to the generation of battle between Octavian and Antony. Because of which it followed that the forces of the peoples were gathered on one side and the other to do battle. Cleopatra, together with Antony, armed and veiled in purple and a veil of gold, like an empress <term>imperadrigie</term>, with a multitude of horses, went into Epirus <note>A region in northwestern Greece; the campaign leading to the Battle of Actium took place along this coast.</note>. Where, once the land battle had begun, and Antony being defeated therein and driven from the field, he retreated with his men to the ships in Antioch <note>original: "Anthiochia". While the text says Antioch, the historical retreat to the ships for a naval engagement occurred at Actium.</note> to test the battle of the sea.

### Of Cleopatra, Queen of the Egyptians

[Octa]vian with Agrippa, his son-in-law <note>Marcus Vipsanius Agrippa, Octavian’s most trusted general. He later married Octavian's daughter, Julia.</note>: with a very great fleet: he comes to meet them with marvelous daring: fighting together, both one and the other side valorously. Mars <note>The Roman god of war, used here metaphorically to describe the shifting tides of battle.</note> suspended victory for some time: but with the forces of Octavian prevailing: and the Antonians appearing defeated, before all others the insolent Cleopatra, with the gilded ship in which she herself was, with sixty other ships, began to flee. Antony, seeing this: and seeing his standards struck down <note>original: "abatere" — meaning the military banners were falling or being lowered in defeat.</note> followed her in flight. 

And having returned to Egypt: and having sent (as the Romans had) their children to the Red Sea: they used their forces in vain for the defense of their realms. For the victorious Octavian, following them with continuous battles, annihilated and reduced to nothing all their state and power. And Antony, too late, asked for a peace agreement, and not being able to have it: as many affirm: falling into despair: he entered the royal tomb constructed for him: and with a knife he killed himself. 

And once Alexandria was taken: Cleopatra, having already tried and tested in vain—with her ancient wits and the art of drawing men into her lust, as she had done with Caesar and Antony—the young Octavian <note>Octavian was famously more resistant to Cleopatra's charms than his predecessors, focusing instead on the political necessity of her capture.</note>: and being unable [to seduce him], indignant and despairing of all safety: having adorned herself in royal robes, she followed her Antony, having first heard that she was to be reserved for the triumph <term>triumpho</term> <note>A "Triumph" was a massive parade in Rome where a victorious general displayed his spoils and famous captives. To Cleopatra, being paraded in chains was a fate worse than death.</note>. 

And having entered the tomb at her husband's side, causing the veins of her arms to be opened: and to the wounds she put the winged serpents <note>original: "pēnati serpēti" (pennati serpenti). In Renaissance lore, asps were sometimes described as "winged" or having small fins.</note>, that is, deaf asps <term>aspidi sordi</term>: they say for certain that they induce death with sleep. In which [sleep] being dissolved: her wickednesses met a harsh end.

# Of Antonia, daughter of Mark Antony the Younger

There are some others who say that Cleopatra died from another kind of death: and they say that Antony feared the outcome of the **Attic battle** <note>original: "bataglia atticha" — a reference to the Battle of Actium (31 BC), the decisive naval conflict between Octavian and the forces of Antony and Cleopatra.</note> regarding the favor of Cleopatra. And he was in the habit of not eating or drinking anything without a **taster** <term>credenza: the ritual practice of having a servant taste food or drink to ensure it was not poisoned</term>. Cleopatra, having learned of this, wished to demonstrate her loyalty toward Antony. She smeared with poison the flowers with which she had decorated the crown placed upon her head the day before. Drawing Antony into a game and joyful sport as their merriment increased, she invited Antony to "drink the crowns" <note>This refers to the ancient custom of plucking flowers from a garland and dropping them into a wine cup before drinking.</note>.

And so, as he was putting the flowers into the cup and was about to drink, Cleopatra stretched out her hand and did not let him drink, saying these words: "Dearest Antony, I am that Cleopatra whom you suspect despite your new and unusual **tastings** <note>original: "pergustatiõe e crdẽza" — referring again to his use of professional food-tasters.</note> and precautions. Therefore, if I wished to, or could endure it, the opportunity and the means are given to me." Therefore, having revealed the trick to him, she sent for a prisoner <note>original: "missela in prigione" — likely referring to a condemned prisoner brought from the dungeon for the experiment.</note> and forced them to drink that which she had forbidden him to drink. And so they say she died; but the first and most widely told version is the common opinion. And it is added: that by the command of Octavian, the **monument** <note>original: "monimẽto" — here meaning the royal tomb or mausoleum.</note> which Antony had begun for Cleopatra was completed, and that they were buried there together.

### Of Antonia, daughter of Mark Antony the Younger, and daughter of Octavia, sister of Octavian

Regarding Antonia, daughter of Mark Antony the Younger and daughter of Octavia, the **sister** <note>original: "sorrochia" — an archaic Italian term for sister.</note> of Octavian, and wife of Drusus, the brother of Tiberius Nero: after the death of her husband, she never wished to remarry. Rather, she maintained such a good and holy life until her death that she surpassed all the other women of that time.

# Of Antonia, daughter of Mark Antony the Younger

**A**ntonia Minor left behind her an eternal example of most illustrious widowhood. And certainly, it is believed she was the daughter of Mark Antony the **Triumvir** <term>triumvir: one of three men—in this case Antony, Octavian, and Lepidus—who shared supreme power in Rome</term> and of Octavia, his wife; and she was called "the Younger" <note>original: "minore"</note> because of another sister named Antonia born before her. And truly she was married to Drusus, the brother of Tiberius Nero and the stepson of Octavian Augustus. She bore him Germanicus and Claudius; then Augustus <note>historical records do not list a son named Augustus for Antonia; this may be a clerical error in the manuscript or a reference to a child who died in infancy</note> and Livilla <note>original: "Linilla," a common typographical variation of Livilla</note>. 

While this Drusus was attending to the campaign of the **Germanic War** <note>The Roman campaigns against Germanic tribes (12–9 BC) led by Drusus until his accidental death</note>, he was—as some judge—put to death by poison through the deeds of Tiberius his brother. After his death, while Antonia was very young and flourishing in her tender age with marvelous loveliness and beauty, it seemed to this most honorable woman that having been married once was enough, and she never wished to marry a second time. 

But the remainder of her entire life, under Livia her mother-in-law, she spent so constantly within the limits of her chamber and the bed of her beloved husband <note>This is a metaphorical expression meaning she remained devoted to his memory and never took another lover</note> that she is celebrated throughout the world; she surpassed the praise of all the women who had lived before her. Truly, among the virtuous women... <note>original: "Cincinate" — likely an allusion to the Roman ideal of domestic virtue associated with the family of Cincinnatus</note>

--- Page 254 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Antonia Minor, daughter of Mark Antony and Octavia. The previous page established her noble lineage and her decision to remain a widow after the death of her husband, Drusus. This page contrasts her unwavering virtue with the scandalous environment of the Roman imperial court and then introduces the tragic story of Agrippina the Elder.</meta>

...among the Fabriciae, Curiae, Lucretiae, and Sulpitiae <note>These names refer to legendary Roman families and women famous for their traditional morality and chastity</note>—most holy and most splendid young women. But even among those of ancient and advanced ages, and among the daughters of Cato, she emerged without any mark of lewdness <note>original: "lasciuiā"</note> and is to be exalted with the highest praise. With what splendors, with what beauty and adornments she is to be honored! 

This young Antonia, of distinguished and marvelous beauty, was the daughter of Mark Antony—a man **beyond measure dissolute**—yet she was raised not in the wilds, nor in the forests, or in solitary places. Rather, she was raised amid the imperial idleness and delights with Julia, the daughter of Octavian; and among the most fervent places of the lust and lewdness of Julia, daughter of Marcus Agrippa; and amid the obscenities of her father Mark Antony, and those later practiced by Tiberius (afterwards the Emperor), that guardian of turmoil and vice <note>original: "tutore de la turbine: & e difecti"</note>. 

Even among so many examples of lewdness and concupiscence, with a most constant soul and a most strong heart, she maintained herself in such whole and **perfect chastity** <term>castimonia: the Roman ideal of sexual purity and moral integrity, especially in a widow</term>. She did this not for a short space of time, nor through the endurance of new marriages, but by following and obeying virtue alone, observing it until her old age <note>original: "senectute"</note> and death. And certainly, I judge nothing worthy of being reduced merely to words; but perhaps something remains for contemplation which truly exceeds the power of writers. Therefore, let it be enough to consider and elevate her with the deserved respect, having left the rest to sacred geniuses <note>The author suggests that Antonia's virtue is so great that it is better contemplated by the mind or left to divine inspiration than described by the pen</note>.

## Of Agrippina, wife of Germanicus: and daughter of Marcus Agrippa: who, seeing that Tiberius had caused her husband to die by poison, and bearing it with endurance, the said Tiberius decided to do her every outrage. She decided not to eat or drink until she died: and so she did.

## Concerning Agrippina, the Wife of Germanicus

Agrippina was the daughter born of Marcus Agrippa and of Julia, the daughter of Octavian Augustus. Although Gaius "Caligula" <note>original: "Gayo gallicola," a corruption of Caligula, the nickname meaning 'Little Boot'</note>, the son of this same Agrippina who was made ruler of the world, disdained the nobility of Agrippina’s <note>Caligula's mother</note> lineage on the side of his grandfather [Agrippa]. He claimed that she [his mother] was descended from Augustus and not from Agrippa, but that she had been fathered by Octavian through incest <term>strupo: here referring to the scandalous (and likely false) claim of an incestuous union between Augustus and his daughter Julia</term> with his daughter Julia. He did this desiring foolishly to be believed more noble as the descendant of a mother conceived so incestuously, rather than from an ignoble [grandfather].

She was married to Germanicus <note>Germanicus Julius Caesar (15 BC – 19 AD), a widely beloved Roman general and heir to the empire</note>, a young man of high virtue, most notable for his age, and of the greatest necessity and utility to the Republic; he was also the adopted son of Tiberius Caesar Augustus. She was then quite [resolute], but much more splendid for having maintained her steadfastness <note>original: "pfidia," likely a transcription error for "fede" or "perseveranza," as the context describes her loyalty</note> against the obstinate purpose of the wicked prince <note>referring to Tiberius</note>. 

After she had born six children to her husband—three sons and three daughters, one of whom was Agrippina <note>Agrippina the Younger</note>, the mother of Nero Caesar—her husband died by poison through the work of Tiberius. Agrippina bore this most heavily, weeping and grieving; in no way could she be consoled for the loss of so celebrated a man and dear husband, and she fell into such...

<meta>This page continues the biography of Agrippina the Elder, picking up after the mention of her children and her life with Germanicus. ## Concerning Agrippina, the Wife of Germanicus

[She aroused] the anger and hatred of Tiberius <note>Tiberius (42 BC – 37 AD) was the second Roman Emperor; his relationship with Agrippina was famously toxic after the suspicious death of her husband, Germanicus</note> in such a way that he began to torment and harass her with diverse insults and slights. While she endured all these things with great impatience, and as they increased every day, it reached the point that—having defamed her with dishonest and false accusations—he had her imprisoned despite her being without fault. 

But the noble woman, knowing that she had been treated unjustly, resolved to end the torments of the cruel prince through death. Not having a ready means for it, she decided within herself with a resolute mind to end her life by hunger, and to never eat again. And she immediately began to abstain entirely from all food. 

This matter was reported to Tiberius. The villainous prince, understanding the end to which her fasting tended, did not want her to escape by such a short path from the tortures <note>original: "stratii," referring to the prolonged agonies or afflictions he intended for her</note> he wished to inflict on her. First, he goaded her with the most severe beatings; then he had food forced down her throat so that she might live. But it was all in vain. For the more he tried to keep the noble woman alive against her will <term>invita: from the Latin for 'unwillingly,' here meaning kept alive against her choice</term> through insults, the more she followed her firm purpose with a stronger spirit. 

And so, dying, she overcame and conquered the wicked prince, demonstrating to him that although he had easily been able to make many die, he could not keep alive a single person who wished to die, even with all the power of his dominion. Through this death, she certainly acquired much glory for herself among her people, and left to Tiberius a no less lasting and much greater infamy.

# Concerning Paulina, a Roman woman

<margin>Paulina, a Roman woman: through a laughably simple act <note>original: "simplicita da ridere," suggesting her naive or gullible nature in the coming story</note>, she attained an almost eternal name. During the reign of Emperor Tiberius Caesar, she was the most excellent and notable among all women of her time for the shapeliness of her body and the beauty of her face. Being thus married, she was regarded by everyone as a most special mirror of renowned **chastity** <term>pudicitia: a Roman virtue representing modesty and sexual virtue, especially in a matron</term>.</margin>

She did not care for, nor was it her study to do anything else, except to please her husband and to worship and revere **Anubis** <note>The original text reads "Danubio," which is likely a typographical error or corruption for Anubis, the Egyptian god of the dead, who features centrally in this historical anecdote</note>, the god of the Egyptians. She held him in such devotion that she did everything—and would have done everything—in his honor to merit his grace. But since beautiful women are loved by young men everywhere—and especially those who take such diligent care of their honest **purity** <term>castimonia: the state of being morally or ritually pure</term>—a young Roman named Mundus, who was very wealthy and of a very great family, fell in love with her beauty beyond measure. Following her with his eyes and with loving and humble gestures, as is the custom of lovers, with prayers...

## Concerning Paulina, a Roman woman

...finding every promise of gifts to be in vain, because all her love was for her husband alone. Seeing and knowing this clearly—that because of the complete constancy of this most honorable lady, every path to his desire was blocked—he turned his wit to malice and fraud.

Paulina was accustomed to visiting the **Temple of Isis** <note>Isis was a major Egyptian goddess whose cult became highly popular in Rome. Her temples were often associated with mystery and ritual</note> almost every day. There, offering continuous sacrifices and sacred items, she revered and adored the god **Anubis** <note>original: "Anube." The jackal-headed Egyptian god of the dead</note> as his most devoted follower.

Noticing this, the young man devised a trick, certain that the priests and ministers of Anubis could greatly assist and favor his desires. He went to the priests and, with entreaties and the greatest of gifts, he drew them to his will and pleasure. It was arranged by them that the oldest and most venerable among them should tell Paulina, when she came to the temple as was her custom, that the god Anubis had appeared to him by night. He was to say the god had commanded him to tell Paulina that she was greatly beloved by him, and that in that very temple where she had worshipped him, he desired and wished for her well-being to have a **meeting** <term>coloquio: literally "conversation," used here as a euphemism for a divine visitation or sexual encounter</term> with her during the night.

Once Paulina had heard this, judging that it happened because of her devotion and sanctity, she felt a pride beyond measure at these words. She believed them to be as true as if she had heard them with her own ears from the god Anubis himself.

And having returned home, she recounted everything to her husband. He, being much more foolish and weak-minded, believed it all to be true and consented that she should go and stay in the temple the following night. Thus, in the sacred temple, nothing else was done, except only

# Concerning Paulina, a Roman woman

The priests sent word to Paulina, knowing the illicit [plan disguised as the] honor of a god. And the earth being already shadowed by the nocturnal darkness, Paulina entered into the appointed place; and after the prayer, everyone went to bed, waiting for **Anubis** <note>The jackal-headed Egyptian god of the afterlife, whom Paulina especially devoted herself to</note>. Once she had fallen asleep, **Mundus** <note>original: "Mundo"</note> came forward, entirely covered in the ornaments of Anubis. Full of the most burning desire, he woke her with the sweetest kisses and embraces; and she was completely astounded.

Mundus commanded her to be certain [and feel safe], for he was Anubis, whom she had worshiped for so long; he said he had descended from heaven because of her prayers and had come for her **embrace** <term>concubiti: a formal or archaic term for sexual union</term>, so that from her a god similar to him might be **born** <note>original: "gñrato," an abbreviation of "generato"</note>. Hearing this, before anything else, Paulina asked if the heavenly beings mingled with mortals.

To which Mundus immediately replied that they did; and he gave her the example of **Jupiter** <note>original: "Gioue"</note>, how in times past he had descended from heaven into the lap of **Danaë** <note>original: "Danes." In Greek mythology, Jupiter visited Danaë as a shower of gold</note>, by whom **Perseus** <note>original: "Perseo"</note> was fathered. Having heard this, Paulina most joyfully consented to the request. Then Mundus entered the bed completely naked, and thus he had the embraces he desired.

And when the night had passed and day was near, at his departure he told Paulina that she had conceived a son, and she departed from the priests. Paulina recounted everything that had happened to her **foolish husband** <note>original: "sioccho marito." The author characterizes her husband's ready belief in the divine encounter as gullibility</note>, who welcomed his wife with a most joyful face and honor, thinking that she was to give birth to a god. There is no doubt that both would have awaited the time of the birth, were it not that the overly-passionate young man revealed the deception **cautiously** <note>original: "cautamente." Mundus likely revealed the trick with a sense of cleverness or pointed mockery three days later</note>. And truly, it seemed to Mundus that as Paulina was in his embraces...

<meta>This page concludes the story of Paulina’s deception by Decius Mundus and transitions to the biography of Agrippina the Younger. The previous page detailed Mundus’s trick of impersonating the god Anubis in the temple to sleep with the devout Paulina.</meta>

[The night] had passed with the greatest pleasures. Mundus believed that by revealing to her how he had overcome her **chastity** <term>pudicitia: a Roman virtue representing a woman's sexual honor and modesty</term> through his trickery, she would be more inclined toward him and consequently easier to persuade, allowing them to meet for other nights in their desired embraces.

As Paulina was walking to the temple, he stepped out in front of her and said in a low, soft voice: “I shall say that you are blessed, Paulina, for you have conceived by the god Anubis.” But something much more significant followed those words than Mundus expected; for Paulina, astonished beyond measure, recalled and turned over in her mind many things said and done during the night, and she recognized the fraud. Greatly distressed, she confessed to her husband and revealed the entire matter. Consequently, her husband immediately went to complain with great **lamentation** <note>original: "querimonía"</note> to **Tiberius Caesar** <note>The second Roman Emperor, known for his strict and sometimes grim administration of justice</note>. 

The Emperor, having found the fraud to be clearly proven, commanded that all the priests of the temple be put to death. Mundus, who had fled, was banished from the country. And the simple and deceived Paulina became a cautionary tale among the Roman people; she became more famous for her simplicity and for the fraud and deception of Mundus than she ever was for her devotion to Anubis or for her carefully guarded **chastity** <note>original: "castimonia"</note>.

She even lay with him <note>A reference to the rumors of incest recorded by ancient historians like Tacitus and Suetonius</note>, and later Nero had her killed, as is contained in the text.

## Concerning Agrippina, mother of Nero

...she acquired for herself and for her son the celebrated Prince **Claudius** <note>Emperor Claudius, who reigned from 41 to 54 AD</note>, despite the fact that he was the brother of Germanicus, her father. On account of her miraculous beauty, and with the freedman Callistus favoring her over **Lollia Paulina** <note>original: "Lelia Paulina," a wealthy noblewoman who was a rival for Claudius's hand</note>, and being helpful against **Aelia Paetina** <note>original: "Elia poetina," Claudius's former second wife</note>—while Narcissus favored another—through the efforts of Pallas, she drew Claudius into a very great desire for marriage with her. 

But through the petitions of the suborned **Vitellius** <note>Lucius Vitellius the Elder, a powerful politician who manipulated the legal system to favor the marriage</note>, it was so arranged that Claudius was seemingly compelled by the pleas of the Senators to do exactly what he desired. A decree was then passed by the Senate making it lawful for **uncles** <note>original: "zyi"</note> to take their **nieces** <note>original: "nepote"</note> as wives. And so Agrippina—the Emperor Claudius and the Senate being willing—was made the bride of the said Claudius, her uncle. 

She was then titled **Agrippina Augusta** and was carried to the Capitol in a **covered carriage** <note>original: "capelo copto," referring to the *carpentum*, a ceremonial carriage used by priests and later granted to imperial women</note>, a distinction previously granted only to the priesthood. Seeing herself in such a high station, she began to take revenge upon her adversaries. Furthermore, being most cunning for her time, despite the fact that Claudius had several sons and daughters, she persuaded him—aided by the advice of **Memmius Pollio** <note>original: "Mellio Pollione"</note>, who was consul, but even more so by the freedman Pallas—to adopt her son Nero. 

Pallas was favorable to this cause because of his **illicit relationship** <term>stupro: here referring to a scandalous sexual affair</term> with Agrippina. She also arranged for Nero to marry **Octavia**, the daughter born to the said Claudius and **Messalina** <note>Claudius's third wife, famous for her own scandals before her execution</note>. Octavia had already been betrothed to the noble youth **Lucius Silanus** <note>original: "Sillāō Lucio"</note>. 

Once these things were obtained, having been freed [from her previous low state], she turned back into a **beast** <note>original: "bellua," implying she became a ruthless predator once her power was secure</note>. She was not so much disgusted by the constant **gluttony** <note>original: "ingurgitatiōe"</note> of Claudius as she was anxious that **Britannicus** <note>Claudius's biological son and the rightful heir</note>, the son of Claudius, should not reach a **formidable age** <note>The age of majority, when he could legally claim the throne</note> before the death of his father. She also lived in fear of **Narcissus** <note>A powerful freedman and advisor to Claudius</note>, who was managing many affairs on behalf of Britannicus, judging that he would be an obstacle to her grand design.

# Concerning Agrippina, Mother of Nero

...at the death of Claudius: and she arranged it with great malice and sagacity. And since it was known that Claudius was fond of eating mushrooms <note>original: "funghi"</note>—so much so that he used to say they were the food of the gods <note>A dark irony, as Roman emperors were "deified" after death; by eating the "food of the gods," he became one</note>—and because he said they grew without any seed, Agrippina, having learned this, studiously gathered the poisonous ones. And so, according to what some say, while he was warmed by wine, she placed them before him. 

Others say that they were placed before him by Halotus the Eunuch <note>original: "Aliocto Spandōe," referring to Halotus, the Emperor's taster</note>, his taster, who had been corrupted by the said Agrippina while he was eating in the castle with the priests. Truly, when it seemed that through vomiting, Claudius’s health might be restored through the work of Xenophon the physician <note>Gaius Stertinius Xenophon, the imperial doctor who was likely in on the plot</note>, the feathers <note>original: "péne," used to tickle the throat to induce vomiting</note> were smeared with poison. When these were given to him to continue the vomiting, that which the wife desired followed: having been put to bed, he died, with no one knowing of it but her alone. 

And immediately, through the help and labor of her supporters, he was set aside and abandoned, as was his younger son Britannicus <note>Claudius's biological son and the rightful heir, whom Agrippina sidelined in favor of Nero</note>. Nero, already grown, was suddenly elected Prince and Emperor; which thing was so acceptable to Nero that, as if it were a deserved reward, he preferred it above all public and private matters, it appearing to him that he alone held the title, while his mother held the sovereignty. And so, from the pinnacle of the Roman Principate, Agrippina shone before the whole world. 

But this so lucid and great splendor was darkened by most foul stains. For she became intoxicated and inflamed by the death and banishment of many men; and it was believed that she loved her son Nero with a dishonorable love <note>An allusion to the rumors of incest recorded by ancient historians like Tacitus and Suetonius</note>. Because of this, Nero took from a public place a prostitute who resembled her, and the signs appeared upon the bedsheets every time he slept with her. ## Concerning Agrippina, Mother of Nero

...others say that she sought this out and induced her son to such a shameful state <note>This likely refers to contemporary rumors that Agrippina attempted to seduce her son to maintain her political influence over him</note> out of a desire to regain control of the government, from which Nero had deprived her because of certain words she had used against him. These are things we wish to confirm. 

Because Nero was in the habit of avoiding her company and conversation, while she wanted to meet with him alone. In the end, Agrippina—who with her beauty had drawn her uncle <note>The Emperor Claudius</note> into marriage and then killed him, and whose son Nero, a youth not yet of full age, had been raised to the Empire <note>original: "límpío," a contraction of "l'imperio"</note> through fraud and force—was led by him to a detestable death, even though she had deserved it. 

Because she was burdensome to her son in many matters, hatred arose between them, for which reason she was deprived by him of every honor. Indignant and angry, she threatened Nero, saying that just as she had made him Emperor, so she would unmake him. Because of these words, Nero was fearful, for he knew her to be of great cunning and boldness; three times he attempted to poison her. 

But the most sagacious woman protected herself with specialized remedies <note>original: "apẽlatí rímedíi," referring to the use of mithridatium or universal antidotes which she supposedly took daily to build immunity</note>. Finally, Nero, realizing that all the traps set by him were countered by her, thought to kill her with a more cautious fraud. Upon inquiring, he was shown a plan by Anicetus, the prefect of the naval fleet at Misenum <note>original: "Miscina"</note>, who had been his tutor from childhood: that a fragile ship could be constructed in which Agrippina, unaware of the deception, could be wrecked <note>original: "pícolata," referring to the famous mechanical "self-sinking" boat described by the historian Tacitus</note>. 

This plan pleased Nero. When Agrippina arrived from Antium <note>original: "Anthíocho," referring to the seaside town of Anzio</note>, Nero, pretending to have entirely forgotten their past hatreds, received her into his arms and accompanied her to the palace. There, the ship having been prepared to go to Misenum <note>original: "Mícẽa"</note>, she boarded it accompanied by Crepereius Gallus and Aceronia...

# Concerning Agrippina, mother of Nero

...freedman: and so, sailing through the night, as she was with the said companions, and the signal having been given by those who knew the secret, the roof of the ship fell. It was heavy with a great deal of lead <note>This refers to a mechanical trap designed by Anicetus; the ceiling was weighted with lead to crush the occupants when released</note> and it killed Crepereius. Afterwards, while the sailors were attempting to manage the situation in the calm sea, the ship overturned to one side. Aceronia, calling out for help, was killed with oars <note>Aceronia cried out that she was Agrippina to save herself, but the conspirators struck her down thinking she was the Empress</note>. Agrippina, stunned and wounded in the shoulder, was pushed into the sea by the oarsmen. Those who came to her aid brought her to Lake Lucrinus, and from there she was conducted to her villa. 

And by her command, she sent word to Nero through Agellius the freedman that she had escaped. Nero commanded that this man be detained, as if he had come to threaten the Emperor's safety <note>Nero framed the messenger to make it look as though Agrippina had sent an assassin, providing a "legal" excuse to kill her</note>. And he sent Anicetus, Herculeus the trierarch <note>The text reads 'tetrarcho,' but this refers to a ship's captain or trierarch</note>, and Obaritus the centurion of the fleet, so that they should kill her. 

With every excuse removed, and the palace already surrounded by Anicetus, they entered the room from which Agrippina had already fled. A single servant girl who had accompanied her also fled. First, Herculeus struck her on the head with a club, and then immediately the Centurion drew his sword to kill her. She, coming toward him and extending her belly, called out and cried for him to strike her there <note>original: "feríſſe" refers to her famous final command to "strike the womb" that had given birth to such a monster as Nero</note>. 

And so she was killed that same night and burned; she was covered with earth in a lowly funeral. Later, they made a small tomb for her on the road near Misenum, at the villa of Julius Caesar. Some others claim that after she was dead, she was inspected by Nero, and he praised some of her limbs and criticized others, and then she was buried with honors suitable to her.

***

### Concerning Epicharis the freedwoman
Who, knowing of the conspiracy <note>This refers to the Pisonian conspiracy, a later plot to overthrow Nero</note> made against the Emperor Nero, the matter came to the ears of the said Nero. She was captured and tortured; but before she would reveal the plot, she hanged herself by the throat, as appears in the text.

## Concerning Epicharis the Freedwoman

It is believed that
Epicharis was
rather a
foreign woman than
a Roman: she was not
adorned with any
brilliance of lineage: but
born of a freedman <note>original: "liberto"; a former slave who has been granted legal freedom</note> father: she was a 
freedwoman <term>libertina<gloss>the social class of emancipated slaves</gloss></term>. And what is a 
more shameful boldness: she had 
not delighted in any 
honest art <note>The author implies she may have been a woman of ill repute or a prostitute, as "honest arts" referred to respectable social roles</note>. Nevertheless, at the end of her life, she 
manifested with virile strength that she was 
of a noble spirit. And truly, 
as the unbearable persistence and wicked lusts of Nero grew 
among the Romans and all others: a conspiracy was 
organized against him through the efforts of Lucius Piso, 
a leader of many senators and other 
citizens. And while they were 
discussing with various arguments how to bring 
the matter to a certain end: by what 
means I do not know. But all the 
secret matters and the names of the conspirators 
came before the said Epicharis: and it seemed to her 
that the execution of the plan was being delayed too long: 
so, weary of this, she went out in company. 
And when she was staying near the ports: so that 
she should not lose time: she went to Volusius Proculus, 
a captain <term>chiliarch<gloss>a high-ranking naval officer, from the Greek 'chiliarchos'</gloss></term> 
and prefect of the Roman fleet, and in the past the 
murderer of Agrippina: being certain that if her 
person could draw him into the conspiracy, it would 
be of the greatest help; and of
