<lang>English</lang>
<header>On Epicharis the Freedwoman</header>

# On Epicharis the Freedwoman

The ancient proverb says: a woman knows not how to keep a secret, except for that which she does not know <note>This refers to a common classical and medieval trope suggesting women are naturally prone to gossip, which the author is about to subvert by praising Epicharis's silence.</note>. And so she left Nero fearful of the plot. This thing, which appears so great in a woman, will seem even greater and more remarkable if one regards the inconstancy of the illustrious men involved in the said conspiracy. Among those men—unlike Epicharis—none, even of such robust youth, could (I do not say sustain for their own safety what the woman sustained for others') even suffer to hear, remember, or name the torments. Rather, sooner than they were even asked, they told everything they knew of the conspiracy. And so there was no one who spared himself or his friends, despite the fact that this renowned woman had spared them. 

Truly, I once believed that the nature of things must err sometimes when it joins minds to mortal bodies, by infusing into a female body a soul which I believed it ought to have infused into a male one. But because the Giver <note>original: "datore"; referring to God or Nature as the creator of souls</note> of these souls cannot err, it is wrong to believe that He sleeps at His work. Therefore, one must judge that we have all things perfect from Him, and He alone judges the work. And certainly, I will judge that men ought to be ashamed when they are surpassed and conquered by force or the intolerance of suffering—not only by a "loose" woman <note>Epicharis was often described as a woman of ill-repute or a prostitute before her heroic silence.</note>, but indeed by any most constant woman. Since men prevail in status by their sex, so they ought to prevail in strength. When this strength is lacking in men, they become effeminate and, by reason, return to being women; they should be seen as women by their habits, if not by their names.

### On Pompeia Paulina, wife of Seneca, and the purest love she bore her husband: who, when she heard the Centurion tell Seneca to choose his death, immediately resolved that she wished to die the very same death; and so she did, rather than live and later be the wife of another.

**P**ompeia Paulina was the wife of Lucius Annaeus Seneca <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca (c. 4 BC – AD 65), also known as Seneca the Younger, was a Stoic philosopher, statesman, and tutor to the Emperor Nero.</note>, the tutor of Nero. Whether she was Roman or a foreigner, I do not remember having read; but when I regard the nobility of her spirit, I believe more readily that she was Roman than a foreigner. Although it happens that we do not know her certain lineage, we are nonetheless not deprived of the testimony of illustrious men regarding the most certain example of the purest love she held toward her husband. 

Indeed, many most honorable men of that time certainly believed that through the ferocity and cruelty of Nero, rather than through any sin of Seneca, the said Seneca—a man of ancient ways and very celebrated—was slandered as being part of the Pisonian conspiracy <note>The Pisonian conspiracy was a plot to overthrow the Roman Emperor Nero in AD 65. Seneca was accused of being involved and was ordered to commit suicide.</note>; if indeed it is a stain or a vice to be said to rightly act against a tyrant. Under the shadow of this conspiracy, and because of the long-standing, or rather innate, hatred of virtue held by the said Nero, the path was covered and found to put to death that perfect ma... <note>original: "perfecto ho"; likely "perfecto homo" or "perfect man," referring to Seneca.</note>

<summary>This page introduces Pompeia Paulina and her husband Seneca, explaining how Seneca was unjustly accused of conspiracy by the tyrant Nero, leading to his death sentence.</summary>
<keywords>Pompeia Paulina, Seneca, Nero, Pisonian conspiracy, Roman history, marital devotion, Stoicism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Seneca’s death and the devotion of his wife, Pompeia Paulina. ## On Pompeia Paulina, wife of Seneca

...man Seneca: although some believe that by the impulse of Poppaea <note>Poppaea Sabina (AD 30–65), the second wife of the Emperor Nero, often depicted as a bad influence on his rule.</note>, the wife of Nero, and of Tigellinus <note>Ofonius Tigellinus, the prefect of the Praetorian Guard and a notorious advisor to Nero known for his cruelty.</note>, the Emperor’s sole advisor in cruelties, [matters] went so far that Seneca was told by a Centurion to choose his own death. When Paulina saw him preparing for this, she set aside all the consolations and endearments of her husband—with which he comforted her toward life—and her own life being driven by the purest love, she disposed herself to end it by that same manner of death, with a most courageous spirit, together with her dear husband. 

This was so that the bonds of marriage, which had joined them in an honest union in life, might be dissolved by a single death. And when, composed and without any fear, she had entered the warm water, and at the same hour her veins were opened together with her husband's to pour out their spirits, she was rescued from death and drawn from the bath <note>Ancient sources, such as Tacitus, record that Nero ordered Paulina’s life to be saved to avoid a reputation for excessive cruelty; her servants and soldiers bound her wounds while she was unconscious.</note>. But no sooner did the blood stop, than a perpetual paleness testified that this excellent woman had sent forth from herself the greater part of her vital spirit with her husband. 

Therefore, having already preserved the memory of her husband with a praiseworthy widowhood for a few years—since she could not do otherwise—she died at least under the name of the wife of Seneca. What thing, if not the sweetness of the most honest love, full of piety, and of venerable and holy marriage, could advise this excellent woman to prefer—had she been able to do so honorably—to die with her aged husband, rather than preserve her life as women most often do? For they begin and carry out new nuptials, not without shame; because to many in these days and times, the greatest honor of matrimonial <term>pudicitia: a Roman virtue representing modesty, sexual virtue, and faithfulness to one's husband</term> is ignored; I will not say the second, or even the third marriage...

<summary>This page describes Pompeia Paulina's attempt to commit suicide alongside her husband Seneca and her subsequent life in pale widowhood after being forcibly saved by Nero's orders. The author uses her devotion to critique the practice of remarriage common among women of the author's own time.</summary>

<keywords>Pompeia Paulina, Seneca, Nero, Tigellinus, suicide, Roman marriage, widowhood, pudicitia, loyalty</keywords>

--- Page 271 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing conclusion to the life of Pompeia Paulina. While the previous page praised her devotion to her husband Seneca, the text now shifts into a sharp critique of contemporary women who remarry multiple times, contrasting them with the virtuous widows of antiquity. This serves as a transition to the next biography, that of Poppaea Sabina, who is presented as a foil to Paulina’s virtue.</meta>

...which thing is almost customary and common to everyone. But as for the seventh and eighth <note>Boccaccio is criticizing the practice of frequent remarriage, which he views as a sign of moral decay compared to the ancient ideal of the "univira"—a woman who married only once.</note>: if the situation allows it to begin, and the making of a marriage becomes familiar to them there, and they do so in the beds of noble husbands, it seems that they have taken their customs from common prostitutes. To such women, it is a habit to change for new embraces and couplings in the nights. And they do not submit with any other face to the buried <note>The Italian *interrate* implies that the marital rights or duties have been "interred" or cast aside along with the previous husband.</note> marital obligations. 

And many times, though they offer themselves with a show of most holy duty to honesty, it is truly not at all certain whether women of this sort should be said to have come out from the cells of a public brothel or from the beds of their dead husbands. And there is no doubt that one should suspect who acts more dishonestly: the one entering the marriage, or more foolishly, the one inviting her in. 

Oh, I leave it to you, wretched men, to see where your customs have lapsed and fallen! The ancients, who had minds ready and inclined toward holiness, used to judge it to be an unbecoming, infamous, and shameful thing—not only a seventh, but even a second time to hold a wedding. Nor could such women be rightly associated with honest ladies. But the women of today plot quite differently and from a great distance; for, stirred by their libidinous itch <note>original: *libidinosa prurigine*</note>, they consider themselves more famous and more esteemed the more times they have, through frequent weddings, overcome the "misfortune" of widowhood by pleasing various husbands.

## On Sabina Poppaea, wife of Nero

**S**abina Poppaea was Roman and an illustrious woman, the daughter of Titus Ollius <note>Titus Ollius was a Roman senator involved in a conspiracy against the Emperor Tiberius; his downfall explains why Poppaea took her grandfather's name instead of his.</note>, a man of great energy and the highest nobility; despite the fact that she did not take her name from her father, but from her grandfather Poppaeus Sabinus, a man of renown, singular honor, and a distinguished consulship. And truly, she did not lack for natural gifts. For she was of marvelous beauty and comeliness, much like her mother, who in her years exceeded all other Roman women in physical beauty. And beyond this, she possessed a delightful way of speaking and a praiseworthy sweetness; she had an exceptional and ready intellect, had she only used it for honorable arts. She made it her constant habit to display temperance and modesty, but in secret she practiced lascivious vice, a common sin among women. For she rarely went out or showed herself in public, and she did this artfully; for, being a cunning mistress of her own image, she knew that by the sight of her most beautiful appearance and face, the [crowd] <note>The text ends mid-sentence with the catchword "la mol," likely referring to "la moltitudine" (the multitude or crowd) being delighted or stirred by her beauty.</note>

<summary>This page introduces Poppaea Sabina, describing her noble lineage, her legendary beauty inherited from her mother, and her deceptive nature, ending with her tragic death at the hands of Nero.</summary>
<keywords>Poppaea Sabina, Nero, Octavia, Titus Ollius, Poppaeus Sabinus, Roman history, Boccaccio, female biography, vanity, imperial Rome</keywords>

## On Sabina Poppaea, wife of Nero.

[the] multitude <note>The text continues from the previous page's catchword 'la moltitudine'</note> for special and greater reasons: she always went about veiled and covered. Truly, she did not seem to hide what she desired to be desired. But she did this so that the eyes of the onlookers would not be sated by too free a demonstration; but rather so that, for the desire of seeing that which she concealed with a veil, they would abandon every other thing. But so that I do not discourse through all her habits—for reputation never pardons—she directed her lust more toward that place where it showed itself most prepared and ready for personal gain <note>Boccaccio suggests Poppaea used her sexuality strategically for social or material advancement.</note>, making no difference at all between husbands and lovers <note>original: "amici" - in this context, it refers to paramours or illicit companions</note>. The woman, then, being marked by such traits, had Fortune very obedient to her, because resources and riches abounded for her to sustain the glory of her lineage. And first, she was married to Rufrius Crispinus <note>A Roman knight and prefect of the Praetorian Guard under Claudius.</note>, a Roman knight; and when she had already borne a son by him, through the suggestion of Otho <note>Marcus Salvius Otho, who would later briefly become Emperor himself.</note>, a valiant, luxurious, and powerful young man through his companionship <term>contubernio|intimate association or shared living quarters</term> and the favor of Nero, the said Otho approached her and had intercourse with her; nor did much time pass before she was his wife. 

Otho, either through a fury of love that was less than cautious, or not being able to endure the ways of the artful and luxurious woman—and by that, striving to draw her into the concupiscence of Nero—or perhaps Fortune thus requiring it for Poppaea: when rising from the banquet of Caesar <note>Nero</note>, he was heard to say as a habit that he wanted to return to her, whom the gods <note>original: "li superni" - those on high</note> had granted at last every nobility of character, and a divine shapeliness and beauty, in which consists all the devotion of mortals, happiness, joys, and the pleasure of the fortunate. By which words [Nero], having been ensnared and captured...

## On Sabina Poppaea, wife of Nero

[the] lust <note>The text continues from the previous page's discussion of Poppaea’s charms and her relationship with Otho.</note> of Nero: and once she had found the way—after no long search—she came into his embraces, both willing and desiring it. Not much time passed before, through her artful and overt advances, Nero was so seized by his pleasure and love for her that he esteemed those things to be most true which Otho used to say <note>Otho had frequently boasted of Poppaea's beauty and refinement to Nero, which backfired by inciting Nero's own desire for her.</note>. When that most shrewd woman recognized this, she began to dissemble what she truly desired. As soon as she had the opportunity, drenched and soaked in infinite tears, she wickedly directed his love toward where she desired. And because she was joined to Otho and bound by the law of marriage, and understanding that the prince Nero was held by the grace and love of Acte <note>Claudia Acte was a former slave and Nero's long-term mistress.</note>, a slave and a whore, she took action. It followed that Otho, under the guise of being honored, was sent away as the prefect of the province of Lusitania <note>A Roman province comprising most of modern-day Portugal and parts of western Spain.</note>, and Acte was entirely excluded and dismissed. Once this was done, Poppaea began to set herself against Agrippina, the mother of Nero, saying at times that the prince could enjoy not only the empire <note>original: "lo ímpío" — likely a transcription error for "l'imperio" (the empire).</note> but his own liberty; and that he was a faint-hearted and submissive man, allowing himself to be led by the will of his mother. No one moved to remedy these things because of the hatred that almost everyone had for the pride of Agrippina. It followed by the command of Nero that his wretched mother was killed, after he had first stripped her of many honors, with Tigellinus <note>Ofonius Tigellinus, the infamous Prefect of the Praetorian Guard and a confidant of Nero.</note>, the Prefect of the Roman armies, favoring Poppaea. Therefore, knowing the prince Nero to be most ardent in his affection for her, and that all the obstacles to her desire had been removed and she alone held his love, she began to spread the nets for her marriage to him.

## On Sabina Poppaea, wife of Nero

After she had given birth to a daughter—while Memmius Regulus and Verginius Rufus were consuls <note>This occurred in 63 AD. The consuls mentioned are Gaius Memmius Regulus and Lucius Verginius Rufus.</note>—Nero received this daughter with supreme joy. Naming her Poppaea, he gave her the title of **Augusta Poppaea**. [Poppaea] immediately began to speak to the prince with more daring logic, saying that she had now spent two nights <note>original: "doi note" — This may be a transcription error for "years" (anni), or a reference to a specific rhetorical complaint about her status as a mistress rather than a wife.</note> without achieving marriage, and that she was not of low or vile lineage. She argued that by her eloquence, her shapeliness, and the beauty of her body, she deserved the marriage of the emperor.

Once she had drawn the lustful and burning prince into the desire for marriage with her, **Octavia**—who had previously been his legitimate wife and the daughter of Claudius Caesar—was the first to suffer. Although she was most innocent, she was confined to the island of Pandateria <note>A small island in the Tyrrhenian Sea used as a place of exile for noblewomen.</note>. Afterward, in the tenth year of her age <note>Octavia was roughly 22 at her death; "decimo anno" likely refers to the duration of her marriage or a scribe's error.</note>, by the arrangement and plot of Poppaea, she was put to death by Nero's command.

Afterward, Poppaea was joined in marriage to Nero. However, she did not long enjoy the heights she had acquired and possessed through her long-practiced arts. Having become pregnant once again, she ended her days after being struck by a kick during a chance fit of Nero's rage. Her body, by Nero's command, was ordered not to be buried according to the Roman custom <note>Roman tradition at the time usually involved cremation.</note>, but according to the rite and order of the **Eastern Kings** <note>original: "Estini Re" — This refers to the practice of embalming and mummification used by foreign or "Eastern" monarchs rather than cremation.</note>. He commanded that she be carried with magnificent funeral pomp, and entirely surrounded and refreshed with perfumes, she was placed in the tomb of the **Julian emperors**. Nero himself, with most ornate orations and courtly speech, praised and magnified her for her exceptional beauty and extraordinary shapeliness, attributing to her a place of most illustrious virtues and many gifts of fortune and nature with which she was marked and adorned. And certainly to me it [seems]...

## On Triaria, wife of Lucius Vitellius

Triaria, a woman known for no other splendor of her lineage except that she was the wife of Lucius Vitellius, a prince of the Romans <note>Lucius Vitellius the Younger was the brother of the Emperor Aulus Vitellius. ## On Triaria, wife of Lucius Vitellius

Triaria, who during that night, following her husband, had entered into the city: desiring her husband’s victory, she was girded with a sword and armed. She mixed herself among the Vitellian knights, <note>The "Vitellians" were the soldiers loyal to the Emperor Vitellius, Lucius's brother.</note> now here, now there through the shadows of the night, amidst the noises, amidst the deadly blades, and the blood spilled from those who were dead. She rushed about, sparing herself no labor and not ceasing from performing any military act; she conducted herself in such a severe and bold manner that it was imputed to her that she acted too cruelly or haughtily against the enemies.

And truly marvelous and great are the powers of <term>conjugal love</term> <note>original: "congiugale amor"; the devoted love between a married couple.</note> in a healthy breast; for those forces have no fear, provided that the glory of the husband be exalted. There is no memory of pity, no shame for the female sex, no consideration for the fragility of bodies; Triaria could endure everything for the honor of her husband. She was not like other women, who most of the time have the habit of being terrified in the night and trembling in their husband's bosom at the rustle or squeak of a mouse; instead, even the warlike and robust youths would have been timid and fearful in her place. 

Who would believe that she was adorned by this single act alone? Considering that other virtues are not used to being solitary within the breasts of mortals. And certainly I judge that, even though they have been withdrawn from memory, Triaria was much more remarkable for other merits as well.

...true Homer. And the said work is called the <term>Cento</term> <note>original: "centona"; a literary work made up of fragments from other authors. Proba’s "Cento Virgilianus" famously rearranged the lines of the Roman poet Virgil to tell stories from the Bible.</note>. And many other marvelous things she did, as appears below in the text.

### On Proba, wife of Adelphus

**P**roba, by her own name and by her knowledge of letters, was most worthy of memory, despite the fact that her origin is unknown to us. It pleases many—and by conjecture I believe it to be said—that she was a Roman woman; but some other most illustrious men affirm that she had her birth in <note>original: "Cefella dorti"; likely a corruption of "Gallaecian" or a specific family name related to the gens Anicia, to which historical Proba belonged.</note>. She was the wife of a man called Adelphus and was of the Christian religion. 

This woman, therefore, under whatever preceptor or master she may have been, was most highly instructed and most skilled in the <term>liberal arts</term> <note>The traditional core subjects of classical education, including grammar, rhetoric, and logic.</note>; this can be easily and clearly understood through one of her works. And truly, among her other studies, she was [driven] by sleepless nights and solicitous care, becoming so learned and so familiar with the verses of **Virgil** <note>The Roman poet Publius Virgilius Maro, author of the Aeneid, whom Proba studied extensively.</note> that—according to the testimony of the work she composed—it seems she almost always held every line before her memory’s sight. 

When Proba read these verses at times with a more piercing affection, she formed the opinion that she could describe, with pleasing, ready, and full verse, the whole history of the **Old Testament**. And certainly this is not without wonder: that into the mind of a woman should enter such sublime and high consideration. But much more...

## On Proba, wife of Adelphus

...it was a marvelous thing to have sent the plan out and put it into execution. Therefore, speaking and applying effort to this high concept—now in one place, now in another—she described [the biblical story] using the *Bucolics*, *Georgics*, and the *Aeneid* <note>The three major works of the Roman poet Virgil: his pastoral poems, his poems on farming, and his epic on the founding of Rome.</note>. She proceeded with leaps, sometimes taking whole verses from those works, sometimes cutting them, and at other times plucking small fragments from the said verses. With marvelous artifice, she brought them to her purpose, placing them so aptly and preserving the law of the feet <note>The metrical rhythm or "poetic meter" of the original Latin verse.</note> and the dignity of the poetry, that no one—except the most expert—could understand or notice where the lines were joined together. 

And making a beginning from the start of the world, she composed so orderly whatever history is read in the Old or New Testaments <note>original: "antiche: ouero noue letere"; literally "ancient or new letters," referring to the scriptures.</note>, down to the mission of the Holy Spirit, that an ignorant man would easily believe that Virgil had been a prophet or an evangelist. Because of these things, it is understood to be no less commendable that she had complete and full knowledge of the sacred volumes—a level of learning which rarely occurs among men in our own time, as we have sorrowfully realized. 

The excellent woman desired, moreover, that the work composed by her labor should be called a **Cento** <term>Cento</term> <note>From the Latin word for "patchwork quilt"; a poem made entirely of fragments taken from other authors.</note>. We have seen this work many times. And as much as we think that work is worthy of perpetual memory, we believe even more that the celebrated intellect of this woman did not rest only in this single labor; indeed, I judge that she lived for many longer years of life, and that she produced many other laudable works. These, through the laziness of scribes and to our great disadvantage and discomfort, were unable to reach us. Among these lost works—as many believe—was a **Homeric Cento**, created by the same art and from the same material, taking [from Homer]...

<summary>This page describes Proba's masterful technique of "patchwork" poetry, where she rearranged Virgil's classical verses to tell the history of the Bible from Creation to Pentecost. The author laments the loss of her other potential works, including a rumored version based on Homer.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Faltonia Betitia Proba, a Roman poet who lived in the 4th century. ...verses of Homer, and Virgil had taken up that same material. For this reason, it is to her even greater and more ample praise that she knew and understood Greek literature as well as Latin. But I ask: what thing is more lasting than to have heard of the woman Proba? She went step by step through the verses of Maro <note>Virgil's full name was Publius Vergilius Maro.</note> and Homer, setting aside verses suitable for her work; and having selected and set them aside, she wove them together, joining them with an artful composition.

She reduced the order of the life of Christ into a single compass of words <note>The text says "words in prose," though Proba’s actual work was written in verse; this may be the translator's way of describing the narrative flow.</note>. Truly, for her, the distaff and the spindle, the needle and the loom <note>Traditional symbols of domestic labor expected of women in antiquity and the Middle Ages.</note> would have been enough, if we consider common female customs, and if—following the habit of most women—she had wished to be lazy and negligent. But because she remained upright and disciplined, continuing in her sacred studies, she entirely wiped away and purged the rust from her soul and entered into eternal light. 

Would to God that other women might look with a good spirit upon such pleasures and idleness! These women think it a great thing to stay in bed or in their chambers, consuming irreplaceable time with vanity and tall tales; from early morning until night, they are chattering—either with various stories, or with new rumors, or indeed just arguing with themselves. Truly, they would see how much difference there is between acquiring fame through a praiseworthy work and burying one's name together with one's body—so that we depart from life just as we came into it, leaving nothing behind.

->Concerning Faustina Augusta<-

# Concerning Faustina Augusta

<gloss>A</gloss>ugusta <note>The text begins with a large initial 'A'.</note> Faustina: she who was then deified among the gods while living and dying; she achieved more glory through the kindness of her husband than through her own deeds. She was the daughter of Antoninus Pius <term>Caesar Augustus</term> <note>Titles given to Roman Emperors; "Pius" refers to his reputation for duty and devotion.</note> and of Faustina his wife; and she was married to Marcus Antoninus <note>Better known today as the Emperor Marcus Aurelius.</note>, the adopted son of Pius Antoninus. And once her father had died, she reigned together with her husband; and by decree of the senate she was called "Augusta," which thing was no small glory for a woman.

But I find that to almost no other woman before her was the name of "Augusta" granted by decree of the senate. <note>While previous empresses held the title, the author emphasizes the specific official recognition by the Roman Senate in her case.</note> Beyond this, Faustina was of such exquisite and becoming splendor of beauty that it was believed by many that something divine was mixed with her mortality. So that this beauty would not be consumed by old age or by death, it was arranged that while she was a young girl of more advanced age, her effigy was sculpted in gold, silver, and metal. This was done in such a way that it lasts until the present day; in which image, though the lip of the mouth, the movement of the eyes, the living color, and the joy of her beautiful face are perhaps missing, nonetheless the features show and testify that she was a most excellent creature. But truly, however celebrated and honored the beauty of her person and life...

<meta>This page continues the biography of Faustina Augusta, moving from a description of her divine beauty and statues to a detailed account of her scandalous reputation and alleged infidelities.</meta>

...her fame and name were known throughout the entire world; that is, she was so polluted and darkened by the foulest mark of unchastity <note>Immoral or lustful behavior.</note>. And it was believed that Faustina was not only content to have one lover besides her husband, but she fell into the embraces of many. The infamy <note>Evil reputation or public shame.</note> surrounding her revealed the names of some of these men.

For a certain man named Vitellius was counted among her other adulterers, as were Orphitus and Moderatius; but the one who surpassed all others was named Tertullus. This man was found by Antoninus <note>The Emperor Marcus Aurelius, often referred to in historical texts as Marcus Antoninus.</note> dining with her; or perhaps Marcus Verus <note>The co-emperor Lucius Verus.</note> came upon them, even though he was Antoninus's son-in-law through his daughter Lucilla.

And what is more shameful than all of this is what they say: that she loved a swordsman <term>scrimitore</term> <note>A gladiator or professional fighter.</note> so passionately that out of longing for him she fell into a near-fatal illness. In her desire for health, she confessed her lust <term>concupiscence</term> <note>Strong sexual desire.</note> to Antoninus. Following the advice of physicians to quench the burning desire of his languishing Faustina, Antoninus had the swordsman killed. He then had the body of the arrogant Faustina anointed with the man's still-warm blood. In this way, he freed his wife from the impetuous heat of love, and from her disease and infirmity.

Believe it, O prudent and wise men: for in the course of time, Commodus <note>The future emperor and son of Faustina, known for his obsession with gladiatorial combat.</note> was conceived at that time—not of pure blood, but rather she invited the gladiator’s embrace through her wicked deeds. By these actions, she left testimony for posterity <note>Future generations.</note> that the son was believed to have been fathered by the gladiator rather than by Antoninus.

Because of these things, and the resounding infamy of Faustina, Antoninus was advised by his friends to put her to death—or at least, which seemed a more humane thing, that he should renounce her. However, because Antoninus was a man of a mild and gentle nature, although he suffered bitterly from his wife's adulteries, he refused and would not follow this advice.

# On Faustina Augusta

And so that he would not fall into even greater dishonor, he preferred to endure it. He gave no other answer to his friends than this: that it would be necessary to return the dowries to his divorced wives <note>Marcus Aurelius famously quipped that if he divorced his wife for her infidelities, he would have to return her dowry—which was the Roman Empire itself, as he had gained the throne through his marriage to her.</note>. He said this knowing that he held the Empire because of Faustina, and that such matters should very often be left alone, certainly even out of the smallest regard. Even less than being openly subjected to the usual mockeries and stains, even the most virtuous reputations return from the clouds into the light. 

While Antoninus <note>Marcus Aurelius, often called Antoninus in historical texts.</note> was magnificently tending to and overseeing the Republic among the Eastern Kings, it happened that Faustina reached her final day due to illness in the village of Halala, at the foot of Mount Taurus. At the request of some, the Senate exalted her among the divine; from then on, she was called the **Divine Faustina**. Such a thing had not been considered suitable for any woman among the Romans before. 

And as he had already named her **Mother of the Camps** <term>Mater Castrorum</term> <note>A title honoring her for accompanying her husband on military campaigns.</note>, Antoninus had a noble temple built for her in the very place where she had died. He also commanded a conspicuous statue be made with her name, and ordained two orders of young girls as priestesses, whom he commanded to be called and named *Faustianae* after her most glorious name. And so, in the place of a goddess, Faustina was celebrated for a time in that location. This was done so that the renown <note>original: "claritade"</note> which her lust seemed to have taken from her might be restored to her through her divinity.

## On Semiamira, a woman of Emesa

<margin>Julia Soaemias</margin>
She was a dishonest prostitute, and through the works of her son Heliogabalus, she was made Empress of Rome. Finally, she and her son were killed and dragged through the streets of Rome, as appears in the written account of his life.

->On Semiamira, a woman of Messina<-

## On Semiamira, a Woman of Messina

**S**emiamira <note>Historically Julia Soaemias, mother of the Emperor Elagabalus. The name "Semiamira" is a common medieval and Renaissance corruption of her name.</note> was a Greek woman from the city of Messina <note>The text confuses the city of Emesa in Syria with Messina in Sicily.</note>: but at what time she was born, we do not know: although it is clear that a certain Varia of Messina <note>Referring to Julia Maesa, the grandmother of Elagabalus.</note>, daughter of Julia Scellina <note>Likely a corruption of Julia Bassiana.</note>, who in the past had been the wife of the severe and persistent Emperor <note>Septimius Severus. Historically, Julia Maesa was the sister-in-law of Septimius Severus, not his wife.</note>, was her mother.

And truly, for a certain time, she was a dishonorable woman: but after this, through the fame of her son and the leadership of the Senate, she was made eminent. This woman, leaving behind her previously honest ways, was the mother of Varius Heliogabalus <note>The Emperor Elagabalus, who was born Varius Avitus Bassianus.</note>, while he was still a child. He did not descend from the grandmother Varia, as some thought; rather, he was called "Varius" by some of his followers because, through the intercourse with various men with whom his mother constantly consorted, it appeared that he had been "variously" generated <note>The author is making a pun on the name "Varius" and the "various" (original: *varii*) men with whom his mother was allegedly involved.</note>. 

But among other things, since Heliogabalus was of most beautiful and remarkable appearance, and well known for his priesthood <note>He was the hereditary high priest of the Syrian sun god Elagabal at Emesa.</note>, and believed—on the affirmation of his mother—by the provincial soldiers <term>cavallieri</term> <note>While *cavallieri* literally means knights or cavalry, here it refers to the Roman legionaries or the equestrian class supporting the coup.</note> to be the son of Caracalla: he was made emperor through the money of his grandmother. This wealth she had gathered, being an imperial consort through her shrewdness. And as the soldiers of Emperor Macrinus <note>The emperor who succeeded Caracalla but was soon overthrown by Elagabalus's faction.</note> were grieving that anything should be plotted against him, the consent of the soldiers went to Heliogabalus without any difficulty. For at that time among the Romans there were...

# On Semiamira, a Woman of Messina

The name of the Antonines <note>Referring to the dynasty of the Antonines, particularly the popularity of the late Emperor Caracalla</note> carried such authority among the armies and within her own family that they desired nothing more than for one of their own to hold the imperial power. And not much time passed before a conspiracy was formed against Macrinus, and Heliogabalus <note>The Emperor Elagabalus</note> was hailed as Emperor and given the name Antoninus. 

When Macrinus, who was then in Antioch, heard of this, he was astonished beyond measure by the audacity of Varia <note>This refers to Julia Maesa, the grandmother of the Emperor, who orchestrated the coup</note>. He realized her hand was certainly behind what had occurred; for when Julianus <note>Ulpius Julianus, a prefect under Macrinus</note> was sent by Macrinus to besiege Heliogabalus, he was killed, and his own knights defected and swore fealty to Heliogabalus. 

When Macrinus himself later went out to do battle, he was defeated, routed, and driven away. Not long after, in a village in Bithynia <note>original: "vico de petie"</note>, he was killed along with his son Diadumenianus. Because of this, Heliogabalus—acting as if he were Caracalla <note>To gain legitimacy, Heliogabalus claimed to be the illegitimate son of the popular Emperor Caracalla</note>—avenged the death of his "father." Through the efforts of his grandmother Varia, he undoubtedly secured the imperial throne. 

And coming to Rome, where he was awaited by the entire Senate with the greatest anticipation, he was received with great honor. Through this sudden change of fortune, Semiamira was exalted to the stars and given the title of **Augusta** <note>The highest title for women in the Roman imperial family, roughly equivalent to "Empress"</note>. Despite the previous dishonor of her reputation, the status of the grandmother of the Roman prince made her appear more radiant and splendid through this new greeting. 

For although Heliogabalus was wicked, he recognized that he was prince through the work of his grandmother and his mother. Consequently, he honored his mother as a form of repayment, to the point that he did almost nothing except by her command. On the very day he entered Rome, it was ordered by the command of the Senate that his mother should be invited to enter the Senate chamber. She consented and went there; a seat was prepared for her in the place where the other senators sat, where she spoke and affirmed

# On Semiamira, a Woman of Messina

<note>While the text says "Messina," the historical Semiamira (Julia Soaemias) was actually from Emesa in Syria. This reflects a common geographical confusion in early modern transcriptions.</note>

...her opinion on the things that needed to be done. No other woman in memory had ever done such a thing. Oh, what an ignominious and shameful spectacle! To see, among the most serious and stern men, a common prostitute <note>The author uses the derogatory "meretricola" to insult Semiamira’s character, a common tactic used by ancient and early modern historians to delegitimize the Severan women.</note> led into the public place just the day before. 

And where the affairs of kingdoms and kings were discussed, one saw a woman accustomed to being among pimps <note>original: "roſſiani," referring to ruffians or procurers</note> talking and giving her own opinion. Oh ancient liberty! Oh primal sanctity! Oh venerable indignation of our ancestors! For by this act, the men of such a celebrated college <note>Referring to the Roman Senate</note> were made less grave, and the institution was so perverted, disfigured, and stained by a mark of shame. 

If you look and watch, perhaps that infamous little woman corrupted them, staining the eyes of men such as the Curii, the Decii, the Fabricii, the Scipios, and the Catos <note>These are names of legendary Roman heroes famous for their austerity and virtue, here contrasted with the decadence of Semiamira’s era.</note>. But why do I blame and reproach the "female senator," when the enemies of the Republic—through wicked and most vicious youths, strangers and unknowns—hold the leadership of the city of Rome and the whole world? 

What is more, when Heliogabalus entered the Senate, did his "most holy" mother not enter alongside him? To her, blind fortune added so much that she was held in great esteem by the common people, and was even placed before all the Sibyls <note>Prophetic women of the ancient world; the author implies the public viewed her with unearned religious awe</note>. 

And besides these annoying things, something laughable followed: this woman was held in such veneration by her ignorant son that on the Quirinal Hill, a place was ordered and built for her, which he called the **Senaculum** <note>A "Little Senate" specifically for women.</note>. Where in the past, on certain solemn days, there used to be gatherings of women, he now ordered that these women—following the customs of the male senators—should hold councils and institute laws regarding the conduct and customs of women. And to this "secret senate"—however much it was covered in ridicule—matters were brought and decisions were given. In that college, it was ordained what clothing and ornaments...

# On Semiamira, a Woman of Messina

<note>original: "Messina"; as noted previously, the author likely confuses the Syrian city of Emesa with the Sicilian city of Messina.</note>

...to use and to wear: and it was permitted to each woman, specifically those in a chariot and those on a horse, to have equal feminine <term>ornamentation</term> <note>original: "ornameto muliebre," referring to the jewelry and luxury goods regulated by sumptuary laws</note>. Or rather, whether she should be subject to similar rules, which seemed rather to be vain and more like a game than the truth. This was especially because these matters were judged as feminine vanity. 

Nevertheless, in that time, through the insane and corrupt judgment of the common people, these were held as matters of great importance. But truly, since nothing forced is perpetual or durable, all these things were easily dissolved like mist into the air. 

Because Semiamira occupied herself rather with <term>meretricious</term> <note>Dealing with prostitution; the author suggests she behaved like a common sex worker rather than an empress.</note> acts with the other women, while her son [Heliogabalus] wandered in vanities and dishonest lusts, it went so far that Heliogabalus was killed together with his mother by their own guards. After her spirit left her, her corpse was dragged through the <term>sewer</term> <note>original: "chiocha," referring to the Cloaca Maxima, Rome's great sewer system</note>, and from there, along with the body of her son, she was thrown into the Tiber <note>original: "teuere"</note> so that the course of her youth did not seem different from the end of her life. Which thing we, living miserably, consider very little.

# On Zenobia, Queen of the Palmyrenes 

<note>The OCR reads "palerminie" (Palermitans), but the context and lineage clearly refer to Zenobia of Palmyra.</note>

And by lineage descended from the Ptolemaic Kings of Egypt, she was a woman of exalted virtue. From her childhood, she despised all feminine customs and had driven away every act of love from herself. However, as it pleased her kin, she was married to Odaenathus <note>original: "Odenato"</note>, a most noble youth. She acquired all the eastern regions, but finally, defeated and captured by the Romans, she ended her life in the said city of Rome, together with her children, as appears in the text.

## Zenobia Regina

**Z**enobia, Queen of the Palmyrenes <note>original: "palermini"; the author likely confuses the Syrian city of Palmyra with the Sicilian city of Palermo, a common error in Renaissance geographical understanding</note>, was a woman of such high <term>virtue</term> <note>In this context, virtue refers to "virtù"—not just moral purity, but strength, excellence, and power</note>: according to what the ancient scriptures testify: for her illustrious fame she ought to be preferred above all pagan women. She descended from a very noble lineage: for she had her origin from the Ptolemies <note>original: "Tholõei"</note>, Kings of Egypt. From her childhood, she completely despised traditional feminine customs and duties. And so much so, that as soon as her girlish body gained any strength: she began to go through woodland places hunting with bow and arrows: and killing roe deer and stags. 

And then, having arrived at a more mature age and form: she was of such dexterity, strength, and such marvelous daring: that she would grapple with bears, and follow leopards and lions: and meeting them, she would wait for them and catch them, and carry them away. And following these such exercises, she completely despised every <term>softness and delicacy</term> <note>original: "molitia e morbideza," terms used to describe the perceived weakness or luxury of the female gender</note> of feminine behavior. 

And she went by day and by night: in heat and in cold: through the forests and through the roughest mountains: running without any fear: searching out hidden, most ferocious, and subterranean places: and often sleeping in the open air in the woods: in the mist and the dew <note>original: "brie e rosade"</note>, in whatever weather it might be. And she had so disposed her most beautiful and well-proportioned body, that every kind of hardship and labor

## About Queen Zenobia

...she passed her time like any man, most tough and strong. And she was of such strength and dexterity that she matched herself against the strongest youths in wrestling, grappling, running, or jumping, or in any other trial; without any comparison, she surpassed and defeated everyone. No amorous act or company of men pleased her; instead, with the greatest persistence and constancy, she studied to maintain her virginity. But nonetheless, having come to the perfect age for marriage, it is said she was forced by her parents to take as a husband one of her own lineage—a man hardened in the exercise of arms, virtuous, most bold, strong, and of matching habits, named **Odenathus**. Zenobia was beautiful in body beyond measure, but her face was dark, as almost everyone in that region is due to the most burning sun. She had eyes that were black and most bright, and her teeth were as white as could be. In every part of her body, she was perfectly proportioned. 

This **Odenathus**, knowing that **Shapur**, King of Persia <note>original: "Sapore Re de persia"; Shapur I, the Sassanid King who famously captured the Roman Emperor Valerian</note>, was occupying the lands of the East—and having defeated **Avilius Selvagio** <note>This name is likely a corruption of a Roman usurper's name, possibly Ballista or Macrianus, or a descriptive epithet meaning "Avilus the Wild"</note>—saw that the condemned Emperor **Valerian** was held captive and that his son **Gallienus** <note>original: "Galieno"</note> did nothing to recover him. Desirous of the Eastern Empire, Odenathus prepared himself with all his strength to go against the said **Shapur**. 

**Zenobia**, not having forgotten her former hardiness and her masculine spirit, served as a soldier under her husband. And once they had taken the royal title together, having gathered a very large army along with **Herodes** her stepson, the said **Zenobia** went against the said **Shapur**, King of Persia, who already widely occupied and ruled **Mesopotamia**. # About Queen Zenobia

And afterwards, when the empire was entered by Quietus <note>original: "Quirito"</note>, the son of Macrianus <note>original: "Machrio"</note>, who ruled under the title of his father’s brother, he was defeated and driven out by her. Wherefore Zenobia, together with her husband, possessed in peace almost the entire Empire of the East belonging to the Romans.

But things did not remain in this state for long, for Odenathus, along with his son Herodes, was killed by Maeonius <note>original: "Meonio"</note>, his cousin. There were some who said that Zenobia consented to the death of Herodes <note>Herodes was Odenathus's son by a previous marriage, making him Zenobia's stepson</note>, whom she had blamed many times and reproached for his softness <note>term: "molleza" — implying a lack of masculine vigor or luxury-loving nature</note>; and perhaps she did so to the end that her own sons, Herennianus and Timolaus, whom she had with Odenathus, might succeed to the kingdom. 

And as for the ruler Maeonius, he rested but a short time, for he was killed by his own knights. And so the lady remained in the kingdom; and immediately, with an orderly and great army, she entered the desired empire. Covered in the imperial mantle, she claimed this for her sons, and under their names while they were still small, she governed and ruled the said empire in such a form and manner that neither Gallienus <note>The Roman Emperor</note>, nor after him the Emperor Claudius, had the daring to take any action against her. Nor did the Easterners, the Egyptians, the Arabs, the Saracens, nor even the Armenians [dare], for each was content to be able to remain within their own borders. 

For this most noble lady was so expert and so accomplished in every art of fighting and the discipline of cavalry <note>term: "cavallaria" — referring to the prestigious and skilled mounted soldiers</note>, that her knights and her armies equally loved and feared her; with whom she never, or very rarely, held any assembly unless she had a helmet on her head. <meta>This page continues the biography of Zenobia, the 3rd-century Queen of Palmyra. ## About Queen Zenobia

...most of the time, she was ahead of everyone by three or four miles. It is said of her that, being by nature most sober, she never disdained while on her marches to eat familiarly with her knights; and similarly, with the princes of Persia and Armenia, she conformed herself to their customs. She was such a complete guardian of her chastity that not only did she have no lustful thoughts after her husband’s death, but one reads of her that she never lay carnally with Odenathus except for the purpose of procreation. She maintained such a rule with him: that after sleeping with him once, she would remain without sleeping with him again until she knew whether or not she had conceived. If she had conceived, she would not join with him again until after the birth was accomplished. But if she had not conceived, at the request of her husband, she would sleep with him carnally. 

Oh, what praiseworthy judgment for a woman! Truly, it seems quite manifest that she judged that sexual intercourse <note>original: "coito"</note> should be used for nothing other than to produce children. And certainly, you will find very few women of such character. And because such high principles <note>original: "miſterii"—literally "mysteries," here referring to her disciplined moral code</note> never left her mind, it is said that in her royal palace, none lived nor frequented her company except for men who were old and grave in their character and age. 

Yet, for all this great virtue <note>original: "honeſta"—translated here as "virtue" or "honor" rather than "honesty"</note>, it was not the case that she failed to live most regally. She wished to be revered, honored, and adored according to the custom of Persia; and she used to hold the grandest banquets <note>original: "cōuiti"</note> in the manner of the Roman emperors. Sometimes, following the usage and custom of the Orientals, she used vessels of gold and precious stones, of which she had a great abundance for the display of her magnificent state. No one was ever more generous in giving or in spending magnificently where and how it was appropriate.

This woman: although she spent much time on the affairs of the realm, it was not the case that she failed to attend to the hunt; she also applied herself to the study of philosophy <note>original: "phĩa"—an abbreviation for *philosophia*</note> and other sciences. Because she had as her master Longinus <note>Cassius Longinus, a renowned Greek rhetorician and philosopher</note>, a philosopher and a very great poet, she heard philosophy and learned it perfectly in the Greek language; she knew and learned not only their writings and those of the Latins, but also the Barbarian and Oriental ones. With great study, she committed these to her memory, reducing them into brevity and into verses, as many affirm. 

And she knew, besides her own language, that of the Persians, of the Indians, of the Egyptians, and that of Syria. To her children, she gave orders and wished that they should speak Latin. What more needs to be said? This woman was of such high virtue and elevated intellect <note>original: "ĩgegniõſi"—intellectual genius</note>, and of so noble a heart, that when the emperors Gallienus and Claudius had already passed away and died, and Aurelian—a man of upright life—was placed in the principate, he was entirely disposed to purge the infamy and the shame of his predecessors, and to recover the honor and state of the Roman Empire and glory for himself. He was magnificently incited to battle by the said Zenobia. 

But Aurelian—once the war of the Marcomanni <note>original: "Marcho Mãico"—referring to the Germanic Marcomannic tribes</note> or rather the war of Africa was finished, and affairs in Rome were ordered and put in place—took up the war against Zenobia. With his legions, he transported himself toward the city of Emesa <note>original: "Meſſa"—modern-day Homs, Syria</note>. This city was held for Zenobia, who, fearing no one, was surrounded and besieged with her army. Against this emperor, Zenobia immediately came out, and with the people of her companion Zaba <note>Zabdas, Zenobia’s chief general</note>, she organized her forces and fought with him. There, one could observe the marvelous virtue and actions of the said Zenobia; but yet, in the end, the strength and valor of the Romans prevailed. ### About Queen Zenobia

...[and] drove [her] back into her own country of Palmyra <note>original: "palermina"—an archaic or erroneous spelling of Palmyra</note>. Immediately Aurelian was upon her with the victorious Romans; but Zenobia, having retreated there, defended herself from within the walls without ever being willing to hear any terms of agreement. She was driven to the very brink of survival; ultimately, the Romans captured the Armenians, barbarians, and Saracens who had come to Zenobia’s aid. They took the city, and though she had boarded certain galleys at sea with her children to escape to Persia, she was pursued by the Romans, captured, and presented with her children to Aurelian. 

From this, he received no less satisfaction than if he had conquered the greatest enemy the Roman Republic ever had. Reserving her for his triumph along with her children, he led her to Rome, where the said notable and marvelous triumph was celebrated for the sake of the sight of Zenobia. In this procession, among other things worthy of memory, was a chariot of gold and precious stones of inestimable value. Zenobia had caused this chariot to be built for her own glory, intending to enter Rome in it not as a prisoner, but as a triumphant Empress to take possession of the Roman Empire <note>original: "impio"—an abbreviation for *imperio*</note>. 

Before this very chariot walked Zenobia together with her two sons. Golden chains were placed upon Zenobia’s hands, feet, and neck, according to the order of the triumph, and upon her head was the royal crown of inestimable value <note>original: "p̃giõ"—abbreviation for *pregio*</note>. She was covered in royal garments set with so many pearls and precious stones that, due to the excessive weight—despite the fact that she was physically very strong—she was several times forced to falter and stop. When the solemnity of the triumphal celebration was completed, the treasure distributed, and all other appropriate matters settled, they say that a beautiful estate at Tivoli <note>original: "Tiboli"—the site of Hadrian's Villa and a popular retreat for Roman elites</note> was granted to Zenobia by the Senate, in which Zenobia grew old in most honorable [circumstances].

## Concerning Joan the English Female Pope

As a young woman she was loved by a scholar. They say that he loved her so fervently that, setting aside virginal shame and feminine fear, she secretly fled from her father's house and followed her lover in the clothing of a young male, changing her name. While living with this scholar in England, she was esteemed by everyone to be his cleric; and thus she served both <term>lust</term> <note>original: "luxuria," referring here to her illicit sexual relationship</note> and the study of letters.

And in the course of time, after the scholar died, she—knowing herself to be of strong and subtle intellect, and attracted by the love and sweetness of knowledge—without wanting to join herself to another man or reveal her true nature, gave herself to the perseverance of study. She did this with such vigilance and devotion that she became most learned first in the liberal arts, and then so excellent in the sacred scriptures that, as a man, she was reputed among everyone to be the most learned of all.

And so, endowed with such immense knowledge, and already being of advanced age, she went from England to Rome. There, lecturing <note>original: "legendo," literally "reading," the standard method of university teaching</note> for some years in the teacher's chair, she had many distinguished and notable listeners. Shining with both admirable knowledge and the greatest honesty and holiness, and being believed and held by everyone to be a man, she was—upon the passing from this life of Leo V <note>While the text specifies Leo V, the legend usually places Joan's election after the death of Pope Leo IV (855 AD)</note>, the Supreme Pontiff—chosen and elected Pope by the common accord of the Cardinals, and was named John.

If she had truly been a man, she would have been Pope John VIII in the succession. She felt no shame or remorse for occupying the chair of the <term>Vicar of Christ</term> <note>A title for the Pope, signifying his role as the earthly representative of Jesus</note>. She obtained the sacrosanct mysteries never granted to any woman by the Christian religion, giving and administering the supreme highness of the apostolic blessing. And so, for several years, the <term>vicariate of Christ</term> <note>original: "uicariato de Xpõ"</note> on earth was exercised by a woman.

<summary>This page narrates the legend of Pope Joan, describing how she disguised herself as a man to pursue studies in England and Rome, eventually becoming so respected for her learning that she was elected Pope.</summary>

## Concerning Joan the English Female Pope

And truly, God Most High, looking down from heaven and showing mercy to His people, [saw] that such a glorious, holy, and honorable place was held to the damnation of so many people, brought about by the "virtuous" error of a woman. Seeing her persevere in her error and audacity, holding that which she should not, without repenting, He left her to her own devices.

For this reason, it happened that through the succession of the dominion she held as a dowry <note>original: "dota," used here metaphorically to describe the papal office as her possession</note> and maintained with such wicked audacity, this woman—who had previously maintained a reputation for perfect virtue—finding herself in such a high position as the Papacy, began to burn with the fires of lust. 

Nor did she, who for so long had known how to feign being a man, lack the craft or the method to hide her lewdness. Having found one who lived in service <note>original: "moro," likely a variant of "dimorò," meaning one who dwelt in the papal household</note> to the successor of Saint Peter and was pleasing to her desires, it followed that the female Pope conceived a child. O wicked and most unworthy vice! O invincible patience of God! 

But what followed was this: she who had so long known how to deceive and hide the unholy <note>original: "linceſtuoſo," though literally "incestuous," here refers to the sacrilegious nature of a Pope having a child</note> birth from the eyes of men, found her ingenuity failed her in concealing it. Being closer to her term than she realized, while traveling from the annual <term>Janiculum</term> <note>One of the hills of Rome; the text refers to a traditional procession route</note> to celebrate the sacred rites at <term>Saint John Lateran</term> <note>The cathedral church of Rome and the official ecclesiastical seat of the Bishop of Rome</note>, between the Colosseum and the Church of <term>San Clemente</term>, without calling for any midwife, she gave birth to a child in public. Thus it was made manifest with what fraud and deceit she had for so long deceived all men, except for her lover. 

Consequently, cast out from the Papacy by the Cardinals into the outer darkness <note>A biblical reference to being cast into hell or exile</note> with her newborn son, the wretched woman passed away. In response to such detestable filth, and as a lasting memory of her name to this day, the Supreme Pontiffs, with that [ceremony] and... <note>The text ends abruptly here, likely referring to the legendary "sedia stercoraria" ritual used to verify a new Pope's biological sex</note>

## Concerning Empress Irene

Of the said Constantine: <note>Referring to Emperor Constantine V, mentioned on the previous page</note> after she was made empress: she bore a son by her husband: who was named Constantine: and after her husband Leo died: she proceeded to rule the empire with her young son for a period of ten years. ### Concerning Empress Irene

...his concubine <note>original: "dora," likely referring to Theodote</note> and crowned her. Because of this scandalous outrage <note>Constantine VI’s controversial second marriage, which alienated the Church</note>, the shrewd and prudent woman Irene, knowing and judging that she could regain the sovereignty through gifts and treasures, spent generously upon the princes and barons. Immediately setting her hand to her treasure, which she had gathered during the time she ruled and had hidden in that same palace where she resided, by donating most lavishly she inclined the minds of all the princes toward her own desires. With those very same men who had previously deposed her from power, she saw to it that they seized the aforementioned Constantine—who had previously deprived her of her rank—and they deprived him of his state and his eyes <note>Constantine VI was blinded in the same purple chamber where he was born</note>. And thus this high-spirited woman regained the empire that had been taken from her; and Constantine her son finished his days and died shortly thereafter. 

But after this excellent woman had ruled for another five <note>The OCR "y" is likely a transcription of the numeral "v"</note> years and once again governed magnificently, she was besieged in the Eleutherios Palace by Nicephorus, a rebel against her. For he, having already taken the crown of the empire from Tarasius <note>original: "Caresiano," a misspelling of the Patriarch Tarasius</note>, the Patriarch of Constantinople, and being favored by the patricians Leo and Triphyllius and the <term>Sacellarius</term> <note>a high-ranking administrative official, in this case, likely the treasurer Ethikos</note>, they went to Irene with humility and flattery. She, knowing all that was happening, asked for nothing of the empire except for the palace where she lived. To obtain the things she requested, she opened all her treasures to them. 

Once that wicked man had obtained these things, breaking and betraying every promise, he confined her and banished her to Lesbos, where, as an old woman, she finished her life. It is true that some others write differently regarding her end, saying that because the mother and son were in such discord and had deprived each other of the rule of the empire several times, the Romans took the empire from them and gave it to **Charlemagne** <note>original: "Carlo Magno"</note>.

It concludes by noting the historical theory that the instability of her reign led to the crowning of Charlemagne as Holy Roman Emperor.</summary>

In which monastery, having grown old, she died.

## Concerning Gualdrada of Florence

**Concerning Gualdrada, a Florentine maiden: who, while the Emperor was in Florence, was given in marriage by the said Emperor because of a certain answer she made to her father in his presence.**

Gualdrada was of most illustrious origin, from the Ravignani <note>A prominent noble family in medieval Florence whose palace once stood near the present-day Piazza della Signoria</note> in the history of our city. She is counted among famous women, and not without merit, for the freedom of her mind and the noble boldness she displayed before the Prince of the Romans in defending the purity of her soul. For while she was in the temple once dedicated to Mars—later dedicated to God under the name of John the Baptist <note>The Florence Baptistery; medieval Florentine tradition held that it was built upon the ruins of a Roman temple to Mars</note>—with many Florentine women during the celebration of his feast, it happened that she caught the eye of Otto IV <note>Holy Roman Emperor, reigned 1198–1215</note>, Emperor of the Romans. At that time, finding himself in our city and honoring the said feast with his presence, he entered the temple; and looking out from his place at the citizens and the ladies standing about, among all the others, his eye came to rest and fixed upon her.

## Concerning Gualdrada of Florence

In Gualdrada—whose shapely beauty, youthful age, and simple attire differed in no way from her virtuous character—Caesar <note>The Emperor Otto IV, who is referred to here by the imperial title "Caesar"</note> marveled, praising her maidenly gravity. A citizen named Bilincione—a man venerable in age, nobility, and knighthood <note>term: "cauallaria" (knighthood or chivalry)</note>—was standing before him. The Emperor turned his words to him, saying: "I pray you, tell me who that maiden is sitting opposite you, who in the beauty of her face and body, and in her grave virtue, exceeds and surpasses all the others."

To which Bilincione replied: "Most Serene Prince, she is such that if you wish it, and I command it, she will kiss you." When the maiden heard these words, she was filled with indignation, suffering bitterly that her father should have such a light opinion of her complete constancy, and that he held such little guard over her virginal modesty <note>original: "pudor"</note> and honor.

Unable to let the offense pass for long without speaking, she stood up before the Prince, her face entirely flushed with a crimson color. Raising her eyes somewhat toward her father and then lowering them to the ground, she spoke with a voice that was not trembling, but firm <note>original: "uirile" (manly); used here to denote a steadfast and resolute tone of voice unusual for a young woman of the period</note>, saying: 

"My most reverend father, be silent. I pray you, say no more, for unless force is used against me, no one shall have me except him to whom you join me in lawful marriage <note>original: "legitimo matrimonio"</note>; only he shall have from me that which you have offered so lavishly."

O good God! Never was that which was so well and cheerfully spoken from the soul of a maiden allowed to fall unheeded. Caesar stood for a while, marveling within himself; he understood from her virginal breast that her purpose was chaste and holy. And after he had praised the maiden's indignation and her words with a long speech, he summoned a noble youth named Guido. So that the maiden would not have to wait long for that "honest kiss" (in his opinion), the Emperor endowed her with a magnificent dowry and married her to the aforementioned Guido in the presence of her father. They consider...

The noble lineage <note>term: "prosapia" (ancestry or lineage)</note> continues through a vast number of men. It has pleased me to say these things to the shame of modern women, who are of such levity of spirit and such unbridled customs that with their eyes and actions they seem to want to throw themselves into the arms of whoever looks at them.

## Concerning Constance, Empress of the Romans and Queen of Sicily, daughter of King William of Sicily, and married to Henry, Emperor and King of Rome.

This Constance was the daughter of William, the excellent King of Sicily. At her birth—as many say—the Abbot <term>Joachim of Calabria</term> <note>Joachim of Fiore (c. 1135–1202), a famous mystic and theologian known for his apocalyptic prophecies</note>, being present and endowed with a prophetic spirit, said to King William: that his daughter would be the future desolation of the Kingdom of Sicily. Stupefied and terrified by this news, the King began to think and turn over in his mind how this could come to pass through this woman. He saw no other way except through a husband or a son that she might have. Having compassion for his kingdom, he decided by counsel that every opportunity for marriage and children should be taken away; by shutting her in a monastery, he made her promise perpetual virginity to God. # Concerning Constance the Empress

In such ways, we fools and weaklings wish to dispose our forces and ingenuity: we are frustrated by a small and unique impulse. After her father had died, and no others of the lineage remaining but her, and all her youth having passed and ended: <term>Tancred</term> <note>Tancred of Lecce (r. 1189–1194), a cousin of Constance who seized the throne</note> having taken the crown as a petty king, and then after him his son William <note>William III of Sicily, the last of the Norman kings</note>, who was still a youth, followed. Whether because of too frequent or less than worthy successions of kings, or through the actions of the greater lords, battle was born from every pretext; with iron and fire the whole kingdom seemed to be in dissolution and ruin. 

For which reason, some having compassion for such great misfortune, it occurred to their minds what eventually followed: that Constance should be given as a wife to some fit and notable prince, so that by his work and power the lands might be pacified and these pestilential tumults removed. And this was not obtained without guile or deceit, and even then with great effort. The <term>Supreme Pontiff</term> <note>Pope Celestine III, who granted the dispensation for Constance to leave her convent</note> consented to it, working with his authority and a dispensation so that she could renounce the vow she had made. 

She, however, stood firm and immobile in her profession <note>her monastic life and vows of celibacy</note>, for it seemed to her that her advanced age should resist [marriage]. But because she renounced the marriage and did not wish to consent, matters were already so far advanced that they could almost not be turned back; almost as if by necessity, she was married to <term>Henry</term> <note>original: "Arigo"; Emperor Henry VI (1165–1197)</note>, Emperor of Rome, the son of Frederick the First <note>original: "Federicho pro."; Frederick I, known as Barbarossa</note>. 

And so the aged woman, leaving the sacred cloisters, became Empress, entering the chamber of the prince and attaining the nuptial bed. Because of this, a thing occurred that caused no small wonder to those who heard it: that being fifty-five years of age, she conceived. And because this conception was viewed with suspicion <note>many doubted a woman of 55 could conceive, leading to rumors that the baby was not hers</note>, to remove the suspicion it was ordained that as the time of the birth approached, all the noble women of the kingdom of Sicily were called who wished to be...

## Concerning Constance the Empress

...present at the birth <note>original: "pto," abbreviation for "parto" (birth)</note>. In the sight of everyone, quite clearly, the elderly Empress gave birth to a son to whom she gave the name Frederick <note>Frederick II (1194–1250), Holy Roman Emperor</note>; and he became a monstrous man. He was a pestilence not only to Sicily but to all of Italy, so that the prophecy of the **Calabrian Abbot** <note>Joachim of Fiore (c. 1135–1202), a mystic who supposedly prophesied that Constance's offspring would be a "viper" that would destroy the world</note> would not be empty. Who, then, would not judge the conception of the aforementioned Constance as monstrous, considering that we have heard of none other like it, not only in our times, but since the arrival of Aeneas in Italy? The only exception is that of Elizabeth, wife of Zacharias, from whom was born John the Baptist; among those born of women, there has not arisen a second like him.

## Concerning Camiola of Siena, Widow

She did this on the condition that he would become her husband; however, the said Roland later did not wish to keep the promise.

# Concerning Camiola of Siena

During the reign of **Frederick III, King of the island of Sicily** <note>Frederick III of Sicily (reigned 1295–1337)</note>, following the death of his heirs, she came to possess all royal luxuries, preserving them with great honor and integrity. When Frederick died, his son **Peter** <note>Peter II of Sicily (reigned 1337–1342)</note> took his place. A great fleet was assembled at Messina by the said King, which—under the leadership and command of **Giovanni de Chiaramonte** <note>A powerful Sicilian nobleman and military leader</note>, a warlike man in those times—set out to aid the inhabitants of **Lipari** <note>The largest of the Aeolian Islands, north of Sicily</note> and its districts, who were then in a state of extreme misery. 

In this fleet went not only those hired for pay, but all the other coastal dwellers, auxiliaries, and the people of Messina, all seeking to acquire the glory of arms. The castle of Lipari was besieged and hemmed in by the vigorous and most valiant man **Guiffredo de Quilatio** <note>Godfrey of Ghighini, an admiral serving the House of Anjou</note>, then the naval prefect of **Robert, King of Jerusalem** <note>Robert the Wise, King of Naples (reigned 1309–1343), who held the title King of Jerusalem</note>. This Guiffredo, through his sudden encirclement and continuous assaults and battles, hoped to take the castle by breaking their ranks. 

However, having learned from signals and the lookout galleys that the enemy fleet was approaching—and that it was much larger than his own—Guiffredo gathered his ships together in good order and withdrew to a safe place, deciding to wait upon fortune. But when the enemy forces arrived and found the siege lifted without any opposition, they provided the Liparians with aid and supplies. 

Because of this glory and the events that followed, Giovanni de Chiaramonte, swollen with pride, challenged and incited Guiffredo to battle. Guiffredo put his forces in order and went to find the enemy. Giovanni, who did not think that Guiffredo would even dare to start the battle—let alone stand the sight of such a great fleet—had not organized his forces for combat, but only to hunt down fugitives. ## Concerning Camiola of Siena

...at first sight, he <note>Referring to Guiffredo de Quilatio, the Neapolitan admiral mentioned on the previous page</note> repented, but it was too late to have asked for that which he did not think he could obtain. Greatly doubting his chances of victory, nevertheless, so as not to show his fear, he ordered his fleet into battle as best he could according to the circumstances. No sooner had he given the signal to his men than the enemies were upon them, engaging in a most fierce and harsh battle. In the end, the **Sicilians** turned their backs and fled; many of the Sicilian ships were sunk by Guiffredo’s men, many were captured, and few were those who escaped. **Giovanni de Chiaramonte**, the prefect of the fleet, was taken prisoner.

After this battle and Guiffredo’s victory, the castle of **Lipari** <note>A strategic island stronghold</note> having already surrendered, he braved many sea storms to lead all the captives, bound in chains, to **Naples**. There, they were locked in prison, including **Roland**, the bastard son of **King Frederick** <note>Frederick III of Sicily</note>. He was a man of strong body, young, and handsome of person and face. While all the other notable prisoners were eventually freed, he alone remained in prison. Abandoned by every living soul, he remained there sad and sorrowful.

For **King Peter** <note>Peter II of Sicily, Roland's half-brother</note>, whose duty it was to free him as his brother, did not care for him; rather, because of the poorly executed battle fought against his command, the King held him in hatred and contempt. While Roland was thus in prison, almost beyond all hope of freedom, it happened by chance that he came to the memory of **Camiola**. Once she saw that he was entirely abandoned by his brothers, she felt compassion for his misfortune. 

She resolved within herself to see if she could honorably restore him to liberty. Reflecting on this, and seeing no other way to do so while preserving the honor of her reputation <note>original: "honeſtade," referring to her sexual propriety and social standing</note> except by taking him as her husband, she sent certain good men to him to secretly ask him if, with such...
