# Concerning Camiola of Siena

Having made the pact <note>original: "facti pacti"; this refers to the legal agreement where Camiola would pay his ransom in exchange for marriage</note> and wishing to leave prison, they very easily obtained the request from him. And so, having observed every ceremony and legal requirement of marriage in his person through the consent of his <term>proxy</term> <note>A person authorized to act on behalf of another; in this case, a legal stand-in for the marriage contract while Roland was still imprisoned</note>; and having paid four thousand ounces of silver, Roland, upon leaving prison, returned to **Messina**. 

Once he arrived there, he behaved toward his **Camiola** no differently than if he had never spoken a word about the marriage. **Marvellous Camiola**, upon hearing of Roland’s ingratitude, felt a strong indignation within herself. But so it would not seem she was moved by mere anger, she calmly requested that he fulfill the **sacred wedding vows**. He, however, denied every act, saying that nothing of the sort had happened and that he knew nothing of this matter. 

Camiola had him summoned before the **ecclesiastical judge** <note>A church official who presided over cases involving marriage and moral conduct</note>. And with the documents and the testimony of honorable men, she proved her case against him. After he had confessed to the matter with great shame and recognized the benefit that Camiola had done for him, he replied—having been persuaded by his brothers and friends to consent to the woman’s demand—that he now asked for her hand and requested the wedding.

This woman, being of a noble soul, seeing Roland asking for that which he had previously denied in the presence of so many people, spoke thus: 

"Roland, I have great reason to give thanks to God; because before my chastity was lowered or broken under the pretext of marriage, you showed the perfidy of your iniquity. That same God has been favorable to me—the very God whose most holy name you strove to mock and deceive with false <term>perjury</term> <note>The act of lying under a sacred oath</note>. And by clear reason, I have rejected and disproved your lie. This outcome, concerning you and your marriage, is of the greatest importance to me; and I further believe that you, while held in prison, thought that I, a woman..."

->V<-

...acted in such a crude and ignorant way <note>original: "tracta & indocta"; likely referring to Roland's behavior or his assumption of her motives</note>. But I was moved by the memory of the favors my father received from your parent—if indeed King Frederick <note>Frederick III of Sicily (r. 1296–1337)</note> was your father. That is a thing I can hardly believe: that from such a celebrated and noble prince could be born such an unfaithful and **dishonest** son. You considered it an unworthy thing that a widow, not of royal blood, should have a husband of royal blood who is young, robust, and beautiful of body and face. This I freely and fully admit. 

But truly, I desire that if you are willing, or if you even know how, you answer me with reason regarding what I shall say. When, in order to make you mine, I paid the vast treasure for your freedom, where then was your royal splendor? Where was your strong vigor? Where was the fresh color of your face? Because of the dark <term>caligine</term> <note>A thick, heavy darkness or gloom</note> of the prison in which you were confined, all these things were covered in rust and the weight of chains, and by the stench of that dark dungeon. 

In that place—powerless, sick, and miserable, abandoned by everyone—you were rotting and decaying in shackles. Did you possess these "gifts" then, which now make you so proud and haughty? These qualities for which you now exalt yourself? When you were captured and brought low, back then you called me worthy—not only of a royal youth, but of a celestial god. 

Oh, how easily and lightly, you most wicked man, as soon as you have seen all your hopes realized and the sky of your homeland regained, you have changed your mind! You have forgotten, now that you are free, that I, Camiola, am the one who remembered you. I alone had mercy on you and your misfortune. I alone, for your safety, paid out my substance and my riches. I, Camiola, am she who freed you with my own <term>pecunia</term> <note>Money or property; derived from the Latin for cattle/wealth</note> from the hands of your mortal enemies.

<summary>Camiola continues her scathing rebuke of Roland, contrasting his current arrogance with the pathetic, decaying state he was in when she rescued him from prison. She highlights his ingratitude, noting that he only found her "worthy" when he was desperate and forgotten by everyone else.</summary>

<keywords>Camiola Turinga, Roland of Sicily, King Frederick, ransom, ingratitude, nobility, prison, 14th century Sicily</keywords>

# Concerning Camiola of Siena
->V ii<-

...account, I raised you up in the hope of returning to your homeland. Now that you have returned and been recalled into the former light <note>The "light" of freedom and social standing, as opposed to the darkness of the dungeon mentioned previously</note>, I remind you and bring back to your memory those things which you yourself ought to remember, and which you cannot deny. 

But for such memorable favors, you have rendered me such a "reward" that you have had the audacity to deny our established marriage. If you could have, you would have stained me with a foul mark and shame. You were ashamed that a widow, born of a [common] man, should be your wife. Oh, how much more just and holy a thing it would have been to have been ashamed to break your pledged faith, and to have held the celebrated name of God in such low esteem! Through your execrable ingratitude, you have demonstrated how abundant you are in vice. 

I confess that I am not a royal woman. But because from my childhood I was always raised and remained in the company of royal women, it is no wonder that I have adopted a spirit of royal customs—which is quite enough to take on royal nobility. But concerning these many matters, I will be happy for you in this: that though you used your strength to oppose me and denied being my husband (even though you truly were, and I have proven you to be mine in court), now that you are not mine by your own will, I grant it to you. I consent. 

Let yours be that royal <term>claritade</term> <note>original: "claritade"; the splendor, fame, or "brightness" associated with high noble birth</note>, now stained and shamed by so many marks of infidelity. Yours be the youthful strength; yours the fleeting <note>original: "caducha," meaning perishable or transitory</note> handsomeness and beauty. From this hour forward, I shall be content with my widowhood. And that fortune and wealth which the highest and omnipotent God has lent and granted to me, I shall leave to more honest heirs than those would have been who...

<summary>Camiola continues her rebuke of Roland, condemning his attempt to deny their marriage after she rescued him. She ultimately rejects him, choosing to keep her dignity and her fortune rather than remain tied to an ungrateful husband.</summary>
<keywords>Camiola of Siena, Roland of Sicily, betrayal, ingratitude, nobility of spirit, marriage law, widowhood, inheritance</keywords>

--- Page 312 ---
<meta>This page concludes the story of Camiola Turinga (a 14th-century Sienese widow living in Sicily) and her rejection of the ungrateful Roland. ...not generated by you. Go then, away, ill-fated and most wretched youth. And since you have made yourself unworthy of possessing me, learn at your own expense with what arts, deceits, mocks, and frauds you might treat other women. For me, it is enough to have been deceived by you once. For this reason, I have firmly resolved in my mind never to be with you, but to preserve a celebrated and holy life. I commit myself to God, and I believe this is by far better than coming into your embraces.

And having spoken these and other similar words, she departed from his confused presence. From that hour forward, she could never be moved from her laudable purpose, whether by entreaties or prayers. Roland, confounded and repenting too late of his <term>ignavia</term> <note>original: "ignauía"; meaning cowardice, sloth, or a lack of noble spirit</note>, was despised by all and held in low esteem. With his face lowered to the ground, not daring to look at his brothers or even the common people, he went away into misfortune. He did not have the courage to reclaim by right that which he had renounced through deceit and fraud.

But King Peter <note>Peter III of Aragon, who was then King of Sicily</note> and all the lords and noble men marveled at the generous and high spirit of the woman; they exalted her with marvelous praises, uncertain which was more commendable: the tenacity of the woman Camiola in ransoming Roland with such a great sum of money, or—having already redeemed and conquered him—her courageous rejection and renunciation of him as one undeserving and unworthy.

->### Concerning Joanna, Queen of Jerusalem and Sicily<-

**J**oanna, Queen of Jerusalem and Sicily, was an illustrious woman, surpassing all other women of our time in lineage, character, and power. Concerning her, were it not that omitting her would seem like an act of malice, it would be a more sacred thing to remain silent than to have written so few things about her. <note>The author suggests that Joanna's life is so significant and complex that a brief summary may not do her justice.</note>

She was, therefore, the daughter of the most serene prince Charles, the illustrious Duke of Calabria and the first-born son of Robert of celebrated memory, King of Jerusalem and Sicily; and of Mary, sister of Philip, King of France. If we wished to examine her ancestors and trace them back to the very end, we would not stop until, ascending through the long line of kings, we came to Dardanus <note>The mythical founder of Troy</note>, the first founder of Ilium <note>Troy</note>, whose father the ancients said was Jove <note>Jupiter</note>. From such an ancient <term>prosapia</term> <note>original: "prosapia"; meaning family stock, lineage, or long-standing ancestry</note> so many distinguished princes have come, from here and there, that there is no King among Christians who is not her partner or relative. And thus, none in our days or those of our fathers shone or gleamed in a more noble fashion.

While she was still a little girl, her father Charles was taken away by an untimely death. King Robert, her grandfather, having no other offspring as an heir of the "better sex" <note>original: "megliore sexo"; a Renaissance-era reference to a male heir, as men were legally preferred for succession</note>, she was [chosen] by reason and by right. And also thus with...

<summary>This page introduces Joanna I of Naples, emphasizing her incomparable noble pedigree, which the author traces back to the mythical founders of Troy. It details her family connections to the French and Neapolitan crowns and notes her status as heir following the death of her father, Charles of Calabria.</summary>
<keywords>Joanna I of Naples, Queen of Jerusalem, Robert the Wise, Charles of Calabria, Dardanus, Trojan ancestry, royal succession, Neapolitan history</keywords>

## On Queen Joanna, Queen of Jerusalem

King Robert <note>Robert the Wise (1275–1343), King of Naples</note> ordered that after his death, she should succeed him as the heir to his kingdoms. And truly, this inheritance was not located at the ends of the earth—beyond the **torrid zone** <note>In ancient geography, the region around the equator thought too hot for habitation</note> or among the **Sarmatians** <note>People of the far north/east, used here to mean the edge of the known world</note> beneath the icy pole—but between the Adriatic and Tyrrhenian seas, from Umbria and Piceno to the ancient homeland of the **Volsci** <note>An ancient Italic tribe in Latium</note>, extending down to the Sicilian sea, contained under a mild sky. Within these borders, her commands are obeyed by the ancient Lucani, Brutii, Salentini, Calabresi, Daunians, Vestini, Samnites, Peligni, Marsi, and many others.

I shall set aside for now the greater matters, such as the Kingdom of Jerusalem, the island of Sicily, and the territory of Piedmont in **Cisalpine Gaul** <note>Northern Italy</note>; these regions are currently occupied by the injury of usurpers <note>A reference to the Aragonese control of Sicily and the loss of Angevin territories in the north and the Levant</note>. And likewise the "Seventh Province" <note>Provence, in modern-day France</note> between Gaul, Narbonne, the Rhone, and the Alps, and the County of **Forcalquier**, equally obey her commands and acknowledge her as their Lady and their Queen. 

Oh, how many illustrious cities are in these regions! How many noble castles are held; how many seaports and shelters for sailors; how many rivers and lakes; how many medicinal springs; how many forests and woods abundant with game; and how many rich and fertile fields. Think of the great number of notable people—counts and barons—and the vast opulence and abundance of all things necessary and useful for human life.

Truly, it would not be an easy or light task to explain it all. Because this dominion is so vast, and not customarily possessed by women, it should be regarded as no less a miracle than a mark of her fame, if we choose to look closely. And what is a much more wonderful thing, and enough to clear her reputation, she retains so many of the noble customs of her ancestors; for since she was content using the

<summary>This page describes the vast geographic extent of Queen Joanna I's inheritance, listing the diverse Italian and French territories under her rule while noting those currently held by usurpers. The author emphasizes that her successful rule over such a large domain is a "miracle" for a woman of her time.</summary>

# On Queen Joanna, Queen of Jerusalem

with much valor and virtue, she purged and cleaned the country of those men who infested not only the cities and inhabited places, but also the rough woods and harsh lands. These men were all so frightened and trembling that they were forced to flee or lock themselves away in the high mountains or fortresses. Against them, she sent great armies of **men-at-arms** <term>Heavy cavalrymen, typically of noble birth, who served as the elite strike force of medieval armies</term> under noble dukes; she did not merely besiege them, but captured the aforementioned fortresses and put to death the thieves and wicked men. This was a feat that none of the preceding Kings either wished to do, knew how to do, or was able to achieve. But in such a manner has she restored the lands she possesses that not only the poor, but even the rich man can go safely by day and night, singing <note>original: "cantando"; this is a literary trope suggesting that the roads are so safe from bandits that even a wealthy person carrying gold feels relaxed enough to sing</note>.

And furthermore, which is no less beneficial: she has so restrained the other men and great lords of the realm, drawing them back from their dissolute customs with such modesty, that they have abandoned that ancient pride with which they used to despise past Kings. Today, they all tremble before the face of the said Queen. Moreover, she is so prompt and so shrewd that you might sooner deceive her by fraud than outwit her; she is magnanimous, possessing a **masculine spirit** <note>original: "uerile animo"; in the 14th century, this was a high compliment for a female ruler, suggesting she possessed the strength and decisiveness typically associated with men</note> rather than a feminine one. She is grateful and mindful of services received, and so constant in her purpose that anyone who seeks to move her from her intent and sacred purpose labors in vain.

This was made quite manifest in the past by the harsh insults of cruel and bitter **Fortune** <note>The personification of fate (Fortuna), often depicted as a goddess turning a wheel that brings rulers up or down</note>. She was struck by these insults with sharp movements, agitated, surrounded, and troubled with various tribulations. For first she endured the internal discords of the royal brothers <note>Likely referring to the power struggles between the different branches of the House of Anjou, including the Hungarian and Durazzo lines</note> and foreign battles fought many times within the heart of her realm; she also suffered flights into exile due to the sins of others, and the exi- <margin>exi-</margin>

<summary>This page praises Queen Joanna I for suppressing banditry and taming the pride of the nobility, while highlighting her "masculine" strength in the face of political turmoil and the "insults of Fortune."</summary>
<keywords>Joanna I of Naples, law and order, banditry, masculine spirit, House of Anjou, Fortune, political stability, 14th century Italy</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the biography of Queen Joanna I of Naples (here titled Queen of Jerusalem), moving from her political achievements to her personal fortitude in the face of domestic and ecclesiastical trials, followed by the printer's colophon.</meta>

# On Queen Joanna, Queen of Jerusalem

...the harsh and fierce behaviors of her husbands <note>original: "asperi: e feri costumi dei mariti"; Joanna I was married four times. Her first husband, Andrew of Hungary, was murdered in a conspiracy that many at the time—perhaps unfairly—blamed on her.</note>, the envies of noblemen, a sinister and unmerited bad reputation, and the threats of the High Pontiff <note>original: "sommo pontifice"; as the ruler of Naples, Joanna was a vassal of the Pope, leading to frequent political friction.</note>, as well as many other things. All of these she had to endure and overcome with a strong breast; she surpasses and overcomes all these things with an upright and unconquered spirit. And certainly, these would be great deeds not only for a female Queen, but for any robust and valiant King. Furthermore, she possesses loveliness and physical beauty: a fine appearance, a gracious and gentle face, mild speech, and an eloquence pleasing to all, as is fitting for solemn majesty. Thus, where reason and the needs of her household require it, she is sweet, humane, pious, and kind to her own, so that they ought not, nor can they, say she is their Queen, but rather their companion—and what greater quality can be found even in a most prudent and most valiant King?

It would undoubtedly be too long a speech if one wished to explain and recount every detail of her intellect and the integrity of her mind. Because all these virtues are found within her, I judge her not only excellent and marvelous for her splendid fame, but the singular splendor of Italy <note>original: "singulare splendore de Italia"; Boccaccio, who wrote the original Latin text, was a contemporary of Joanna and spent time at her court in Naples.</note>, the likes of which has never been seen in the past.

->THE END<-
